## IV Chitterlow / 四 奇特洛
===Original===

The hour of the class on the following Thursday found Kipps in a state of nearly incredible despondency. He was sitting with his eyes on the reading room clock, his chin resting on his fists and his elbows on the accumulated comic papers that were comic alas! in vain! He paid no heed to the little man in spectacles glaring opposite to him, famishing for *Fun*. In this place it was he had sat night after night, each night more blissful than the last, waiting until it should be time to go to Her! And then﻿—bliss! And now the hour had come and there was no class! There would be no class now until next October; it might be there would never be a class so far as he was concerned again.
It might be there would never be a class again, for Shalford, taking exception at a certain absentmindedness that led to mistakes and more particularly to the ticketing of several articles in Kipps’ Manchester window upside down, had been “on to” him for the past few days in an exceedingly onerous manner.﻿ ﻿…
He sighed profoundly, pushed the comic papers back﻿—they were rent away from him instantly by the little man in spectacles﻿—and tried the old engravings of Folkestone in the past, that hang about the room. But these, too, failed to minister to his bruised heart. He wandered about the corridors for a time and watched the library indicator for awhile. Wonderful thing that! But it did not hold him for long. People came and laughed near him and that jarred with him dreadfully. He went out of the building and a beastly cheerful barrel organ mocked him in the street. He was moved to a desperate resolve to go down to the beach. There it might be he would be alone. The sea might be rough﻿—and attuned to him. It would certainly be dark.
“If I ’ad a penny I’m blest if I wouldn’t go and chuck myself off the end of the pier.﻿ ﻿… *She’d* never miss me.﻿ ﻿…” He followed a deepening vein of thought.
“Penny though! It’s tuppence,” he said after a space.
He went down Dover Street in a state of profound melancholia﻿—at the pace and mood as it were of his own funeral procession﻿—and he crossed at the corner of Tontine Street heedless of all mundane things. And there it was that Fortune came upon him, in disguise and with a loud shout, the shout of a person endowed with an unusually rich, full voice, followed immediately by a violent blow in the back.

===Chinese===

第二个星期四，上课的钟点到了，基普斯（Kipps）却陷在一种近乎难以置信的消沉里。他坐在阅览室里，眼睛盯着钟，下巴抵在两个拳头上，胳膊肘支在一摞漫画报上——唉，这些漫画再逗乐也是白搭！对面那个戴眼镜的小个子正冲他瞪眼，如饥似渴地等着那份《趣闻》（*Fun*）。正是在这地方，他一夜又一夜地坐过，一夜比一夜更幸福，等着到点去见她！然后——便是至福！可如今钟点到了，课却没有了！要到十月里才再有课；就他而论，说不定永远不会再有课了。
说不定永远不会再有课了，因为沙尔福德（Shalford）对他这几天来某种心不在焉的毛病挑刺——那毛病叫他出差错，尤其是叫他负责的曼彻斯特橱窗里好几件商品的标签挂倒了——这几天一直跟他「过不去」，那架势苦不堪言。……
他长叹一声，把漫画报往回一推——戴眼镜的小个子一把就抢了过去——又去看屋里挂着的那些福克斯通（Folkestone）往昔的老版画。可这些也无从抚慰他受伤的心。他在走廊里转悠了一阵，又对着图书指示牌看了一会儿。妙极了的玩意儿！可也没拴住他多久。人们在近旁谈笑，吵得他心烦意乱之极。他走出大楼，街上一架手摇风琴欢快得可恶，像在嘲弄他。他横下一条心，往海滩去。在那儿兴许能独自待着。海也许正起浪——正合他的心境。天肯定也黑了。
「我要是有个便士，我不跳下防波堤头儿才怪呢。……*她*才不会想我呢。……”他顺着这条思路越陷越深。
「便士！得两便士。」过了半晌他说。
他顺着多佛街往下走，满心凄凉——那步子和心绪，倒像给自己送殡——在唐廷街街口过马路时，人间万事全都浑然不觉。就在那儿，命运女神找上了他：化了装，大喝一声——那嗓音出奇地浑厚洪亮——紧接着背上重重挨了一记。

===Original===

His hat was over his eyes and an enormous weight rested on his shoulders and something kicked him in the back of his calf.
Then he was on all fours in some mud that Fortune, in conjunction with the Folkestone corporation and in the pursuit of equally mysterious ends, had heaped together even lavishly for his reception.
He remained in that position for some seconds awaiting further developments and believing almost anything broken before his heart. Gathering at last that this temporary violence of things in general was over, and being perhaps assisted by a clutching hand, he arose, and found himself confronting a figure holding a bicycle and thrusting forward a dark face in anxious scrutiny.
“You aren’t hurt, Matey?” gasped the figure.
“Was that *you* ’it me?” said Kipps.
“It’s these handles, you know,” said the figure with an air of being a fellow sufferer. “They’re too *low*. And when I go to turn, if I don’t remember, Bif!﻿—and I’m *in* to something.”
“Well﻿—you give me a oner in the back﻿—anyhow,” said Kipps, taking stock of his damages.
“I was coming down hill, you know,” explained the bicyclist. “These little Folkestone hills are a Fair Treat. It isn’t as though I’d been on the level. I came rather a whop.”
“You did *that*,” said Kipps.
“I was back pedalling for all I was worth anyhow,” said the bicyclist. “Not that I *am* worth much back pedalling.”
He glanced round and made a sudden movement almost as if to mount his machine. Then he turned as rapidly to Kipps again, who was now stooping down, pursuing the tale of his injuries.
“Here’s the back of my trouser leg all tore down,” said Kipps, “and I believe I’m bleeding. You reely ought to be more careful﻿—”
The stranger investigated the damage with a rapid movement. “Holy Smoke, so you are!” He laid a friendly hand on Kipps’ arm. “I say﻿—look here! Come up to my diggings and sew it up. I’m﻿—Of course I’m to blame, and I say﻿—” his voice sank to a confidential friendliness. “Here’s a slop. Don’t let on I ran you down. Haven’t a lamp, you know. Might be a bit awkward, for *me*.”
Kipps looked up towards the advancing policeman. The appeal to his generosity was not misplaced. He immediately took sides with his assailant. He stood up as the representative of the law drew nearer. He assumed an air which he considered highly suggestive of an accident not having happened.
“All right,” he said, “go on!”

===Chinese===

帽子扣到了眼睛上，肩上压着好大一个分量，小腿肚上又不知什么东西踹了一脚。
跟着他趴在了泥地里——这泥是命运女神会同福克斯通市政当局、为着同样神秘的目的，为他接驾而慷慨堆聚起来的。
他保持那个姿势待了几秒钟，静候事态进一步发展，只觉得自己身上几乎处处都断了，只有心还囫囵。后来他总算明白，天下万物这一场暂时的暴乱已经过去，或许还有一只手把他拽了起来；他站起身，面对着一个推自行车的人影，一张黑黑的脸探过来，焦灼地端详他。
「没伤着吧，老伙计？」那人影喘着气说。
「是*你*撞的我？」基普斯说。
「是这车把，你知道，」那人影摆出一副同病相怜的口气说。「装得太*低*了。我一拐弯，稍一忘记，砰！——就撞上什么东西了。」
「哼——反正你在我背上狠狠来了一下。」基普斯说着，清点起自己的损失来。
「我那会儿正下坡呢，你知道，」骑车人解释道。「福克斯通这些小山坡真够劲儿的。可不像在平地上跑。我摔得不轻。」
「可不是摔得不轻。」基普斯说。
「反正我玩命地倒蹬来着，」骑车人说。「倒蹬我也蹬不出多大名堂就是了。」
他四下一望，突然一动，几乎像要跨上车去。随即又飞快地转向基普斯，基普斯正弯着腰，继续查点伤情。
「我裤腿后头撕开了一大块，」基普斯说，「我看还出血了。你实在应该当心点儿——」
那陌生人飞快地查看了一下伤处。「好家伙，真出血了！」他把一只手友好地搭在基普斯胳膊上。「我说——这样吧！上我住的地方去，把它缝上。我——当然是我不好，我说——」他的声音低下来，成了推心置腹的口吻。「那边来了个警察。别说我撞了你。没点车灯，你知道。对我来说，也许有点麻烦。」
基普斯朝走近的警察望了一眼。这份求他高抬贵手的用心没白费。他立刻站到了撞他的人一边。法律的代表走近时，他站起身，摆出一副他认为足以暗示「并未发生事故」的神气。
「没事，」他说，「走吧！」

===Original===

“Right you are,” said the cyclist promptly, and led the way, and then, apparently with some idea of deception, called over his shoulder, “I’m tremendous glad to have met you, old chap.
“It really isn’t a hundred yards,” he said after they had passed the policeman, “it’s just round the corner.”
“Of course,” said Kipps, limping slightly. “I don’t want to get a chap into trouble. Accidents *will* happen. Still﻿—”
“Oh! *Rather!* I believe you. Accidents *will* happen. Especially when you get *me* on a bicycle.” He laughed. “You aren’t the first I’ve run down not by any manner of means! I don’t think you can be hurt much either. It isn’t as though I was scorching. You didn’t see me coming. I was back pedalling like anything. Only naturally it seems to you I must have been coming fast. And I did all I could to ease off the bump as I hit you. It was just the treadle I think came against your calf. But it was All Right of you about that policeman, you know. That was a Fair Bit of All Right. Under the Circs, if you’d told him I was riding it might have been forty bob! Forty bob! I’d have had to tell ’em Time is Money. Just now for Mr. H. C.
“I shouldn’t have blamed you either, you know. Most men after a bump like that might have been spiteful. The least I can do is to stand you a needle and thread. And a clothes brush. It isn’t everyone who’d have taken it like you.
“Scorching! Why if I’d been scorching you’d have﻿—coming as we did﻿—you’d have been knocked silly.
“But I tell you, the way you caught on about that slop was something worth seeing. When I asked you, I didn’t half expect it. Bif! Right off. Cool as a cucumber. Had your line at once. I tell you that there isn’t many men would have acted as you have done, I *will* say that. You acted like a gentleman over that slop.”
Kipps’ first sense of injury disappeared. He limped along a pace or so behind, making depreciatory noises in response to these flattering remarks and taking stock of the very appreciative person who uttered them.
As they passed the lamps he was visible as a figure with a slight anterior plumpness, progressing buoyantly on knickerbockered legs, with quite enormous calves, legs that, contrasting with Kipps’ own narrow practice, were even exuberantly turned out at the knees and toes.

===Chinese===

「好嘞，」骑车人应声说道，在前头带路，随即大约是耍了个障眼法，回头喊道，「遇见你真太高兴了，老伙计。」
「真不到一百码，」他们走过警察身边后他说，「一拐弯就到。」
「当然，」基普斯微微跛着说。「我不想叫人倒霉。意外*总是*难免的。不过——」
「哦！*可不！*我信你的。意外总是难免的。尤其一等我这样的骑上车。」他笑了。「撞倒的人里你可不是头一个，差得远呢！我看你伤得也不重。我又不是在飙车。你没看见我过来。我玩命地倒蹬来着。只是你自然觉得我一定来得好快。撞上你的时候，我还一个劲儿想法子减轻冲撞来着。我记得是脚蹬子磕着你的小腿肚。不过你刚才对那警察真够意思，你知道。真是够意思透了。换了那个情形，你要是供出我骑着车，说不定就是四十先令！四十先令！我就只好跟他们说时间就是金钱。这话眼下对 H. C. 先生尤其作数。
「换了别人我也不能怪你，你知道。挨这么一下，多数人都会怀恨。我能做的起码是请你针线一顿，外带一把衣刷。不是谁都像你这么想得开的。
「飙车！嗐，我要真在飙车，照咱们那个撞法——你早给撞糊涂了。
「可我告诉你，你方才会意那个警察的样子，真值得一看。我求你那会儿，压根儿没抱指望。砰！一口应下。凉快得像根黄瓜。词儿立马就有了。我告诉你，能像你这样行事的人可不多，这话我*一定*要说。你对那警察的事办得像个绅士。」
基普斯最初那点委屈烟消云散了。他落后一两步一瘸一拐地走着，对这些奉承话报以谦逊的咕哝，一边打量这位十分赏识他的人。
路灯下一望，那人身子前部微微发胖，两条灯笼裤腿迈得轻快，腿肚子大得惊人；那两条腿跟基普斯自己练出来的细腿一比，从膝盖到脚尖都外撇得生气勃勃。

===Original===

A cycling cap was worn very much on one side, and from beneath it protruded carelessly straight wisps of dark red hair, and ever and again an ample nose came into momentary view round the corner. The muscular cheeks of this person and a certain generosity of chin he possessed were blue shaven and he had no moustache. His carriage was spacious and confident, his gestures up and down the narrow deserted back street they traversed, were irresistibly suggestive of ownership; a suggestion of broadly gesticulating shadows were born squatting on his feet and grew and took possession of the road and reunited at last with the shadows of the infinite, as lamp after lamp was passed. Kipps saw by the flickering light of one of them that they were in Little Fenchurch Street, and then they came round a corner sharply into a dark court and stopped at the door of a particularly ramshackle looking little house, held up between two larger ones, like a drunken man between policemen.
The cyclist propped his machine carefully against the window, produced a key and blew down it sharply. “The lock’s a bit tricky,” he said, and devoted himself for some moments to the task of opening the door. Some mechanical catastrophe ensued and the door was open.
“You’d better wait here a bit while I get the lamp,” he remarked to Kipps; “very likely it isn’t filled,” and vanished into the blackness of the passage. “Thank God for matches!” he said, and Kipps had an impression of a passage in the transitory pink flare and the bicyclist disappearing into a further room. Kipps was so much interested by these things that for the time he forgot his injuries altogether.
An interval and Kipps was dazzled by a pink shaded kerosene lamp. “You go in,” said the red-haired man, “and I’ll bring in the bike,” and for a moment Kipps was alone in the lamp-lit room. He took in rather vaguely the shabby ensemble of the little apartment, the round table covered with a torn, red, glass-stained cover on which the lamp stood, a mottled looking-glass over the fireplace reflecting this, a disused gas bracket, an extinct fire, a number of dusty postcards and memoranda stuck round the glass, a dusty, crowded paper rack on the mantel with a number of cabinet photographs, a table littered with papers and cigarette ash and a syphon of soda water.

===Chinese===

一顶骑车的便帽歪戴在头上，帽檐下不修边幅地支棱出几绺暗红色直发，一只肥大的鼻子时不时从旁边闪现一下。这位先生肌肉发达的面颊和相当宽厚的下巴剃得发青，没留上髭。他步态轩昂自信，在那条空无一人的狭窄后街上指指画画，不由分说地让人觉得这条街也是他的产业；路灯一盏盏过去，他那挥洒宽阔的身影蹲落在脚边，生长起来，占据了整条街，最后又与无穷的夜影重新合为一体。基普斯借着一盏路灯闪烁的光看清，他们是在小芬彻奇街上，随后他们猛地拐过一个街角，进了一条黑黢黢的小巷，在一座看着特别破烂的小房子门前停下——那房子夹在两座大房子中间，活像醉汉夹在两个警察中间。
骑车人小心地把车支在窗前，掏出钥匙，对着锁眼狠狠吹了一口气。「这锁有点别扭，」他说，接着便专心致志地对付起开门的差事来。随即发生了一场机械上的灾难，门开了。
「你在这儿等一下，我去拿灯，」他对基普斯说，「多半还没灌油呢。」说罢没入过道的黑暗里。「感谢上帝还有火柴！」他说，粉红色的一瞬火光里，基普斯瞥见过道的模样，骑车人走进里屋不见了。基普斯看得津津有味，一时间把自己的伤全忘了。
过了一会儿，一盏罩着粉红罩子的煤油灯亮得基普睁不开眼。「你进去，」红头发的人说，「我把车搬进来。」基普斯独自在灯光下的屋里待了片刻。他迷迷糊糊地打量这间小屋寒酸的全套陈设：一张圆桌，铺着撕裂的、染了玻璃杯渍的红桌布，灯就立在桌上；壁炉上方一面斑驳的穿衣镜映着这一切；一个废弃的煤气壁架；一炉熄了的火；镜子周围贴着许多蒙尘的明信片和字条；壁炉架上落满灰尘、塞得满满的文件架上立着好些装框的相片；一张桌上乱堆着纸张、烟灰和一只苏打水瓶。

===Original===

Then the cyclist reappeared and Kipps saw his blue-shaved, rather animated face and bright-reddish, brown eyes for the first time. He was a man perhaps ten years older than Kipps, but his beardless face made them in a way contemporary.
“You behaved all right about that policeman﻿—anyhow,” he repeated as he came forward.
“I don’t see ’ow else I could ’ave done,” said Kipps quite modestly. The cyclist scanned his guest for the first time and decided upon hospitable details.
“We’d better let that mud dry a bit before we brush it. Whiskey there is, good old Methusaleh, Canadian Rye, and there’s some brandy that’s all right. Which’ll you have?”
“*I* dunno,” said Kipps, taken by surprise, and then seeing no other course but acceptance, “well﻿—whiskey, then.”
“Right you are, old boy, and if you’ll take my advice you’ll take it neat. I may not be a particular judge of this sort of thing, but I do know old Methusaleh pretty well. Old Methusaleh﻿—four stars. That’s me! Good old Harry Chitterlow and good old Methusaleh. Leave ’em together. Bif! He’s gone!”
He laughed loudly, looked about him, hesitated and retired, leaving Kipps in possession of the room and free to make a more precise examination of its contents.
He particularly remarked the photographs that adorned the apartment. They were chiefly photographs of ladies, in one case in tights, which Kipps thought a “bit ’ot,” but one represented the bicyclist in the costume of some remote epoch. It did not take Kipps long to infer that the others were probably actresses and that his host was an actor, and the presence of the half of a large, coloured playbill seemed to confirm this. A note framed in an Oxford frame that was a little too large for it, he presently demeaned himself to read. “Dear Mr. Chitterlow,” it ran its brief course, “if after all you will send the play you spoke of I will endeavour to read it,” followed by a stylish but absolutely illegible signature, and across this was written in pencil, “What price, Harry, now?” And in the shadow by the window was a rough and rather able sketch of the bicyclist in chalk on brown paper, calling particular attention to the curvature of the forward lines of his hull and calves and the jaunty carriage of his nose, and labelled unmistakably “Chitterlow.” Kipps thought it “rather a takeoff.” The papers on the table by the syphon were in manuscript.

===Chinese===

骑车人随后重新出现，基普斯这才头一回看清他那张剃得发青、颇为生动的脸和亮亮的红棕色眼睛。这人比基普斯大着十岁上下，但下巴上无须，倒显得两人算得上同辈。
「你对那警察的事办得漂亮——不管怎么说。」他走上前来又说了一遍。
「我想不出*还能*怎么办。」基普斯相当谦虚地说。骑车人头一回把客人上下打量了一遍，拿定了殷勤招待的主意。
「那滩泥先晾干点再刷。这儿有威士忌，老玛土撒拉，加拿大黑麦的；还有白兰地，也不赖。你喝哪样？」
「我*不知道*，」基普斯冷不防被问到，随即看出除了接受没有别的路，「那就——威士忌吧。」
「好嘞，老弟，你要肯听我的，就喝纯的。这种事我未必算得上行家，可老玛土撒拉我熟。老玛土撒拉——四星。说的就是我！好汉哈利·奇特洛（Harry Chitterlow），配上老玛土撒拉。把它俩搁一块儿。砰！完事！」
他放声大笑，四下望了望，犹豫了一下，退了出去，把屋子留给基普斯，任他细细考察屋里的物件。
他特别留意了点缀这间屋子的那些相片。多半是女士们的相片，有一张穿着紧身衣，基普斯觉得有点「辣」；还有一张照的是骑车人穿着某个远古时代的行头。基普斯没费多大工夫就推断出其余的想必都是女演员，主人是个演员，半张彩色大戏招贴的存在似乎也证实了这一点。有一封镶在牛津框里的信——框子对信来说大了点——他屈尊读了一遍。「亲爱的奇特洛先生，」短信一挥而就，「倘若您终究肯把您说过的那个剧本寄来，我当勉力一读。」署名花哨却全然无法辨认，信上用铅笔横批了一句：「哈利，如今作价几何？」窗口的阴影里有一张棕色纸上的粉笔素描，笔法粗犷而颇为高明，画的正是骑车人，特意强调了他前身和腿肚子的前部曲线、鼻子的得意洋洋，明确无误地标着「奇特洛」二字。基普斯觉得这画「挖苦得够味」。苏打水瓶旁边桌上的纸都是手稿。

===Original===

Kipps observed manuscript of a particularly convulsive and blottesque sort and running obliquely across the page.
Presently he heard the metallic clamour as if of a series of irreparable breakages with which the lock of the front door discharged its function, and then Chitterlow reappeared, a little out of breath as if from running and with a starry labelled bottle in his large, freckled hand.
“Sit down, old chap,” he said, “sit down. I had to go out for it after all. Wasn’t a solitary bottle left. However, it’s all right now we’re here. No, don’t sit on that chair, there’s sheets of my play on that. That’s the one﻿—with the broken arm. I think this glass is clean, but anyhow wash it out with a squizz of syphon and shy it in the fireplace. Here! I’ll do it! Lend it here!”
As he spoke Mr. Chitterlow produced a corkscrew from a table drawer, attached and overcame good old Methusaleh’s cork in a style a bartender might envy, washed out two tumblers in his simple, effectual manner, and poured a couple of inches of the ancient fluid into each. Kipps took his tumbler, said “Thenks” in an offhand way, and after a momentary hesitation whether he should say “here’s to you!” or not, put it to his lips without that ceremony. For a space fire in his throat occupied his attention to the exclusion of other matters, and then he discovered Mr. Chitterlow with an intensely bulldog pipe alight, seated on the opposite side of the empty fireplace and pouring himself out a second dose of whiskey.
“After all,” said Mr. Chitterlow, with his eye on the bottle and a little smile wandering to hide amidst his larger features, “this accident might have been worse. I wanted someone to talk to a bit, and I didn’t want to go to a pub, leastways not a Folkestone pub, because as a matter of fact I’d promised Mrs. Chitterlow, who’s away, not to, for various reasons, though of course if I’d wanted to I’m just that sort I should have all the same, and here we are! It’s curious how one runs up against people out bicycling!”
“Isn’t it!” said Kipps, feeling that the time had come for him to say something.
“Here we are, sitting and talking like old friends, and half an hour ago we didn’t know we existed. Leastways we didn’t know each other existed.

===Chinese===

基普斯看出那手稿写得歪歪扭扭、墨渍斑斑，一行行斜着爬过纸页。
不一会儿，他听见前门锁开动时那阵金属的喧嚣，仿佛一连串无可挽回的砸碎，随后奇特洛重新出现，微微喘着，像刚跑过步，大而有雀斑的手里拎着一瓶贴着星标的酒。
「坐下，老伙计，」他说，「坐。我到底还是得出去买。一瓶都没剩了。不过，咱们眼下人在这儿就好办了。别坐那把椅子，上面有我剧本的稿子。坐那把——断了扶手的那把。这杯子我看是干净的，不过反正用苏打水涮涮，泼进壁炉就是了。来！我来！给我！」
奇特洛先生一面说，一面从抽屉里摸出一把开塞钻，用酒保也要眼红的手段制服了老玛土撒拉的软木塞，用他那简单而奏效的法子涮了两只大酒杯，往每只里倒了两指深的陈年酒液。基普斯接过酒杯，随口道了声「谢了」，犹豫了一下要不要说「敬你一杯」，终于免了这道客套，把杯子举到了唇边。有一阵子，喉咙里的一团火占据了他全部的注意力，把别的事一概排除在外；随后他发现奇特洛先生已点上一只斗牛犬似的陶土烟斗，坐在空壁炉对面，正给自己斟第二份威士忌。
「说到底，」奇特洛先生说，眼睛瞧着酒瓶，一丝笑意要藏进他宽大的五官里去，「这事故本可能更糟。我正想找人说说话，又不想上酒馆，至少不想上福克斯通的酒馆，因为不瞒你说，我答应过奇特洛太太——她出门去了——不上那儿去，缘由有好几条；当然，我要真想去，我这人偏还是会去的，于是就有了今晚！骑个车子，怎么就撞上个把人，真是不可思议！」
「可不是！」基普斯说，觉得该自己说点什么了。
「瞧咱们，坐在这儿像老朋友似的聊着，半个钟头前咱们还不知道彼此存在呢。至少不知道对方存在。

I might have passed you in the street perhaps and you might have passed me, and how was I to tell that, put to the test, you would have behaved as decently as you have behaved. Only it happened otherwise, that’s all. You’re not smoking!” he said. “Have a cigarette?”
Kipps made a confused reply that took the form of not minding if he did, and drank another sip of old Methusaleh in his confusion. He was able to follow the subsequent course of that sip for quite a long way. It was as though the old gentleman was brandishing a burning torch through his vitals, lighting him here and lighting him there until at last his whole being was in a glow. Chitterlow produced a tobacco pouch and cigarette papers and with an interesting parenthesis that was a little difficult to follow about some lady named Kitty something or other who had taught him the art when he was as yet only what you might call a nice boy, made Kipps a cigarette, and with a consideration that won Kipps’ gratitude suggested that after all he might find a little soda water an improvement with the whiskey. “Some people like it that way,” said Chitterlow, and then with voluminous emphasis, “*I don’t.*”
Emboldened by the weakened state of his enemy Kipps promptly swallowed the rest of him and had his glass at once hospitably replenished. He began to feel he was of a firmer consistency than he commonly believed, and turned his mind to what Chitterlow was saying with the resolve to play a larger part in the conversation than he had hitherto done. Also he smoked through his nose quite successfully, an art he had only very recently acquired.
Meanwhile Chitterlow explained that he was a playwright, and the tongue of Kipps was unloosened to respond that he knew a chap, or rather one of their fellows knew a chap, or at least to be perfectly correct this fellow’s brother did, who had written a play. In response to Chitterlow’s enquiries he could not recall the title of the play, nor where it had appeared nor the name of the manager who produced it, though he thought the title was something about “Love’s Ransom” or something like that.
“He made five ’undred pounds by it, though,” said Kipps. “I know that.”
“That’s nothing,” said Chitterlow, with an air of experience that was extremely convincing. “Nothing. May seem a big sum to *you*, but *I* can assure you it’s just what one gets any day.

说不定我从前在街上跟你擦肩而过，你也跟我擦肩而过，我哪能想到，一经试炼，你竟行事这样体面。只是事情偏偏这样发生了，仅此而已。你还没抽烟！」他说。「来支烟？」
基普斯含含糊糊回了一句，意思是不介意来一支，又慌乱中呷了一口老玛土撒拉。那一口下去的路径，他足足追踪了好长一程。仿佛那位老绅士举着一支火把穿过他的五脏六腑，这儿照一亮，那儿照一亮，最后他整个人都亮堂堂的了。奇特洛掏出烟丝袋和卷烟纸，中间插进一段不太好懂的闲话，说的是某位叫基蒂什么的太太教会了他这门手艺，那时他还只是个你所谓的好孩子；他给基普斯卷了一支烟，又体贴地——这份体贴赢得了基普斯的感激——提议威士忌里到底还是兑点苏打水也许更好。「有人喜欢那么喝，」奇特洛说，随即以浓墨重彩的强调补上一句，「*我不喜欢。*」
趁敌人元气大伤，基普斯一口吞了余下的，酒杯随即又被殷勤地斟满。他开始觉得自己比平常所以为的更要过硬些，便打定主意，在谈话里比先前担当大一点的角色，把心思转到奇特洛正在说的话上去。他还相当成功地改用鼻子喷烟了，这门本事他新近才学会。
这时奇特洛说明了自己是个剧作家，基普斯的舌头也松动了，回应说他认识一个伙计——更确切说，是他们店里一个伙计认识一个伙计，或者最准确地讲，是那伙计的哥哥认识——那人写过一出戏。对奇特洛的追问，他想不起戏名，想不起在哪儿上演，也想不起排戏的经理叫什么，只觉得戏名大概是《爱的赎金》之类的什么。
「他可是靠它挣了五百镑呢，」基普斯说。「这我知道。」
「那算什么，」奇特洛说，那副内行口气极有说服力。「不算什么。在*你*看来兴许是笔大钱，可*我*可以向你保证，这种钱哪天都能挣到。

===Original===

There’s any amount of money, an-ny amount, in a good play.”
“I dessay,” said Kipps, drinking.
“Any amount of money!”
Chitterlow began a series of illustrative instances. He was clearly a person of quite unequalled gift for monologue. It was as though some conversational dam had burst upon Kipps, and in a little while he was drifting along upon a copious rapid of talk about all sorts of theatrical things by one who knows all about them, and quite incapable of anticipating whither that rapid meant to carry him. Presently somehow they had got to anecdotes about well-known theatrical managers, little Teddy Bletherskite, artful old Chumps, and the magnificent Behemoth, “petted to death, you know, fair sickened, by all these society women.” Chitterlow described various personal encounters with these personages, always with modest self-depreciation, and gave Kipps a very amusing imitation of old Chumps in a state of intoxication. Then he took two more stiff doses of old Methusaleh in rapid succession.
Kipps reduced the hither end of his cigarette to a pulp as he sat “dessaying” and “quite believing” Chitterlow in the sagest manner and admiring the easy way in which he was getting on with this very novel and entertaining personage. He had another cigarette made for him, and then Chitterlow, assuming by insensible degrees more and more of the manner of a rich and successful playwright being interviewed by a young admirer, set himself to answer questions which sometimes Kipps asked and sometimes Chitterlow, about the particulars and methods of his career. He undertook this self-imposed task with great earnestness and vigour, treating the matter indeed with such fullness that at times it seemed lost altogether under a thicket of parentheses, footnotes and episodes that branched and budded from its stem. But it always emerged again, usually by way of illustration to its own degressions. Practically it was a mass of material for the biography of a man who had been everywhere and done everything (including the Hon. Thomas Norgate, which was a Record), and in particular had acted with great distinction and profit (he dated various anecdotes, “when I was getting thirty, or forty or fifty, dollars a week”) throughout America and the entire civilised world.

「兴许，」基普斯喝着酒说。

奇特洛开始举例说明。他显然是个独角戏方面天赋无双的人物。仿佛一道话匣子的堤坝在基普斯面前决了口，不多时，他便漂在一条滔滔的急流上，听一个无所不知的人大谈戏剧界种种，全然无法预料这条急流要把自己冲向哪里。不知怎么，他们已经谈到了名经理们的轶事：小泰迪·布莱瑟斯凯特、狡猾的老钱普斯，还有气派堂堂的比希莫斯——「让那帮交际花宠坏了，你知道，简直宠腻了。」奇特洛讲述了他与这些人物当面打交道的种种经历，始终带着谦虚的自贬，还惟妙惟肖地给基普斯学了一段老钱普斯醉酒的样子，逗人极了。紧接着他又接连灌下两大口老玛土撒拉。
基普斯坐在那儿，把烟卷的近嘴端嚼成了纸浆，一面用最老成的气派对奇特洛「兴许」着、「很是相信」着，一面暗自佩服自己同这位新鲜有趣的先生竟这样一见如故。他又让人给卷了一支，随后奇特洛不知不觉地越来越拿出一位富有的成功剧作家接受青年崇拜者采访的派头，着手回答关于他生平和方法的种种问题——这些问题有时是基普斯提的，有时是奇特洛自己提的。他承担起这项自派的任务时极其认真起劲，把事情讲得那样周到，以至有时题目本身彻底淹没在由它生发出来的插叙、脚注和枝节的密林里。但它总会重新冒头，通常是给自己的枝节作个例证。实际上，这通篇是一位走遍天下、样样干过（包括跟托马斯·诺盖特阁下的交情，那可是破了纪录的）的人的传记素材，尤其讲到他曾以出色的报酬（他给各段轶事标着年代：「那会儿我一礼拜挣三十、四十、五十块美金」）演遍美国和整个文明世界。

And as he talked on and on in that full, rich, satisfying voice he had, and as old Methusaleh, indisputably a most drunken old reprobate of a whiskey, busied himself throughout Kipps, lighting lamp after lamp until the entire framework of the little draper was illuminated and glowing like some public building on a festival, behold Chitterlow and Kipps with him and the room in which they sat, were transfigured! Chitterlow became in very truth that ripe, full man of infinite experience and humour and genius, fellow of Shakespeare and Ibsen and Maeterlinck (three names he placed together quite modestly far above his own) and no longer ambiguously dressed in a sort of yachting costume with cycling knickerbockers, but elegantly if unconventionally attired, and the room ceased to be a small and shabby room in a Folkestone slum, and grew larger and more richly furnished, and the flyblown photographs were curious old pictures, and the rubbish on the walls the most rare and costly bric-a-brac, and the indisputable paraffin lamp, a soft and splendid light. A certain youthful heat that to many minds might have weakened old Methusaleh’s starry claim to a ripe antiquity, vanished in that glamour, two burnt holes and a claimant darn in the table cloth, moreover, became no more than the pleasing contradictions natural in the house of genius, and as for Kipps!﻿—Kipps was a bright young man of promise, distinguished by recent quick, courageous proceedings not too definitely insisted upon, and he had been rewarded by admission to a sanctum and confidences, for which the common prosperous, for which “society women” even, were notoriously sighing in vain. “Don’t *want* them, my boy; they’d simply play old Harry with the work, you know! Chaps outside, bank clerks and university fellows, think the life’s all *that* sort of thing. Don’t you believe ’em. Don’t you believe ’em.”
And then﻿—!
“Boom.﻿ ﻿… Boom.﻿ ﻿… Boom.﻿ ﻿… Boom﻿ ﻿…” right in the middle of a most entertaining digression on flats who join touring companies under the impression that they are actors, Kipps much amused at their flatness as exposed by Chitterlow.
“Lor’!” said Kipps like one who awakens, “that’s not eleven!”
“Must be,” said Chitterlow. “It was nearly ten when I got that whiskey. It’s early yet﻿—”
“All the same I must be going,” said Kipps, and stood up. “Even now﻿—maybe. Fact is﻿—I ’ad *no* idea.

他就这样滔滔不绝，用他那副饱满、洪亮、令人心满意足的嗓音；而老玛土撒拉——无可争辩，是威士忌里顶顶荒唐的一个老酒鬼——则在基普斯全身忙个不停，一盏接一盏点灯，直到这位小绸布伙计的整个骨架灯火通明，像节日里张灯的公共建筑；看哪，奇特洛，连同基普斯和这间屋子，一齐换了模样！奇特洛当真成了那样一位阅历无穷、幽默无穷、天赋无穷的成熟完人，与莎士比亚、易卜生、梅特林克称兄道弟（这三个名字他相当谦虚地摆在远远高于自己的地方），身上那身游艇装束配灯笼裤的暧昧打扮也不见了，换上了虽不循规蹈矩却优雅的衣着；这屋子不再是福克斯通贫民窟里那间又小又寒酸的屋子，变得宽敞起来，陈设丰丽起来，那些落满蝇屎的照片成了古怪的旧画，墙上的破烂成了最珍稀贵重的古玩，那盏明明白白的煤油灯成了一团柔和而辉煌的光。那股在某些人看来会有损老玛土撒拉星标陈酿名头的年轻燥烈，也在那光晕里消失了；台布上的两个烧痕和一处等着认领的补丁，更不过是天才之家里自然而然的可喜的不谐和音；至于基普斯！——基普斯成了一位前程远大的聪明青年，新近以敏捷勇敢的举动——不必说得太具体——出了名，如今蒙恩进入一间内室，得聆机密，而那些平平安安的有钱人，连「交际花」们，都在人尽皆知地望穿秋水而不可得。「*不想要*她们，我的孩子；她们只会把作品搅得一塌糊涂，你知道！外头那帮人，银行小职员啊，大学里的先生们啊，以为戏剧界的生活尽是那一套。别信他们。别信他们。」
接着——！
「咚。……咚。……咚。……咚……”正讲到一个最有趣的枝节上——讲的是那种自以为是演员、混进巡回剧团的角色，基普斯正为奇特洛揭出的那股「角色味儿」乐不可支。
「哎呀！」基普斯像猛然惊醒的人似的说，「不会到十一点了吧！」
「准是，」奇特洛说。「我去买威士忌那会儿就快十点了。还早呢——」
「不行，我得走了，」基普斯说着站起身。「这会儿也许都……说不准。实情是——我压根儿*没*想到。

===Original===

The ’ouse door shuts at ’arf past ten, you know. I ought to ’ave thought before.”
“Well, if you *must* go! I tell you what. I’ll come, too.﻿ ﻿… Why! There’s your leg, old man! Clean forgot it! You can’t go through the streets like that. I’ll sew up the tear. And meanwhile have another whiskey.”
“I ought to be getting on *now*,” protested Kipps feebly, and then Chitterlow was showing him how to kneel on a chair in order that the rent trouser leg should be attainable and old Methusaleh on his third round was busy repairing the temporary eclipse of Kipps’ arterial glow. Then suddenly Chitterlow was seized with laughter and had to leave off sewing to tell Kipps that the scene wouldn’t make a bad bit of business in a farcical comedy, and then he began to sketch out the farcical comedy and that led him to a digression about another farcical comedy of which he had written a ripping opening scene which wouldn’t take ten minutes to read. It had something in it that had never been done on the stage before, and was yet perfectly legitimate, namely, a man with a live beetle down the back of his neck trying to seem at his ease in a roomful of people.﻿ ﻿…
“*They* won’t lock you out,” he said, in a singularly reassuring tone, and began to read and act what he explained to be (not because he had written it, but simply because he knew it was so on account of his exceptional experience of the stage) and what Kipps also quite clearly saw to be, one of the best opening scenes that had ever been written.
When it was over Kipps, who rarely swore, was inspired to say the scene was “damned fine” about six times over, whereupon as if by way of recognition, Chitterlow took a simply enormous portion of the inspiring antediluvian, declaring at the same time that he had rarely met a “finer” intelligence than Kipps’ (stronger there might be, *that* he couldn’t say with certainty as yet, seeing how little after all they had seen of each other, but a finer *never*); that it was a shame such a gallant and discriminating intelligence should be nightly either locked up or locked out at ten﻿—well, ten thirty then﻿—and that he had half a mind to recommend old somebody or other (apparently the editor of a London daily paper) to put on Kipps forthwith as a dramatic critic in the place of the current incapable.
“I don’t think I’ve ever made up anything for print,” said Kipps; “﻿—ever.

「宿舍的门十点半就锁，你知道。我该早点想到的。」
「唉，你*非*走不可，那就这样吧。我有个主意。我也送你一段。……嗐！你的腿，老伙计！忘得干干净净了！这副样子可不能上街。我把那口子缝上。趁这工夫再来一杯威士忌。」
「我*现在*就该动身了，」基普斯有气无力地抗议道，可转眼间奇特洛已经在教他怎么跪到椅子上去，好让撕破的裤腿够得着，而第三轮的老玛土撒拉正忙着修补基普斯血脉里那点红光的暂时黯淡。忽然奇特洛爆发出一阵大笑，缝不下去了，非得告诉基普斯这场面放进闹剧里准是一段好噱头不可；跟着他就给那出闹剧搭起架子来，一搭又扯到另一出闹剧上去——那出戏他写了个精彩的开场，念一遍用不了十分钟。戏里有个舞台上从未有过的玩意儿，又完全合乎正道：一个汉子的后脖颈里落进一只活甲虫，还得在一屋子人跟前强作镇定。……
「*他们*不会把你锁在外头的，」他用一种异常叫人安心的口气说，接着便又念又演起来——他解释说，这并非因为出自他手，只是凭他对舞台的非凡阅历，他知道事实如此；而基普斯也看得清清楚楚，这确是有史以来写得最好的开场之一。
念完了，难得骂一句粗话的基普斯受了启发，一连六遍说这场戏「他妈的精采」；奇特洛仿佛作为嘉许，随即给自己斟了满满一份那激励人心的洪荒老酒，同时宣称他极少遇到比基普斯「更精采的」头脑（比他强的兴许有，这他眼下还不能断言，毕竟两人见面还不久，可比这更精采的绝没有）；又说，这样勇敢而有眼力的头脑，竟得夜夜十点被锁进去或者锁出来——好吧，十点半——真是咄咄怪事；还说他有心推荐某位老什么什么（似乎是一家伦敦日报的主编）立刻起用基普斯，顶替现任那位不称职的，去当戏剧评论员。
「我想我从来没给报纸写过东西，」基普斯说；「——从来没有。

That won’t hamper you anyhow. You’re wasted down here, I tell you. Just as I was, before I took to acting. I’m hanged if I wouldn’t like your opinion on these first two acts of that tragedy I’m on to. I haven’t told you about that. It wouldn’t take me more than an hour to read.﻿ ﻿…”
Then so far as he could subsequently remember, Kipps had “another,” and then it would seem that suddenly, regardless of the tragedy, he insisted that he “reelly *must* be getting on,” and from that point his memory became irregular. Certain things have remained quite clearly, and as it is a matter of common knowledge that intoxicated people forget what happens to them, it follows that he was not intoxicated. Chitterlow came with him partly to see him home and partly for a freshener before turning in. Kipps recalled afterwards very distinctly how in Little Fenchurch Street he discovered that he could not walk straight and also that Chitterlow’s needle and thread in his still unmended trouser leg was making an annoying little noise on the pavement behind him. He tried to pick up the needle suddenly by surprise and somehow tripped and fell and then Chitterlow, laughing uproariously, helped him up. “It wasn’t a bicycle this time, old boy,” said Chitterlow, and that appeared to them both at the time as being a quite extraordinarily good joke indeed. They punched each other about on the strength of it.
For a time after that Kipps certainly pretended to be quite desperately drunk and unable to walk and Chitterlow entered into the pretence and supported him.

===Chinese===

「不过真有机会，我会好好试它一把的。真会！橱窗标签我可是常写。连编带写，全套都会。可那是两码事。」
「正因为没干过，你上手才更新鲜。你把那场戏里每一点都一下子抓住了，老弟，真叫漂亮！我告诉你，你能把威廉·阿彻打得落花流水。当然，你不如他文，可我本来就不信什么文人评论家，正如我不信文人剧作家。剧本*不是*文学——这正是他们看不透的一点。剧本就是剧本。不！这反正碍不着你。你在这儿是屈才了，我跟你说。就像我当年上台之前一样。见鬼，我真爱听听你对我手里这部悲剧头两幕的意见。我还没跟你说起过它。念一遍个把钟头都用不了。……」
后来——就他事后所能记得的而言——基普斯又『来了一个』，再往后，似乎是他突然把那出悲剧丢在脑后，非说『真·的·得走了』不可，从那一刻起，他的记忆就乱了套。有些事他倒记得清清楚楚；而人人都知道，喝醉的人会把发生的事忘掉，由此可见，他并没有醉。奇特洛陪他走一段，一半为送他回家，一半为自己睡前醒醒神。基普斯事后记得顶清楚的是：在小芬彻奇街上，他发现自己走不直了，还发现奇特洛那根针线——还别在那条没缝完的裤腿上——在身后的人行道上发出烦人的细碎声响。他冷不防想抄起那根针，不知怎么绊了一跤，摔倒在地，奇特洛哈哈大笑，把他扶起来。「这回可不是自行车了，老伙计，」奇特洛说，这话当时在两人听来实在是妙不可言的绝好笑话。为这个笑话，他俩你捶我一拳、我捶你一拳。
打那以后有一阵，基普斯分明是装出烂醉如泥、寸步难行的样子，奇特洛也入了戏，架着他走。

===Original===

After that Kipps remembered being struck with the extremely laughable absurdity of going down hill to Tontine Street in order to go up hill again to the Emporium, and trying to get that idea into Chitterlow’s head and being unable to do so on account of his own merriment or Chitterlow’s evident intoxication, and his next memory after that was of the exterior of the Emporium, shut and darkened, and, as it were, frowning at him with all its stripes of yellow and green. The chilly way in which “Shalford” glittered in the moonlight printed itself with particular vividness on his mind. It appeared to Kipps that that establishment was closed to him forevermore. Those gilded letters, in spite of appearances, spelt finis for him and exile from Folkestone. He would never do woodcarving, never see Miss Walshingham again. Not that he had ever hoped to see her again. But this was the knife, this was final. He had stayed out, he had got drunk, there had been that row about the Manchester window dressing only three days ago.﻿ ﻿… In the retrospect he was quite sure that he was perfectly sober then and at bottom extremely unhappy, but he kept a brave face on the matter nevertheless, and declared stoutly he didn’t care if he *was* locked out.
Whereupon Chitterlow slapped him on the back very hard and told him that was a “Bit of All Right,” and assured him that when he himself had been a clerk in Sheffield before he took to acting he had been locked out sometimes for six nights running.
“What’s the result?” said Chitterlow. “I could go back to that place now, and they’d be glad to have me.﻿ ﻿… Glad to have me,” he repeated, and then added, “that is to say, if they remember me﻿—which isn’t very likely.”
Kipps asked a little weakly, “What am I to do?”
“Keep out,” said Chitterlow. “You can’t knock ’em up now﻿—that would give you Right away. You’d better try and sneak in in the morning with the Cat. That’ll do you. You’ll probably get in all right in the morning if nobody gives you away.”
Then for a time﻿—perhaps as the result of that slap in the back﻿—Kipps felt decidedly queer, and acting on Chitterlow’s advice went for a bit of a freshener upon the Leas. After a time he threw off the temporary queerness and found Chitterlow patting him on the shoulder and telling him that he’d be all right now in a minute and all the better for it﻿—which he was.

===Chinese===

再往后，基普斯记得自己忽然觉得一事荒唐透顶、笑死个人：为了再爬坡回商场，却先下坡到唐廷街去；他竭力要把这个道理讲进奇特洛的脑袋，却讲不成——不是他自己笑得讲不下去，就是奇特洛醉得明显听不懂。再往后的记忆，就是商场的外观了：门户紧闭，一片漆黑，黄一道绿一道的条纹仿佛都朝他皱着眉。「沙尔福德」几个字在月光里泛着冷光，格外分明地印上了他的心头。在基普斯看来，那家商号从此永远对他关上大门了。那几个描金的字母，不管表面如何，对他拼出的是「完」，是放逐，从此告别福克斯通。木刻再也学不成了，沃尔辛厄姆（Walshingham）小姐也再见不着了。倒不是他原先还存什么再见的指望。可这一刀砍下来了，这回是完了。他夜不归宿，他喝醉了，三天前还为曼彻斯特橱窗的布置挨过那顿排揎。……回头看，他相当确信自己当时完全清醒，骨子里痛苦至极，可他在这件事上仍硬撑着面子，斩钉截铁地宣称，自己就是被锁在外头也*满不在乎*。
于是奇特洛狠狠拍了一下他的背，说他这态度「没得说」，又向他担保，说自己当年在谢菲尔德当小职员、还没上台那阵，也曾一连六夜被锁在门外。
「结果呢？」奇特洛说。「我如今再回那地方，他们还巴不得要我。……巴不得要我，」他重复了一遍，又补充道，「就是说，倘若他们还记得我——多半是记不得的。」
基普斯有气无力地问：「我怎么办呢？」
「先别回去，」奇特洛说。「这会儿不能去敲门——那就全露馅了。你最好明儿一早跟着那只猫一块儿溜进去。就这么办。早上多半进得去，只要没人告发你。」
随后有一阵——也许是背上那一巴掌的结果——基普斯觉得说不出的难受，遵照奇特洛的忠告，到利斯草坪上去吹吹风。过了一会儿，那阵难受劲儿过去了，他发现奇特洛正拍着他的肩膀，告诉他再过一分钟就没事了，吐出来反倒好——果然如此。

===Original===

And the wind having dropped and the night being now a really very beautiful moonlight night indeed, and all before Kipps to spend as he liked and with only a very little tendency to spin round now and again to mar its splendour, they set out to walk the whole length of the Leas to the Sandgate lift and back, and as they walked Chitterlow spoke first of moonlight transfiguring the sea and then of moonlight transfiguring faces, and so at last he came to the topic of Love, and upon that he dwelt a great while, and with a wealth of experience and illustrative anecdote that seemed remarkably pungent and material to Kipps. He forgot his lost Miss Walshingham and his outraged employer again. He became as it were a desperado by reflection.
Chitterlow had had adventures, a quite astonishing variety of adventures in this direction; he was a man with a past, a really opulent past, and he certainly seemed to like to look back and see himself amidst its opulence.
He made no consecutive history, but he gave Kipps vivid, momentary pictures of relations and entanglements. One moment he was in flight﻿—only too worthily in flight﻿—before the husband of a Malay woman in Cape Town. At the next he was having passionate complications with the daughter of a clergyman in York. Then he passed to a remarkable grouping at Seaford.
“They say you can’t love two women at once,” said Chitterlow. “But I tell you﻿—” He gesticulated and raised his ample voice. “It’s *Rot! Rot!*”
“I know that,” said Kipps.
“Why, when I was in the smalls with Bessie Hopper’s company there were three.” He laughed and decided to add, “Not counting Bessie, that is.”
He set out to reveal Life as it is lived in touring companies, a quite amazing jungle of interwoven “affairs” it appeared to be, a mere amorous winepress for the crushing of hearts.
“People say this sort of thing’s a nuisance and interferes with Work. I tell you it isn’t. The Work couldn’t go on without it. They *must* do it. They haven’t the Temperament if they don’t. If they hadn’t the Temperament they wouldn’t want to act, if they have﻿—Bif!”
“You’re right,” said Kipps. “I see that.”
Chitterlow proceeded to a close criticism of certain historical indiscretions of Mr. Clement Scott respecting the morals of the stage. Speaking in confidence and not as one who addresses the public, he admitted regretfully the general truth of these comments.

===Chinese===

风已停歇，夜色当真美极了，一轮明月，眼前又有随他消磨的整宿光阴，只是偶尔还得打上一个小旋转，稍损其圆满；于是两人打定主意，沿利斯草坪从头走到桑德盖特升降梯，再走回来。一路走着，奇特洛先谈月光如何点化海，再谈月光如何点化人的脸，最后便谈到了爱情这个题目，并在其上流连许久，凭着那一大把阅历和现身说法的轶事，在基普斯听来格外有血有肉、扎扎实实。他又把失去的沃尔辛厄姆小姐和被冒犯的东家忘了个干净。他俨然成了个借来的亡命之徒。
奇特洛是有过奇遇的，这一路上奇遇之多之杂，令人惊叹；他是个有往事的人，而且往事阔绰，他也确实乐于回头张望，看自己置身于那片富丽之中。
他讲的不是连贯的历史，而是给基普斯一幅幅鲜亮的瞬息画面，讲种种关系与纠葛。这一刻，他还在开普敦为一个马来女人的丈夫所追——追得完全事出有因；下一刻，他已在约克同一位牧师的女儿闹得天翻地覆的热烈纠葛。接着又讲到西福德一场非同寻常的组合。
「人家说你不能同时爱两个女人，」奇特洛说。「可我告诉你——」他手舞足蹈，放开他那洪亮的嗓门。「纯属*放屁！放屁！*」
「这我知道。」基普斯说。
「嗐，我跟着贝西·霍珀的班子跑龙套那阵，同时有三个。」他笑了，想想又补上一句，「这还不算贝西。」
他着手揭示巡回剧团里的人生实况：那竟是一座「私情」交织丛生的惊人丛林，一架专碾人心的多情葡萄榨床。
「人家说这种事讨厌，妨碍工作。我告诉你，不是那么回事。没有这种事，工作进行不下去。他们*非*这么干不可。不这么干，就是没有艺术气质。没有气质，就压根儿不想演戏；有了——砰！」
「你说得对，」基普斯说。「我明白。」
奇特洛接着对克莱门特·斯科特先生关于戏剧界风化的那些不慎言论作了一番精到的批评。他以密友交心的口气——而非对公众发言的口气——遗憾地承认，那些议论大体属实。

===Original===

He proceeded to examine various typical instances that had almost forced themselves upon him personally, and with especial regard to the contrast between his own character towards women and that of the Hon. Thomas Norgate, with whom it appeared he had once been on terms of great intimacy.﻿ ﻿…
Kipps listened with emotion to these extraordinary recollections. They were wonderful to him, they were incredibly credible. Of course the tumultuous, passionate course was the way life ran﻿—except in high-class establishments! Such things happened in novels, in plays﻿—only he had been fool enough not to understand they happened. His share in the conversation was now indeed no more than faint writing in the margin; Chitterlow was talking quite continuously. He expanded his magnificent voice into huge guffaws, he drew it together into a confidential intensity, it became drawlingly reminiscent, he was frank, frank with the effect of a revelation, reticent also with the effect of a revelation, a stupendously gesticulating, moonlit black figure, wallowing in itself, preaching Adventure and the Flesh to Kipps. Yet withal shot with something of sentiment, with a sort of sentimental refinement very coarsely and egotistically done. The Times he had had!﻿—even before he was as old as Kipps he had had innumerable times.
Well, he said with a sudden transition, he had sown his wild oats﻿—one had to somewhen﻿—and now he fancied he had mentioned it earlier in the evening, he was happily married. She was, he indicated, a “born lady.” Her father was a prominent lawyer, a solicitor in Kentish Town, “done a lot of public house business”; her mother was second cousin to the wife of Abel Jones, the fashionable portrait painter﻿—“almost Society people in a way.” That didn’t count with Chitterlow. He was no snob. What *did* count was that she possessed, what he ventured to assert without much fear of contradiction, was the very finest, completely untrained contralto voice in all the world. (“But to hear it properly,” said Chitterlow, “you want a Big Hall.”) He became rather vague and jerked his head about to indicate when and how he had entered matrimony. She was, it seemed, “away with her people.” It was clear that Chitterlow did not get on with these people very well.

===Chinese===

他进而检视了几桩几乎是主动找上门来的典型事例，特别比较了他本人对女人的心性与托马斯·诺盖特阁下的心性之差异——他跟这位阁下从前显然有过一段过从甚密的交情。……
基普斯满怀激动地听这些非凡的回忆。它们在他听来妙极了，可信得不可思议。当然，这种狂风暴雨般的热烈历程才是生活的常态——只是上等铺子里例外！这种事小说里有，戏里有——只怪他傻，从前竟不懂生活里也有。他在谈话中的份额如今实在不过页边几行淡淡的批注；奇特洛的话滔滔不绝。他那堂皇的嗓音忽而放开成放声大笑，忽而收拢成交头接耳的郑重，忽而又拖长成怀旧的低吟；他坦率，坦率得如同一番启示；又含蓄，含蓄得也如同一番启示；月光下，一个黑色的身影比划得惊天动地，自得其乐地打滚，向基普斯布道冒险与肉欲。然而其间又透着几分柔情，一种做出来非常粗俗、非常自私的多情雅致。他那些风光时辰！——早在像基普斯这么大的岁数之前，他就已不知有过多少了。
后来他话锋一转，说他早已收心了——人总有这么个时候——又说（他想起来晚上早些时候提过）他如今婚姻美满。她是个——他这么暗示——「天生的淑女」。她父亲是位显赫的律师，在肯蒂什镇开业的状师，「做过好些酒馆的生意」；她母亲是时髦肖像画家亚伯·琼斯的太太的表姐妹——「某种意义上，差不多算上流人家」。这在奇特洛眼里不算什么。他不是势利眼。算数的是她拥有——他斗胆断言，不大怕人反驳——全世界最好的一副全然未经调教的女低音。（「不过，」奇特洛说，「要好好听它，得有一座大厅。」）至于他何时何地结的婚，他讲得含含糊糊，脑袋乱晃一通了事。她呢，据说是「回娘家人那儿去了」。显然，奇特洛跟那些娘家人处得不怎么样。

===Original===

It would seem they failed to appreciate his playwright, regarding it as an unremunerative pursuit, whereas as he and Kipps knew, wealth beyond the dreams of avarice would presently accrue. Only patience and persistence were needful.
He went off at a tangent to hospitality. Kipps must come down home with him. They couldn’t wander about all night, with a bottle of the right sort pining at home for them. “You can sleep on the sofa. You won’t be worried by broken springs anyhow, for I took ’em all out myself two or three weeks ago. I don’t see what they even put ’em in for. It’s a point I know about. I took particular notice of it when I was with Bessie Hopper. Three months we were and all over England, North Wales and the Isle of Man, and I never struck a sofa in diggings anywhere that hadn’t a broken spring. Not once﻿—all the time.”
He added almost absently: “It happens like that at times.”
They descended the slant road towards Harbour Street and went on past the Pavilion Hotel.
They came into the presence of old Methusaleh again, and that worthy under Chitterlow’s direction at once resumed the illumination of Kipps’ interior with the conscientious thoroughness that distinguished him. Chitterlow took a tall portion to himself with an air of asbestos, lit the bulldog pipe again, and lapsed for a space into meditation, from which Kipps roused him by remarking that he expected “an acter ’as a lot of ups and downs like, now and then.”
At which Chitterlow seemed to bestir himself. “Rather,” he said. “And sometimes it’s his own fault and sometimes it isn’t. Usually it is. If it isn’t one thing it’s another. If it isn’t the manager’s wife it’s bar-bragging. I tell you things happen at times. I’m a fatalist. The fact is Character has you. You can’t get away from it. You may think you do, but you don’t.”
He reflected for a moment. “It’s that what makes tragedy Psychology really. It’s the Greek irony﻿—Ibsen and﻿—all that. Up to date.”
He emitted this exhaustive summary of high-toned modern criticism as if he was repeating a lesson while thinking of something else, but it seemed to rouse him as it passed his lips, by including the name of Ibsen.

===Chinese===

看起来，他们赏识不了他的剧作，把编剧看作不赚钱的营生；而他跟基普斯都知道，贪婪都梦想不到的财富不久就会滚滚而来。需要的只是耐心和坚持。
他岔开去大谈待客之道。基普斯必须跟他回家去。总不能在街上晃荡一整夜，家里还有一瓶好酒眼巴巴地盼着他们。「你可以睡沙发。反正弹簧硌不着你，两三个礼拜前我亲手把它们全拆了。我真不明白他们装弹簧做什么。这一点我内行。跟着贝西·霍珀那阵我特别留心过。三个月跑遍全英格兰、北威尔士和马恩岛，我住过的寓所里就没碰上一张沙发不是断弹簧的。一回也没有——整整三个月。」
他近乎心不在焉地加了一句：「有时候事情就是这样。」
他们顺着斜坡路下到海港街，走过帕维利恩旅馆。
他们重新来到老玛土撒拉面前；这位可敬的老者在奇特洛指挥下，立刻以它一贯的一丝不苟，继续照亮基普斯的内部。奇特洛给自己满满斟上一杯，神色如石棉般泰然，重新点上斗牛犬烟斗，沉入一时遐想；基普斯把他唤回来，说想必「当演员的有时也难免起起落落，什么的」。
这话似乎让奇特洛振作起来。「可不，」他说。「有时怪他自己，有时不怪他。多半怪他自己。不是这档子事就是那档子事。不是经理太太，就是吹牛酗酒。我告诉你，事情有时候就是这么巧。我是个宿命论者。事实是，性格抓着你不放。你躲不开它。你自以为躲开了，其实没有。」
他寻思了片刻。「这正是悲剧之为心理学的道理。就是希腊式的反讽——易卜生，还有——那一套。迄今为止。」
他把这段格调高雅的现代批评的总纲一口气吐了出来，像背着书、心里想着别的事的学生；可「易卜生」这名字一出口，倒似乎又把他自己提溜醒了。

===Original===

He became interested in telling Kipps, who was indeed open to any information whatever about this quite novel name, exactly where he thought Ibsen fell short, points where it happened that Ibsen was defective just where it chanced that he, Chitterlow, was strong. Of course he had no desire to place himself in any way on an equality with Ibsen; still the fact remained that his own experience in England and America and the colonies was altogether more extensive than Ibsen could have had. Ibsen had probably never seen “one decent bar scrap” in his life. That, of course, was not Ibsen’s fault or his own merit, but there the thing was. Genius, he knew, was supposed to be able to do anything or to do without anything; still he was now inclined to doubt that. He had a play in hand that might perhaps not please William Archer﻿—whose opinion, after all, he did not value as he valued Kipps’ opinion﻿—but which he thought was at any rate as well constructed as anything Ibsen ever did.
So with infinite deviousness Chitterlow came at last to his play. He decided he would not read it to Kipps, but tell him about it. This was the simpler because much of it was still unwritten. He began to explain his plot. It was a complicated plot and all about a nobleman who had seen everything and done everything and knew practically all that Chitterlow knew about women; that is to say, “all about women” and suchlike matters. It warmed and excited Chitterlow. Presently he stood up to act a situation﻿—which could not be explained. It was an extremely vivid situation.
Kipps applauded the situation vehemently. “Tha’s dam’ fine,” said the new dramatic critic, quite familiar with his part now, striking the table with his fist and almost upsetting his third portion (in the second series) of old Methusaleh. “Tha’s dam’ fine, Chit’low!”
“You see it?” said Chitterlow, with the last vestiges of that incidental gloom disappearing. “Good, old boy! I thought you’d see it. But it’s just the sort of thing the literary critic can’t see. However, it’s only a beginning﻿—”
He replenished Kipps and proceeded with his exposition.
In a little while it was no longer necessary to give that over-advertised Ibsen the purely conventional precedence he had hitherto had. Kipps and Chitterlow were friends and they could speak frankly and openly of things not usually admitted.

===Chinese===

他来了兴致，要告诉基普斯——这位对易卜生这个崭新名字本来就什么都乐于领教——易卜生究竟差在哪些点上，而巧的是，易卜生欠缺之处，恰是他奇特洛在行之处。当然，他无意在任何意义上把自己同易卜生摆平；不过事实终归是事实：他本人在英格兰、美国和殖民地的阅历，全都比易卜生可能有的广得多。易卜生这辈子八成连「一场像样的酒馆斗殴」都没见过。这当然不是易卜生的错，也算不得他的功劳，可事情明摆在那儿。天才嘛，人们总说无所不能，无所不有也能对付；可他如今倒有点怀疑这话了。他手头有个本子，也许不合威廉·阿彻的口味——毕竟，阿彻的意见他不像看重基普斯的意见那样看重——但他认为，论结构的严整，它至少不输易卜生的任何作品。
奇特洛就这样拐了一万个弯，终于拐到了他的剧本上。他决定不念给基普斯听，而是讲给他听。这样做简单些，因为其中好大一半还没写出来呢。他开始讲他的情节。那是个复杂的情节，讲一位无所不见、无所不为的贵族，对女人懂得的实际上正是奇特洛所懂的一切；也就是说，「关于女人的一切」以及诸如此类。这情节越讲越叫他兴奋。不一会儿，他站起来表演一个情境——那情境没法讲解。是个生动至极的情境。
基普斯为这情境热烈鼓掌。「这他妈真精采，」新任戏剧评论员说，如今对自己的角色已了然于胸，一拳捶在桌上，差点打翻他第二轮的第三杯老玛土撒拉。「这他妈真精采，奇特洛！」
「你看出好来了？」奇特洛说，那点偶发的阴郁消失得无影无踪。「好样的，老伙计！我就知道你看得出来。可这种东西，文人评论家偏偏看不出来。不过，这只是个开头——」
他给基普斯续上酒，继续往下讲。
不多时，那位被吹得过火的易卜生，先前享有的那点纯属客套的优先地位，也不必再给了。基普斯和奇特洛成了朋友，可以坦率而敞开地谈论通常不许谈论的事情了。

===Original===

“Any ’ow,” said Kipps, a little irrelevantly and speaking over the brim of the replenishment, “what you read jus’ now was dam’ fine. Nothing can’t alter that.”
He perceived a sort of faint, buzzing vibration about things that was very nice and pleasant and with a little care he had no difficulty whatever in putting his glass back on the table. Then he perceived Chitterlow was going on with the scenario, and then that old Methusaleh had almost entirely left his bottle. He was glad there was so little more Methusaleh to drink because that would prevent his getting drunk. He knew that he was not now drunk, but he knew that he had had enough. He was one of those who always know when they have had enough. He tried to interrupt Chitterlow to tell him this, but he could not get a suitable opening. He doubted whether Chitterlow might not be one of those people who did not know when they had had enough. He discovered that he disapproved of Chitterlow. Highly. It seemed to him that Chitterlow went on and on like a river. For a time he was inexplicably and quite unjustly cross with Chitterlow and wanted to say to him, “you got the gift of the gab,” but he only got so far as to say “the gift,” and then Chitterlow thanked him and said he was better than Archer any day. So he eyed Chitterlow with a baleful eye until it dawned upon him that a most extraordinary thing was taking place. Chitterlow kept mentioning someone named Kipps. This presently began to perplex Kipps very greatly. Dimly but decidedly he perceived this was wrong.
“Look ’ere,” he said suddenly, “*what* Kipps?”
“This chap Kipps I’m telling you about.”
“What chap Kipps you’re telling which about?”
“I told you.”
Kipps struggled with a difficulty in silence for a space. Then he reiterated firmly, “*What* chap Kipps?”
“This chap in my play﻿—man who kisses the girl.”
“Never kissed a girl,” said Kipps; “leastwise﻿—” and subsided for a space. He could not remember whether he had kissed Ann or not﻿—he knew he had meant to. Then suddenly in a tone of great sadness and addressing the hearth he said, “*My* name’s Kipps.”
“Eh?” said Chitterlow.
“Kipps,” said Kipps, smiling a little cynically.
“What about him?”
“He’s me.” He tapped his breastbone with his middle finger to indicate his essential self.
He leant forward very gravely towards Chitterlow.

===Chinese===

「反正，」基普斯有点前言不搭后语，隔着刚续满的杯沿说，「你刚才念的那些真他妈精采。谁也改变不了这个。」
他觉得万事万物间有一种微微的、嗡嗡的震颤，又好又受用；稍加小心，把杯子放回桌上毫无困难。随后他察觉奇特洛还在往下讲梗概，又察觉老玛土撒拉在瓶里差不多只剩个底儿了。他很高兴玛土撒拉所剩无几，因为这样他就醉不成了。他知道自己现在没醉，可他也知道自己已经够了。他是那种永远知道自己够了的人。他想打断奇特洛，把这一点告诉他，可总找不着合适的空当。他怀疑奇特洛兴许正是那种不知道自己够了的人。他发现自己对奇特洛很不以为然。非常不以为然。他觉得奇特洛就这么滔滔不绝，像条河。有一阵，他莫名其妙、毫无道理地对奇特洛着恼，想对他说「你可真是能说」，可只说到「真是能」三个字，奇特洛已经道了谢，说他哪天都比阿彻强。于是他恶狠狠地盯着奇特洛，直到恍然发觉一件顶顶了不得的事正在发生。奇特洛嘴里不断提到一个叫基普斯的人。这事很快使基普斯大惑不解。他模模糊糊却又斩钉截铁地觉得这里头不对。
「喂，」他突然说，「*哪个*基普斯？」
「我正说到的这个基普斯啊。」
「你正说到哪个基普斯？」
「我说过了。」
基普斯默默跟这个难题搏斗了一阵。然后他坚定地重申：「*哪个*基普斯？」
「我剧本里的这个基普斯——亲那姑娘的那个人。」
「我可没亲过姑娘，」基普斯说；「顶多是——」便沉默了一阵。他想不起自己亲过安（Ann）没有——他知道自己是有那个意思的。随后他忽然用极悲伤的声调，对着壁炉说：「*我*就叫基普斯。」
「嗯？」奇特洛说。
「基普斯，」基普斯说，微微冷笑了一下。
「他怎么啦？」
「那就是我。」他用中指敲敲自己的胸骨，指明他那个本我。
他极郑重地向奇特洛俯过身去。

===Original===

“Look ’ere, Chit’low,” he said, “you haven’t no business putting my name into play. You mustn’t do things like that. You’d lose me my crib, right away.” And they had a little argument﻿—so far as Kipps could remember. Chitterlow entered upon a general explanation of how he got his names. These, he had for the most part got out of a newspaper that was still, he believed, “lying about.” He even made to look for it, and while he was doing so Kipps went on with the argument, addressing himself more particularly to the photograph of the girl in tights. He said that at first her costume had not commended her to him, but now he perceived she had an extremely sensible face. He told her she would like Buggins if she met him; he could see she was just that sort. She would admit, all sensible people would admit, that using names in plays was wrong. You could, for example, have the law of him.
He became confidential. He explained that he was already in sufficient trouble for stopping out all night without having his name put in plays. He was certain to be in the deuce of a row, the deuce of a row. Why had he done it? Why hadn’t he gone at ten? Because one thing leads to another. One thing, he generalized, always does lead to another.﻿ ﻿…
He was trying to tell her that he was utterly unworthy of Miss Walshingham, when Chitterlow gave up the search and suddenly accused him of being drunk and talking “Rot﻿—.”

===Chinese===

「听着，奇特洛，」他说，「你无权把我的名字写进戏里去。这种事你可干不得。你会害我把饭碗砸了，当下就砸。」接着——就基普斯记忆所及——他们小小抬了一回杠。奇特洛就自己怎么起名字的问题作了一通泛论。那些名字，多半是从一张报纸上得来的，他相信那张报「这会儿还扔在哪儿」。他甚至起身去找；趁他找的工夫，基普斯继续抬他的杠，尤其对着那张穿紧身衣的姑娘的相片讲。他说，起初她那身行头没讨他喜欢，如今他看出她有张极通情达理的脸。他告诉她，她要是遇见巴金斯（Buggins）准会喜欢他；他看得出她正是那一类人。她会承认——凡是通情达理的人都会承认——戏里用人家的真名字是不对的。闹出去，比方说，是可以告他的。
他推心置腹起来。他解释说，自己在外头过了整宿，麻烦已经够多的了，犯不上再把名字塞进戏里。他准得挨一顿好排揎，一顿好排揎。他干吗要这样？干吗十点不回去？因为一件事牵着另一件事。一件事，他概括道，总是牵着另一件事的。……
他正想告诉她，自己压根儿配不上沃尔辛厄姆小姐，奇特洛却放弃了寻找，突然指责他喝醉了，满口「浑话——」。

## VI Discords / 六 不谐
===Original===

One day Kipps set out upon his newly-mastered bicycle to New Romney to break the news of his engagement to his Uncle and Aunt﻿—this time positively. He was now a finished cyclist, but as yet an unseasoned one; the southwest wind, even in its summer guise, as one meets it in the Marsh, is the equivalent of a reasonable hill, and ever and again he got off and refreshed himself by a spell of walking. He was walking just outside New Romney preparatory to his triumphal entry (one hand off) when abruptly he came upon Ann Pornick.
It chanced he was thinking about her at the time. He had been thinking curious things; whether, after all, the atmosphere of New Romney and the Marsh had not some difference, some faint impalpable quality that was missing in the great and fashionable world of Folkestone behind there on the hill. Here there was a homeliness, a familiarity. He had noted as he passed that old Mr. Cliffordown’s gate had been mended with a fresh piece of string. In Folkestone he didn’t take notice and he didn’t care if they built three hundred houses. Come to think of it, that was odd. It was fine and grand to have twelve hundred a year; it was fine to go about on trams and omnibuses and think not a person aboard was as rich as oneself; it was fine to buy and order this and that and never have any work to do and to be engaged to a girl distantly related to the Earl of Beauprés, but yet there had been a zest in the old time out here, a rare zest in the holidays, in sunlight, on the sea beach and in the High Street, that failed from these new things. He thought of those bright windows of holiday that had seemed so glorious to him in the retrospect from his apprentice days. It was strange that now, amidst his present splendours, they were glorious still!
All those things were over now﻿—perhaps that was it! Something had happened to the world and the old light had been turned out. He himself was changed, and Sid was changed, terribly changed, and Ann no doubt was changed.
He thought of her with the hair blown about her flushed cheeks as they stood together after their race.﻿ ﻿…
Certainly she must be changed, and all the magic she had been fraught with to the very hem of her short petticoats gone no doubt forever.

===Chinese===

一天，基普斯（Kipps）骑上他新近驯熟的自行车，动身去新罗姆尼（New Romney），把订婚的消息报给叔父叔母——这一回是千真万确的了。他如今车技已经到家，火候却还没炼出来；西南风纵然摆出夏日的面孔，在沼泽地带迎面碰上，也抵得上一道像样的山坡，他不时下车，走上一段缓缓劲儿。他正走在新罗姆尼城外，预备着凯旋式的入城（撒开一只手），冷不防迎面撞见了安·波尼克（Ann Pornick）。
说来也巧，他那会儿正想着她呢。他一直想着些古怪的事：新罗姆尼和这片沼泽的氛围，究竟是不是有点什么不同，有点什么福克斯通（Folkestone）山上那边伟大时兴的世界里所缺的、说不清道不明的东西。这里有一种家常，一种熟稔。路过时他留意到，克利福德当老先生家的大门用新绳子捆好了。在福克斯通，他什么都不留意，人家盖三百座房子他也不关心。细想起来，这可就怪了。一年一千二百镑固然阔气；坐电车公共汽车到处走、想着满车没一个跟自己一样阔，固然痛快；买这买那、随点随要、什么活计都不干、跟博普雷伯爵（Earl of Beauprés）沾亲带故的姑娘订了婚，固然都好；可是从前在外头这边，日子里有股子滋味，假日里有股子难得的滋味，在太阳光里，在海滩上，在大街上——这些新鲜玩意儿里偏偏没有。他想起学徒那些年回头望去、显得那样辉煌的假日明窗。怪的是，如今置身眼前的荣华之中，它们依然辉煌！
这一切如今都完了——兴许毛病就出在这儿！世界出了什么事，旧日的灯熄了。他自己变了，希德（Sid）也变了，变得厉害，安无疑也变了。
他想起那次赛跑之后两人并肩站着，她两颊绯红、头发被风吹得四散。……
她一定变了，她那通身的魔力——连短短裙边上都缀满的魔力——无疑也一去不返了。

===Original===

And as he thought that, or before and while he thought it, for he came to all these things in his own vague and stumbling way, he looked up, and there was Ann!
She was seven years older and greatly altered; yet for the moment it seemed to him that she had not changed at all. “Ann!” he said, and she, with a lifting note, “It’s Art Kipps!”
Then he became aware of changes﻿—improvements. She was as pretty as she had promised to be, her blue eyes as dark as his memory of them, and with a quick, high colour, but now Kipps by several inches was the taller again. She was dressed in a simple grey dress that showed her very clearly as a straight and healthy little woman, and her hat was Sundayfied with pink flowers. She looked soft and warm and welcoming. Her face was alight to Kipps with her artless gladness at their encounter.
“It’s Art Kipps!” she said.
“Rather,” said Kipps.
“You got your holidays?”
It flashed upon Kipps that Sid had not told her of his great fortune. Much regretful meditation upon Sid’s behaviour had convinced him that he himself was to blame for exasperating boastfulness in that affair, and this time he took care not to err in that direction. He erred in the other.
“I’m taking a bit of a ’oliday,” he said.
“So’m I,” said Ann.
“You been for a walk?” asked Kipps.
Ann showed him a bunch of wayside flowers.
“It’s a long time since I seen you, Ann. Why, ’ow long must it be? Seven﻿—eight years nearly.”
“It don’t do to count,” said Ann.
“It don’t look like it,” said Kipps, with the slightest emphasis.
“You got a moustache,” said Ann, smelling her flowers and looking at him over them, not without admiration.
Kipps blushed.﻿ ﻿…
Presently they came to the bifurcation of the roads.
“I’m going down this way to mother’s cottage,” said Ann.
“I’ll come a bit your way if I may.”
In New Romney social distinctions that are primary realities in Folkestone are absolutely nonexistent, and it seemed quite permissible for him to walk with Ann, for all that she was no more than a servant. They talked with remarkable ease to one another, they slipped into a vein of intimate reminiscence in the easiest manner. In a little while Kipps was amazed to find Ann and himself at this:
“You r’ember that half sixpence? What you cut for me?”
“Yes.”
“I got it still.”
She hesitated. “Funny, wasn’t it?” she said, and then, “you got yours, Artie?”
“Rather,” said Kipps.

===Chinese===

他正这样想着——或者是在这念头成形之前和当中，因为他到什么念头都是这样迷迷糊糊、跌跌撞撞——一抬头，安就在跟前！
她大了七岁，模样大变；可一时之间，他觉得她一点没变。「安！」他说；她呢，声音扬起来：「是阿特·基普斯（Art Kipps）呀！」
随后他才觉出变化来——是长进了。她兑现了小时候许下的那份标致：蓝眼睛还跟他记忆里一样深，气色又快又鲜；只是这一回，基普斯又高出她好几寸了。她穿一条素净的灰裙子，把她分明衬成一个小巧笔直、健健实实的女人；帽子是过节做派，缀着粉花。她看上去温柔、暖人、一团和气。重逢的欢喜不加雕饰，使她整张脸对着基普斯发亮。
「是阿特·基普斯呀！」她说。
「可不。」基普斯说。
「你们放假了？」
基普斯猛然明白，希德没把她发财的事告诉她。对希德那番作为，他懊悔地想了又想，早认定那回是自己逞能吹牛惹的祸；这回他小心着别往那边栽。他栽到了另一边。
「我歇几天假。」他说。
「我也是。」安说。
「你出来遛弯儿？」基普斯问。
安给他看一束野花。
「好久没见你了，安。嗐，有多久了？七——快八年了吧。」
「别数了，数它做什么。」安说。
「可不像。」基普斯说，字字都轻轻加了点重。
「你留胡子了。」安说，嗅着花，从花束上头看他，不无倾慕。
基普斯脸红了。……
不多时，他们走到岔路口。
「我打这边下去，回母亲的村舍。」安说。
「要是可以，我陪你走一段。」
在新罗姆尼，福克斯通里天经地义的身份之别压根儿不存在；尽管安不过是个女仆，他同她并肩走走，似乎全无不可。他们谈得异常投机，最自然不过地滑进了亲切的旧话。不多时，基普斯惊讶地发现，安和他自己已经到了这一步：
「你还记得那半块六便士不？你割给我的那半块？」
「记得。」
「我还留着呢。」
她迟疑了一下。「怪好玩的，是不是？」她说，接着又问：「你那半块呢，阿蒂？」
「可不。」基普斯说。

===Original===

“What do you think?” and wondered in his heart of hearts why he had never looked at that sixpence for so long.
Ann smiled at him frankly.
“I didn’t expect you’d keep it,” she said. “I thought often﻿—it was silly to keep mine. Besides,” she reflected, “it didn’t mean anything really.”
She glanced at him as she spoke and met his eye.
“Oh, didn’t it!” said Kipps, a little late with his response, and realising his infidelity to Helen even as he spoke.
“It didn’t mean much anyhow,” said Ann. “You still in the drapery?”
“I’m living at Folkestone,” began Kipps and decided that that sufficed. “Didn’t Sid tell you he met me?”
“No! Here?”
“Yes. The other day. ’Bout a week or more ago.”
“That was before I came.”
“Ah! that was it,” said Kipps.
“ ’E’s got on,” said Ann. “Got ’is own shop now, Artie.”
“ ’E tole me.”
They found themselves outside Muggett’s cottages. “You going in?” said Kipps.
“I s’pose so,” said Ann.
They both hung upon the pause. Ann took a plunge.
“D’you often come to New Romney?” she said.
“I ride over a bit at times,” said Kipps.
Another pause. Ann held out her hand.
“I’m glad I seen you,” she said.
Extraordinary impulses arose in neglected parts of Kipps’ being. “Ann,” he said and stopped.
“Yes,” said she, and was bright to him.
They looked at one another.
All and more than all of those first emotions of his adolescence had come back to him. Her presence banished a multitude of countervaling considerations. It was Ann more than ever. She stood breathing close to him, with her soft-looking lips a little apart and gladness in her eyes.
“I’m awful glad to see you again,” he said; “it brings back old times.”
“Doesn’t it?”
Another pause. He would have liked to have had a long talk to her, to have gone for a walk with her or something, to have drawn nearer to her in any conceivable way, and, above all, to have had some more of the appreciation that shone in her eyes, but a vestige of Folkestone still clinging to him told him it “wouldn’t do.” “Well,” he said, “I must be getting on,” and turned away reluctantly, with a will under compulsion.﻿ ﻿…
When he looked back from the corner she was still at the gate. She was perhaps a little disconcerted by his retreat. He felt that. He hesitated for a moment, half turned, stood and suddenly did great things with his hat. That hat! The wonderful hat of our civilisation!﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

「你猜呢？」心里却暗暗纳罕，自己怎么这么久都没看一眼那半块六便士。
安坦然朝他一笑。
「我没想到你会留着，」她说。「我常想——留着它真傻。再说，」她想了一想，「其实它也不代表什么。」
她说话时瞟了他一眼，正碰上他的目光。
「哦，不代表吗！」基普斯说，应得慢了半拍，一边说一边就意识到自己对海伦（Helen）的不忠。
「反正没多大意思，」安说。「你还在绸布庄？」
「我住在福克斯通，」基普斯起了个头，觉得这么说就够了。「希德没告诉你他碰到我了？」
「没有！在这儿？」
「对。前几天。约莫一个多礼拜前。」
「那我来之前。」
「啊！怪不得。」基普斯说。
「他有出息了，」安说。「如今有自己的铺子了，阿蒂。」
「他跟我说了。」
他们发现自己已站在马格特家的村舍外头。「你进去吗？」基普斯说。
「大概吧。」安说。
两人都悬在这一停顿上。安鼓足了劲。
「你常来新罗姆尼吗？」她说。
「隔三差五骑过来一趟。」基普斯说。
又停了停。安伸出手来。
「见到你真高兴。」她说。
基普斯身子里久已荒废的角落，涌起种种不寻常的冲动。「安，」他说，又止住了。
「嗯？」她说，对他亮亮的。
两人对望着。
他少年时代最初那番情感，全都回来了，还多出几分。她在跟前，一大堆掂量利害的念头便都散了。这还是安，比以往更是安。她站在离他一臂之内，呼吸着，柔软的嘴唇微微张开，眼里全是欢喜。
「再见到你，我高兴透了，」他说；「想起从前了。」
「可不是？」
又停了停。他很想同她长谈，同她去散个步什么的，用一切想得出的法子挨她再近些，尤其想再多领受一些她眼睛里闪烁的那份赏识；可他还剩着的一点福克斯通气告诉他「使不得」。「唉，」他说，「我得走了。」恋恋不舍地转过身去，意志在受着强迫。……
从街角回头望，她还立在门口。他这一撤，她也许有点发窘。他觉出来了。他犹豫片刻，半转过身，站住，忽然对他的帽子大干了一番。那顶帽子！我们文明的那顶奇帽！……

===Original===

In another minute he was engaged in a singularly absentminded conversation with his Uncle about the usual topics.
His Uncle was very anxious to buy him a few upright clocks as an investment for subsequent sale. And there were also some very nice globes, one terrestrial and the other celestial, in a shop at Lydd that would look well in a drawing-room and inevitably increase in value.﻿ ﻿… Kipps either did or did not agree to this purchase; he was unable to recollect.
The southwest wind perhaps helped him back, at any rate he found himself through Dymchurch without having noticed the place. There came an odd effect as he drew near Hythe. The hills on the left and the trees on the right seemed to draw together and close in upon him until his way was straight and narrow. He could not turn around on that treacherous, half-tamed machine, but he knew that behind him, he knew so well, spread the wide, vast flatness of the Marsh shining under the afternoon sky. In some way this was material to his thoughts. And as he rode through Hythe he came upon the idea that there was a considerable amount of incompatibility between the existence of one who was practically a gentleman and of Ann.
In the neighbourhood of Seabrook he began to think he had, in some subtle way, lowered himself by walking along by the side of Ann.﻿ ﻿… After all, she was only a servant.
Ann!
She called out all the least gentlemanly instincts of his nature. There had been a moment in their conversation when he had quite distinctly thought it would really be an extremely nice thing for someone to kiss her lips.﻿ ﻿… There was something warming about Ann﻿—at least for Kipps. She impressed him as having somewhen during their vast interval of separation contrived to make herself in some distinctive way his.
Fancy keeping that half sixpence all this time!
It was the most flattering thing that had ever happened to Kipps.
He found himself presently sitting over *The Art of Conversing*, lost in the strangest musings. He got up, walked about, became stagnant at the window for a space, roused himself and by way of something lighter tried *Sesame and Lilies*. From that, too, his attention wandered. He sat back. Anon he smiled, anon sighed. He arose, pulled his keys from his pocket, looked at them, decided and went upstairs.

===Chinese===

再过一分钟，他已在同叔父谈开了，一场格外心不在焉的谈话，谈的都是平常那些话题。
叔父正急着想替他买几座立式座钟，作为日后再出手的投资。利德一家铺子里还有几只很好的地球仪，一只地球的，一只天球的，摆在客厅里体面，价钱又必定看涨。……这几样东西基普斯究竟答应买没答应，他回想不起来了。
回程多半是西南风帮的忙，反正他穿过迪姆彻奇，竟全然没留意到这个地方。快到海斯时，来了种古怪的感觉：左边的丘冈和右边的树木仿佛渐渐合拢，向他夹逼过来，他的路变得又直又窄。在那辆半驯服的、靠不住的车上他没法回头，可他知道身后——他熟得很——铺展着沼泽那片辽阔浩大的平川，在午后的天光下闪亮。不知怎么，这景象同他的思绪大有干系。骑车穿过海斯时，他忽然想到：一个事实上已是绅士的人，同安之间，存在着相当程度的不相容。
到了西布鲁克一带，他开始觉得，方才同安并肩走路，他是在某个微妙之处自贬了身份。……说到底，她不过是个女仆。
安！
她把他天性里最不绅士的本能统统召唤了出来。方才那番谈话里，有一瞬他清清楚楚地想：有谁来亲一亲她的嘴唇，实在是件妙透了的事。……安身上有种发暖的东西——至少对基普斯是如此。她给他的印象是：在他们分别的漫长岁月里，她不知怎么已经让自己用某种独特的方式归他所有了。
想想看，那半块六便士她竟留了这么多年！
这是基普斯平生遇到的最长脸的事。
不一会儿，他发现自己枯坐在《谈话的艺术》上，陷入最古怪的冥想。他站起身，踱来踱去，在窗前呆立了一阵，振作起来，换点轻松的，又试《芝麻与百合》。注意力又从那上头溜走了。他仰回椅背。忽而微笑，忽而叹息。他站起身，从口袋里掏出钥匙，看了看，拿定主意，上楼去了。

===Original===

He opened the little yellow box that had been the nucleus of all his possessions in the world, and took out a small escritoire, the very humblest sort of present, and opened it﻿—kneeling. And there, in the corner, was a little packet of paper, sealed as a last defence against any prying invader, with red sealing wax. It had gone untouched for years. He held this little packet between finger and thumb for a moment, regarding it, and then put down the escritoire and broke the seal.﻿ ﻿…
As he was getting into bed that night he remembered something for the first time!
“Dash it!” he said. “Dashed if I told ’em *this* time.﻿ ﻿… *Well*! I shall ’ave to go over to New Romney again!”
He got into bed and remained sitting pensively on the pillow for a space.
“It’s a rum world,” he reflected after a vast interval.
Then he recalled that she had noticed his moustache and embarked upon a sea of egotistical musings.
He imagined himself telling Ann how rich he was. What a surprise that would be for her!
Finally he sighed profoundly, blew out his candle and snuggled down, and in a little while he was asleep.﻿ ﻿…
But the next morning and at intervals afterwards he found himself thinking of Ann﻿—Ann, the bright, the desirable, the welcoming, and with an extraordinary streakiness he wanted quite badly to go and then as badly not to go over to New Romney again.
Sitting on the Leas in the afternoon, he had an idea. “I ought to ’ave told ’er, I suppose, about my being engaged.
“Ann!”
All sorts of dreams and impressions that had gone clean out of his mental existence came back to him, changed and brought up to date to fit her altered presence. He thought of how he had gone back to New Romney for his Christmas holidays, determined to kiss her, and of the awful blankness of the discovery that she had gone away.
It seemed incredible now, and yet not wholly incredible, that he had cried real tears for her﻿—how many years was it ago?
Daily I should thank my Maker that He did not appoint me Censor of the world of men. I should temper a fierce injustice with a spasmodic indecision that would prolong rather than mitigate the bitterness of the Day. For human dignity, for all conscious human superiority I should lack the beginnings of charity, for bishops, prosperous schoolmasters, judges and all large respect-pampered souls.

===Chinese===

他打开那只小黄木箱——他全部家当发端的那个核——取出一个小小的写字匣，一份顶寒酸的礼物，跪着打开了它。瞧，角落里有一小包纸，用红火漆封着，作为对任何窥探之徒的最后一道防御。几年了，没人动过它。他用拇指和食指捏着这个小纸包端详了片刻，然后放下写字匣，拆了封。……
那天夜里上床的时候，他才头一回想起一桩事！
「该死！」他说。「真该死，这回又没告诉他们。……好吧！我还得再跑一趟新罗姆尼！」
他上了床，在枕头上坐着出神地坐了一阵。
「这个世界真邪门。」隔了老大半天他想。
跟着他想起她注意到了他的胡子，便一头扎进自恋的汪洋。
他想象着自己告诉安自己多么有钱。那对她该是多大的意外啊！
末了他长叹一声，吹灭蜡烛，钻进被窝，不多时便睡着了。……
可是第二天早晨，以及此后隔三差五，他发现自己总在想安——安，那个明亮的、可人的、满脸欢迎的安；他心里横一道竖一道地闹起别扭：一阵子急得要命想去新罗姆尼，一阵子又同样急得要命不想去。
下午坐在利斯草坪上，他起了一个念头。「我该告诉她我订了婚的，大概。
「安！」
种种早从他精神生活里扫地出门的梦想与印象又回来了，变了样，翻了个新，来配她如今改换的模样。他想起当年回新罗姆尼过圣诞假期、打定主意要吻她，以及发现她已远走时那一片可怕的空白。
如今想来不可思议——可又不全然不可思议——他曾为她掉过真的眼泪——那是多少年前的事了？
我但愿天天感谢造物主，幸而他没有派我做人世的监察官。我会用一阵一阵的优柔寡断去稀释凛冽的不公，结果只会把那大日子的苦味拖长，而不会冲淡。为了人的尊严，为了人一切自命的优越，我身上将连慈爱的雏形都没有——对主教们、得意洋洋的校长们、法官们，以及一切被敬重喂得脑满肠肥的灵魂。

===Original===

And more especially bishops, towards whom I bear an atavistic, Viking grudge, dreaming not infrequently and with invariable zest of galleys and landings and well known living ornaments of the episcopal bench sprinting inland on twinkling gaiters before my thirsty blade﻿—all these people, I say, should treat below their deserts, but, on the other hand, for such as Kipps﻿—. There the exasperating indecisions would come in. The Judgment would be arrested at Kipps. Everyone and everything would wait. *You* would wait. The balance would sway and sway, and whenever it heeled towards an adverse decision, my finger would set it swaying again. Kings, warriors, statesmen, brilliant women of our first families, personalities, gallants, panting with indignation, headline humanity in general, would stand undamned, unheeded, or be damned in the most casual manner for their importunity, while my eye went about for anything possible that could be said on behalf of Kipps.﻿ ﻿… Albeit I fear nothing can save him from condemnation upon this present score, that within two days he was talking to Ann again.
One seeks excuses. Overnight there had been an encounter of Chitterlow and young Walshingham in his presence, that had certainly warped his standards. They had called within a few minutes of each other, and the two swayed by virile attentions to Old Methuselah Four Stars, had talked against each other, over and at the hospitable presence of Kipps. Walshingham had seemed to win at the beginning, but finally Chitterlow had made a magnificent display of vociferation and swept him out of existence. At the beginning Chitterlow had opened upon the great profits of playwrights and young Walshingham had capped him at once with a cynical, but impressive, display of knowledge of the High Finance. If Chitterlow boasted his thousands, young Walshingham boasted his hundreds of thousands, and was for a space left in sole possession of the stage, juggling with the wealth of nations. He was going on by way of Financial Politics to the Overman, before Chitterlow recovered from his first check, and came back to victory.

===Chinese===

尤其是主教们，我对他们怀着一种隔代遗传的、北欧海盗式的宿怨，不时就做起梦来——梦得津津有味——梦见大桨船、登陆，以及圣公会主教席上几位在世的知名点缀，穿着闪闪的绑腿，在我饥饿的刀锋前面撒腿往内陆狂奔——我说的是，这号人本该受低于他们分内的待遇；可是反过来，轮到基普斯这号人——那叫人为难的犹豫就来了。大审判到了基普斯跟前就得暂停。人人、样样都得等着。*你*也得等着。天平摇过来，摇过去，每当它倾向不利的裁决，我的手指就把它拨得再摇起来。国王们、武士们、政治家们、名门望族的才女们、各路名士、情场骁将，一个个气得直喘，连同报纸头条上的全体人类，都将站着不受审判、无人理会，或者因其纠缠不休而被随随便便打发一顿，我的眼睛却转来转去，替基普斯找一切找得出的说词。……虽然我担心，眼下这一条罪状是没法替他开脱的了：不出两天，他又跟安说话去了。
替他找找借口吧。前一天晚上，奇特洛（Chitterlow）和小沃尔辛厄姆（Walshingham）当着他的面较了一场劲，那准是把他的标准带坏了。两人前后脚来访，都被「老玛土撒拉四星」的雄性款待推着，当着殷勤的主人基普斯，你一言我一语地顶着说。开初沃尔辛厄姆似乎占了上风，末了奇特洛作了一回气势磅礴的大嗓门表演，把他扫荡得无影无踪。起头奇特洛先吹剧作家如何发财，小沃尔辛厄姆立时用一套对高级金融的冷嘲式却令人折服的见识压过了他。奇特洛夸口他的几千几千，小沃尔辛厄姆就夸口他的几十万几十万，一度独占舞台，把各国财富玩弄于股掌之间。他正要顺着金融政治一路讲下去直讲到「超人」，奇特洛才从第一回合的挫败里缓过来，反攻取胜。

===Original===

“Talking of Women,” said Chitterlow, coming in abruptly upon some things not generally known, beyond Walshingham’s more immediate circle, about a recently departed empire-builder; “Talking of Women and the way they Get at a man﻿—”
[Though as a matter of fact they had been talking of the Corruption of Society by Speculation.]
Upon this new topic Chitterlow was soon manifestly invincible. He knew so much, he had known so many. Young Walshingham did his best with epigrams and reservations, but even to Kipps it was evident that this was a book-learned depravity. One felt Walshingham had never known the inner realities of passion. But Chitterlow convinced and amazed. He had run away with girls, he had been run away with by girls, he had been in love with several at a time﻿—“not counting Bessie”﻿—he had loved and lost, he had loved and refrained, and he had loved and failed. He threw remarkable lights upon the moral state of America﻿—in which country he had toured with great success. He set his talk to the tune of one of Mr. Kipling’s best known songs. He told an incident of simple, romantic passion, a delirious dream of love and beauty in a Saturday to Monday steamboat trip up the Hudson, and tagged his end with, “I learnt about women from ’er!” After that he adopted the refrain and then lapsed into the praises of Kipling. “Little Kipling,” said Chitterlow, with the familiarity of affection, “*he* knows,” and broke into quotation:

> “I’ve taken my fun where I found it;

I’ve rogued and I’ve ranged in my time;

I’ve ’ad my picking of sweet’earts,

An’ four of the lot was Prime.”

(These things, I say, affect the moral standards of the best of us.)
“*I’d* have liked to have written that,” said Chitterlow. “That’s life, that is! But go and put it on the stage, put even a bit of the realities of life on the stage, and see what they’ll do to you! Only Kipling could venture on a job like that. That poem *knocked* me! I don’t say Kipling hasn’t knocked me before and since, but that was a fair knock out. And yet﻿—you know﻿—there’s one thing in it﻿ ﻿… this:

> “I’ve taken my fun where I’ve found it,

And now I must pay for my fun,

For the more you ’ave known o’ the others,

The less will you settle to one﻿—

===Chinese===

「说到女人，」奇特洛说，猛地插到一桩沃尔辛厄姆小圈子之外鲜有人知的事上，说的是一位新近谢世的帝国缔造者；「说到女人，说到她们怎样把男人拿住——」
［虽然他们方才谈的其实是投机如何败坏社会。］
换到这个新题目上，奇特洛很快显出不可战胜。他知道得那么多，他见识过那么多人。小沃尔辛厄姆竭力用警句和保留条件招架，可连基普斯都听得出，这是一种书本上学来的堕落。谁都感觉得到，沃尔辛厄姆从未领教过激情的内幕。奇特洛却令人信服、令人叫绝。他同姑娘们私奔过，也被姑娘们带着私奔过；他一次爱过好几个——「这还不算贝西」——他爱过又丢了，他爱过又克制了，他还爱过又砸了锅。他对美国的道德状况投下异样的光——在那个国度他巡回演出大获成功过。他把谈话配上了吉卜林先生一首最有名的歌子的调子。他讲了一段纯朴而浪漫的热恋，一段从星期六到星期一乘轮船溯哈得孙河而上的、爱与美的迷梦，末了缀上一句：「我对女人的门道，就是跟她学的！」此后他就把这句话当了口头禅，随即又转入对吉卜林的颂扬。「小吉卜林，」奇特洛说，带着亲昵的熟稔，「*他*懂。」随即朗声引道：

> 「哪里找得到乐子，我就在哪里取乐；
> 我这辈子放荡过，也闯荡过；
> 情人由我挑了又拣，
> 里头顶尖的足足四位。」

（这类事情，我说，会影响我们最好的人的道德标准。）
「*我*倒真愿意这几行是我写的，」奇特洛说。「那才叫生活！可你把它搬上舞台试试，把生活真实的一角搬上舞台试试，瞧他们怎么收拾你！这种事只有吉卜林敢碰。那首诗把我*打*蒙了！我不是说吉卜林从前以后没打蒙过我，可那一回是干脆利落的一记闷倒。然而——你知道——里头有一句……是这句：

> 「哪里找得到乐子，我就在哪里取乐，
> 如今乐子该我来付账，
> 因为你见识过的别人越多，
> 就越难安分守着一个——」

===Original===

“Well. In my case anyhow﻿—I don’t know how much that proves, seeing I’m exceptional in so many things and there’s no good denying it﻿—but so far as I’m concerned﻿—I tell you two, but of course you needn’t let it go any farther﻿—I’ve been perfectly faithful to Muriel ever since I married her﻿—ever since.﻿ ﻿… Not once. Not even by accident have I ever said or done anything in the slightest﻿—.” His little, brown eye became pensive after this flattering intimacy and the gorgeous draperies of his abundant voice fell into graver folds. “*I learnt about women from ’er*,” he said impressively.
“Yes,” said Walshingham, getting into the hinder spaces of that splendid pause, “a man must know about women. And the only sound way of learning is the experimental method.”
“If you want to know about the experimental method, my boy,” said Chitterlow, resuming.﻿ ﻿…
So they talked. *Ex pede Herculem*, as Coote, that cultivated polyglot, would have put it. And in the small hours Kipps went to bed, with his brain whirling with words and whiskey, and sat for an unconscionable time upon his bed edge, musing sadly upon the unmanly monogamy of soul that had cast its shadow upon his career, musing with his thoughts pointing around more and more certainly to the possibility of at least duplicity with Ann.
For some days he had been refraining with some insistence from going off to New Romney again.﻿ ﻿…
I do not know if this may count in palliation of his misconduct. Men, real Strong-Souled, Healthy Men, should be, I suppose, impervious to conversational atmospheres, but I have never claimed for Kipps a place at these high levels. The unquenchable fact remains that the next day he spent the afternoon with Ann and found no scruple in displaying himself a budding lover.
He had met her in the High Street, had stopped her, and almost on the spur of the moment had boldly proposed a walk, “for the sake of old times.”
“*I* don’t mind,” said Ann.
Her consent almost frightened Kipps. His imagination had not carried him to that. “It would be a lark,” said Kipps, and looked up the street and down. “Now?” he said.
“I don’t mind a bit, Artie. I was just going for a walk along towards St. Mary’s.”
“Let’s go that way be’ind the church,” said Kipps, and presently they found themselves drifting seaward in a mood of pleasant commonplace. For a while they talked of Sid.

===Chinese===

「唉。反正我是这样——这能证明多少我不敢说，要知道我在好多事情上都是例外，这没必要否认——不过就我而论——我告诉你们两个，当然你们别往外传——自打娶了缪丽尔，我对她是一片忠心，从那以后一直。……一回也没有。连无意之中，我也从没说过一句、做过一丝一毫——。」这句讨人欢心的知心话说过，他那棕色的小眼睛变得若有所思，他那副丰沛嗓音的华丽帷褶，也换上了更庄重的皱。「*我对女人的门道，就是跟她学的*，」他郑重其事地说。
「是啊，」沃尔辛厄姆说，钻进那壮丽停顿的后部空当，「男人必须懂女人。而唯一靠得住的学法，是实验法。」
「你要想了解实验法，我的孩子，」奇特洛说，重又开了头。……
他们就这样谈着。*Ex pede Herculem*——由一足而识海格力斯——库特（Coote）那位博学的通晓数国语文的先生就会这么说的。后半夜基普斯上床时，脑子里搅满了词句和威士忌，在床沿上坐了好一阵不该有的工夫，哀哀地冥想着那种给他前程罩上阴影的、不丈夫的心灵上的专一，想着想着，念头越来越明确地指向一点：对安，至少来个脚踏两只船。
好几天了，他一直颇为起劲地克制着自己，不再往新罗姆尼跑。……
这能不能算他过错的一条减轻情节，我不知道。换了真正的强者之魂、健康男子，我想，理当不为谈话的空气所动；可我从来没说基普斯也够得上那样高的境界。无可抹杀的事实是：第二天，他同安消磨了一个下午，而且毫无愧色地摆出一副初出茅庐的情郎架势。
他是在大街上碰见她的，叫住了她，几乎当场就大着胆子提议散散步，「看在旧日的情分上」。
「*我*无所谓。」安说。
她一答应，基普斯反倒几乎吓着了。他的想象力还没跑到那么远过。「就当撒个欢儿，」基普斯说着，朝街两头望了望。「这就去？」他说。
「我一点也不反对，阿蒂。我正好要往圣玛丽那边散散步。」
「咱们打教堂后头那条道走吧。」基普斯说；不多时，两人已怀着一种愉快而平淡的心情，飘飘悠悠往海边去了。起先他们谈的是希德。

===Original===

It went clean out of Kipps’ head at that early stage even that Ann was a “girl” according to the exposition of Chitterlow, and for a time he remembered only that she was Ann. But afterwards, with the reek of that talk in his head, he lapsed a little from that personal relation. They came out upon the beach and sat down in a tumbled, pebbly place, where a meagre grass and patches of sea poppy were growing, and Kipps reclined on his elbow and tossed pebbles in his hand, and Ann sat up, sunlit, regarding him. They talked in fragments. They exhausted Sid, they exhausted Ann, and Kipps was chary of his riches.
He declined to a faint lovemaking. “I got that ’arf sixpence still,” he said.
“Reely?”
That changed the key. “I always kept mine, some’ow,” said Ann, and there was a pause.
They spoke of how often they had thought of each other during those intervening years. Kipps may have been untruthful, but Ann perhaps was not. “I met people here and there,” said Ann; “but I never met anyone quite like you, Artie.”
“It’s jolly our meeting again, anyhow,” said Kipps. “Look at that ship out there. She’s pretty close in.﻿ ﻿…”
He had a dull period, became indeed almost pensive, and then he was enterprising for a while. He tossed up his pebbles so that as if by accident they fell on Ann’s hand. Then, very penitently, he stroked the place. That would have led to all sorts of coquetries on the part of Flo Banks, for example, but it disconcerted and checked Kipps to find Ann made no objection, smiled pleasantly down on him, with eyes half shut because of the sun. She was taking things very much for granted.
He began to talk, and Chitterlow standards resuming possession of him he said he had never forgotten her.
“I never forgotten you either, Artie,” she said. “Funny, isn’t it?”
It impressed Kipps also as funny.
He became reminiscent, and suddenly a warm summer’s evening came back to him. “Remember them cockchafers, Ann?” he said. But the reality of the evening he recalled was not the chase of cockchafers.
