The great reality that had suddenly arisen between them was that he had never kissed Ann in his life. He looked up and there were her lips.
He had wanted to very badly, and his memory leaped and annihilated an interval. That old resolution came back to him and all sorts of new resolutions passed out of mind. And he had learnt something since those boyish days.

===Chinese===

在那么早的阶段，就连「安是奇特洛分类学意义上的『姑娘』」这一条，也从基普斯脑袋里跑得干干净净，有一阵他只记得她是安。可后来，那晚谈话的余味在脑中泛起，他从这份人身关系上滑开了一点。他们来到海滩上，在一片乱石嶙峋的砾石地坐下，那儿长着稀疏的草和成片的海罂粟；基普斯支着胳膊肘半躺着，手里抛着卵石，安坐得笔直，沐着阳光，端详他。他们有一搭没一搭地谈。谈完了希德，谈完了安自己，基普斯呢，对自己的家财守口如瓶。
他渐渐降格为一点浅浅的调情。「那半块六便士我还留着呢。」他说。
「真的？」
这一句换了调门。「我那半块也不知怎么一直留着。」安说，停了一停。
他们说起这些年间彼此想起过多少回。基普斯也许说了假话，安多半没有。「这儿那儿我也碰到过人，」安说；「可再没碰到过像你这样的，阿蒂。」
「又碰上了总是好的，怎么讲都好。」基普斯说。「瞧外头那条船。靠得真近。……」
他有一阵发闷，当真几乎忧郁起来，随后又进取了一阵。他把卵石往天上抛，让它们仿佛失手似的落在安手上，接着又万分懊悔地抚摸那落点。换了弗洛·班克斯，这一手准引出百般撒娇；可安半点不推拒，只叫他又是发窘又是收不住势——她笑吟吟地低头看他，阳光刺眼，眼睛眯成了一条缝。她把一切都当作理所当然。
他开始说话，奇特洛的标准重新占领了他，他说他从来没忘记过她。
「我也从没忘记过你，阿蒂。」她说。「怪不怪？」
基普斯也觉得怪。
他变得怀旧起来，忽然一个暖和的夏夜回到了他心头。「记得那些金龟子不，安？」他说。可他记起的那个夜晚，其实质并不是扑金龟子。两人之间猛然升起的那个巨大的实在，是他平生从没吻过安。他抬头，她的嘴唇就在那儿。
他曾经极想吻，他的记忆一跃，把中间那些年抹了个干净。旧日的决心回来了，种种新的决心全溜出了脑子。而这些年，他又学了一些乖。

===Original===

This time he did not ask. He went on talking, his nerves began very faintly to quiver and his mind grew bright.
Presently, having satisfied himself that there was no one to see, he sat up beside her and remarked upon the clearness of the air, and how close Dungeness seemed to them. Then they came upon a pause again.
“Ann,” he whispered, and put an arm that quivered about her.
She was mute and unresisting, and, as he was to remember, solemn.
He turned her face towards him, and kissed her lips, and she kissed him back again﻿—kisses frank and tender as a child’s.
It was curious that in the retrospect he did not find nearly the satisfaction in this infidelity he had imagined was there. It was no doubt desperately doggish, doggish to an almost Chitterlowesque degree to recline on the beach at Littlestone with a “girl,” to make love to her and to achieve the triumph of kissing her, when he was engaged to another “girl” at Folkestone, but somehow these two people were not “girls,” they were Ann and Helen. Particularly Helen declined to be considered as a “girl.” And there was something in Ann’s quietly friendly eyes, in her frank smile, in the naive pressure of her hand, there was something undefended and welcoming that imparted a flavour to the business upon which he had not counted. He had learnt about women from her. That refrain ran through his mind and deflected his thoughts, but as a matter of fact he had learnt about nothing but himself.
He wanted very much to see Ann some more and explain. He did not clearly know what it was he wanted to explain.
He did not clearly know anything. It is the last achievement of the intelligence to get all of one’s life into one coherent scheme, and Kipps was only in a measure more aware of himself as a whole than is a tree. His existence was an affair of dissolving and recurring moods. When he thought of Helen or Ann or any of his friends, he thought sometimes of this aspect and sometimes of that﻿—and often one aspect was finally incongruous with another. He loved Helen, he revered Helen. He was also beginning to hate her with some intensity. When he thought of that expedition to Lympne, profound, vague, beautiful emotions flooded his being; when he thought of paying calls with her perforce, or of her latest comment on his bearing, he found himself rebelliously composing fierce and pungent insults, couched in the vernacular.

===Chinese===

这一回他没有开口问。他继续说着话，神经开始极轻微地颤，脑子发起亮来。
过了一阵，确信四下无人看见，他坐起身挨着她，议论起空气多么清朗，邓杰内斯离得多么近。然后他们又停了下来。
「安，」他悄声说，一条微微发颤的胳膊揽住了她。
她不作声，也不挣，据他日后的记忆——庄重得很。
他扳过她的脸，吻了她的嘴唇，她也回吻了他——那吻坦白而温柔，像孩子的一样。
奇怪的是，事后回想，这桩负心并不像他想象的那样叫他受用。他当时在福克斯通同另一个「姑娘」订着婚，却躺在利特尔斯通的海滩上陪着一个「姑娘」，向她调情，并且挣到了吻她的胜利——这无疑下作透了，下作得几乎到了奇特洛的份上；可是不知怎么，这两个人都不是「姑娘」，她们是安和海伦。海伦尤其拒绝被当作「姑娘」。而安那双安静友善的眼睛、她坦白的微笑、她天真的握手，里头有一种不设防的、欢迎的东西，给这桩事添了一种他没料到的滋味。他对女人的门道，是从她那儿学的。那句口头禅在他脑子里流淌，引偏他的念头；可事实上，他学到的无非是他自己。
他非常想再见见安，解释解释。他自己也不清楚要解释什么。
他自己什么都不清楚。把一个人的全部生活收进一个贯通的图景，那是智能的最后成就；而基普斯对自身的整体知觉，只不过比一棵树强些有限。他的存在是些时聚时散的心绪。想起海伦、安或哪个朋友，他有时想到这一面，有时想到那一面——而两面又往往根本凑不到一块儿。他爱海伦，他敬海伦。他也开始顶顶起劲地恨她。想起那次去利姆尼的远足，深沉、朦胧、美好的情感漫过他全身；想起不得已陪她拜客，想起她最近对他举止仪态的评语，他发现自己正倔头倔脑地编派着狠毒泼辣的骂人话，全用土话。

===Original===

But Ann, whom he had seen so much less of, was a simpler memory. She was pretty, she was almost softly feminine, and she was possible to his imagination just exactly where Helen was impossible. More than anything else, she carried the charm of respect for him, the slightest glance of her eyes was balm for his perpetually wounded self-conceit.
Chance suggestions it was set the tune of his thoughts, and his state of health and repletion gave the colour. Yet somehow he had this at least almost clear in his mind, that to have gone to see Ann a second time, to have implied that she had been in possession of his thoughts through all this interval, and, above all, to have kissed her, was shabby and wrong. Only unhappily this much of lucidity had come now just a few hours after it was needed.
Four days after this it was that Kipps got up so late. He got up late, cut his chin while shaving, kicked a slipper into his sponge bath and said, “Desh!”
Perhaps you know those intolerable mornings, dear Reader, when you seem to have neither the heart nor the strength to rise, and your nervous adjustments are all wrong and your fingers thumbs, and you hate the very birds for singing. You feel inadequate to any demand whatever. Often such awakenings follow a poor night’s rest, and commonly they mean indiscriminate eating, or those subtle mental influences old Kipps ascribed to “Foozle Ile” in the system, or worry. And with Kipps﻿—albeit Chitterlow had again been his guest overnight﻿—assuredly worry had played a leading role. Troubles had been gathering upon him for days, there had been a sort of concentration of these hosts of Midian overnight, and in the grey small hours Kipps had held his review.
The predominating trouble marched under this banner:

> Mrs. **Bindon Botting**
> At Home
> Thursday, September 16th
> Anagrams, 4 to 6:30
> R.S.V.P.

===Chinese===

而安，他见得少得多的这个安，是一段简单些的记忆。她标致，她几乎称得上温柔女人味十足，而且她恰恰在想象力上处处可能，海伦则处处不可能。比什么都要紧的是，她带着一份对他的敬意；她眼睛里最轻微的一瞥，都是他那份永远在受伤的自尊心的香膏。
定他思绪调子的，是偶然的提示；给它上色的，是他的健康和饱胀的程度。可是不知怎么，至少有这一层他是清楚的：跑去看安，看了第二回，暗示她这些年一直占着他的心思，尤其还吻了她——这是卑鄙，是错。只是不幸，这一点清明来得晚了几个钟头，偏偏在正用得着它之后。
此后第四天，基普斯起了个大晚。他起晚了，刮脸割了下巴，一脚把拖鞋踢进洗脸盆，说了声：「该死！」
这样的早晨你是不是也知道，亲爱的读者：仿佛没心气也没力气起床，浑身的神经都调错了弦，手指头全成了大拇指，连鸟叫你都恨。你觉得应付不了任何一点要求。这种醒来，常跟着一夜没睡好之后；通常它意味着不分青红皂白地乱吃，或者老基普斯归之于体内「富泽尔油」作祟的那种微妙的心理影响，要么就是忧愁。而基普斯——虽说奇特洛又在他家过了夜——起主导作用的千真万确是忧愁。祸事已往他头上聚了好几天，一夜之间又仿佛米甸各路大军合了营，灰蒙蒙的后半夜，基普斯检阅了他们。
打头的那桩祸事，打着这面旗：

> **宾登·博廷**夫人
> 在家会客
> 星期四，九月十六日
> 字谜，四时至六时半
> 敬候赐复

===Original===

a banner that was the facsimile of a card upon his looking glass in the room below. And in relation to this terribly significant document things had come to a pass with Helen that he could only describe in his own expressive idiom as “words.”
It had long been a smouldering issue between them that Kipps was not availing himself with any energy or freedom of the opportunities he had of social exercises, much less was he seeking additional opportunities. He had, it was evident, a peculiar dread of that universal afternoon enjoyment, the Call, and Helen made it unambiguously evident that this dread was “silly” and had to be overcome. His first display of this unmanly weakness occurred at the Coote’s on the day before he kissed Ann. They were all there, chatting very pleasantly, when the little servant with the big cap announced the younger Miss Wace.
Whereupon Kipps manifested a lively horror and rose partially from his chair. “O Gum!” he protested. “Carn’t I go upstairs?”
Then he sank back, for it was too late. Very probably the younger Miss Wace had heard him as she came in.
Helen said nothing of that, though her manner may have shown her surprise, but afterwards she told Kipps he must get used to seeing people, and suggested that he should pay a series of calls with Mrs. Walshingham and herself. Kipps gave a reluctant assent at the time and afterwards displayed a talent for evasion that she had not suspected in him. At last she did succeed in securing him for a call upon Miss Punchafer, of Radnor Park﻿—a particularly easy call because Miss Punchafer being so deaf one could say practically what one liked﻿—and then outside the gate he shirked again. “I can’t go in,” he said in a faded voice.
“You *must*,” said Helen, beautiful as ever, but even more than a little hard and forbidding.
“I can’t.”
He produced his handkerchief hastily, thrust it to his face, and regarded her over it with rounded, hostile eyes.
“ ’Possible,” he said in a hoarse, strange voice out of the handkerchief. “Nozzez bleedin’.”
But that was the end of his power of resistance, and when the rally for the Anagram Tea occurred she bore down his feeble protests altogether. She insisted. She said frankly, “I am going to give you a good talking to about this,” and she did.﻿ ﻿…
From Coote he gathered something of the nature of Anagrams and Anagram parties.

===Chinese===

这面旗，原样复制自楼下他梳妆镜旁的一张卡片。而围绕这份煞有介事的文书，他和海伦之间闹到这一步，用他自己传神的说法，就是「吵了」。
他们之间早就闷着一桩悬案：基普斯面对社交演练的大好机会，既不积极利用，更谈不上主动添造机会。他对那项全民性的下午消遣——拜客——显然怀着一种特殊的恐惧；海伦则毫不含糊地指出，这种恐惧「蠢」，必须克服。他头一回露出这副没出息的怂相，是在库特家里，就在他吻安的前一天。大家正谈得高高兴兴，那个戴大便帽的小女仆进来通报：韦斯家二小姐到。
基普斯当即现出一副活灵活现的惊恐，从椅子里半站起来。「我的老天！」他告饶。「我不能上楼去躲躲？」
随即又坐了回去——已经来不及了。韦斯家二小姐进门时多半听见了。
那件事海伦一个字没提，虽然她的神色也许露了惊讶；可后来她告诉基普斯，他必须习惯见人，并提议他跟着沃尔辛厄姆太太和她本人去作一轮拜客。基普斯当时勉强应承了，事后却显出一种她不曾疑心过他的躲闪才能。末了她总算把他敲定，去拉德纳园区拜访庞查弗小姐——一趟格外好对付的拜客，因为庞查弗小姐耳朵背得厉害，你爱说什么几乎就可以说什么——结果到了大门口他又临阵脱逃。「我进不去。」他有气无力地说。
「你*必须*进。」海伦说，人还跟往常一样美，只是比往常更硬、更拒人。
「我进不去。」
他慌忙掏出手帕，捂在脸上，从手帕上头瞪着她，眼睛瞪得滚圆，充满敌意。
「不——可能，」他从手帕里发出一种沙哑陌生的声音。「鼻子出血了。」
可他抵抗的本事到此为止；等到「字谜茶会」动员令下来，她把他微弱的抗议一鼓荡平。她不容分说。她坦率地说：「这件事我要好好说你一顿。」她也真说了。……
从库特那儿，他摸清了字谜和字谜茶会的底细。

===Original===

An anagram, Coote explained, was a word spelt the same way as another, only differently arranged, as, for instance, T O C O E would be an anagram for his own name, Coote.
“T O C O E,” repeated Kipps very carefully.
“Or T O E C O,” said Coote.
“Or T O E C O,” said Kipps, assisting his poor head by nodding it at each letter.
“Toe Company like,” he said in his efforts to comprehend.
When Kipps was clear what an anagram meant, Coote came to the second heading, the Tea. Kipps gathered there might be from thirty to sixty people present, and that each one would have an anagram pinned on. “They give you a card to put your guesses on, rather like a dance programme, and then, you know, you go around and guess,” said Coote. “It’s rather good fun.”
“Oo rather!” said Kipps, with simulated gusto.
“It shakes everybody up together,” said Coote.
Kipps smiled and nodded.﻿ ﻿…
In the small hours all his painful meditations were threaded by the vision of that Anagram Tea; it kept marching to and fro and in and out of all his other troubles, from thirty to sixty people, mostly ladies and callers, and a great number of the letters of the alphabet, and more particularly P I K P S and T O E C O, and he was trying to make one word out of the whole interminable procession.﻿ ﻿…
This word, as he finally gave it with some emphasis to the silence of the night, was “*Demn!*”
Then, wreathed as it were in this lettered procession, was the figure of Helen as she had appeared at the moment of “words”; her face a little hard, a little irritated, a little disappointed. He imagined himself going around and guessing under her eye.﻿ ﻿…
He tried to think of other things, without lapsing upon a still deeper uneasiness that was wreathed with yellow sea poppies, and the figures of Buggins, Pierce and Carshot, three murdered Friendships, rose reproachfully in the stillness and changed horrible apprehensions into unspeakable remorse. Last night had been their customary night for the banjo, and Kipps, with a certain tremulous uncertainty, had put old Methuselah amidst a retinue of glasses on the table and opened a box of choice cigars. In vain. They were in no need, it seemed, of *his* society. But instead Chitterlow had come, anxious to know if it was all right about that syndicate plan.

===Chinese===

字谜，库特解释说，就是一个词跟另一个词字母相同、只是排法不同，比如 T O C O E 就是他的名字库特（Coote）的一个字谜。
「T、O、C、O、E。」基普斯一个字母一个字母、仔仔细细地复述。
「或者 T O E C O。」库特说。
「或者 T、O、E、C、O。」基普斯说，每念一个字母点一下头，给他那可怜的脑袋帮帮忙。
「像『脚趾连队』似的。」他努力领会着说。
等基普斯弄清了字谜是什么，库特讲到第二项：茶会。基普斯听下来，到场的可能有三十到六十人，每人身上都别一个字谜。「他们发你一张卡片写答案，挺像舞会节目单，然后呢，你知道，你就挨个儿去猜，」库特说。「挺有意思的。」
「哦，可不！」基普斯说，装出一副兴头。
「把大家伙儿都搅和到一块儿。」库特说。
基普斯微笑点头。……
后半夜，他所有的愁思都让那个字谜茶会的幻象串着：它在他别的祸事里进进出出、来来回回地行军——三十到六十个人，多半是太太们和拜客的相识，还有字母表上好大一批字母，尤其是 P、I、K、P、S 和 T、O、E、C、O——他正想从这没完没了的队列里拼出一个词来。……
这个词，他终于对着寂静的夜、加重语气说了出来，是「该——死！」
随后，缠在这支字母大军里的，是海伦的形象，「吵架」那一刻的她：脸有点硬，有点恼，有点失望。他想象自己在她的眼皮底下挨个儿去猜。……
他想想想别的，又不敢落到那桩更深处的不安上——那不安缠着黄色的海罂粟；巴金斯（Buggins）、皮尔斯（Pierce）和卡肖特（Carshot）这三段被谋害的交情，在寂静里满怀责备地站了起来，把骇人的预感换成了说不出的悔恨。昨天夜里本是他们班卓琴的例会；基普斯还带着几分哆哆嗦嗦的没底，把老玛土撒拉连同一队玻璃杯摆上桌，开了一盒上等雪茄。全白搭。看样子，他们是不需要*他*作伴了。来的却是奇特洛，急急地来问辛迪加那桩计划到底行不行。

===Original===

He had declined anything but a very weak whiskey and soda, “just to drink,” at least until business was settled, and had then opened the whole affair with an effect of great orderliness to Kipps. Soon he was taking another whiskey by sheer inadvertency, and the complex fabric of his conversation was running more easily from the broad loom of his mind. Into that pattern had interwoven a narrative of extensive alterations in the Pestered Butterfly﻿—the neck and beetle business was to be restored﻿—the story of a grave difference of opinion with Mrs. Chitterlow, where and how to live after the play had succeeded, the reasons why the Hon. Thomas Norgate had never financed a syndicate, and much matter also about the syndicate now under discussion. But if the current of their conversation had been vortical and crowded, the outcome was perfectly clear. Kipps was to be the chief participator in the syndicate, and his contribution was to be two thousand pounds. Kipps groaned and rolled over and found Helen, as it were, on the other side. “Promise me,” she had said, “you won’t do anything without consulting me.”
Kipps at once rolled back to his former position, and for a space lay quite still. He felt like a very young rabbit in a trap.
Then suddenly, with extraordinary distinctness, his heart cried out for Ann, and he saw her as he had seen her at New Romney, sitting amidst the yellow sea poppies with the sunlight on her face. His heart called out for her in the darkness as one calls for rescue. He knew, as though he had known it always, that he loved Helen no more. He wanted Ann, he wanted to hold her and be held by her, to kiss her again and again, to turn his back forever on all these other things.﻿ ﻿…
He rose late, but this terrible discovery was still there, undispelled by cockcrow or the day. He rose in a shattered condition, and he cut himself while shaving, but at last he got into his dining-room and could pull the bell for the hot constituents of his multifarious breakfast. And then he turned to his letters. There were two real letters in addition to the customary electric belt advertisement, continental lottery circular and betting tout’s card. One was in a slight mourning envelope and addressed in an unfamiliar hand. This he opened first and discovered a note:

> Mrs. **Raymond Wace**

Requests the pleasure of

Mr. **Kipps’**

Company at Dinner

===Chinese===

他什么也不肯喝，只除了极淡的威士忌苏打，「润润嗓子」，至少等生意谈妥再说；随后便把整个事情头头是道地对基普斯摊开。不多时，他又全然出于无心续上了第二杯威士忌，他那谈话的复杂织物，从他心智的阔幅织机上流泻得越发顺畅。织进那图案里去的，有《受气的蝴蝶》大改特改的消息——脖颈和甲虫那一场要恢复了——有他同奇特洛太太一场严重的意见分歧：戏成功之后到哪儿住、怎么住；有托马斯·诺盖特阁下始终没肯给一个辛迪加出钱的缘由；还有眼下这个辛迪加本身的诸多事项。他们那晚的谈话纵然漩涡丛生、拥挤不堪，结论却清清楚楚：基普斯将是这个辛迪加的头号出资人，出资两千镑。基普斯哼了一声，翻个身，发现海伦仿佛就在另一侧。「答应我，」她说过，「凡事不跟我商量，什么都别应承。」
基普斯立刻翻回原来那一侧，有一阵躺着一动不动。他觉得自己像一只落进夹子的、很小很小的兔子。
突然，异乎寻常地清晰，他的心朝着安呼喊起来，他看见她像在新罗姆尼那时一样，坐在黄色的海罂粟中间，阳光照在她脸上。他的心在黑暗里朝她呼救，像人喊救命那样。他知道了——仿佛从来就知道——他不再爱海伦了。他要安，他要抱住她也被她抱住，要一而再、再而三地吻她，要永远背过身去，丢开所有这一切。……
他起晚了，可这个可怕的发现还在，鸡鸣和白昼都驱不散它。他起来时浑身散了架，刮脸又割了口子，但总算进了饭厅，能拉铃叫那顿五花八门的早饭的热腾腾的组成部分了。然后他转身看信。除了例行的电腰带广告、欧洲大陆彩票传单和赌马掮客的名片，有两封真正的信。一封装在窄边黑框的信封里，字迹陌生。他先拆这封，发现一份请帖：

> **雷蒙德·韦斯**夫人
> 敬请
> **基普斯**先生
> 光临晚宴

===Original===

on Tuesday, September 21st, at 8 o’clock

With a hasty movement Kipps turned his mind to the second letter. It was an unusually long one from his Uncle, and ran as follows:

> “My Dear Nephew
> “We are considerably startled by your letter though expecting something of the sort and disposed to hope for the best. If the young lady is a relation to the Earl of Beauprés well and good but take care you are not being imposed upon for there are many who will be glad enough to snap you up now your circumstances are altered﻿—I waited on the old Earl once while in service and he was remarkably close with his tips and suffered from corns. A hasty old gent and hard to please﻿—I daresay he has forgotten me altogether﻿—and anyhow there is no need to rake up bygones. Tomorrow is bus day and as you say the young lady is living near by we shall shut up shop for there is really nothing doing now what with all the visitors bringing everything with them down to their very children’s pails and say how de do to her and give her a bit of a kiss and encouragement if we think her suitable﻿—she will be pleased to see your old uncle﻿—We wish we could have had a look at her first but still there is not much mischief done and hoping that all will turn out well yet I am
> Your affectionate Uncle
> Edward George Kipps

“My heartburn still very bad. I shall bring over a few bits of rhubub I picked up, a sort you won’t get in Folkestone and if possible a good bunch of flowers for the young lady.”
“Comin’ over today,” said Kipps, standing helplessly with the letter in his hand.
“ ’Ow, the Juice﻿—?
“I carn’t.
“Kiss ’er!”
“I carn’t even face ’er﻿—!”
A terrible anticipation of that gathering framed itself in his mind﻿—a hideous, impossible disaster.
His voice went up to a note of despair, “And it’s too late to telegrarf and stop ’em!”
About twenty minutes after this, an outporter in Castle Hill Avenue was accosted by a young man, with a pale, desperate face, an exquisitely rolled umbrella and a heavy Gladstone bag.
“Carry this to the station, will you?” said the young man. “I want to ketch the nex’ train to London.﻿ ﻿… You’ll ’ave to look sharp﻿—I ’aven’t very much time.”

===Chinese===

于九月二十一日星期二，八时
基普斯慌忙把心思转到第二封信上。那是叔父来的一封少见的长信，全文如下：

> 「亲爱的侄儿：
> 「你的信叫我们大吃一惊，虽然也料到有这么一出，且往好里盼吧。那位年轻小姐既是博普雷伯爵的亲戚，那敢情好；可千万当心别叫人算计了，如今你的光景变了，乐得把你一口吞下去的人多着呢——当年我帮佣时伺候过老伯爵一回，他给赏钱抠得吓人，还长鸡眼。一位火性子的老先生，难伺候——保准早把我忘了个精光——反正旧账也不必翻了。明天是班车日，你既说那位年轻小姐就住在近边，我们就关一天铺子吧，如今游客们连小孩的水桶都自带，实在没什么生意好做，我们去跟她问个好，要是我们看中意，就亲她一下、勉励几句——她见到你的老叔父准高兴——本想先看一看她，可惜没看成，好在也没闹出什么乱子，盼着一切到头来圆满，那么我就是
> 爱你的叔父
> 爱德华·乔治·基普斯

「我心口烧得还是厉害。我会捎几块我淘来的大黄，福克斯通买不着的那种；要是赶得上，再给年轻小姐带一大捧花。」
「今儿就要过来。」基普斯说，手里攥着信，束手无策。
「嗐，这搞的什么名堂——？
「我不行。
「亲她！」
「我连见都不敢见她——！」
那场聚会的可怕预感在他脑子里成了形——一场骇人的、断无可能收拾的大祸。
他的嗓门升到绝望的调门：「这会儿打电报也拦不住他们了！」
此后约莫二十分钟，城堡山大道上一个搬行李的脚夫被一个小伙子叫住：一张煞白的、豁出去的脸，一把卷得极讲究的雨伞，一只沉甸甸的格拉德斯通旅行包。
「把这个拎到车站去，行吗？」小伙子说。「我要赶下一班去伦敦的火车。……你得麻利点——我工夫不多了。」

## III Engaged / 三 订婚
===Original===

Within two months, within a matter of three and fifty days, Kipps had clambered to the battlements of Heart’s Desire.
It all became possible by the Walshinghams﻿—it would seem at Coote’s instigation﻿—deciding, after all, not to spend the holidays at Bruges. Instead, they remained in Folkestone, and this happy chance gave Kipps just all these opportunities of which he stood in need.
His crowning day was at Lympne, and long before the summer warmth began to break, while indeed August still flamed on high. They had organized﻿—no one seemed to know who suggested it first﻿—a water party on the still reaches of the old military canal at Hythe, the canal that was to have stopped Napoleon if the sea failed us, and they were to picnic by the brick bridge, and afterwards to clamber to Lympne Castle. The host of the gathering, it was understood very clearly, was Kipps.
They went, a merry party. The canal was weedy, with only a few inches of water at the shallows, and so they went in three Canadian canoes. Kipps had learned to paddle﻿—it had been his first athletic accomplishment, and his second﻿—with the last three or four of ten private lessons still to come﻿—was to be cycling. But Kipps did not paddle at all badly; muscles hardened by lifting pieces of cretonne could cut a respectable figure by the side of Coote’s executions, and the girl with the freckles, the girl who understood him, came in his canoe. They raced the Walshinghams, brother and sister; and Coote, in a liquefying state and blowing mightily, but still persistent and always quite polite and considerate, toiled behind with Mrs. Walshingham. She could not be expected to paddle (though, of course, she “offered”) and she reclined upon specially adjusted cushions under a black and white sunshade and watched Kipps and her daughter, and feared at intervals that Coote was getting hot.
They were all more or less in holiday costume, the eyes of the girls looked out under the shade of wide-brimmed hats; even the freckled girl was unexpectedly pretty, and Helen, swinging sunlit to her paddle, gave Kipps, almost for the first time, the suggestion of a graceful body. Kipps was arrayed in the completest boating costume, and when his fashionable Panama was discarded and his hair blown into disorder he became, in his white flannels, as sightly as most young men. His complexion was a notable asset.

===Chinese===

不出两个月，前后五十三天的工夫，基普斯（Kipps）已经攀上了心愿之堡的城垛。
这一切之所以可能，是因为沃尔辛厄姆（Walshingham）一家——看样子是库特（Coote）撺掇的——到底决定不去布鲁日度假了。他们留在了福克斯通（Folkestone），这个可喜的巧合，恰把基普斯用得着的机会全数送到他手里。
他的加冕日在利姆尼，那时盛夏的暑气还没开始消退，八月还在头顶上熊熊燃烧。他们组织了——起头是谁先提议的似乎没人知道——一场水上聚会，地点在海斯那条古军运河平静的河段上；那条运河，本是万一海上失守时用来挡拿破仑（Napoleon）的；他们要在砖桥边野餐，饭后去爬利姆尼城堡。谁做东，大家心照不宣：基普斯。
他们出发了，一支快活的队伍。运河里长满水草，浅处只有几寸水，所以他们分乘三条加拿大式独木舟。基普斯已经学会了划桨——那是他头一项体育成绩；第二项是骑车，十节私人课还剩三四节没上。不过基普斯划得一点也不赖；一副抬印花布抬硬了的筋骨，摆在库特那套动作旁边，居然也拿得出手；那位生雀斑的姑娘，那位懂他的姑娘，坐进了他的独木舟。他们同沃尔辛厄姆家兄妹比赛；库特落在后面，浑身化成一滩水，呼哧呼哧喘得厉害，却仍然锲而不舍，而且始终客客气气、体贴周到，同沃尔辛厄姆太太一道在后头苦熬。总不能指望太太也划桨（当然，她「表示过」），她斜倚在特意安放妥帖的坐垫上，撑一把黑白阳伞，望着基普斯和她女儿，还时不时担心库特热着了。
大家多多少少都是假日打扮，姑娘们的眼睛从宽边帽檐的荫凉里望出来；连那位生雀斑的姑娘也出落得出人意料地好看；海伦迎着阳光摇桨，几乎头一回让基普斯觉出她身段的秀美。基普斯一身最齐全的划船装束，等他那顶时新的巴拿马草帽一摘、头发吹乱了之后，穿着白法兰绒的他，跟大多数年轻人一样耐看了。他的脸色是笔了不起的家当。

===Original===

Things favoured him, the day favoured him, everyone favoured him. Young Walshingham, the girl with the freckles, Coote and Mrs. Walshingham, were playing up to him in the most benevolent way, and between the landing place and Lympne, Fortune, to crown their efforts, had placed a small, convenient field entirely at the disposal of an adolescent bull. Not a big, real, resolute bull, but, on the other hand, no calf; a young bull, in the same stage of emotional development as Kipps, “standing where the two rivers meet.” Detachedly our party drifted towards him.
When they landed young Walshingham, with the simple directness of a brother, abandoned his sister to Kipps and secured the freckled girl, leaving Coote to carry Mrs. Walshingham’s light wool wrap. He started at once, in order to put an effectual distance between himself and his companion, on the one hand, and a certain persuasive chaperonage that went with Coote, on the other. Young Walshingham, I think I have said, was dark, with a Napoleonic profile, and it was natural for him, therefore, to be a bold thinker and an epigrammatic speaker, and he had long ago discovered great possibilities of appreciation in the freckled girl. He was in a very happy frame that day because he had just been entrusted with the management of Kipps’ affairs (old Bean inexplicably dismissed), and that was not a bad beginning for a solicitor of only a few months’ standing, and, moreover, he had been reading Nietzsche, and he thought that in all probability he was the Non-Moral Overman referred to by that writer. He wore fairly large-sized hats. He wanted to expand the theme of the Non-Moral Overman in the ear of the freckled girl, to say it over, so to speak, and in order to seclude his exposition they went aside from the direct path and trespassed through a coppice, avoiding the youthful bull. They escaped to these higher themes but narrowly, for Coote and Mrs. Walshingham, subtle chaperones both, and each indisposed for excellent reasons to encumber Kipps and Helen, were hot upon their heels. These two kept direct route to the stile of the bull’s field, and the sight of the animal at once awakened Coote’s innate aversion to brutality in any shape or form. He said the stiles were too high, and that they could do better by going around by the hedge, and Mrs. Walshingham, nothing loath, agreed.

===Chinese===

事事都帮着他，天帮着他，人人都帮着他。小沃尔辛厄姆、生雀斑的姑娘、库特和沃尔辛厄姆太太，都怀着最大的善意给他配戏；而在登岸处与利姆尼之间，命运女神为了给众人的努力加冕，还安排了一块又小又合用的小牧场，整个儿交给了一头青春期的小公牛。不是那种高大、认真、有决断的公牛，可另一方面也不是牛犊；一头年轻的公牛，感情发育的阶段同基普斯不相上下，「站在两条河交汇之处」。我们的队伍若无其事地朝它漂过去。
一上岸，小沃尔辛厄姆拿出做兄长的爽快利落，把妹妹丢给基普斯，自己占住了生雀斑的姑娘，把替沃尔辛厄姆太太拿薄羊毛披肩的差事丢给了库特。他当即抢在前头走，好在自己和女伴这一头，同库特连同他那套富有说服力的监护之间，拉开一段有效的距离。小沃尔辛厄姆——我大概说过——皮肤黝黑，一副拿破仑式的侧影；因此他天生就该是大胆的思想家和善用警句的演说家，而且他早就发现生雀斑的姑娘身上大有赏识的余地。那天他心绪极佳，因为他刚刚受托经管基普斯的事务（老比恩莫名其妙地被辞退了），这对一个开业才几个月的律师不算个坏开头；何况他新近还在读尼采，并且认定那位作家所说的非道德超人，多半就是指他本人。他戴的帽子尺码相当大。他要在生雀斑的姑娘耳边把「非道德超人」这个题目铺陈开来，可以说是把它再诵一遍；为了给他的阐述寻个清静，他们偏离正路，穿进一片小树林，避开了那头少年公牛。他们逃向这些崇高的题目，也只是险险脱身，因为库特和沃尔辛厄姆太太——两位细腻的监护，各自出于绝好的理由不愿拖累基普斯和海伦——正紧紧跟在他们脚跟后头。这两位直奔公牛那块地的栅栏踏级，那牲口一入眼，立刻唤起了库特对任何形式、任何形状的暴力的天生反感。他说栅栏踏级太高，绕树篱走更好，沃尔辛厄姆太太正求之不得，立刻赞成。

===Original===

This left the way clear for Kipps and Helen, and they encountered the bull. Helen did not observe the bull, but Kipps did; but, that afternoon at any rate, he was equal to facing a lion. And the bull really came at them. It was not an affair of the bullring exactly, no desperate rushes and gorings; but he came; he regarded them with a large, wicked, bluish eye, opened a mouth below his moistly glistening nose and booed, at any rate, if he did not exactly bellow, and he shook his head wickedly and showed that tossing was in his mind. Helen was frightened, without any loss of dignity, and Kipps went extremely white. But he was perfectly calm, and he seemed to her to have lost the last vestiges of his accent and his social shakiness. He directed her to walk quietly towards the stile, and made an oblique advance towards the bull.
“You be orf!” he said.﻿ ﻿…
When Helen was well over the stile Kipps withdrew in good order. He got over the stile under cover of a feint, and the thing was done﻿—a small thing, no doubt, but just enough to remove from Helen’s mind an incorrect deduction that a man who was so terribly afraid of a teacup as Kipps must necessarily be abjectly afraid of everything else in the world. In her moment of reaction she went perhaps too far in the opposite direction. Hitherto Kipps had always had a certain flimsiness of effect for her. Now suddenly he was discovered solid. He was discovered possible in many new ways. Here, after all, was the sort of back a woman can get behind!﻿ ﻿…
As so these heirs of the immemorial ages went past the turf-crowned mass of Portus Lemanus up the steep slopes towards the medieval castle on the crest the thing was also manifest in her eyes.
Everyone who stays in Folkestone gets, sooner or later, to Lympne. The castle became a farmhouse long ago, and the farmhouse, itself now ripe and venerable, wears the walls of the castle as a little man wears a big man’s coat. The kindliest of farm ladies entertains a perpetual stream of visitors and shows her vast mangle, and her big kitchen, and takes you out upon the sunniest little terrace garden in all the world, and you look down the sheep-dotted slopes to where, beside the canal and under the trees, the crumpled memories of Rome sleep forever. For hither to this lonely spot the galleys once came, the legions, the emperors, masters of the world.

===Chinese===

这就给基普斯和海伦让出了道，他们同公牛打了个照面。海伦没瞧见公牛，基普斯瞧见了；不过，那天下午至少，他面对一头狮子也顶得住。那公牛也当真朝他们来了。倒不是斗牛场上的那种阵仗，没有拼命的冲刺和挑角；可它来了；它用一只大大的、凶恶的蓝眼睛打量他们，在湿亮鼻头下面张开一张嘴，就算没吼，至少也哞了一声，还恶狠狠地摇头，表明它心里存着挑人的念头。海伦害怕了，怕得毫不失体面；基普斯脸白极了。可他镇定得很，而且在她看来，他乡音的最后一点残迹、社交上的最后一点瑟缩，全都不见了。他吩咐她安静地朝栅栏踏级走，自己斜着朝公牛逼近过去。
「你给我走开！」他说。……
等海伦稳稳过了栅栏，基普斯才从容撤退。他借一个佯攻作掩护翻过栅栏，事情就办成了——一件小事，无疑，但恰恰够把海伦心里一个不正确的推论扫掉：一个像基普斯这样见了茶杯都怕成那样的人，必定对世上其余一切也都怕得卑卑怯怯。她在心潮回涌的那一瞬，也许又走到了相反的极端。在此之前，基普斯在她眼里向来有点单薄。如今他忽然被发现是坚实的。被发现许许多多新的方面都可能了。这毕竟是一副女人可以躲在后头的那种脊背！……
当这些古老年代的继承人走过莱马努斯港那草皮覆顶的一大片残垣、顺着陡坡向山头上那座中世纪城堡攀登时，这份发现在她眼睛里也是明摆着的。
凡在福克斯通住过的人，早晚都会到利姆尼来。城堡早就改成了农舍，而农舍如今自己也熟透古雅了，把城堡的墙穿在身上，像小个子穿大个子的外套。最和善的农舍女主人接待着一茬接一茬川流不息的来客，领人看她那台巨大的轧干机、她的大厨房，把你带上那片全世界阳光最充足的小平台花园，你可以顺着点缀着羊群的坡地望下去，望到运河边、树荫下，罗马的皱缩的记忆在那里长眠。因为大桨船当年就是到这个僻静的地方来的，还有军团，还有皇帝们，世界的主人们。

===Original===

The castle is but a thing of yesterday, King Stephen’s time or thereabout, in that retrospect. One climbs the pitch of perforation, and there one is lifted to the centre of far more than a hemisphere of view. Away below one’s feet, almost at the bottom of the hill, the Marsh begins, and spreads and spreads in a mighty crescent that sweeps about the sea, the Marsh dotted with the church towers of forgotten medieval towns and breaking at last into the low, blue hills of Winchelsea and Hastings; east hangs France, between the sea and the sky, and round the north, bounding the wide prospectives of farms and houses and woods, the Downs, with their hangers and chalk pits, sustain the passing shadows of the sailing clouds.
And here it was, high out of the world of everyday, and in the presence of spacious beauty, that Kipps and Helen found themselves agreeably alone. All six, it had seemed, had been coming for the Keep, but Mrs. Walshingham had hesitated at the horrid little stairs, and then suddenly felt faint, and so she and the freckled girl had remained below, walking up and down in the shadow of the house, and Coote had remembered they were all out of cigarettes, and had taken off young Walshingham into the village. There had been shouting to explain between ground and parapet, and then Helen and Kipps turned again to the view, and commended it and fell silent.
Helen sat fearlessly in an embrasure, and Kipps stood beside her.
“I’ve always been fond of scenery,” Kipps repeated, after an interval.
Then he went off at a tangent. “D’you reely think that was right what Coote was saying?”
She looked interrogation.
“About my name?”
“Being really C U Y P S? I have my doubts. I thought at first﻿—. What makes Mr. Coote add an S to Cuyp?”
“I dunno,” said Kipps, foiled. “I was jest thinking﻿—”
She shot one wary glance at him and then turned her eyes to the sea.
Kipps was out for a space. He had intended to lead from this question to the general question of surnames and change of names; it had seemed a light and witty way of saying something he had in mind, and suddenly he perceived that this was an unutterably vulgar and silly project. The hitch about that *s* had saved him. He regarded her profile for a moment, framed in weather-beaten stone, and backed by the blue elements.
He dropped the question of his name out of existence and spoke again of the view.

===Chinese===

相形之下，城堡不过是昨天的东西，斯蒂芬王那个年头的，或者前后不远。爬上那段孔洞累累的陡坡，人便被托举到远不止半个地球视野的中心。脚底下，几乎在山脚，沼泽开始了，铺开去，铺开去，成一道扫过大海的浩大新月；沼泽上点缀着被遗忘的中世纪小镇的教堂尖塔，最后断成温切尔西和黑斯廷斯低低的青山；东边悬着法兰西，在海与天之间；北面环抱着农庄、房舍和树林的宽阔远景的，是白垩丘陵，连同它们的悬林和采石场，承接着一朵朵过路云帆的阴影。
而正是在这里，高踞于日常世界之外，面对着壮阔的美，基普斯和海伦发现他们称心如意地单独在一起了。看那阵势，六个人原都是要上主堡去的，可沃尔辛厄姆太太在那道可怕的小楼梯前犹豫了，跟着忽然觉得发晕，于是她和生雀斑的姑娘留在下面，在房子的阴影里来回走动；库特又想起大家的烟都抽完了，带着小沃尔辛厄姆到村里去了。堡下堡上喊话解释了一通，随后海伦和基普斯又转过身去看风景，赞美了一阵，沉默下来。
海伦毫无惧色地坐在一个箭窗上，基普斯站在她身旁。
「我一向喜欢风景。」隔了一阵，基普斯又说了一遍。
随后他岔了开去。「你说库特讲的那个，当真是那么回事么？」
她投来疑问的一眼。
「关于我的姓？」
「真是拼作 C U Y P S 么？我有点疑心。我起初以为——。库特先生干吗要给 Cuyp 添个 S？」
「我不知道，」基普斯被将了一军，说。「我正想——」
她警觉地瞥了他一眼，随即把目光转向大海。
基普斯一时语塞。他本打算借这个问题引到姓氏和改姓的大题目上去；那似乎是一条又轻巧又俏皮的路子，好说出他心里的一件事，可他猛然看出，这是一个俗不可耐、蠢不可及的计划。卡在那个 *s* 上的岔子救了他。他端详了片刻她的侧影，镶在风吹雨打的石头框子里，背衬着蓝的天、蓝的海。
他把姓氏的问题丢到九霄云外，重新谈起风景来。

===Original===

“When I see scenery, and things that are beautiful, it makes me feel﻿—”
She looked at him suddenly, and saw him fumbling for his words.
“Silly like,” he said.
She took him in with her glance, the old look of proprietorship it was, touched with a certain warmth. She spoke in a voice as unambiguous as her eyes. “You needn’t,” she said. “You know, Mr. Kipps, you hold yourself too cheap.”
Her eyes and words smote him with amazement. He stared at her like a man who awakens. She looked down.
“You mean﻿—” he said; and then, “don’t you hold me cheap?”
She glanced up again and shook her head.
“But﻿—for instance﻿—you don’t think of me﻿—as an equal like.”
“Why not?”
“Oo! But reely﻿—”
His heart beat very fast.
“If I thought,” he said, and then, “you know so much.”
“That’s nothing,” she said.
Then, for a long time, as it seemed to them, both kept silence, a silence that said and accomplished many things.
“I know what I am,” he said, at length.﻿ ﻿… “If I thought it was possible.﻿ ﻿… If I thought *you*.﻿ ﻿… I believe I could do anything﻿—”
He stopped, and she sat downcast and strikingly still.
“Miss Walshingham,” he said, “is it possible that you﻿ ﻿… could care for me enough to﻿—to ’elp me? Miss Walshingham, do you care for me at all?”
It seemed she was never going to answer. She looked up at him. “I think,” she said, “you are the most generous﻿—look at what you have done for my brother﻿—the most generous and the most modest of men. And this afternoon﻿—I thought you were the bravest.”
She turned her head, glanced down, waved her hand to someone on the terrace below, and stood up.
“Mother is signalling,” she said. “We must go down.”
Kipps became polite and deferential by habit, but his mind was a tumult that had nothing to do with that.
He moved before her towards the little door that opened on the winding stairs﻿—“always precede a lady down or up stairs”﻿—and then on the second step he turned resolutely. “But,” he said, looking up out of the shadow, flannel-clad and singularly like a man.
She looked down on him, with her hand upon the stone lintel.
He held out his hand as if to help her. “Can you tell me?” he said. “You must know﻿—”
“What?”
“If you care for me?”
She did not answer for a long time. It was as if everything in the world had drawn to the breaking point, and in a minute must certainly break.
“Yes,” she said, at last, “I know.”

===Chinese===

「我一看见风景，看见美的东西，我就觉得——」
她忽然看着他，看见他在字句里摸索。
「傻乎乎的。」他说。
她一眼把他收了进去，还是那副物主的老眼光，只是掺了一点暖意。她的声音同她的眼睛一样毫不含糊。「你不必这样，」她说。「你要知道，基普斯先生，你把自己看得太贱了。」
她的目光和话音打得他目瞪口呆。他像猛醒过来的人一样瞪着她。她垂下了眼。
「你是说——」他说；接着又问：「你不把我看贱？」
她又抬眼望了望，摇了摇头。
「可是——比方说——你不把我——当个平等的人看。」
「为什么不？」
「哦！可真的——」
他的心跳得飞快。
「我要是觉得，」他说，又说，「你知道的那么多。」
「那不算什么。」她说。
于是，在两人感觉里过了好久好久，都沉默着；那沉默说了许多事，也成了许多事。
「我知道自己是块什么料，」他终于说。……「我要是觉得这有可能。……我要是觉得*你*。……我相信我什么事都办得到——」
他停住了，她坐着，低着头，静得出奇。
「沃尔辛厄姆小姐，」他说，「你有没有可能……肯对我好到——到拉我一把？沃尔辛厄姆小姐，你到底对我有没有一点意思？」
她仿佛永远不打算回答了。她抬起眼看他。「我认为，」她说，「你是最大度的——看看你为我哥哥做的一切——最大度、最谦逊的男人。还有今天下午——我觉着你最勇敢。」
她转过头，朝下面，朝平台上的什么人挥了挥手，站起身来。
「母亲在打手势，」她说。「咱们得下去了。」
基普斯出于习惯变得殷勤恭顺，可他心里翻腾着一团乱麻，同这些毫不相干。
他走在她前头，朝那扇开在旋转楼梯上的小门走去——「上下楼梯永远走在女士前头」——走到第二级，他毅然回过身来。「可是，」他说，从阴影里仰起脸，一身法兰绒，出奇地像个男子汉。
她低头看他，一只手扶着石头门楣。
他伸出手去，仿佛要扶她。「你能告诉我吗？」他说。「你一定知道——」
「知道什么？」
「你对我有没有意思？」
她好半天没有回答。仿佛世上的一切都绷到了断裂的临界，再过一分钟必断无疑。
「是的，」她终于说，「我知道。」

===Original===

Abruptly, by some impalpable sign, he knew what the answer would be, and he remained still.
She bent down over him and softened to her wonderful smile.
“Promise me,” she insisted.
He promised with his still face.
“If *I* do not hold you cheap, you will never hold yourself cheap﻿—”
“If you do not hold me cheap, you mean?”
She bent down quite close beside him. “I hold you,” she said, and then whispered, “*dear.*”
“Me?”
She laughed aloud.
He was astonished beyond measure. He stipulated, lest there might be some misconception, “You will marry me?”
She was laughing, inundated by the sense of bountiful power, of possession and success. He looked quite a nice little man to have. “Yes,” she laughed. “What else could I mean?” and, “Yes.”
He felt as a praying hermit might have felt, snatched from the midst of his quiet devotions, his modest sackcloth and ashes, and hurled neck and crop over the glittering gates of Paradise, smack among the iridescent wings, the bright-eyed Cherubim. He felt like some lowly and righteous man dynamited into Bliss.﻿ ﻿…
His hand tightened upon the rope that steadies one upon the stairs of stone. He was for kissing her hand and did not.
He said not a word more. He turned about, and with something very like a scared expression on his face led the way into the obscurity of their descent.
Everyone seemed to understand. Nothing was said, nothing was explained, the merest touch of the eyes sufficed. As they clustered in the castle gateway Coote, Kipps remembered afterwards, laid hold of his arm as if by chance and pressed it. It was quite evident he knew. His eyes, his nose, shone with benevolent congratulations, shone, too, with the sense of a good thing conducted to its climax. Mrs. Walshingham, who had seemed a little fatigued by the hill, recovered, and was even obviously stirred by affection for her daughter. There was, in passing, a motherly caress. She asked Kipps to give her his arm in walking down the steep. Kipps in a sort of dream obeyed. He found himself trying to attend to her, and soon he was attending.
She and Kipps talked like sober, responsible people and went slowly, while the others drifted down the hill together, a loose little group of four. He wondered momentarily what they would talk about and then sank into his conversation with Mrs. Walshingham.

===Chinese===

借着某种无形的征兆，他蓦地知道了答案会是什么，他一动不动。
她朝他俯下身来，软化成她那个美妙的微笑。
「答应我。」她坚持道。
他那张一动不动的脸应承了。
「倘若*我*不把你看贱，你就永远不许把自己看贱——」
「你是说，倘若你不把我看贱？」
她俯下来，挨得他极近。「我把你看作，」她说，随即低声补上，「*亲爱的*。」
「我？」
她笑出了声。
他惊异得无以复加。为免有什么误会，他钉了一句：「你肯嫁给我？」
她笑着，沉浸在一种富足的权能感、占有感和成就感里。他瞧着真是个怪可人意的小男人，值得拥有。「是的，」她笑道。「不然我能是什么意思？」接着又是一句：「是的。」
他的感受，好比一位祈祷的隐士，正做着他安静的功课，披着他简朴的麻衣，忽然被人拎起，倒栽葱扔过乐园光闪闪的大门，正扔进那些虹彩的翅膀、那些眼睛雪亮的基路伯中间。他像一个低微而正直的人，被炸药轰进了至福。……
他握紧了那段在石头楼梯上稳住身子的绳索。他想吻她的手，没敢。
他再没说一个字。他转过身，脸上带着近乎惊惶的神情，领头走进下楼的黑影里。
大家仿佛都心领神会。什么也没说，什么也没解释，眼睛轻轻一碰就够了。众人聚在城堡门洞里的时候，库特——基普斯事后记得——像是无意似的抓住他的胳膊，捏了一把。他显然知道。他的眼睛、他的鼻子，闪耀着慈祥的祝贺，也闪耀着一件好事被护送到高潮的得意。沃尔辛厄姆太太本来像被那座山累得有点乏了，此刻缓了过来，甚至显然动了爱怜女儿之情。擦身而过时，有一番慈母的爱抚。她请基普斯在下陡坡时给她挽住胳膊。基普斯如在梦里，依了。他发觉自己竭力想听她讲话，不多时就当真听起来了。
她和基普斯像两个清醒而负责任的人那样交谈，走得慢；其余四个松松散散做一小群，先漂下山去了。他一时纳罕他们会谈些什么，随即便沉入他同沃尔辛厄姆太太的谈话里。

===Original===

He conversed, as it were, out of his superficial personality, and his inner self lay stunned in unsuspected depths within. It had an air of being an interesting and friendly talk, almost their first long talk together. Hitherto he had had a sort of fear of Mrs. Walshingham, as of a person possibly satirical, but she proved a soul of sense and sentiment, and Kipps, for all of his abstraction, got on with her unexpectedly well. They talked a little upon scenery and the inevitable melancholy attaching to the old ruins and the thought of vanished generations.
“Perhaps they jousted here,” said Mrs. Walshingham.
“They was up to all sorts of things,” said Kipps, and then the two came round to Helen. She spoke of her daughter’s literary ambitions. “She will do something, I feel sure. You know, Mr. Kipps, it’s a great responsibility to a mother to feel her daughter is﻿—exceptionally clever.”
“I dessay it is,” said Kipps. “There’s no mistake about that.”
She spoke, too, of her son﻿—almost like Helen’s twin﻿—alike, yet different. She made Kipps feel quite fatherly. “They are so quick, so artistic,” she said, “so full of ideas. Almost they frighten me. One feels they need opportunities﻿—as other people need air.”
She spoke of Helen’s writing. “Even when she was quite a little dot she wrote verse.”
(Kipps, sensation.)
“Her father had just the same tastes﻿—” Mrs. Walshingham turned a little beam of half-pathetic reminiscence on the past. “He was more artist than business man. That was the trouble.﻿ ﻿… He was misled by his partner, and when the crash came everyone blamed him.﻿ ﻿… Well, it doesn’t do to dwell on horrid things﻿—especially today. There are bright days, Mr. Kipps, and dark days. And mine have not always been bright.”
Kipps presented a face of Coote-like sympathy.
She diverged to talk of flowers, and Kipps’ mind was filled with the picture of Helen bending down towards him in the Keep.﻿ ﻿…
They spread the tea under the trees before the little inn, and at a certain moment Kipps became aware that everyone in the party was simultaneously and furtively glancing at him. There might have been a certain tension had it not been first of all for Coote and his tact, and afterwards for a number of wasps. Coote was resolved to make this memorable day pass off well, and displayed an almost boisterous sense of fun.

===Chinese===

他的谈话，仿佛是从他那层表层人格里发出来的，而他的内里自我，昏死在意识不到的深处。这场谈话有一种有趣而友好的空气，几乎是他们头一回长谈。此前他对沃尔辛厄姆太太怀着一类畏惧，怕她是个可能刻薄的人；可她原来是位有见识有性情的夫人，基普斯虽然身在神外，同她竟处得出奇地好。他们稍稍谈了谈风景，谈到古废墟必然牵起的那点怅惘、想到逝去的世代而生的那点愁绪。
「兴许他们在这儿比过武呢。」沃尔辛厄姆太太说。
「他们什么把戏都玩过的。」基普斯说；随后两人转到了海伦身上。她谈起女儿的文学抱负。「她会成器的，我深信。您知道，基普斯先生，做母亲的，一旦觉得自己的女儿——聪明得出格，那可是一副千斤担子。」
「可不。」基普斯说。「这错不了。」
她也谈起她的儿子——几乎同海伦像双生——像，又不像。她说得基普斯几乎生出为人父的心情。「他们学得多快，多有艺术气，」她说，「主意多得要命，简直叫我心惊。总觉得他们需要机会——就像别人需要空气。」
她谈到海伦的写作。「她还是个小不点儿的时候就写诗了。」
（基普斯，心头一震。）
「她父亲爱好一模一样——」沃尔辛厄姆太太朝过去投来一缕半带伤感的微光。「他艺术家的成分多，生意人的成分少。毛病就出在这儿。……他叫合伙人骗了；生意一垮，人人都怪他。……唉，恼人的事不宜多想——尤其今天。人生有亮堂的日子，基普斯先生，也有阴暗的日子。我那些日子，也不都是亮堂的。」
基普斯摆出一副库特式的同情的面孔。
她岔开去谈花；基普斯的脑子却灌满了海伦在主堡里向他俯下身来的画面。……
他们在小旅店门前的树下摆开茶点；到了某一刻，基普斯发觉全队人都在同时偷偷看他。要不是头里有库特和他的圆通，随后又来了一大群黄蜂，那阵子本会有些局促的。库特铁了心要让这个值得纪念的日子圆圆满满，拿出一种近乎喧闹的诙谐来。

===Original===

Then young Walshingham began talking of the Roman remains below Lympne, intending to lead up to the Overman. “These old Roman chaps,” he said, and then the wasps arrived. They killed three in the jam alone.
Kipps killed wasps, as if it were in a dream, and handed things to the wrong people, and maintained a thin surface of ordinary intelligence with the utmost difficulty. At times he became aware, aware with an extraordinary vividness, of Helen. Helen was carefully not looking at him and behaving with amazing coolness and ease. But just for that one time there was the faintest suggestion of pink beneath the ivory of her cheeks.﻿ ﻿…
Tacitly the others conceded to Kipps the right to paddle back with Helen; he helped her into the canoe and took his paddle, and, paddling slowly, dropped behind the others. And now his inner self stirred again. He said nothing to her. How could he ever say anything to her again? She spoke to him at rare intervals about reflections and the flowers and the trees, and he nodded in reply. But his mind moved very slowly forward now from the point at which it had fallen stunned in the Lympne Keep, moving forward to the beginnings of realisation. As yet he did not say even in the recesses of his heart that she was his. But he perceived that the goddess had come from her altar amazingly, and had taken him by the hand!
The sky was a vast splendour, and then close to them were the dark, protecting trees and the shining, smooth, still water. He was an erect, black outline to her; he plied his paddle with no unskilful gesture, the water broke to snaky silver and glittered far behind his strokes. Indeed, he did not seem bad to her. Youth calls to youth the wide world through, and her soul rose in triumph over his subjection. And behind him was money and opportunity, freedom and London, a great background of seductively indistinct hopes. To him her face was a warm dimness. In truth, he could not see her eyes, but it seemed to his love-witched brain he did and that they shone out at him like dusky stars.
All the world that evening was no more than a shadowy frame of darkling sky and water and dripping bows about Helen. He seemed to see through things with an extraordinary clearness; she was revealed to him certainly, as the cause and essence of it all.
He was indeed at his Heart’s Desire.

===Chinese===

随后小沃尔辛厄姆谈起利姆尼下面的古罗马遗址，打算引到超人上去。「这些罗马老家伙，」他刚起了个头，黄蜂就到了。光果酱里他们就拍死了三只。
基普斯像在梦里一样拍着黄蜂，把东西递错了人，勉力维持着一层薄薄的、寻常智力的表层。他不时觉出——觉得出奇地真切——海伦。海伦小心着不朝他看，举止冷静从容得惊人。可就那一回，她象牙色的双颊底下，透出最淡最淡的一抹粉红。……
大家心照不宣，把同海伦同舟划回去的权利让给了基普斯；他扶她进独木舟，操起桨，慢慢地划，落在了别人后面。他那个内里的自我又动起来了。他对她一句话也没说。往后他还能对她说什么呢？她隔上好久才说一句，说倒影，说花，说树，他点头作答。可他的头脑此刻从它在利姆尼主堡里昏倒的那一点上，极慢极慢地往前挪，挪向醒悟的开端。此刻，连在他心的密室里，他都还没有说她是他的。可他看出来了：女神竟然从神坛上走了下来，牵住了他的手！
天空是一片浩大的霞光；紧挨着他们的，是黑黢黢、护庇人的树，和发亮、平滑、静止的水。在她眼里，他是一个笔直的黑色剪影；他划桨的姿势不见得生疏，水在他的桨后碎成蜿蜒的银，粼粼地闪到很远。的确，她看他也不赖。青春隔着整个世界向青春呼唤，她的灵魂在他臣服的顶点上升起来，得胜了。而他背后是金钱和机会，自由和伦敦，一大片诱人地模糊着的希望的背景。对他来说，她的脸是一团温暖的朦胧。说真的，他看不清她的眼睛，可他那个中了爱魔的脑子自以为看得清，自以为它们像两颗暗色的星，照定了他。
那天傍晚，整个世界不过是暗下来的天、暗下来的水、滴水的船头，围着海伦结成的一个朦胧的框子。他仿佛带着一种异样的透彻看穿了万物；她向他显明，确凿地显明为这一切的因由和本体。
他确实置身于他的心愿之中了。

===Original===

It was one of those times when there seems to be no future, when Time has stopped and we are at an end. Kipps, that evening, could not have imagined a tomorrow, all that his imagination had pointed towards was attained. His mind stood still and took the moments as they came.
About nine that night Coote came around to Kipps’ new apartment in the Upper Sandgate Road﻿—the house on the Leas had been let furnished﻿—and Kipps made an effort toward realisation. He was discovered sitting at the open window and without a lamp, quite still. Coote was deeply moved, and he pressed Kipps’ palm and laid a knobby, white hand on his shoulder and displayed the sort of tenderness becoming in a crisis. Kipps was too moved that night, and treated Coote like a very dear brother.
“She’s splendid,” said Coote, coming to it abruptly.
“Isn’t she?” said Kipps.
“I couldn’t help noticing her face,” said Coote.﻿ ﻿… “You know, my dear Kipps, that this is better than a legacy.”
“I don’t deserve it,” said Kipps.
“You can’t say that.”
“I don’t. I can’t ’ardly believe it. I can’t believe it at all. No!”
There followed an expressive stillness.
“It’s wonderful,” said Kipps. “It takes me like that.”
Coote made a faint blowing noise, and so again they came for a time of silence.
“And it began﻿—before your money?”
“When I was in ’er class,” said Kipps, solemnly.
Coote, speaking out of a darkness which he was illuminating strangely with efforts to strike a match, said that it was beautiful. He could not have *wished* Kipps a better fortune.﻿ ﻿…
He lit a cigarette, and Kipps was moved to do the same, with a sacramental expression. Presently speech flowed more freely.
Coote began to praise Helen and her mother and brother. He talked of when “it” might be, he presented the thing as concrete and credible. “It’s a county family, you know,” he said. “She is connected, you know, with the Beaupres family﻿—you know Lord Beaupres.”
“No!” said Kipps, “reely!”
“Distantly, of course,” said Coote. “Still﻿—”
He smiled a smile that glimmered in the twilight.
“It’s too much,” said Kipps, overcome. “It’s so all like that.”
Coote exhaled. For a time Kipps listened to Helen’s praises and matured a point of view.
“I say, Coote,” he said. “What ought I to do now?”
“What do you mean?” said Coote.
“I mean about calling on ’er and all that.”
He reflected. “Naturally, I want to do it all right.”
“Of course,” said Coote.

===Chinese===

世上有那样的时刻：仿佛再没有将来，时间停了，我们到了头。那天傍晚的基普斯想象不出一个明天；他的想象力指向过的一切，都到手了。他的头脑静静站着，来一刻，收一刻。
夜里九点来钟，库特转到上桑德盖特路基普斯的新寓所来——利斯那所房子连家具租出去了——基普斯朝醒悟努了一把力。他被发现坐在敞开的窗前，没点灯，一动不动。库特深深感动了，他攥住基普斯的手心，把一只骨节突出的白手搭在他肩上，拿出一场危机之中得体的那种温存。基普斯那天夜里感动得太厉害，把库特当亲哥哥待。
「她真了不起，」库特突然开门见山。
「可不是？」基普斯说。
「我忍不住看她脸上的神气，」库特说。……「你要知道，亲爱的基普斯，这可比一笔遗产还好。」
「我配不上。」基普斯说。
「可不能这么说。」
「我就是这么说。我简直不敢信。压根儿不敢信。不敢！」
接着是一阵意味深长的安静。
「真神，」基普斯说。「我整个儿被它拿住了。」
库特轻轻吹了一口气，于是两人又静了一阵。
「这事——是在你有钱之前起的头？」
「还在她那个班上的时候。」基普斯郑重地说。
库特在黑暗里说话，正费劲划火柴，火柴的光古怪地一下下照亮他——他说，这真美。他*巴不得*基普斯有这样的好命。……
他点上一支烟，基普斯受了感染，也照样点上一支，神情像领圣餐。不多时，话就流得畅了。
库特开始夸海伦，夸她母亲和她弟弟。他谈「那一天」可能定在什么时候，把这件事说得又具体又可信。「这可是个郡里的世家，你知道，」他说。「她们家同博普雷家沾着亲，你知道——博普雷勋爵你总知道吧。」
「不会吧！」基普斯说，「真的！」
「远房的，当然，」库特说。「不过——」
他微笑了一下，那笑在暮色里微微一闪。
「太多了，」基普斯招架不住地说。「桩桩都这么好。」
库特吐出一口烟。有一阵，基普斯听着对海伦的赞美，慢慢酿成一个观点。
「我说，库特，」他说，「这会儿我该怎么办？」
「你指什么？」库特说。
「我是说去拜访她什么的。」
他想了想。「自然，我要把事情全办得妥妥当当。」
「那当然。」库特说。

===Original===

“It would be awful to go and do something﻿—now﻿—all wrong.”
Coote’s cigarette glowed as he meditated. “You must call, of course,” he decided. “You’ll have to speak to Mrs. Walshingham.”
“ ’Ow?” said Kipps.
“Tell her you mean to marry her daughter.”
“I dessay she knows,” said Kipps, with defensive penetration.
Coote’s head was visible, shaking itself judiciously.
“Then there’s the ring,” said Kipps. “What ’ave I to do about that?”
“What ring do you mean?”
“ ’Ngagement Ring. There isn’t anything at all about that in *Manners and Rules of Good Society*﻿—not a word.”
“Of course you must get something﻿—tasteful. Yes.”
“What sort of a ring?”
“Something nace. They’ll show you in the shop.”
“Of course. I ’spose I got to take it to ’er, eh? Put it on her finger.”
“Oh, no! Send it. Much better.”
“Ah!” said Kipps, for the first time, with a note of relief.
“Then, ’ow about this call﻿—on Mrs. Walshingham, I mean. ’Ow ought one to go?”
“Rather a ceremonial occasion,” reflected Coote.
“Wadyer mean? Frock coat?”
“I *think* so,” said Coote, with discrimination.
“Light trousers and all that?”
“Yes.”
“Rose?”
“I think it might run to a buttonhole.”
The curtain that hung over the future became less opaque to the eyes of Kipps. Tomorrow, and then other days, became perceptible at least as existing. Frock coat, silk hat and a rose! With a certain solemnity he contemplated himself in the process of slow transformation into an English gentleman, Arthur Cuyps, frock-coated on occasions of ceremony, the familiar acquaintance of Lady Punnet, the recognised wooer of a distant connection of the Earl of Beaupres.
Something like awe at the magnitude of his own fortune came upon him. He felt the world was opening out like a magic flower in a transformation scene at the touch of this wand of gold. And Helen, nestling beautiful in the red heart of the flower. Only ten weeks ago he had been no more than the shabbiest of improvers and shamefully dismissed for dissipation, the mere soil-burned seed, as it were, of these glories. He resolved the engagement ring should be of expressively excessive quality and appearance, in fact, the very best they had.
“Ought I to send ’er flowers?” he speculated.
“Not necessarily,” said Coote. “Though, of course, it’s an attention.”﻿ ﻿…
Kipps meditated on flowers.
“When you see her,” said Coote, “you’ll have to ask her to name the day.”
Kipps started.

===Chinese===

「要是这会儿跑去办砸一件事——那才可怕。」
库特的烟头在他沉吟时一亮一亮。「你当然得去拜访，」他定了调子。「你得跟沃尔辛厄姆太太谈。」
「怎么谈？」基普斯说。
「告诉她，你打算娶她的女儿。」
「我猜她知道。」基普斯说，带着防御性的机锋。
看得见库特的头在审慎地摇。
「再有就是戒指，」基普斯说。「这个我该怎么办？」
「你说什么戒指？」
「订——婚戒指。《礼俗与上流社会规范》里关于这个一个字也没有——一个字也没有。」
「你当然得买个像样的——要有品味。对。」
「买什么样的戒指？」
「件好东西。铺子里的人会拿给你看的。」
「当然。我猜我得把它送给她吧，呃？给她戴上。」
「哦，不！寄去。寄去好得多。」
「啊！」基普斯头一回带上了如释重负的调子。
「那么，那次拜访呢——我是说拜访沃尔辛厄姆太太。该怎么去法？」
「相当隆重的场合，」库特寻思着说。
「什么意思？燕尾服？」
「我*想*是的。」库特说，语气很有鉴别力。
「浅色裤子，全套？」
「对。」
「玫瑰？」
「我看可以戴一朵钮孔花。」
悬在未来的那道帷幔，在基普斯眼里变得不那么不透光了。明天，以及往后的日子，至少作为「存在」被觉出来了。燕尾服、大礼帽、一朵玫瑰！他怀着某种庄严，注视着自己正缓慢地蜕变为一位英国绅士：亚瑟·凯普斯，逢大典则着燕尾服，普尼特夫人的熟朋友，博普雷伯爵一位远亲的公开的追求者。
一种近乎敬畏的心情，为他这笔家产的浩大而升起。他觉得世界正在绽开，像变换布景里一朵魔花，一碰这根金杖就开；而海伦，娇美地偎在花心那点红里。仅仅十个星期以前，他还不过是学徒里最寒酸的一个，还因为放浪被羞辱地辞退过，这些荣华，可以说，不过是从他那颗烧焦的种子里发出来的。他打定主意，订婚戒指要质量好到、气派到超过一切表达，事实上，就买他们店里最好的。
「我该不该给她送花？」他盘算。
「不一定，」库特说。「当然，那也算一份心意。」……
基普斯盘算着花。
「你见到她的时候，」库特说，「你就得请她定日子了。」
基普斯一惊。

===Original===

“That won’t be just yet a bit, will it?”
“Don’t know any reason for delay.”
“Oo, but﻿—a year, say.”
“Rather a long time,” said Coote.
“Is it?” said Kipps, turning his head sharply. “But﻿—”
There was quite a long pause.
“I say,” he said, at last, and in an unaltered voice, “you’ll ’ave to ’elp me about the wedding.”
“Only too happy,” said Coote.
“Of course,” said Kipps, “I didn’t think﻿—” He changed his line of thought. “Coote,” he asked, “wot’s a ‘state-eh-tate’?”
“A ‘tate-ah-tay’!” said Coote, improvingly, “is a conversation alone together.”
“Lor’!” said Kipps, “but I thought﻿—. It says *strictly* we oughtn’t to enjoy a tater-tay, not sit together, walk together, ride together or meet during any part of the day. That don’t leave much time for meeting, does it?”
“The books says that?” asked Coote.
“I jest learnt it by ’eart before you came. I thought that was a bit rum, but I s’pose it’s all right.”
“You won’t find Miss Walshingham so strict as all that,” said Coote. “I think that’s a bit extreme. They’d only do that now in very strict old aristocratic families. Besides, the Walshinghams are so modern﻿—advanced, you might say. I expect you’ll get plenty of chances of talking together.”
“There’s a tremendous lot to think about,” said Kipps, blowing a profound sigh. “D’you mean﻿—p’raps we might be married in a few months or so.”
“You’ll *have* to be,” said Coote. “Why not?”﻿ ﻿…
Midnight found Kipps alone, looking a little tired and turning over the leaves of the red-covered textbook with a studious expression. He paused for a moment on page 233, his eye caught by the words:
“*For an uncle or aunt by marriage* the period is six weeks black, with jet trimmings.”
“No,” said Kipps, after a vigorous mental effort. “That’s not it.” The pages rustled again. He stopped and flattened out the little book decisively at the beginning of the chapter on “Weddings.”
He became pensive. He stared at the lamp wick. “I suppose I ought to go over and tell them,” he said, at last.
Kipps called on Mrs. Walshingham, attired in the proper costume for ceremonial Occasions in the Day. He carried a silk hat, and he wore a deep-skirted frock coat, his boots were patent leather and his trousers dark grey. He had generous white cuffs with gold links, and his grey gloves, one thumb in which had burst when he put them on, he held loosely in his hand.

===Chinese===

「总不至于就定在眼跟前吧？」
「我想不出有什么理由要拖。」
「哦，可是——比方说，一年。」
「那可太长了。」库特说。
「是吗？」基普斯猛地转过头来。「可是——」
停了好长一停。
「我说，」他终于开口，声调没变，「办婚事的事，你得帮我。」
「乐意之至。」库特说。
「当然，」基普斯说，「我原先没想到——」他换了思路。「库特，」他问，「『斯特特-埃-特特』是个啥？」
「『太特-阿-太特』（tête-à-tête）！」库特纠正道，「就是两个人单独的谈话。」
「嚯！」基普斯说，「可我还当——。书上说，我们*严格*不许太特-阿-太特，不许同坐，不许同行，不许同车，一天里任何时候不许相会。那剩不下多少见面的工夫了，是不是？」
「书上是这么说的？」库特问。
「你来之前我刚把它背熟的。我就觉得这条有点邪门，不过我猜总没错。」
「你不会发现沃尔辛厄姆小姐严格到那个地步，」库特说。「我看那条太过了。如今只有顶严格的老贵族人家才那样。何况沃尔辛厄姆一家这么摩登——你甚至可以说先进。我看你们单独谈话的机会有的是。」
「要想的事可真多，」基普斯说，长叹了一声。「你是说——兴许过几个月我们就能结婚？」
「你们*必须*结，」库特说。「为什么不？」……
到了半夜，基普斯独自一人，带着一点倦容，用功地翻着那本红皮教科书。他在第233页上停了停，目光落在这几行字上：
「*姻伯叔、姻姑姨*，服丧六周，黑衣，缀煤玉饰边。」
「不对，」基普斯使了老大一番脑力之后说。「不是这条。」书页又沙沙响起来。他停下来，决然把小书翻平在「婚礼」一章的开头。
他出了神。他盯着灯芯。「我猜我该过去告诉他们。」他终于说。
基普斯拜访沃尔辛厄姆太太去了，穿的是白天大典场合的全套正装。他手捧大礼帽，身穿深下摆燕尾服，靴子是漆皮的，裤子深灰。他戴着宽宽的白袖口，缀金链扣；灰手套松松攥在手里——戴上时一只拇指还绽了线。

===Original===

He carried a small umbrella rolled to an exquisite tightness. A sense of singular correctness pervaded his being and warred with the enormity of the occasion for possession of his soul. Anon he touched his silk cravat. The world smelt of his rosebud.
He seated himself on a new recovered chintz armchair and stuck out the elbow of the arm that held his hat.
“I know,” said Mrs. Walshingham, “I know everything,” and helped him out most amazingly. She deepened the impression he had already received of her sense and refinement. She displayed an amount of tenderness that touched him.
“This is a great thing,” she said, “to a mother,” and her hand rested for a moment on his impeccable coat sleeve.
“A daughter, Arthur,” she explained, “is so much more than a son.”
Marriage, she said, was a lottery, and without love and toleration there was much unhappiness. Her life had not always been bright﻿—there had been dark days and bright days. She smiled rather sweetly. “This is a bright one,” she said.
She said very kind and flattering things to Kipps, and she thanked him for his goodness to her son. (“That wasn’t anything,” said Kipps.) And then she expanded upon the theme of her two children. “Both so accomplished,” she said, “so clever. I call them my Twin Jewels.”
She was repeating a remark that she had made at Lympne, that she always said her children needed opportunities, as other people needed air, when she was abruptly arrested by the entry of Helen. They hung on a pause, Helen perhaps surprised by Kipps’ weekday magnificence. Then she advanced with outstretched hand.
Both the young people were shy. “I jest called ’round,” began Kipps, and became uncertain how to end.
“Won’t you have some tea?” asked Helen.
She walked to the window, looked out at the familiar outporter’s barrow, turned, surveyed Kipps for a moment ambiguously, said “I will get some tea,” and so departed again.
Mrs. Walshingham and Kipps looked at one another and the lady smiled indulgently. “You two young people mustn’t be shy of each other,” said Mrs. Walshingham, which damaged Kipps considerably.
She was explaining how sensitive Helen always had been, even about quite little things, when the servant appeared with the tea things, and then Helen followed, and taking up a secure position behind the little bamboo tea table, broke the ice with officious teacup clattering.

===Chinese===

他携带一把卷得极紧致的小伞。一种异样周正的感觉流遍全身，同这个场合的重大争夺起他的灵魂来。他不时碰一碰他的绸领结。全世界都闻得到他那朵玫瑰花苞的香气。
他在一把新换套子的印花布扶手椅上坐下，把捧帽子的那条胳膊的肘支楞出去。
「我知道，」沃尔辛厄姆太太说，「我什么都知道了。」她惊人地替他解了围。她加深了他已经得到的那份印象：她有见识，有教养。她表露的那份柔情打动了他。
「这件大事，」她说，「对做母亲的来说——」她的手在他那无可挑剔的衣袖上放了一放。
「女儿啊，亚瑟，」她解释道，「可比儿子金贵得多。」
婚姻，她说，是一桩抽彩；没有爱和宽容，就有的是苦。她这一生也不都是亮堂的日子——有过阴暗的日子，也有过亮堂的日子。她笑得相当甜。「今天是亮堂的。」她说。
她对基普斯说了许多又亲切又奉承的话，又为他对她儿子的好意道谢。（「那算不了什么。」基普斯说。）跟着她把她那对儿女的题目铺陈开来。「两个都这么多才，」她说，「这么聪明。我叫他们我的孪生双璧。」
她正重复着她在利姆尼说过的话——她总说，她的孩子们需要机会，就像别人需要空气——海伦进来了，她的话戛然而止。两人悬在一停顿上；海伦多半是叫基普斯平日的排场吓了一跳。随后她伸着手走上前来。
两个年轻人都害臊。「我就是过来坐坐。」基普斯起了个头，又拿不准怎么收尾。
「喝点茶好吗？」海伦问。
她走到窗前，望着外面那辆熟悉的搬运工小推车，转过身，含义不明地把基普斯端详了片刻，说了句「我去叫茶来」，便又出去了。
沃尔辛厄姆太太和基普斯面面相觑，太太宽容地笑了。「你们两个年轻人，可不许互相害臊。」沃尔辛厄姆太太说——这话把基普斯伤得不轻。
她正解释海伦打小就多么敏感，连顶小的事也搁不下，女仆端着茶具进来了，海伦跟着进来，在小竹茶桌后面占据了稳固的阵地，用一阵殷勤的茶杯脆响破了冰。

===Original===

Then she introduced the topic of a forthcoming open-air performance of *As You Like It*, and steered past the worst of the awkwardness. They discussed stage illusion. “I mus’ say,” said Kipps, “I don’t quite like a play in a theayter. It seems sort of unreal, some’ow.”
“But most plays are written for the stage,” said Helen, looking at the sugar.
“I know,” admitted Kipps.
They finished tea. “Well,” said Kipps, and rose.
“You mustn’t go yet,” said Mrs. Walshingham, rising and taking his hand. “I’m sure you two must have heaps to say to each other,” and so she escaped towards the door.
Among other projects that seemed almost equally correct to Kipps at that exalted moment was one of embracing Helen with ardour as soon as the door closed behind her mother and one of headlong flight through the open window. Then he remembered he ought to hold the door open for Mrs. Walshingham, and turned from that duty to find Helen still standing, beautifully inaccessible, behind the tea things. He closed the door and advanced toward her with his arms akimbo and his hands upon his coat skirts. Then, feeling angular, he moved his right hand to his moustache. Anyhow, he was dressed all right. Somewhere at the back of his mind, dim and mingled with doubt and surprise, appeared the perception that he felt now quite differently towards her, that something between them had been blown from Lympne Keep to the four winds of heaven.﻿ ﻿…
She regarded him with an eye of critical proprietorship.
“Mother has been making up to you,” she said, smiling slightly.
She added, “It was nice of you to come around to see her.”
They stood through a brief pause, as though each had expected something different in the other and was a little perplexed at its not being there. Kipps found he was at the corner of the brown covered table, and he picked up a little flexible book that lay upon it to occupy his mind.
“I bought you a ring today,” he said, bending the book and speaking for the sake of saying something, and then he was moved to genuine speech. “You know,” he said, “I can’t ’ardly believe it.”
Her face relaxed slightly again. “No?” she said, and may have breathed, “Nor I.”
“No,” he went on. “It’s as though everything ’ad changed. More even than when I got my money. ’Ere we are going to marry. It’s like being someone else. What I feel is﻿—”
He turned a flushed and earnest face to her.

===Chinese===

跟着她起了个话头，谈即将上演的露天《皆大欢喜》，把最尴尬的一段撑了过去。他们讨论舞台的真实感。「我得说，」基普斯说，「我不大喜欢戏园子里的戏。总觉得有点假，说不出的。」
「可大多数戏本来就是为舞台写的呀。」海伦说，眼睛看着糖罐。
「这我知道。」基普斯认了。
茶用完了。「那么，」基普斯说着起身。
「先别走，」沃尔辛厄姆太太说，站起来握住他的手。「我敢说你们俩有满肚子的话要说。」说着她就朝门口撤了。
在那个升华的时刻，在基普斯看来旗鼓相当的两个方案是：一个，等她母亲的门一关就热烈地拥抱海伦；一个，从敞开的窗户里夺路飞奔。随后他想起该替沃尔辛厄姆太太扶着门，等那差事办完回过头来，只见海伦还站在茶具后面，美得不可接近。他关上门，两手叉在衣摆上朝她走去。随即又觉得自己姿势生硬，把右手挪到了胡子上。不管怎么说，他这身穿戴是挑不出错的。在他心底某处，隐隐地掺着疑虑与惊异，浮起了这样一层知觉：他如今待她的心情全然不同了，横在他们中间的那个东西，已经从利姆尼主堡被吹散到天上四方的风里去了。……
她用物主的、品评的一眼打量他。
「母亲把你收买了。」她微微带笑说。
她又补上一句：「你肯过来看她，真好。」
两人站着挨过短短的一停，仿佛各自都盼着对方身上有点什么不同，又为那点什么不在而微微发窘。基普斯发觉自己正站在那张棕布面桌子的角上，便拿起摊在桌上的一本软面小书，好占住心思。
「我今天给你买了只戒指。」他说，一面弯着那本书，为说话而说话；随即他被逼出了真话。「你知道，」他说，「我简直不敢信。」
她的脸又微微松弛下来。「是么？」她说，也许还轻轻吐出一句：「我也不敢。」
「是啊，」他接着说。「就像什么都变了个样。比我得那笔钱变得还厉害。咱们就要结婚了。像变成了另外一个人。我的感觉是——」
他朝她转过一张涨红的、诚恳的脸。

===Original===

He seemed to come alive to her with one natural gesture. “I don’t *know* things. I’m not good enough. I’m not refined. The more you’ll see of me the more you’ll find me out.”
“But I’m going to help you.”
“You’ll ’ave to ’elp me a fearful lot.”
She walked to the window, glanced out of it, made up her mind, turned and came towards him, with her hands clasped behind her back.
“All these things that trouble you are very little things. If you don’t mind﻿—if you will let me tell you things﻿—”
“I wish you would.”
“Then I will.”
“They’re little things to you, but they aren’t to me.”
“It all depends, if you don’t mind being told.”
“By you?”
“I don’t expect you to be told by strangers.”
“Oo!” said Kipps, expressing much.
“You know, there are just a few little things. For instance, you know, you are careless with your pronunciation.﻿ ﻿… You don’t mind my telling you?”
“I like it,” said Kipps.
“There’s aitches.”
“I know,” said Kipps, and then, endorsingly, “I been told. Fact is, I know a chap, a Nacter, *he’s* told me. He’s told me, and he’s going to give me a lesso nor so.”
“I’m glad of that. It only requires a little care.”
“Of course. On the stage they got to look out. They take regular lessons.”
“Of course,” said Helen, a little absently.
“I dessay I shall soon get into it,” said Kipps.
“And then there’s dress,” said Helen, taking up her thread again.
Kipps became pink, but he remained respectfully attentive.
“You don’t mind?” she said.
“Oo, no.”
“You mustn’t be too﻿—too dressy. It’s possible to be over-conventional, over-elaborate. It makes you look like a shop﻿—like a common, well-off person. There’s a sort of easiness that is better. A real gentleman looks right, without looking as though he had tried to be right.”
“Jest as though ’e’d put on what came first?” said the pupil, in a faded voice.
“Not exactly that, but a sort of ease.”
Kipps nodded his head intelligently. In his heart he was kicking his silk hat about the room in an ecstasy of disappointment.
“And you must accustom yourself to be more at your ease when you are with people,” said Helen. “You’ve only got to forget yourself a little and not be anxious﻿—”
“I’ll try,” said Kipps, looking rather hard at the teapot. “I’ll do my best to try.”
“I know you will,” she said, and laid a hand for an instant upon his shoulder and withdrew it.
He did not perceive her caress. “One has to learn,” he said.

===Chinese===

一个天然的手势，仿佛让他整个人对她活了过来。「我*不懂*事情。我配不上。我不文雅。你越同我处，越要把我看穿。」
「可我就是要帮你的呀。」
「你得帮我帮上老大一堆呢。」
她走到窗前，望了望外面，拿定了主意，转过身朝他走来，两手交叠在背后。
「这些叫你发愁的事，都是很小很小的事。只要你不介意——只要你肯让我告诉你——」
「我巴不得呢。」
「那我就说。」
「在你眼里是小事，在我可不是。」
「全看你自己了，看你受不受得了别人说。」
「受你的说？」
「我总不指望叫外人来告诉你。」
「哦！」基普斯说，一言道尽万语。
「你知道，也就那么几桩小事。比方说，你发音不大经心。……你不介意我这么说吧？」
「我喜欢。」基普斯说。
「是 H 的毛病。」
「我知道，」基普斯说，随即又附和道，「有人跟我说过。不瞒你说，我认识一个伙计，当演员的，*他*跟我说过。他说了，还说要给我上一课什么的。」
「那就好。只消稍加留意就行。」
「当然。台上的人非留意不可。他们正经上课呢。」
「当然。」海伦说，有点心不在焉。
「我猜我很快就改得过来。」基普斯说。
「再有是穿著。」海伦说，重新拾起线头。
基普斯红了脸，可仍旧恭敬地听着。
「你不介意吧？」她说。
「哦，不介意。」
「你可不能太——太讲究穿戴。太拘泥成规、太繁缛也是可能的。那会让你看着像个铺子——像个俗气的暴发户。有一种随和劲儿更好。真正的绅士看着得体，却不像费过心思才得体的。」
「就像随手抓着什么穿什么？」学生有气无力地说。
「也不尽然，是一种自在劲儿。」
基普斯聪明地点头。他心里正把他的大礼帽满屋子踢得团团转，失望到了狂喜的地步。
「还有，同人们在一起，你要习惯放自在些，」海伦说。「你只消稍稍忘了自己，别那么慌——」
「我试，」基普斯说，眼睛盯着茶壶。「我尽力试。」
「我知道你会的。」她说，一只手在他肩上放了一瞬，又收回去。
他没有觉出她的爱抚。「人总得学嘛。」他说。

===Original===

His attention was distracted by the strenuous efforts that were going on in the back of his head to translate, “I say, didn’t you ought to name the day?” into easy as well as elegant English, a struggle that was still undecided when the time came for them to part.﻿ ﻿…
He sat for a long time at the open window of his sitting-room with an intent face, recapitulating that interview. His eyes rested at last almost reproachfully on the silk hat beside him. “ ’Ow is one to know?” he asked. His attention was caught by a rubbed place in the nap, and, still thoughtful, he rolled up his handkerchief skilfully into a soft ball and began to smooth this down.
His expression changed slowly.
“ ’Ow the Juice is one to know?” he said, putting down the hat with some emphasis.
He rose up, went across the room to the sideboard, and, standing there, opened and began to read *Manners and Rules*.

===Chinese===

他的注意力被后脑勺里一场费劲的搏斗岔开了：那边正试着把「我说，你们是不是该把日子定了？」翻译成又流利又体面的英语；这场搏斗到分手的时候还没分出胜负。……
他在起坐间敞开的窗前坐了许久，一脸专注，把那次会面从头回味。末了他的目光带着近乎责备的神气，落在身旁那顶大礼帽上。「叫人怎么知道呢？」他问。他注意到帽绒上有一块磨秃的地方，仍旧想着心事，灵巧地把手绢卷成一团软球，开始把那块绒抹平。
他的表情慢慢变了。
「这他妈叫人怎么知道？」他说，把手往下一按，放下了帽子，颇有点分量。
他站起身，穿过屋子走到餐具柜前，站在那儿翻开《礼俗与规范》读了起来。

===Original===

> “Those individuals who have lead secluded or isolated lives, or have hitherto moved in other spheres than those wherein well-bred people move, will gather all the information necessary from these pages to render them thoroughly conversant with the manners and amenities of society.”
> — *Manners and Rules of Good Society*, by a Member of the Aristocracy

===Chinese===

> 「凡素来深居简出、离群索居，或一向周旋于上流人士之外的人士，自本书诸页中当可获得一切必要的知识，从而对社交界的礼仪与情趣融会贯通。」
> ——《礼俗与上流社会规范》，一位贵族阶级成员著

## VIII Kipps Enters Society / 八 基普斯进入社交界
===Original===

Submission to Inexorable Fate took Kipps to the Anagram Tea.
At any rate he would meet Helen there in the presence of other people and be able to carry off the worst of the difficulty of explaining his little jaunt to London. He had not seen her since his last portentous visit to New Romney. He was engaged to her, he would have to marry her, and the sooner he faced her again the better. Before wild plans of turning socialist, defying the world and repudiating all calling forever, his heart on second thoughts sank. He felt Helen would never permit anything of the sort. As for the Anagrams he could do no more than his best and that he was resolved to do. What had happened at the Royal Grand, what had happened at New Romney, he must bury in his memory and begin again at the reconstruction of his social position. Ann, Buggins, Chitterlow, all these, seen in the matter-of-fact light of the Folkestone train, stood just as they stood before; people of an inferior social position who had to be eliminated from his world. It was a bother about Ann, a bother and a pity. His mind rested so for a space on Ann until the memory of these Anagrams drew him away. If he could see Coote that evening he might, he thought, be able to arrange some sort of connivance about the Anagrams, and his mind was chiefly busy sketching proposals for such an arrangement. It would not, of course, be ungentlemanly cheating, but only a little mystification. Coote very probably might drop him a hint of the solution of one or two of the things, not enough to win a prize, but enough to cover his shame. Or failing that he might take a humorous, quizzical line and pretend he was pretending to be very stupid. There were plenty of ways out of it if one kept a sharp lookout.﻿ ﻿…
The costume Kipps wore to the Anagram Tea was designed as a compromise between the strict letter of high fashion and seaside laxity, a sort of easy, semi-state for afternoon. Helen’s first reproof had always lingered in his mind. He wore a frock coat, but mitigated it by a Panama hat of romantic shape with a black band, grey gloves, but for relaxation brown button boots. The only other man besides the clergy present, a new doctor with an attractive wife, was in full afternoon dress. Coote was not there.
Kipps was a little pale, but quite self-possessed, as he approached Mrs. Bindon Botting’s door.

===Chinese===

向不可抗的命运屈服，把基普斯（Kipps）送进了字谜茶会。
至少他能在众人面前见到海伦，把去伦敦小小游荡那件事最难交代的部分搪塞过去。自打那回煞有介事的新罗姆尼之行，他还没见过她。他同她订了婚，他非娶她不可，早一天再面对她也好。至于索性当社会主义者、同世界对着干、从此把拜客一并唾弃——这类狂想，他的心再一掂量便沉了下去。他觉出海伦绝不会容许那一套。至于字谜，他只能竭尽全力，而他也拿定了主意要竭尽全力。皇家大饭店出的事、新罗姆尼出的事，他必须埋进记忆，从头再建他的社交地位。安、巴金斯、奇特洛，这一干人，放在福克斯通班车的务实光里看，还同原先一样：些地位低微的人，必须从他的世界里清除出去。安的事是个麻烦，一桩麻烦，一桩可惜。他的心思这样在安身上搁了一阵，直到那些字谜又把它拽开。要是当晚能见到库特（Coote），他想，兴许能就字谜串通出一点什么名堂；他的脑子主要就忙着起草这类方案的腹稿。那当然不算不绅士的作弊，只是一点小小的障眼法。库特很可能给他递一两个谜底的暗号，不够赢奖，够遮羞。再不行，还可以走幽默打趣一路，装作自己是装傻。只要留神，出路有的是。……
他去字谜茶会的那身行头，是上流时尚的严格条文与海滨松垮之间的一种折中，一种下午穿的、半正式的便礼服。海伦头一回的训诫始终盘踞在他脑子里。他穿了燕尾服，却用一顶缀黑带、样式浪漫的巴拿马草帽来冲淡，灰手套，却为宽松配了棕色扣靴。除教区牧师外到场的唯一男人——一位带着娇妻的新来的医生——穿的是全套下午礼服。库特不在。
走近宾登·博廷夫人家大门的时候，基普斯脸色有点白，人却十分镇定。

===Original===

He took a turn while some people went in and then faced it manfully. The door opened and revealed﻿—Ann!
In the background through a draped doorway behind a big fern in a great art pot the elder Miss Botting was visible talking to two guests; the auditory background was a froth of feminine voices.﻿ ﻿…
Our two young people were much too amazed to give one another any formula of greeting, though they had parted warmly enough. Each was already in a state of extreme tension to meet the demands of this great and unprecedented occasion of an Anagram Tea. “Lor’!” said Ann, her sole remark, and then the sense of Miss Botting’s eye ruled her straight again. She became very pale, but she took his hat mechanically, and he was already removing his gloves. “Ann,” he said in a low tone, and then “Fency!” The eldest Miss Botting knew Kipps was the sort of guest who requires nursing, and she came forward vocalising charm. She said it was “Awfully jolly of him to come, awfully jolly. It was awfully difficult to get any good men!”
She handed Kipps forward, mumbling in a dazed condition, to the drawing-room, and there he encountered Helen looking unfamiliar in an unfamiliar hat. It was as if he had not met her for years.
She astonished him. She didn’t seem to mind in the least his going to London. She held out a shapely hand, and smiled encouragingly. “You’ve faced the anagrams?” she said.
The second Miss Botting accosted them, a number of oblong pieces of paper in her hand, mysteriously inscribed. “Take an anagram,” she said; “take an anagram,” and boldly pinned one of these brief documents to Kipps’ lapel. The letters were “Cypshi,” and Kipps from the very beginning suspected this was an anagram for Cuyps. She also left a thing like a long dance programme, from which dangled a little pencil in his hand. He found himself being introduced to people, and then he was in a corner with the short lady in a big bonnet, who was pelting him with gritty little bits of small talk that were gone before you could take hold of them and reply.
“Very hot,” said this lady. “Very hot, indeed﻿—hot all the summer﻿—remarkable year﻿—all the years remarkable now﻿—don’t know what we’re coming to﻿—don’t you think so, Mr. Kipps?”
“Oo rather,” said Kipps, and wondered if Ann was still in the hall. Ann!
He ought not to have stared at her like a stuck fish and pretended not to know her.

===Chinese===

他踱了一圈，等一批人进去了，才硬着头皮面对它。门开了，露出的是——安！
背景里，透过挂帘的门洞、隔着一盆大艺术陶盆里的巨型蕨草，博廷家大小姐正同两位客人说话；听觉的背景，是一团女性嗓音的泡沫。……
我们这一对年轻人惊得连一句寒暄的套话都对不上来，虽说他们分手时分得够热乎。两人都已绷到极点，好去应付字谜茶会这桩重大而空前的场面。「哎呀！」安说——这是她唯一的评语——随即博廷小姐眼色的管辖又把她立刻扳直了。她变得煞白，可还是机械地接过他的帽子，而他已经在一根一根褪手套。「安，」他低声说，接着又冒出一句：「真想不到！」博廷家大小姐知道基普斯属于需要护理的客人，便迎上前来，浑身上下发出魅力。说他「肯来真是太好了，太好了。像样的男人实在难请！」
她把晕头转向、咕咕哝哝的基普斯送到客厅里；在那儿他遇见了海伦，戴着一顶不熟悉的帽子，人都显得陌生了，仿佛多年没见过她。
她叫他吃惊。她对他去伦敦似乎毫不介意。她伸出一只好看的手，鼓励地笑着。「字谜你领教过了？」她说。
博廷家二小姐迎上来，手里一把长方纸片，写着神秘的花字。「拿一个字谜，」她说，「拿一个字谜，」大着胆子把一张简短文书别在基普斯的翻领上。字母是「Cypshi」，基普斯从头一个起就疑心这是 Cuyps 的字谜。她还留下一件像长长的舞会节目单似的东西，一支小铅笔从上头垂下来，吊在他手里。他发现自己正被人一个个引见，随后他已经同一个戴大风帽的矮太太缩在一个角落里，那位太太拿一颗颗带着沙砾的小聊天朝他打来，你还没抓住、还没答上，它们就没影了。
「真热，」这位太太说。「真是热——一夏都热——了不起的年头——如今年年都了不起——不知道我们还要落到什么地步——您说是不是，基普斯先生？」
「哦，可不。」基普斯说，心里惦着安还在不在门厅里。安！
他不该像条钉在案子上的鱼那样瞪她，还装不认识。

===Original===

That couldn’t be right. But what *was* right?
The lady in the big bonnet proceeded to a second discharge. “Hope you’re fond of anagrams, Mr. Kipps﻿—difficult exercise﻿—still one must do something to bring people together﻿—better than Ludo anyhow. Don’t you think so, Mr. Kipps?”
Ann fluttered past the open door. Her eyes met his in amazed enquiry. Something had got dislocated in the world for both of them.﻿ ﻿…
He ought to have told her he was engaged. He ought to have explained things to her. Perhaps even now he might be able to drop her a hint.
“Don’t you think so, Mr. Kipps?”
“Oo rather,” said Kipps for the third time.
A lady with a tired smile, who was labelled conspicuously “Wogdelenk,” drifted towards Kipps’ interlocutor and the two fell into conversation. Kipps found himself socially aground. He looked about him. Helen was talking to a curate and laughing. Kipps was overcome by a vague desire to speak to Ann. He was for sidling doorward.
“What are *you*, please?” said an extraordinarily bold, tall girl, and arrested him while she took down “Cypshi.”
“I’m sure I don’t know what it means,” she explained. “I’m Sir Bubh. Don’t you think anagrams are something chronic?”
Kipps made stockish noises, and the young lady suddenly became the nucleus of a party of excited friends who were forming a syndicate to guess, and barred his escape. She took no further notice of him. He found himself jammed against an occasional table and listening to the conversation of Mrs. “Wogdelenk” and his lady with the big bonnet.
“She packed her two beauties off together,” said the lady in the big bonnet. “Time enough, too. Don’t think much of this girl; she’s got as housemaid now. Pretty, of course, but there’s no occasion for a housemaid to be pretty﻿—none whatever. And she doesn’t look particularly up to her work either. Kind of ’mazed expression.”
“You never can tell,” said the lady labelled “Wogdelenk;” “you never can tell. My wretches are big enough, Heaven knows, and do they work? Not a bit of it!”﻿ ﻿…
Kipps felt dreadfully out of it with regard to all these people, and dreadfully in it with Ann.
He scanned the back of the big bonnet and concluded it was an extremely ugly bonnet indeed. It got jerking forward as each short, dry sentence was snapped off at the end and a plume of osprey on it jerked excessively. “She hasn’t guessed even one!”

===Chinese===

那样不可能是对的。可什么才是对的呢？
大风帽太太放了第二排枪。「希望您喜欢字谜，基普斯先生——费脑筋的玩意儿——不过总得想个法子把人凑到一块儿——总比鲁多棋强。您说是不是，基普斯先生？」
安从敞开的门外飘过。她的眼睛同他的撞了个满怀，满是惊讶的探问。对他们两个，世界都有一处脱了榫。……
他早该告诉她他订了婚。他早该向她把事情说明白。也许这会儿还来得及递给她一点口风。
「您说是不是，基普斯先生？」
「哦，可不。」基普斯第三遍说。
一位带着倦容微笑的太太，身上惹眼地别着「Wogdelenk」，朝基普斯的谈话对手飘过来，两人聊上了。基普斯发觉自己在社交上搁了浅。他四下张望。海伦正同一位副牧师谈笑。基普斯被一阵模糊的、想同安说话的愿望攫住。他悄悄朝门口蹢。
「请问，您*是*什么？」一个胆大包天的高个子姑娘说，拦住他，抄下「Cypshi」。
「我敢说我不知道这是什么意思，」她解释道，「我是 Sir Bubh。您不觉得字谜真是没治吗？」
基普斯发出呆板的声音，年轻小姐忽然成了一个小集团的核心——一帮兴奋的朋友正要组个猜谜辛迪加——把他的退路堵死了。她不再理他。他发现自己挤在一盏落地小桌边，听「Wogdelenk」太太同大风帽太太谈话。
「她把两个俏丫头一齐打发走了，」大风帽太太说。「也到时候了。这丫头我看平平；如今当了客厅女仆。人是俊，当然，可女仆用不着俊——一点也用不着。而且活计也看不出多么拿得起来。一副迷迷瞪瞪的神气。」
「谁也说不准，」别着「Wogdelenk」的太太说，「谁也说不准。我那几个宝贝个头够大了，天知道，可她们干活吗？一点不干！」……
基普斯面对这满屋子人，觉得自己可怕地格格不入；面对安，又可怕地脱不开身。
他端详大风帽的后脑勺，断定那实在是一顶丑到家的帽子。每一句干脆的短话甩出去，它就朝前一颠，帽上一支鹭羽颠得尤其厉害。

===Original===

followed by a shriek of girlish merriment, came from the group about the tall, bold girl. They’d shriek at him presently, perhaps. Beyond thinking his own anagram might be Cuyps, he hadn’t a notion. What a chatter they were all making! It was just like a summer sale! Just the sort of people who’d give a lot of trouble and swap you! And suddenly the smouldering fires of rebellion leapt to flame again. These were a rotten lot of people, and the anagrams were rotten nonsense, and he, Kipps, had been a rotten fool to come. There was Helen away there, still laughing, with her curate. Pity she couldn’t marry a curate and leave him (Kipps) alone! Then he’d know what to do. He disliked the whole gathering collectively and in detail. Why were they all trying to make him one of themselves? He perceived unexpected ugliness everywhere about him. There were two great pins jabbed through the tall girl’s hat, and the swirls of her hair below the brim with the minutest piece of tape tie-up showing did not repay close examination. Mrs. “Wogdelenk” wore a sort of mumps bandage of lace, and there was another lady perfectly dazzling with beads, and jewels and bits of trimming. They were all flaps and angles and flounces﻿—these women. Not one of them looked as neat and decent a shape as Ann’s clean, trim, little figure. Echoes of Masterman woke up in him again. Ladies indeed! Here were all these chattering people, with money, with leisure, with every chance in the world, and all they could do was to crowd like this into a couple of rooms and jabber nonsense about anagrams.
“Could Cypshi really mean Cuyps?” floated like a dissolving wreath of mist across his mind.
Abruptly resolution stood armed in his heart. He was going to get out of this!
“ ’Scuse me,” he said, and began to wade neck deep through the bubbling tea party.
He was going to get out of it all!
He found himself close by Helen. “I’m orf,” he said, but she gave him the briefest glance. She did not appear to hear him. “Still, Mr. Spratlingdown, you *must* admit there’s a limit even to conformity,” she was saying.﻿ ﻿…
He was in a curtained archway, and Ann was before him carrying a tray supporting several small sugar bowls.
He was moved to speech. “*What* a Lot!” he said, and then mysteriously, “I’m engaged to *her*.” He indicated Helen’s new hat, and became aware of a skirt he had stepped upon.

===Chinese===

「她连一个都还没猜着！」——随着一声少女的欢叫，从高个子胆大姑娘那一群里传出来。回头她们说不定要冲他叫唤呢。除了疑心自己的字谜可能是 Cuyps，他一点头绪也没有。他们闹得多响啊！活像夏季大减价！正是那种又要多麻烦你、又要同你掉包的人！忽然，闷着的反叛之火又蹴成了明火。这是一伙烂人，字谜是烂透的胡闹，他基普斯跑来是烂透的傻瓜。那边海伦还在同她的副牧师笑。可惜她不能嫁给一个副牧师，放他（基普斯）一马！那他就知道该怎么办了。他厌恶这整个聚会，厌恶整体，也厌恶每一个。他们干吗都想把他收编成他们中的一员？他发觉四下到处是意想不到的丑。高个子姑娘的帽子上戳着两根大别针，帽檐下面发卷连同露出的一小截系带经不起细看。「Wogdelenk」太太围着一副腮腺炎绷带似的花边，还有一位太太浑身珠子亮得晃眼，缀着零碎的滚边。她们全是些荷叶边、尖棱角、飘带——这些女人。没有一个人的身形，看起来有安那副干净利落的小身段那样齐整像样。马斯特曼的回声又在他心里醒来。好一个「太太们」！瞧这帮叽叽喳喳的人，有钱，有闲，天大的机会全在手边，干得出的事却不过是像这样挤在两间屋里，围着字谜胡扯。
「Cypshi 真会是 Cuyps 么？」一缕化开的雾，从他脑子里飘过。
蓦地，决心全副武装地立在他心里。他要脱身！
「失陪，」他说，开始蹚过咕嘟冒泡的茶会，水深齐颈。
他要整个儿脱身！
他发现自己挨近了海伦。「我走了，」他说，她却只给了他极短的一瞥，仿佛没听见。「话虽如此，斯普拉特林唐先生，您*必须*承认，随大俗也得有个限度。」她正说。……
他走到一道挂帘的拱门里，安正端着托盘迎面走来，托盘上几只小糖碗。
他憋出了话。「好——多——人！」他说，随即又神秘兮兮地，「我同*她*订了婚。」他朝海伦那顶新帽子的方向一指，随即觉察自己踩着了一条裙子。

===Original===

Ann stared at him helplessly, borne past in the grip of incomprehensible imperatives.
Why shouldn’t they talk together?
He was in a small room, and then at the foot of the staircase in the hall. He heard the rustle of a dress, and what was conceivable his hostess was upon him.
“But you’re not going, Mr. Kipps?” she said.
“I must,” he said; “I got to.”
“But, Mr. Kipps!”
“I must,” he said. “I’m not well.”
“But before the guessing! Without any tea!”
Ann appeared and hovered behind him.
“I got to go,” said Kipps.
If he parleyed with her Helen might awake to his desperate attempt.
“Of course if you *must* go.”
“It’s something I’ve forgotten,” said Kipps, beginning to feel regrets. “Reely I must.”
Mrs. Botting turned with a certain offended dignity, and Ann in a state of flushed calm that evidently concealed much came forward to open the door.
“I’m very sorry,” he said; “I’m very sorry,” half to his hostess and half to her, and was swept past her by superior social forces﻿—like a drowning man in a millrace﻿—and into the Upper Sandgate Road. He half turned upon the step, and then slam went the door.﻿ ﻿…
He retreated along the Leas, a thing of shame and perplexity﻿—Mrs. Botting’s aggrieved astonishment uppermost in his mind.﻿ ﻿…
Something﻿—reinforced by the glances of the people he was passing﻿—pressed its way to his attention through the tumultuous disorder of his mind.
He became aware that he was still wearing his little placard with the letters “Cypshi.”
“Desh it!” he said, clutching off this abomination. In another moment its several letters, their task accomplished, were scattering gleefully before the breeze down the front of the Leas.
Kipps was dressed for Mrs. Wace’s dinner half an hour before it was time to start, and he sat waiting until Coote should come to take him around. *Manners and Rules of Good Society* lay before him neglected. He had read the polished prose of the member of the aristocracy, on page 96, as far as﻿—
