> “the acceptance of an invitation is in the eyes of diners out, a binding obligation which only ill-health, family bereavement, or some all-important reason justifies its being set on one side or otherwise evaded”﻿—

===Chinese===

安无可奈何地瞪着他，被一串无法理解的指令裹挟着，飘过去了。
他们为什么就不能说说话？
他已在一件小屋里，随后已在门厅楼梯脚。他听见一阵绸衣窸窣，想也知道，女主人撵上他来了。
「您这不是要走么，基普斯先生？」她说。
「我得走，」他说；「我非走不可。」
「可是，基普斯先生！」
「我非走不可，」他说。「我不舒服。」
「可还没猜谜呢！茶也没喝一口！」
安出现了，在他身后徘徊。
「我非走不可。」基普斯说。
再同她磨下去，海伦就该发觉他这孤注一掷的企图了。
「当然，您*非*走不可的话。」
「有件事我忘下了，」基普斯说，开始觉得懊悔。「真的非走不可。」
博廷太太带着几分受冒犯的尊严转过身去，安呢，带着一副显然藏了许多东西的、烧得红红的镇定，上前开门。
「真对不起，」他说；「真对不起，」一半对女主人，一半对她；随即被更强大的社交势力扫出门去——像磨坊水道里一个溺水的人——扫进了上桑德盖特路。他在台阶上半个转身，砰，门关上了。……
他沿利斯草坪退去，一个又羞又乱的活物——脑子里顶醒目的是博廷太太那副又委屈又惊愕。……
有一样东西——加上迎面走过的那些人的目光——穿过他脑子里乱作一团的喧腾，挤进了他的注意。
他发觉自己胸前还别着那块小招贴，字母「Cypshi」。
「该死！」他说，一把扯下这个祸物。转眼间，使命已毕的几个字母，欢天喜地散成几片，顺着风，沿利斯草坪的正面飘下去了。
赴韦斯太太的晚宴之前半个钟头，基普斯已经穿戴停当，坐着等库特来接他。《礼俗与上流社会规范》摊在他面前，无人理睬。第96页上那位贵族阶级成员的锦绣文章，他读到——

> 「应邀而允，在惯于赴宴的人眼里，是一桩有约束力的义务；唯抱恙、丧亲，或某种至关重大的事由，方许搁置或另行回避」——

===Original===

and then he had lapsed into gloomy thoughts.
That afternoon he had had a serious talk with Helen.
He had tried to express something of the change of heart that had happened to him. But to broach the real state of the matter had been altogether too terrible for him. He had sought a minor issue. “I don’t like all this Seciety,” he had said.
“But you must *see* people,” said Helen.
“Yes, but﻿—. It’s the sort of people you see.” He nerved himself. “I didn’t think much of that lot at the Enegram Tea.”
“You have to see all sorts of people if you want to see the world,” said Helen.
Kipps was silent for a space and a little short of breath.
“My dear Arthur,” she began, almost kindly, “I shouldn’t ask you to go to these affairs if I didn’t think it good for you, should I?”
Kipps acquiesced in silence.
“You will find the benefit of it all when we get to London. You learn to swim in a tank before you go out into the sea. These people here are good enough to learn upon. They’re stiff and rather silly and dreadfully narrow and not an idea in a dozen of them, but it really doesn’t matter at all. You’ll soon get savoir faire.”
He made to speak again, and found his powers of verbal expression lacking. Instead he blew a sigh.
“You’ll get used to it all very soon,” said Helen helpfully.﻿ ﻿…
As he sat meditating over that interview and over the vistas of London that opened before him, on the little flat, and teas and occasions and the constant presence of Brudderkins and all the bright prospect of his new and better life, and how he would never see Ann any more, the housemaid entered with a little package, a small, square envelope to “Arthur Kipps, Esquire.”
“A young woman left this, Sir,” said the housemaid, a little severely.
“Eh?” said Kipps; “what young woman?” and then suddenly began to understand.
“She looked an ordinary young woman,” said the housemaid coldly.
“Ah!” said Kipps. “*That’s* orlright.”
He waited till the door had closed behind the girl, staring at the envelope in his hand, and then, with a curious feeling of increasing tension, tore it open. As he did so, some quicker sense than sight or touch told him its contents. It was Ann’s half sixpence. And, besides, not a word!
Then she must have heard him﻿—!
She had kept the half sixpence all these years!

===Chinese===

读到这儿，他便坠入了愁思。
那天下午，他同海伦有过一场严肃的谈话。
他本想多少吐一吐自己心里发生的那番变化。可是要把实情和盘托出，对他实在太过可怕。他找了个次一等的话题。「我不喜欢这一套社——交。」他说。
「可你总得*见*人啊。」海伦说。
「是，可是——。是你见的这路人。」他鼓起勇气。「那帮人，我在字谜茶会上看不上眼。」
「想见识世界，就得什么样的都见。」海伦说。
基普斯沉默了一阵，呼吸有点短。
「亲爱的亚瑟，」她开口了，几乎是好心的，「我要是看不出这些对你有好处，就不会叫你去了，是不是？」
基普斯默然认了。
「等咱们到了伦敦，你就见出这一切的好处来。下水先在水池里学，再出海。这些人拿来练手正合适。他们拘谨，相当蠢，眼界窄得吓人，一打里头挑不出一个有主意的，可这全不相干。你很快就会有处事的老练。」
他想再说话，发觉语言的本领告了乏，只好叹了口气。
「这一切你很快就习惯了。」海伦帮腔说。……
他正坐在那儿，回味那场谈话，望着在眼前展开的伦敦远景——那套小公寓、茶会、大大小小的场合、布鲁德金斯们无休无止的在场、他这场更美好新生活的全部光明前程，还有他从此再也见不着安——女仆进来了，捧着一个小包裹，一只小方信封，写着「亚瑟·基普斯先生启」。
「一位年轻太太留下的，先生。」女仆说，口气有点严厉。
「嗯？」基普斯说；「哪位年轻太太？」随即忽然开始明白了。
「瞧着是位普普通通的年轻太太。」女仆冷冷地说。
「啊！」基普斯说。「*那*就对了。」
他等到房门在姑娘身后关上，盯着手里的信封，然后怀着一种越来越紧的古怪心情，撕开了它。撕开的一瞬，一种比视觉触觉更快的知觉告诉了他里面的东西。是安那半块六便士。而且，此外一个字也没有！
那么说，她一定听见了——！
那半块六便士，她留了这么多年！

===Original===

He was standing with the envelope in his hand, trying to get on from that last inference, when Coote became audible without.
Coote appeared in evening dress, a clean and radiant Coote, with large, greenish, white gloves and a particularly large white tie, edged with black. “For a third cousin,” he presently explained. “Nace, isn’t it?” He could see Kipps was pale and disturbed and put this down to the approaching social trial. “You keep your nerve up, Kipps, my dear chap, and you’ll be all right,” said Coote, with a big, brotherly glove on Kipps’ sleeve.
The dinner came to a crisis so far as Kipps’ emotions were concerned, with Mrs. Bindon Botting’s talk about servants, but before that there had been several things of greater or smaller magnitude to perturb and disarrange his social front. One little matter that was mildly insurgent throughout the entire meal was, if I may be permitted to mention so intimate a matter, the behaviour of his left brace. The webbing﻿—which was of a cheerful scarlet silk﻿—had slipped away from its buckle, fastened no doubt in agitation, and had developed a strong tendency to place itself obliquely in the manner rather of an official decoration, athwart his spotless front. It first asserted itself before they went in to dinner. He replaced this ornament by a dexterous thrust when no one was looking and thereafter the suppression of his novel innovation upon the stereotyped sombreness of evening dress became a standing preoccupation. On the whole, he was inclined to think his first horror excessive; at any rate no one remarked upon it. However you imagine him constantly throughout the evening, with one eye and one hand, whatever the rest of him might be doing, predominantly concerned with the weak corner.
But this, I say, was a little matter. What exercised him much more was to discover Helen quite terribly in evening dress.
The young lady had let her imagination rove Londonward, and this costume was perhaps an anticipation of that clever little flat not too far west which was to become the centre of so delightful a literary and artistic set. It was, of all the feminine costumes present, most distinctly an evening dress. One was advised Miss Walshingham had arms and shoulders of a type by no means despicable, one was advised Miss Walshingham was capable not only of dignity but charm, even a certain glow of charm.

===Chinese===

他正捏着信封站在那里，想从最后一层推断里往前挪，外面传来了库特的动静。
库特一身晚礼服出场了，一个干净而容光焕发的库特，戴一双发绿的大白手套，一条格外宽大的白领结，镶着黑边。「给三表亲戴的，」他随即解释道。「不错吧？」他看出基普斯脸色发白、心神不宁，把这归到即将来临的社交考验头上。「稳住神，基普斯，亲爱的伙计，你就没事。」库特说，一只大大的、兄弟般的手套落在基普斯袖子上。
就基普斯的情绪而言，晚宴到博廷太太谈仆人那段才算到了危急关头；可在那之前，已经有好几件或大或小的事，搅扰过、弄乱过他的社交门面。有一桩小小的祸事，一整顿饭都在悄悄作乱——请允许我提一件这么贴近私处的事——那就是他左背带的行为。那条带子——鲜亮的猩红丝织品——从扣襻里脱了出来（无疑是在慌乱中扣的），养成了一股强烈的倾向，要斜着横贯他一尘不染的胸前，颇像一枚官家勋章。它在入席之前就头一回发作了。他趁人不备，手一掖，把这枚勋章送了回去；此后，压住这个对晚礼服程式化肃穆的新式突破，就成了他一门常年的功课。总的算来，他倾向于认为自己起初的惊恐是过了头；反正谁也没吭声。随你怎么想象他这一晚上的样子：一只眼、一只手，不管其余部分在做什么，始终主要在关照那个软肋。
可我说了，这是小事。更叫他受用的，是发现海伦穿上了晚礼服，可怕极了。
这位年轻小姐的想象早已飞向伦敦，这身衣裳，兴许就是那套不太偏西的、聪明的小公寓的预支——那套公寓，将成为一个多么可人的文学艺术小圈子的中心。在场一切女装里，它最最分明是一件晚礼服。人家由此得知，沃尔辛厄姆小姐的胳膊和肩膀，绝非等闲之属；人家由此得知，沃尔辛厄姆小姐不但端得出端庄，还端得出魅力，甚至魅力灼人。

===Original===

It was, you know, her first evening dress, a tribute paid by Walshingham finance to her brightening future. Had she wanted keeping in countenance, she would have had to have fallen back upon her hostess, who was resplendent in black and steel. The other ladies had to a certain extent compromised. Mrs. Walshingham had dressed with just a refined, little V and Mrs. Bindon Botting, except for her dear mottled arms, confided scarcely more of her plump charm to the world. The elder Miss Botting stopped short of shoulders, and so did Miss Wace. But Helen didn’t. She was﻿—had Kipps had eyes to see it﻿—a quite beautiful human figure; she knew it and she met him with a radiant smile that had forgotten all the little difference of the afternoon. But to Kipps her appearance was the last release. With that, she had become as remote, as foreign, as incredible as a wife and mate, as though the Cnidian Venus herself, in all her simple elegance, was before witnesses, declared to be his. If, indeed, she had ever been credible as a wife and mate.
She ascribed his confusion to modest reverence, and having blazed smiling upon him for a moment turned a shapely shoulder towards him and exchanged a remark with Mrs. Bindon Botting. Ann’s poor little half sixpence came against Kipps’ fingers in his pocket and he clutched at it suddenly as though it was a talisman. Then he abandoned it to suppress his Order of the Brace. He was affected by a cough. “Miss Wace tells me Mr. Revel is coming,” Mrs. Botting was saying.
“Isn’t it delightful?” said Helen. “We saw him last night. He’s stopped on his way to Paris. He’s going to meet his wife there.”
Kipps’ eyes rested for a moment on Helen’s dazzling deltoid, and then went enquiringly, accusingly almost to Coote’s face. Where, in the presence of this terrible emergency, was the gospel of suppression now﻿—that Furtive treatment of Religion and Politics, and Birth and Death and Bathing and Babies, and “all those things” which constitutes your True Gentleman? He had been too modest even to discuss this question with his Mentor, but surely, surely this quintessence of all that is good and nice could regard these unsolicited confidences only in one way.

要知道，这是她的第一件晚礼服，是沃尔辛厄姆家的财力对她愈发明亮的前程进贡的一笔。她若需要有人作伴壮胆，本也只好靠女主人了——女主人一身黑呢配钢饰，光华夺目。其余几位太太多少打了折。沃尔辛厄姆太太只开了一个精致的、小小的 V 字；宾登·博廷太太呢，除了那双亲爱的斑驳胳膊，她那丰腴的魅力差不多再没向世人多托出什么。博廷家大小姐在肩膀前止步，韦斯小姐也是。可海伦不止。她——倘使基普斯有眼看得出——是一具相当美的人体；她自己知道，她迎他时那容光焕发的一笑，早把下午那点小小的不快忘光了。可对基普斯，她这副模样是最后的解脱。有了这一下，她做妻子、做伴侣，已经同他隔得像异邦一样遥远、一样不可信，仿佛当众宣告归他所有的，是尼多斯的维纳斯本尊，披着她全部的素雅。假如说，她此前当真可信作妻子和伴侣的话。
她把他的慌乱归之于谦卑的敬畏，朝他璀璨地笑了一阵，随即把一只好看的肩膀转向他，同宾登·博廷太太交换了一句话。安那半块可怜的小六便士，在口袋里碰到基普斯的手指，他猛地攥住它，仿佛那是一件法宝。跟着又只能丢开它，去镇压他的「背带勋章」。他喉咙发痒，咳了一声。「韦斯太太告诉我，雷维尔先生要来。」博廷太太正说。
「这不叫人欢喜么？」海伦说。「我们昨晚见到他的。他是在去巴黎的路上停一停。他要去那边同太太会合。」
基普斯的目光在海伦那片晃眼的三角肌上停了一停，随即探问地、几乎是控诉地转到库特脸上。处在这个可怕的紧急关头，避而不谈的福音如今何在——那套对宗教与政治、出身与死亡、洗澡与婴孩「避而不谈」的隐秘规程，那套成全真正绅士的规程？他连这个问题都不好意思同他的导师讨论；可是当然，当然，这位集一切良善精美之大成的化身，对这路不请自来的知心话，只可能有一种看法。

With something between relief and the confirmation of his worst fears he perceived, by a sort of twitching of the exceptionally abundant muscles about Coote’s lower jaw, in a certain deliberate avoidance of one particular direction by these pale, but resolute, grey eyes, by the almost convulsive grip of the ample, greenish white gloves behind him, a grip broken at times for controlling pats at the black-bordered tie and the back of that spacious head, and by a slight but increasing disposition to cough, that *Coote did not approve*!
To Kipps Helen had once supplied a delicately beautiful dream, a thing of romance and unsubstantial mystery. But this was her final materialisation, and the last thin wreath of glamour about her was dispelled. In some way (he had forgotten how and it was perfectly incomprehensible) he was bound to this dark, solid and determined young person whose shadow and suggestion he had once loved. He had to go through with the thing as a gentleman should. Still﻿—
And when he was sacrificing Ann!
He wouldn’t stand this sort of thing, whatever else he stood.﻿ ﻿… Should he say something about her dress to her﻿—tomorrow?
He could put his foot down firmly. He could say, “Look ’ere. I don’t care. I ain’t going to stand it. See?”
She’d say something unexpected, of course. She always did say something unexpected.
Suppose for once he overrode what she said? Simply repeated his point?
He found these thoughts battling with certain conversational aggressions from Mrs. Wace, and then Revel arrived and took the centre of the stage.
The author of that brilliant romance, “Red Hearts a-Beating,” was a less imposing man than Kipps had anticipated, but he speedily effaced that disappointment by his predominating manners. Although he lived habitually in the vivid world of London, his collar and tie were in no way remarkable, and he was neither brilliantly handsome nor curly nor long-haired. His personal appearance suggested arm chairs, rather than the equestrian exercises and amorous toyings and passionate intensities of his masterpiece; he was inclined to be fat, with whitish flesh, muddy coloured straight hair, he had a rather shapeless and truncated nose and his chin was asymmetrical. One eye was more inclined to stare than the other.

怀着一种介于宽慰和坐实最坏预感之间的心情，他看出来了——凭库特下颌周围格外发达的肌肉一阵抽动，凭那双灰白而坚决的眼睛执意避开某个方向，凭那双肥大的绿白手套在背后近乎痉挛地一攥（这攥劲不时松开，去拍一拍镶黑边的领结和那颗轩昂的后脑勺），再凭一阵渐涨的咳嗽倾向——*库特不赞成！*
对基普斯来说，海伦曾经供给他一个精美入微的梦，一件浪漫而缥缈的秘物。可这是她最后的显形，缠在她身上的最后一缕薄薄的光晕散尽了。不知怎么（他忘了是怎么结上的，而且全然无从索解），他同这个黝黑、结实、拿定主意的年轻女人拴在了一起——他曾经爱过的，不过是她的影子和暗示。他得像个绅士那样把这桩事走完。可是——
何况他牺牲的是安！
别的都能忍，这一路他忍不了。……要不要明天同她谈谈她那身衣裳？
他可以一脚踩死。他可以说：「听着。我不管。这事我不干了。懂吗？」
她当然会说句意料之外的话。她说的话总是意料之外。
要是这一回他硬压过她的话头呢？只管把自己的话重复一遍？
他发觉这些念头正同一阵阵韦斯太太的谈话攻势缠斗，随后雷维尔到了，占据了舞台中央。
《红心怦怦》那部才情横溢的言情小说的作者，比基普斯预想的不那么威风，但他很快就用压倒一切的仪态抹掉了这点失望。他虽然常年住在五光十色的伦敦，领子领结却毫无出奇之处，人既不英俊，也不鬈发，也不披长发。他的仪表叫人想起扶手椅，而不是他杰作里的鞍马功夫、情场抚弄和火热强度；他偏胖，肉色发白，直头发是泥色的，鼻子多少没个正形、像被截短了些，下巴不对称。一只眼睛比另一只更爱瞪人。

===Original===

He might have been esteemed a little undistinguished looking were it not for his beeswaxed moustache, which came amidst his features with a pleasing note of incongruity, and the whimsical wrinkles above and about his greater eye. His regard sought and found Helen’s as he entered the room and they shook hands presently with an air of intimacy Kipps, for no clear reason, found objectionable. He saw them clasp their hands, heard Coote’s characteristic cough﻿—a sound rather more like a very, very old sheep, a quarter of a mile away, being blown to pieces by a small charge of gunpowder than anything else in the world﻿—did some confused beginnings of a thought, and then they were all going in to dinner and Helen’s shining bare arm lay along his sleeve. Kipps was in no state for conversation. She glanced at him, and, though he did not know it, very slightly pressed his elbow. He struggled with strange respiratory dislocations. Before them went Coote, discoursing in amiable reverberations to Mrs. Walshingham, and at the head of the procession was Mrs. Bindon Botting talking fast and brightly beside the erect military figure of little Mr. Wace. (He was not a soldier really, but he had caught a martinet bearing by living so close to Shorncliffe.) Revel came last, in charge of Mrs. Wace’s queenly black and steel, politely admiring in a flute-like cultivated voice the mellow wall paper of the staircase. Kipps marvelled at everybody’s self-possession.
From the earliest spoonful of soup it became evident that Revel considered himself responsible for the table talk. And before the soup was over it was almost as manifest that Mrs. Bindon Botting inclined to consider his sense of responsibility excessive. In her circle Mrs. Bindon Botting was esteemed an agreeable rattle, her manner and appearance were conspicuously vivacious for one so plump, and she had an almost Irish facility for humorous description. She would keep people amused all through an afternoon call, with the story of how her jobbing gardener had got himself married and what his home was like, or how her favourite butt, Mr. Stigson Warder, had all his unfortunate children taught almost every conceivable instrument because they had the phrenological bump of music abnormally large. “They got to trombones, my dear!” she would say, with her voice coming to a climax.

若不是那两撇上了蜂蜡的髭须——插在他五官中间，带着一点讨人喜欢的不协调——和那只较大的眼睛上边、周围的诙谐皱纹，他简直要被看成一个相貌平平的人了。他一进屋，目光就找到海伦的目光，两人随即握手，那股熟稔劲儿，基普斯说不清为什么，看着不受用。他看见他们把手握在一起，听见库特那声代表性的咳嗽——那声响，比起世上任何东西，都更像一只很远很远、隔着四分之一英里的老绵羊，被一小包火药炸得四散——刚刚起了个模糊的念头，大家已经鱼贯入席，海伦那发亮的裸臂搭上了他的袖子。基普斯全无谈天的状态。她瞟了他一眼——他自己并不知道——极轻地按了按他的臂肘。他同种种奇怪的呼吸错乱搏斗着。前头走着库特，对沃尔辛厄姆太太发出和蔼的回响；队伍的排头是宾登·博廷太太，在小个儿韦斯先生那挺直的军人身形旁边，说得又快又亮。（他其实不是军人，只因住得离肖恩克利夫太近，沾上了一副铁面教官的做派。）雷维尔殿后，照应着韦斯太太那身王后般的黑呢钢饰，用一种笛子般有教养的嗓音，客气地赞赏楼梯的壁纸温润可人。基普斯对人人的镇定都叹服。
汤匙才下去几口，就已明白：雷维尔认为自己对席间谈话负有全责。汤未喝完，又几乎同样明白：宾登·博廷太太倾向于认为他这份责任感未免过分。在她的圈子里，宾登·博廷太太被推许为一位可人的话匣子；就她那副富态而言，她的风度容貌都出奇地活泼，还有一副几乎爱尔兰式的说笑话的伶牙俐齿。她能让人家一整个下午拜客笑个不停，讲的不是她的零工花匠怎么娶了亲、家里是什么光景，就是她的头号笑柄斯蒂格森·沃德先生，怎么因为儿女们音乐的那块头骨出奇地大，就叫每个倒霉孩子学的乐器几乎样样想到。「都学到长号上了，亲爱的！」她会说，嗓门一路爬到顶点。

===Original===

Usually her friends conspired to draw her out, but on this occasion they neglected to do so, a thing that militated against her keen desire to shine in Revel’s eyes. After a time she perceived that the only thing for her to do was to cut in on the talk, on her own account, and this she began to do. She made several ineffectual snatches at the general attention and then Revel drifted towards a topic she regarded as particularly her own, the ordering of households.
They came to the thing through talk about localities. “We are leaving our house in The Boltons,” said Revel, “and taking a little place at Wimbledon, and I think of having rooms in Dane’s Inn. It will be more convenient in many ways. My wife is furiously addicted to golf and exercise of all sorts, and I like to sit about in clubs﻿—I haven’t the strength necessary for these hygienic proceedings﻿—and the old arrangement suited neither of us. And, besides, no one could imagine the demoralisation the domestics of West London have undergone during the last three years.”
“It’s the same everywhere,” said Mrs. Bindon Botting.
“Very possibly it is. A friend of mine calls it the servile tradition in decay and regards it all as a most hopeful phenomenon﻿—”
“He ought to have had my last two criminals,” said Mrs. Bindon Botting.
She turned to Mrs. Wace while Revel came again a little too late with a “Possibly﻿—”
“And I haven’t told you, my dear,” she said, speaking with voluble rapidity, “I’m in trouble again.”
“The last girl?”
“The last girl. Before I can get a cook, my hard won housemaid”﻿—she paused﻿—“chucks it.”
“Panic?” asked young Walshingham.
“Mysterious grief! Everything merry as a marriage bell until my Anagram Tea! Then in the evening a portentous rigour of bearing, a word or so from my Aunt, and immediately﻿—Floods of Tears and Notice!” For a moment her eye rested thoughtfully on Kipps, as she said: “Is there anything heartrending about Anagrams?”
“I find them so,” said Revel. “I﻿—”
But Mrs. Bindon Botting got away again. “For a time it made me quite uneasy﻿—”
Kipps jabbed his lip with his fork rather painfully, and was recalled from a fascinated glare at Mrs. Botting to the immediate facts of dinner.
“﻿—whether anagrams might not have offended the good domestic’s Moral Code﻿—you never can tell. We made enquiries. No. No. No. She *must* go and that’s all!”

===Chinese===

通常朋友们总串通好了引她发挥，这一回却疏忽了没引，这可有损她在雷维尔眼里一放光彩的热切心愿。过了一阵她明白了，唯一的办法是凭自己的力量插进谈话里去，她也这么开了头。她朝总体的注意扑了几把，没扑着；随后雷维尔飘向了一个她视为禁脔的题目——家政的料理。
他们从谈地段谈到了正题。「我们要离开博尔顿区的房子，」雷维尔说，「在温布尔登置一个小地方，我还想在戴恩客栈开两间房。好多事情都方便。我太太狂热地迷高尔夫和一切运动，我喜欢在俱乐部里坐坐——这些卫生功课我没了那份体力——原先的安排咱们俩都不合适。再说，谁也想象不到，这三年里西伦敦的仆佣败坏成了什么样子。」
「哪儿都一样。」宾登·博廷太太说。
「很可能。我有位朋友管它叫奴性传统的衰亡，还把这一切看作顶有希望的现象——」
「他该领教领教我最近那两个犯人。」宾登·博廷太太说。
她转向韦斯太太，雷维尔的「也许——」又来迟了半步。
「还有件事没告诉您呢，亲爱的，」她说，说得又急又溜，「我又遇上麻烦了。」
「上一个丫头？」
「上一个丫头。我厨子还没请着，我那千辛万苦请来的客厅丫头」——她顿了顿——「撔挑子了。」
「吓着了？」小沃尔辛厄姆问。
「莫名地伤心！在我那字谜茶会之前，家里欢得像一挂结婚铃！到了晚上，忽然端出一副凛然不可犯的架势，我姑太太说了她一两句，跟着立刻——泪如泉涌加辞工！」她的目光在基普斯身上若有所思地掠了一掠，说：「字谜这东西，有什么叫人肝肠寸断的地方么？」
「我觉得有。」雷维尔说。「我——」
可宾登·博廷太太又抢了回去。「有那么一阵子，这真叫我不安——」
基普斯一叉子戳着了嘴唇，戳得不轻，从对博廷太太的入迷瞪视里，被召回到眼前的饭菜上来。
「——想这字谜会不会冲撞了那位好仆佣的道德律——谁也说不准。我们打听了。不是。不是。不是。她*非走不可*，就这么回事！」

===Original===

“One perceives,” said Revel, “in these disorders, dimly and distantly, the last dying glow of the age of Romance. Let us suppose, Mrs. Botting, let us at least try to suppose﻿—it is Love.”
Kipps clattered with his knife and fork.
“It’s love,” said Mrs. Botting; “what else can it be? Beneath the orderly humdrum of our lives these romances are going on, until at last they bust up and give Notice and upset our humdrum altogether. Some fatal, wonderful soldier﻿—”
“The passions of the common or house domestic,” said Revel, and recovered possession of the table.
Upon the troubled disorder of Kipps’ table manners there had supervented a quietness, an unusual calm. For once in his life he had distinctly made up his mind on his own account. He listened no more to Revel. He put down his knife and fork and refused anything that followed. Coote regarded him with tactful concern and Helen flushed a little.
About half-past nine that night came a violent pull at the bell of Mrs. Bindon Botting, and a young man in a dress suit, a gibus and other marks of exalted social position stood without. Athwart his white expanse of breast lay a ruddy bar of patterned silk that gave him a singular distinction and minimised the glow of a few small stains of burgundy. His gibus was thrust back and exposed a disorder of hair that suggested a reckless desperation. He had, in fact, burnt his boats and refused to join the ladies. Coote, in the subsequent conversation, had protested quietly, “You’re going on all right, you know,” to which Kipps had answered he didn’t care a “Eng” about that, and so, after a brief tussle with Walshingham’s detaining arm, had got away. “I got something to do,” he said. “ ’Ome.” And here he was﻿—panting an extraordinary resolve. The door opened, revealing the pleasantly furnished hall of Mrs. Bindon Botting, lit by rose-tinted lights, and in the centre of the picture, neat and pretty in black and white, stood Ann. At the sight of Kipps her colour vanished.
“Ann,” said Kipps, “I want to speak to you. I got something to say to you right away. See? I’m﻿—”
“This ain’t the door to speak to me at,” said Ann.
“But, Ann! It’s something special.”
“You spoke enough,” said Ann.
“Ann!”
“Besides. That’s my door, down there. Basement. If I was caught talking at *this* door﻿—!”
“But, Ann, *I’m*﻿—”
“Basement after nine. Them’s my hours. I’m a servant and likely to keep one.

===Chinese===

「从这些错乱里，」雷维尔说，「人影影绰绰、遥遥地辨认出浪漫时代最后一缕将熄的余光。让我们设想一下吧，博廷太太，至少试着设想——是爱情。」
基普斯的刀叉哐啷一响。
「是爱情，」博廷太太说；「不然还能是什么？在我们井然如常的日子底下，这些罗曼史一直在进行，到头来它们轰然发作，递辞呈，把我们的如常整个掀翻。哪个要命的、奇妙的兵——」
「家常仆佣们的七情六欲，」雷维尔说，收回了席面的掌控权。
基普斯紊乱的吃相上，这时覆上了一层安静，一层反常的镇定。平生头一回，他自作主张地拿定了一个主意。他不再听雷维尔了。他放下刀叉，再上的菜一概辞谢。库特怀着分寸得体的关切望着他，海伦微微红了脸。
夜里九点半来钟，宾登·博廷太太家的门铃被狠狠拽了一把，一位穿晚礼服、戴折叠礼帽、带着种种高华社交地位标记的年轻人立在门外。在他那片白领口上斜横着一条绛色织花绸带，给了他一种异样的气派，也遮掩了几小点红酒渍。他的折叠礼帽推在后脑勺上，露出一头乱发，透着一股不管不顾的孤注一掷。事实上，他是烧了船，拒绝入座陪太太们了。在后来那番谈话里，库特曾安安静静地抗议：「你一切都好好的，你知道。」基普斯回答说他对此连一个「哼」都不在乎；于是同沃尔辛厄姆那只好言挽留的胳膊小小较量了一番之后，他脱了身。「我有事。」他说。「回家。」瞧，他到了——喘着，揣着一个异乎寻常的决定。门开了，露出宾登·博廷太太家那间陈设可人的门厅，玫瑰色的灯光照着；画面正中，一身黑白、干净俏丽，站着安。一看见基普斯，她的血色褪尽了。
「安，」基普斯说，「我要同你说话。我有话这就要对你说。懂吗？我——」
「跟我说话不走这个门。」安说。
「可是，安！是特别的事。」
「你说得够多了。」安说。
「安！」
「再说，那是我的门，在那边。地下层。我要是叫人撞见在这*个门*口说话——！」
「可是，安，*我*——」
「九点以后，地下层。那是我的钟点。我是仆人，往后多半还是。
你要是来拜客，请问尊姓？可你有你的朋友，我有我的，你不许同*我*搭话。」

===Original===

If you’re calling here, what name please? But you got your friends and I got mine and you mustn’t go talking to *me*.”
“But, Ann, I want to ask you﻿—”
Someone appeared in the hall behind Ann. “Not here,” said Ann. “Don’t know anyone of that name,” and incontinently slammed the door in his face.
“What was that, Ann?” said Mrs. Bindon Botting’s invalid Aunt.
“Ge’m a little intoxicated, Ma’am﻿—asking for the wrong name, Ma’am.”
“What name did he want?” asked the lady, doubtfully.
“No name that *we* know, Ma’am,” said Ann, hustling along the hall towards the kitchen stairs.
“I hope you weren’t too short with him, Ann.”
“No shorter than he deserved, considering ’ow he be’aved,” said Ann, with her bosom heaving.
And Mrs. Bindon Botting’s invalid Aunt, perceiving suddenly that this call had some relation to Ann’s private and sentimental trouble, turned, after one moment of hesitating scrutiny, away.
She was an extremely sympathetic lady, was Mrs. Bindon Botting’s invalid Aunt; she took an interest in the servants, imposed piety, extorted confessions and followed human nature, blushing and lying defensively, to its reluctantly revealed recesses, but Ann’s sense of privacy was strong and her manner under drawing out and encouragement, sometimes even alarming.﻿ ﻿…
So the poor old lady went upstairs again.
The basement door opened and Kipps came into the kitchen. He was flushed and panting.
He struggled for speech.
“ ’Ere,” he said, and held out two half sixpences.
Ann stood behind the kitchen table﻿—face pale and eyes round, and now﻿—and it simplified Kipps very much﻿—he could see she had indeed been crying.
“Well?” she said.
“Don’t you see?”
Ann moved her head slightly.
“I kep’ it all these years.”
“You kep’ it too long.”
His mouth closed and his flush died away. He looked at her. The amulet, it seemed, had failed to work.
“Ann!” he said.
“Well?”
“Ann.”
The conversation still hung fire.
“Ann,” he said, made a movement with his hands that suggested appeal, and advanced a step.
Ann shook her head more defiantly, and became defensive.
“Look here, Ann,” said Kipps. “I been a fool.”
They stared into each other’s miserable eyes.
“Ann,” he said. “I want to marry you.”
Ann clutched the table edge. “You can’t,” she said faintly.
He made as if to approach her around the table, and she took a step that restored their distance.
“I must,” he said.
“You can’t.”
“I must.

「那是什么人，安？」宾登·博廷太太家养病的姑太太问。
「有点儿醉了，太太——找错了名字，太太。」
「他要找什么名字？」太太将信将疑地问。
「不是*咱们*认得的名字，太太。」安说着，赶紧顺着门厅往厨房楼梯那边去了。
「但愿你没太怠慢他，安。」
「比起他的行为来，不算太怠慢。」安说，胸口一起一伏。
宾登·博廷太太家养病的姑太太蓦地觉出，这次叩门同安那桩私人的、伤心的烦恼有些干系；迟疑地端详了片刻之后，她转过身，上楼去了。
她是位极端慈爱的太太，宾登·博廷太太家养病的姑太太；她对仆人们饶有兴趣，推行虔诚，逼取忏悔，跟踪人性——看它红着脸、撒着防御的谎——一直跟到它万般无奈才肯袒露的角落；可安的私己之感极强，一经引逗怂恿，她的神色有时甚至吓人。……
于是可怜的老太太又上楼去了。
地下层的门开了，基普斯走进厨房。他红着脸，喘着。
他挣扎着要说话。
「喏。」他说，摊出两个半块的六便士。
安站在厨房桌子后面——脸白，眼睛圆睁；而这时——这大大简化了基普斯的处境——他看得出，她方才确实哭过。
「怎么样？」她说。
「你还不明白么？」
安把头微微动了动。
「我留了它这么多年。」
「你留得太久了。」
他的嘴闭上了，脸上的红也退了。他看着她。看来，那个护符不灵了。
「安！」他说。
「怎么样？」
「安。」
谈话还是僵着。
「安，」他说，双手做了个近乎恳求的动作，朝前迈了一步。
安把头摇得更倔，摆出了防备的架势。
「听着，安，」基普斯说。「我一直是个傻瓜。」
两人瞪着对方悲惨的眼睛。
「安，」他说。「我要娶你。」
安一把抓住桌沿。「你不能。」她轻轻地说。
他做出要绕桌子挨过去的样子，她跨了一步，把两人间的距离又拉开。
「我非娶不可。」他说。
「你不能。」
「我非娶不可。你*非*嫁我不可，安。」

You *got* to marry me, Ann.”
“You can’t go marrying everybody. You got to marry ’er.”
“I shan’t.”
Ann shook her head. “You’re engaged to that girl. Lady, rather. You can’t be engaged to me.”
“I don’t want to be engaged to you. I *been* engaged. I want to be married to you. See? Rightaway.”
Ann turned a shade paler. “But what d’you mean?” she asked.
“Come right off to London and marry me. Now.”
“What d’you mean?”
Kipps became extremely lucid and earnest.
“I mean come right off and marry me now before anyone else can. *See?*”
“In London?”
“In London.”
They stared at one another again. They took things for granted in the most amazing way.
“I couldn’t,” said Ann. “For one thing my month’s not up for mor’n free weeks yet.”
They hung before that for a moment as though it was insurmountable.
“Look ’ere, Ann! Arst to go. Arst to go!”
“*She* wouldn’t,” said Ann.
“Then come without arsting,” said Kipps.
“She’s keep my box﻿—”
“She won’t.”
“She will.”
“She won’t.”
“You don’t know ’er.”
“Well, desh’er﻿—let’er! *Let’er!* Who cares? I’ll buy you a ’undred boxes if you’ll come.”
“It wouldn’t be right towards Her.”
“It isn’t Her you got to think about, Ann. It’s me.”
“And you ’aven’t treated me properly,” she said. “You ’aven’t treated me properly, Artie. You didn’t ought to ’ave﻿—”
“I didn’t say I *’ad*,” he interrupted, “did I, Ann?” he appealed. “I didn’t come to arguefy. I’m all wrong. I never said I wasn’t. It’s yes or no. Me or not.﻿ ﻿… I been a fool. There! See? I been a fool. Ain’t that enough? I got myself all tied up with everyone and made a fool of myself all around.﻿ ﻿…”
He pleaded, “It isn’t as if we didn’t care for one another, Ann.”
She seemed impassive and he resumed his discourse.
“I thought I wasn’t likely ever to see you again, Ann. I reely did. It isn’t as though I was seein’ you all the time. I didn’t know what I wanted, and I went and be’aved like a fool﻿—jest as anyone might. I know what I want and I know what I don’t want now.
“Ann!”
“Well?”
“Will you come?﻿ ﻿… Will you come?﻿ ﻿…”
Silence.
“If you don’t answer me, Ann﻿—I’m desprit﻿—if you don’t answer me now, if you don’t say you’ll come I’ll go right out now﻿—”
He turned doorward passionately as he spoke, with his threat incomplete.
“I’ll go,” he said; “I ’aven’t a friend in the world! I been and throwed everything away. I don’t know why I done things and why I ’aven’t.

他哀告道：「咱们又不是彼此无心，安。」

「安！」

===Original===

All I know is I can’t stand nothing in the world any more.” He choked. “The pier,” he said.
He fumbled with the door latch, grumbling some inarticulate self-pity, as if he sought a handle, and then he had it open.
Clearly he was going.
“Artie!” said Ann, sharply.
He turned about and the two hung, white and tense.
“I’ll do it,” said Ann.
His face began to work, he shut the door and came a step back to her, staring; his face became pitiful and then suddenly they moved together. “Artie!” she cried, “don’t go!” and held out her arms, weeping.
They clung close to one another.﻿ ﻿…
“Oh! I *been* so mis’bel,” cried Kipps, clinging to this lifebuoy, and suddenly his emotion, having no further serious work in hand, burst its way to a loud *boohoo*! His fashionable and expensive gibus flopped off and fell and rolled and lay neglected on the floor.
“I been so mis’bel,” said Kipps, giving himself vent. “Oh! I *been* so mis’bel, Ann.”
“Be quiet,” said Ann, holding his poor, blubbering head tightly to her heaving shoulder, and herself all a-quiver; “be quiet. She’s there! Listenin’. She’ll ’ear you, Artie, on the stairs.﻿ ﻿…”
Ann’s last words when, an hour later, they parted, Mrs. and Miss Bindon Botting having returned very audibly upstairs, deserve a section to themselves.
“I wouldn’t do this for everyone, mind you,” whispered Ann.

他的脸开始抽动，他关上门，朝她退回一步，瞪着；他的脸变得可怜，随后两人忽然凑到了一起。「阿蒂！」她喊道，「别走！」张开手臂，哭着。
两人紧紧抱在一起。……
「哦！我——一直——惨透了。」基普斯喊，死死抱住这只救生圈，他的感情既然再没有正经差事在手，索性轰然一声，放声大嚎！他那顶时新昂贵的折叠礼帽啪嗒掉了下去，滚了滚，没人理，躺在地板上。

「你记着，这可不是我肯为谁都做的。」安悄悄说。

## Book / 三 基普斯一家
===Original===

III Kippses

===Chinese===

三 基普斯一家

## VII London / 七 伦敦
===Original===

London was Kipps’ third world. There were no doubt other worlds, but Kipps knew only these three; firstly, New Romney and the Emporium, constituting his primary world, his world of origin, which also contained Ann; secondly, the world of culture and refinement, the world of which Coote was chaperon, and into which Kipps was presently to marry, a world it was fast becoming evident absolutely incompatible with the first, and, thirdly, a world still to a large extent unexplored, London. London presented itself as a place of great, grey spaces and incredible multitudes of people, centring about Charing Cross station and the Royal Grand Hotel, and containing at unexpected arbitrary points shops of the most amazing sort, statuary, Squares, Restaurants﻿—where it was possible for clever people like Walshingham to order a lunch item by item, to the waiters’ evident respect and sympathy﻿—exhibitions of incredible things﻿—the Walshinghams had taken him to the Arts and Crafts and to a picture gallery﻿—and theatres. London, moreover, is rendered habitable by hansom cabs. Young Walshingham was a natural cab taker, he was an all-round large minded young man, and he had in the course of their two days’ stay taken Kipps into no less than nine, so that Kipps was singularly not afraid of these vehicles. He knew that whereever you were, so soon as you were thoroughly lost you said “Hi!” to a cab, and then “Royal Grand Hotel.” Day and night these trusty conveyances are returning the strayed Londoner back to his point of departure, and were it not for their activity in a little while the whole population, so vast and incomprehensible is the intricate complexity of this great city, would be hopelessly lost forever. At any rate, that is how the thing presented itself to Kipps, and I have heard much the same from visitors from America.
His train was composed of corridor carriages, and he forgot his trouble for a time in the wonders of this modern substitute for railway compartments. He went from the non-smoking to the smoking carriage and smoked a cigarette, and strayed from his second-class carriage to a first and back. But presently Black Care got aboard the train and came and sat beside him. The exhilaration of escape had evaporated now, and he was presented with a terrible picture of his Aunt and Uncle arriving at his lodgings and finding him fled.

===Chinese===

伦敦是基普斯（Kipps）的第三个世界。别的世界无疑还有，可基普斯只认得这三个：第一，新罗姆尼和商场，构成他的本源世界、他出生的世界，安也在其中；第二，教养与文雅的世界，库特（Coote）做监护的那个世界，基普斯不久就要娶进去的世界——这个世界同第一个世界的不能相容，正日渐分明；第三，一个很大程度上还未勘探的世界——伦敦。伦敦呈现为一片浩大的灰色空间，人群多得难以置信，以查令十字车站和皇家大饭店为中心，在出人意料的随意各点，分布着顶顶惊人的铺子、雕像、广场、饭馆——在饭馆里，像沃尔辛厄姆那样聪明的人可以一样一样地点午餐，博得侍者明白可见的敬意与同情——还有不可思议的展览——沃尔辛厄姆一家带他看过工艺美术展和一家画廊——还有戏院。此外，伦敦之所以住得下去，靠的是双轮马车。小沃尔辛厄姆天生会打车，他是位面面俱到、器量大方的青年，在两天的逗留里带基普斯坐进了不下九辆，于是基普斯对这些车辆怕得异乎寻常地少。他知道，不管你在哪儿，只要彻底迷了路，就朝马车喊一声「喂！」，再喊「皇家大饭店」。日日夜夜，这些忠实的载具把走失的伦敦人送回出发点；若没有它们的忙碌，这座大城市结构复杂得那么浩大、那么无从索解，用不了多久，全体居民就会永远迷失。至少，事情在基普斯眼里是这个样子，而从美国来的游客那里，我也听到过大同小异的高见。
他这趟车是通廊车厢编组的，他一时忘了烦恼，只顾惊叹这种代替火车单间的现代发明。他从无烟车厢走到吸烟车厢，抽了一支烟，又从二等车厢溜达到头等，再溜达回来。可不多时，那黑色的忧愁也上了火车，过来坐在他身边。出逃的兴奋如今蒸发尽了，一幅可怕的图景摆到他面前：叔父叔母赶到他的住处，发现他人去楼空。

===Original===

He had left a hasty message that he was called away suddenly on business, “ver’ important business,” and they were to be sumptuously entertained. His immediate motive had been his passionate dread of an encounter between these excellent but unrefined old people and the Walshinghams, but now that end was secured, he could see how thwarted and exasperated they would be.
How to explain to them?
He ought never to have written to tell them!
He ought to have got married and told them afterwards.
He ought to have consulted Helen.
“Promise me,” she had said.
“Oh, *desh*!” said Kipps, and got up and walked back into the smoking car and began to consume cigarettes.
Suppose, after all, they found out the Walshingham’s address and went there!
At Charing Cross, however, there were distractions again. He took a cab in an entirely Walshingham manner, and was pleased to note the enhanced respect of the cabman when he mentioned the Royal Grand. He followed Walshingham’s routine on their previous visit with perfect success. They were very nice in the office, and gave him an excellent room at fourteen shillings the night.
He went up and spent a considerable time in examining the furniture of his room, scrutinising himself in its various mirrors and sitting on the edge of the bed whistling. It was a vast and splendid apartment, and cheap at fourteen shillings. But, finding the figure of Ann inclined to resume possession of his mind, he roused himself and descended by the staircase after a momentary hesitation before the lift. He had thought of lunch, but he drifted into the great drawing-room and read a guide to the Hotels of Europe for a space, until a doubt whether he was entitled to use this palatial apartment without extra charge arose in his mind. He would have liked something to eat very much now, but his inbred terror of the table was very strong. He did at last get by a porter in uniform towards the dining-room, but at the sight of a number of waiters and tables, with remarkable complications of knives and glasses, terror seized him, and he backed out again, with a mumbled remark to the waiter in the doorway about this not being the way.
He hovered in the hall and lounge until he thought the presiding porter regarded him with suspicion, and then went up to his room again by the staircase, got his hat and umbrella and struck boldly across the courtyard.

===Chinese===

他留下了一张仓促的字条，说自己因业务突然被叫走，「特——别要紧的业务」，吩咐把他们招待得丰盛体面。他当时最直接的动机，是死怕这对可敬却不文雅的老人同沃尔辛厄姆一家撞上；如今这一层保住了，他却看得出他们会多么扫兴、多么光火。
怎么向他们解释呢？
他压根儿就不该写信告诉他们！
他该先结了婚，再告诉他们。
他该同海伦商量。
「答应我，」她说过。
「哦，*该死*！」基普斯说，站起来走回吸烟车厢，开始一支接一支地抽烟。
万一，他们到底打听到了沃尔辛厄姆家的地址，找上门去呢！
不过到了查令十字，又有了分心的事。他用十足沃尔辛厄姆式的派头打了一辆马车，还高兴地注意到，一提「皇家大饭店」，车夫的敬意顿增。他把上一回沃尔辛厄姆的例行步骤照做了一遍，大获成功。写字间里的人客气得很，给了他一个顶好的房间，一晚十四先令。
他上楼去，花了相当工夫检视房间家具，对着各面镜子端详自己，坐在床沿上吹口哨。这是一间宽大堂皇的屋子，十四先令不算贵。可发现安的身影大有重新占领他脑子的势头，他振作起来，在电梯前迟疑了一瞬，还是顺楼梯下去。他本想吃午饭，却飘进了大客厅，读了一阵《欧洲旅馆指南》，直到心里起疑：这宫殿般的屋子，不加费他是否有权使用。这会儿他很想吃点东西了，可他对餐桌的胎里带的恐惧非常强大。末了他总算由一个穿制服的门房引到饭厅那边，但一见那么些侍者、那么些桌子，刀叉杯盘错综得那么厉害，恐惧攫住了他，他又退了出来，对门口的侍者咕哝了一句走错了路。
他在门厅和休息厅里徘徊，直到觉得那管事的门房看他的眼神起了疑，才又走楼梯上楼回房，拿了帽子和伞，大胆穿过院子。

===Original===

He would go to a restaurant instead.
He had a moment of elation in the gateway. He felt all the Strand must notice him as he emerged through the great gate of the Hotel. “One of these here rich swells,” they would say. “Don’t they do it just!” A cabman touched his hat. “No fear,” said Kipps, pleasantly.
Then he remembered he was hungry again.
Yet he decided he was in no great hurry for lunch, in spite of an internal protest, and turned eastward along the Strand in a leisurely manner. He tried to find a place to suit him soon enough. He tried to remember the sort of things Walshingham had ordered. Before all things he didn’t want to go into a place and look like a fool. Some of these places rook you dreadful, besides making fun of you. There was a place near Essex Street where there was a window brightly full of chops, tomatoes and lettuce. He stopped at this and reflected for a time, and then it occurred to him that you were expected to buy these things raw and cook them at home. Anyhow, there was sufficient doubt in the matter to stop him. He drifted on to a neat window with champagne bottles, a dish of asparagus and a framed menu of a two shilling lunch. He was about to enter, when fortunately he perceived two waiters looking at him over the back screen of the window with a most ironical expression, and he sheered off at once. There was a wonderful smell of hot food halfway down Fleet Street and a nice looking Tavern with several doors, but he could not decide which door. His nerve was going under the strain.
He hesitated at Farringdon Street and drifted up to St. Paul’s and round the church yard, full chiefly of dead bargains in the shop windows, to Cheapside. But now Kipps was getting demoralised, and each house of refreshment seemed to promise still more complicated obstacles to food. He didn’t know how you went in and what was the correct thing to do with your hat, he didn’t know what you said to the waiter or what you called the different things; he was convinced absolutely he would “fumble,” as Shalford would have said, and look like a fool. Somebody might laugh at him! The hungrier he got the more unendurable was the thought that anyone should laugh at him. For a time he considered an extraordinary expedient to account for his ignorance. He would go in and pretend to be a foreigner and not know English. Then they might understand.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

他改去饭馆。
在过街楼里他得意了一小会儿。他从大饭店的大门里走出来，觉得整条斯特兰德街必定都在注意他。「这又是一位阔佬，」他们会说。「瞧人家那派头！」一个车夫碰了碰帽檐。「不用。」基普斯和气地说。
随后他又想起自己饿了。
可他决定并不急着吃午饭，尽管肚子里抗议，还是悠着步子沿斯特兰德街往东走。他想尽快找个合意的地方。他试着回想沃尔辛厄姆点过的那几样。头一条，他可不想进了哪家铺子活像个傻瓜。这些地方有的宰起人来厉害着呢，还要取笑你。埃塞克斯街附近有一家，橱窗里亮亮堂堂摆满了排骨、西红柿和莴苣。他停在跟前琢磨了一阵，忽然想到，这些东西兴许是买生的、拿回家自己烧的。反正这一层疑心足够拦住他。他又飘到一家干净的橱窗前，摆着香槟酒瓶、一碟芦笋，还有一张装框的两先令午饭菜单。他正要进去，幸好看见两个侍者从橱窗后屏上方看他，一脸顶顶讥诮的神气，他立刻闪开了。舰队街走到半截，有一股热食的浓香，还有一家门脸好几扇门的好看酒馆，可他拿不定进哪扇门。他的胆气在这种压力下渐渐泄了。
他在法林顿街踌躇，飘到圣保罗大教堂，绕着教堂墓地转了一圈——橱窗里摆的多半是死了的便宜货——再转到奇普赛德。可这会儿基普斯已经乱了方寸，每一家饮食店都像是许诺了更繁复的取食障碍。他不懂得你怎么进去，帽子怎么办才得体，不知道对侍者说什么，各样东西叫什么名字；他绝对相信，用沙尔福德的话说，他会「抓瞎」，会活像个傻瓜。说不定有人要笑他！他越饿，想到有人会笑他这件事就越受不了。有一阵他甚至盘算起一条不寻常的妙计来解释自己的无知：进去就装外国人，不懂英语。那样他们兴许就体谅了。……

===Original===

Presently he had drifted into a part of London where there did not seem to be any refreshment places at all.
“Oh, *desh*!” said Kipps, in a sort of agony of indecisiveness. “The very nex’ place I see, in I go.”
The next place was a fried fish shop in a little side street, where there were also sausages on a gas-lit grill.
He would have gone in, but suddenly a new scruple came to him, that he was too well dressed for the company he could see dimly through the steam sitting at the counter and eating with a sort of nonchalant speed.
He was half minded to resort to a hansom and brave the terrors of the dining-room of the Royal Grand﻿—they wouldn’t know why he had gone out really﻿—when the only person he knew in London appeared (as the only person one does know will do in London) and slapped him on the shoulder. Kipps was hovering at a window at a few yards from the fish shop, pretending to examine some really strikingly cheap pink baby linen, and trying to settle finally about those sausages.
“Hullo, Kipps!” cried Sid; “spending the millions?”
Kipps turned, and was glad to perceive no lingering vestige of the chagrin that had been so painful at New Romney. Sid looked grave and important, and he wore a quite new silk hat that gave a commercial touch to a generally socialistic costume. For a moment the sight of Sid uplifted Kipps wonderfully. He saw him as a friend and helper, and only presently did it come clearly into his mind that this was the brother of Ann.
He made amiable noises.
“I’ve just been up this way,” Sid explained, “buying a secondhand ’namelling stove.﻿ ﻿… I’m going to ’namel myself.”
“Lor’!” said Kipps.
“Yes. Do me a lot of good. Let the customer choose his colour. See? What brings *you* up?”
Kipps had a momentary vision of his foiled Uncle and Aunt. “Jest a bit of a change,” he said.
Sid came to a swift decision. “Come down to my little show. I got someone I’d like to see talking to you.”
Even then Kipps did not think of Ann in this connection.
“Well,” he said, trying to invent an excuse on the spur of the moment. “Fact is,” he explained, “I was jest looking ’round to get a bit of lunch.”
“Dinner, we call it,” said Sid. “But that’s all right. You can’t get anything to eat hereabout. If you’re not too haughty to do a bit of slumming, there’s some mutton spoiling for me now﻿—”
The word “mutton” affected Kipps greatly.

===Chinese===

不多时，他飘进了伦敦的一个似乎压根儿没有饮食店的地界。
「哦，*该死*！」基普斯说，在一种优柔寡断的苦楚里。「再见到一家，我就进。」
下一家是小巷里的一爿炸鱼铺，煤气灯的烤架上还烤着香肠。
他本来要进去的，可忽然又来了一个新的顾忌：隔着蒸汽影影绰绰看得见柜台边坐着的人，吃得一副满不在乎的快活，他这一身穿得太讲究，配不上那一路伙伴。
他正想索性叫辆双轮马车，回去硬闯皇家大饭店的饭厅——反正没人知道他出门的真实缘故——这时，他在伦敦认识的唯一一个人出现了（人在伦敦，你认识的唯一一个人总会这样出现），一巴掌拍在他肩上。基普斯当时正在离鱼铺几码远的一扇橱窗前徘徊，装着端详几件便宜得吓人的粉色婴儿内衣，心里为那几根香肠做最后的裁决。
「喂，基普斯！」希德（Sid）喊道；「几百万花着呢？」
基普斯转过身，高兴地看到，在新罗姆尼时那份扎心的恼怒，如今连一点残迹也不剩。希德显得又郑重又了不起，戴一顶崭新的绸礼帽，给他那身总体上社会主义的装束添了一点生意人气息。乍一见希德，基普斯的精神奇妙地为之一振。他把他看作朋友和帮手，过了一会儿才清清楚楚想起：这是安的哥哥。
他发出友好的声音。
「我刚到这一带来，」希德解释道，「买了只二手的搪——瓷炉子。……我打算自己搪瓷。」
「嚯！」基普斯说。
「对。好处大着呢。让顾客自己挑颜色。明白？*你*怎么上来了？」
基普斯眼前闪过他那落了空的叔父叔母。「就是换换空气。」他说。
希德当机立断。「上我那小店来。我有个人，想叫他同你聊聊。」
即便那时，基普斯也没往安身上想。
「这个，」他说，一时编不出借口。「实情是，」他解释道，「我正想找个地方吃点午饭。」
「我们那儿叫正餐。」希德说。「不过不要紧。这左近你什么也吃不着。你要是不嫌掉价、肯来一趟贫民窟，我这会儿正有块羊肉在放坏——」
「羊肉」两个字大大打动了基普斯。

===Original===

“It won’t take us ’arf an hour,” said Sid, and Kipps was carried.
He discovered another means of London locomotion in the Underground Railway, and recovered his self-possession in that interest. “You don’t mind going third?” asked Sid, and Kipps said, “Nort a *bit* of it.” They were silent in the train for a time, on account of strangers in the carriage, and then Sid began to explain who it was that he wanted Kipps to meet. “It’s a chap named Masterman﻿—do you no end of good.
“He occupies our first floor front room, you know. It isn’t so much for gain I let as company. We don’t *want* the whole ’ouse, and another, I knew the man before. Met him at our Sociological, and after a bit he said he wasn’t comfortable where he was. That’s how it came about. He’s a first-class chap﻿—first-class. Science! You should see his books!
“Properly he’s a sort of journalist. He’s written a lot of things, but he’s been too ill lately to do very much. Poetry he’s written, all sorts. He writes for the *Commonweal* sometimes, and sometimes he reviews books. ’E’s got ’eaps of books﻿—’eaps. Besides selling a lot.
“He knows a regular lot of people, and all sorts of things. He’s been a dentist, and he’s a qualified chemist, an’ I seen him often reading German and French. Taught ’imself. He was here﻿—”
Sid indicated South Kensington, which had come opportunely outside the carriage windows, with a nod of his head, “﻿—three years. Studying science. But you’ll see ’im. When he really gets to talking﻿—he *pours* it out.”
“Ah!” said Kipps, nodding sympathetically, with his two hands on his umbrella knob.
“He’ll do big things some day,” said Sid. “He’s written a book on science already. *Physiography*, it’s called. *Elementary Physiography*! Some day he’ll write an Advanced﻿—when he gets time.”
He let this soak into Kipps.
“I can’t introduce you to Lords and swells,” he went on, “but I *can* show you a Famous Man, that’s going to be. I *can* do that. Leastways﻿—unless﻿—”
Sid hesitated.
“He’s got a frightful cough,” he said.
“He won’t care to talk with me,” weighed Kipps.
“That’s all right; *he* won’t mind. He’s fond of talking. He’d talk to anyone,” said Sid, reassuringly, and added a perplexing bit of Londonized Latin. “He doesn’t *pute* anything, *non alienum*. You know.”
“*I* know,” said Kipps, intelligently, over his umbrella knob, though of course that was altogether untrue.

===Chinese===

「用不了半个钟头。」希德说，基普斯就被裹挟走了。
他又发现了伦敦的一种交通手段——地下铁路，并在这份新奇里找回了镇定。「坐三等你不介意吧？」希德问，基普斯说：「一点——也不。」车上有生人，两人静了一阵，随后希德开始解释他要基普斯见的是谁。「是个叫马斯特曼（Masterman）的家伙——对你好处没完。
「他住我们二楼前屋，你知道。我出租也不大为钱，图个作伴。整所*房子*我们用不着，再说这人我早认识。在我们社会学社碰上的，处了一阵，他说他住的那儿不自在。就这么成的。他是头一等的家伙——头一等。科学！你该瞧瞧他的书！
「正经说他算个新闻界的人。他写过好些东西，只是近来病得厉害，干不动多少。诗他也写过，各样都来。他有时给《公益》写稿，有时写书评。他的书多得很——海了去。卖掉的还不算。
「他认得的人多得要命，什么事都懂。他当过牙医，还是有执照的药剂师，我还常见他读德文法文。自学的。他从前在这儿——」
希德朝车窗外恰到好处出现的南肯辛顿扬了扬下巴，「——待过三年。学科学。不过你自会见到他。他真到了兴头上——那话是*倒*出来的。」
「啊！」基普斯说，两手拄着伞头，同情地点着头。
「他早晚要干大事。」希德说。「科学书他已经写了一本。叫《自然地理学》。《初级自然地理学》！赶明儿有空了，他还要写一部《高级》的。」
他让这话往基普斯心里渗。
「勋爵啊阔佬啊，我引见不了你，」他接着说，「可我*能*让你见一位未来的名人。这个我*能*办到。至少——除非——」
希德迟疑了。
「他咳得凶。」他说。
「他怕不肯同我说话。」基普斯掂量道。
「那不碍事；*他*不挑人。他爱说话。逮谁跟谁说。」希德说，宽慰着，又添了一句伦敦化的拉丁文。「他什么都不*见外*，*non alienum*。你懂的。」
「*我*懂。」基普斯说，拄着伞头，一脸聪明——当然，这话全然不真。

===Original===

Kipps found Sid’s shop a practical looking establishment, stocked with the most remarkable collection of bicycles and pieces of bicycle that he had ever beheld. “My hiring stock,” said Sid, with a wave to this ironmongery, “and there’s the best machine at a democratic price in London, The Red-Flag, built by *me*. See?”
He indicated a graceful, grey-brown framework in the window. “And there’s my stock of accessories﻿—store prices.
“Go in for motors a bit,” added Sid.
“Mutton?” said Kipps, not hearing him distinctly.
“Motors, I *said*.﻿ ﻿… ’Owever, Mutton Department ’ere,” and he opened a door that had a curtain guarded window in its upper panel, to reveal a little room with red walls and green furniture, with a white clothed table and the generous promise of a meal. “Fanny!” he shouted. “Here’s Art Kipps.”
A bright-eyed young woman of five or six and twenty in a pink print appeared, a little flushed from cooking, and wiped a hand on an apron and shook hands and smiled, and said it would all be ready in a minute. She went on to say she had heard of Kipps and his luck, and meanwhile Sid vanished to draw the beer, and returned with two glasses for himself and Kipps.
“Drink that,” said Sid, and Kipps felt all the better for it.
“I give Mr. Masterman *’is* upstairs a hour ago,” said Mrs. Sid. “I didn’t think ’e ought to wait.”
A rapid succession of brisk movements on the part of everyone, and they were all four at dinner﻿—the fourth person being Master Walt Whitman Pornick, a cheerful young gentleman of one and a half, who was given a spoon to hammer on the table with to keep him quiet, and who got “Kipps” right at the first effort and kept it all through the meal, combining it first with this previous acquisition, and then that. “Peacock Kipps” said Master Walt, at which there was great laughter, and also “More Mutton, Kipps.”
“He’s a regular oner,” said Mrs. Sid, “for catching up words. You can’t say a word but what ’e’s on to it.”
There were no serviettes and less ceremony, and Kipps thought he had never enjoyed a meal so much. Everyone was a little excited by the meeting and chatting, and disposed to laugh, and things went off easily from the very beginning. If there was a pause Master Walt filled it in. Mrs.

===Chinese===

基普斯发现希德的铺子是家看上去挺实干的下处，堆着他生平仅见的一批最了不起的自行车和自行车部件。「我出租的那批，」希德朝这堆铁器一挥手说，「那是伦敦民主价格里顶好的车，《红旗》牌，*我*亲手造的。明白？」
他指了指橱窗里一副优雅的灰褐色车架。「那是我的配件存货——铺子价。
「汽车我也捎带着搞。」希德补了一句。
「羊肉？」基普斯问，没听清。
「汽车，我*说的是*。……反正，羊肉部在这儿。」他推开一扇上节嵌着挂帘小窗的门，露出一间红墙绿椅的小屋，一张白桌布的桌子，和一顿饭的丰盛许诺。「范妮！」他喊，「阿特·基普斯来了。」
一个二十五六岁、眼睛亮亮的年轻女人出来了，穿一身粉红印花布，做饭熏得脸有点红，在围裙上擦了擦手，握了握手，笑着说，马上就都好了。她接着说，基普斯和他的好运气她早听说了；这其间希德出去打啤酒，端回来两杯，自己一杯，基普斯一杯。
「把它喝了。」希德说，基普斯喝完觉得周身熨帖。
「马斯特曼先生*他*那份，一个钟头前我就送上去了。」希德太太说。「我想不能叫他干等。」
人人一阵风似的忙了一阵，四个人就都上了桌——第四位是沃尔特·惠特曼·波尼克小少爷，一位一岁半的快乐小绅士，给了他一把勺子敲桌子，好叫他安分；他头一遍就咬准了「基普斯」，一顿饭从头叫到底，先同他原有的家当拼在一起，再同那件拼。「孔雀基普斯。」沃尔特小少爷说，引得满堂大笑；还有「再来羊肉，基普斯」。
「他学话真是把好手，」希德太太说，「一个字刚出口，他这就捡去了。」
没有餐巾，更没什么礼数，基普斯觉得自己从没吃得这么痛快过。人人都被这场见面和闲谈弄得有点兴奋，都爱笑，事情从头就顺顺当当。偶有冷场，沃尔特小少爷就补上。希德太太

===Original===

Sid, who tempered her enormous admiration for Sid’s intellect and his socialism and his severe business methods by a motherly sense of her sex and seniority, spoke of them both as “you boys,” and dilated﻿—when she was not urging Kipps to have some more of this or that﻿—on the disparity between herself and her husband.
“Shouldn’t ha’ thought there was a year between you,” said Kipps; “you seem jest a match.”
“*I’m his* match, anyhow,” said Mrs. Sid, and no epigram of young Walshingham’s was ever better received.
“Match,” said young Walt, coming in on the trail of the joke and getting a round for himself.
Any sense of superior fortune had long vanished from Kipps’ mind, and he found himself looking at host and hostess with enormous respect. Really, old Sid was a wonderful chap, here in his own house at two and twenty, carving his own mutton and lording it over wife and child. No legacies needed by him! And Mrs. Sid, so kind and bright and hearty! And the child, old Sid’s child! Old Sid had jumped round a bit. It needed the sense of his fortune at the back of his mind to keep Kipps from feeling abject. He resolved he’d buy young Walt something tremendous in toys at the first opportunity.
“Drop more beer, Art?”
“Right you are, old man.”
“Cut Mr. Kipps a bit more bread, Sid.”
“Can’t I pass *you* a bit?”
Sid was all right, Sid was, and there was no mistake about that.
It was growing up in his mind that Sid was the brother of Ann, but he said nothing about her for excellent reasons. After all, because he remembered Sid’s irritation at her name when they had met in New Romney seemed to show a certain separation. They didn’t tell each other much.﻿ ﻿… He didn’t know how things might be between Ann and Sid, either.
Still, for all that, Sid was Ann’s brother.
The furniture of the room did not assert itself very much above the cheerful business at the table, but Kipps was impressed with the idea that it was pretty. There was a dresser at the end with a number of gay plates and a mug or so, a Labour Day poster, by Walter Crane, on the wall, and through the glass and over the blind of the shop door one had a glimpse of the bright coloured advertisement cards of bicycle dealers, and a shelfful of boxes labelled, The Paragon Bell, The Scarum Bell, and The Patent Omi! Horn.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

希德太太，她对希德的才智、他的社会主义和他那套严格生意经怀着一望无际的钦佩，又拿她那份母性的性别感和年长感加以调和——把两个男人都叫作「你们这两个孩子」，在不见劝基普斯再添这个那个的当口，就大谈她自己同丈夫的差距。
「真看不出你们差着一岁。」基普斯说；「你们真是天生一对。」
「*反正我是他*的那一对。」希德太太说，小沃尔辛厄姆顶好的警句也没得过这样的满堂彩。
「一对。」小沃尔特说，追着笑柄赶来，也给自己赢得一片。
优越感在基普斯心里早已烟消云散，他发觉自己带着莫大的敬意看着这一对主人。说真的，老希德真是个了不起的家伙：二十二岁上就有了自己的家，切自己的羊肉，当家作主，管着老婆孩子。他用不着什么遗产！还有希德太太，这么和气、这么鲜亮、这么热乎！还有那孩子，老希德的孩子！老希德是绕了点弯子，可到底成了家。全靠脑后那笔家产垫着，基普斯才不觉得自己寒碜。他打定主意，头一个机会就给小沃尔特买件了不得的大玩具。
「再来点啤酒，阿特？」
「好嘞，老伙计。」
「给基普斯先生再切点面包，希德。」
「*你*要不要来一块？」
希德这人没得说，希德真是没得说。
他心里越来越清楚，希德是安的哥哥，可他绝口不提她，缘由很充分。毕竟，他在新罗姆尼碰见时，希德一听她的名字就冒火，可见兄妹间多少有了隔膜。他们彼此也并不多讲。……安同希德之间如今怎样，他也不知道。
可不管怎么说，希德是安的哥哥。
屋里的家具在饭桌上的热闹底下没怎么显山露水，可基普斯觉得它怪好看的。顶头立着一架碗柜，摆着好些花哨的盘子和一两只大杯子；墙上贴着沃尔特·克兰画的劳动节招贴画；隔着店门的玻璃和布帘，能瞥见自行车商花花绿绿的广告卡，还有一架子盒子，上面印着：至品铃、天不怕铃、专利奥米！喇叭。……

===Original===

It seemed incredible that he had been in Folkestone that morning, and even now his Aunt and Uncle﻿—!
Brrr. It didn’t do to think of his Aunt and Uncle.
When Sid repeated his invitation to come and see Masterman, Kipps, now flushed with beer and Irish stew, said he didn’t mind if he did, and after a preliminary shout from Sid that was answered by a voice and a cough, the two went upstairs.
“Masterman’s a rare one,” said Sid over his arm and in an undertone. “You should hear him speak at a meeting.﻿ ﻿… If he’s in form, that is.”
He rapped and went into a large, untidy room.
“This is Kipps,” he said. “You know. The chap I told you of. With twelve ’undred a year.”
Masterman sat gnawing at an empty pipe and as close to the fire as though it was alight and the season midwinter. Kipps concentrated upon him for a space, and only later took in something of the frowsy furniture, the little bed half behind, and evidently supposed to be wholly behind, a careless screen, the spittoon by the fender, the remains of a dinner on the chest of drawers and the scattered books and papers. Masterman’s face showed him a man of forty or more, with curious hollows at the side of his forehead and about his eyes. His eyes were very bright; there was a spot of red in his cheeks, and the wiry black moustache under his short, red nose had been trimmed with scissors into a sort of brush along his upper lip. His teeth were darkened ruins. His jacket collar was turned up about a knitted white neck wrap, and his sleeves betrayed no cuffs. He did not rise to greet Kipps, but held out a thin wristed hand and pointed with the other to a bedroom arm chair.
“Glad to see you,” he said. “Sit down and make yourself at home. Will you smoke?”
Kipps said he would, and produced his store. He was about to take one, and then, with a civil afterthought, handed the packet first to Masterman and Sid. Masterman pretended surprise to find his pipe out before he took one. There was an interlude of matches. Sid pushed the end of the screen out of his way, sat down on the bed thus frankly admitted, and prepared, with a certain quiet satisfaction of manner, to witness Masterman’s treatment of Kipps.
“And how does it feel to have twelve hundred a year?” asked Masterman, holding his cigarette to his nose tip in a curious manner.
“It’s rum,” confided Kipps, after a reflective interval. “It feels juiced rum.”

===Chinese===

想想真不可思议，他早晨还在福克斯通；就是这会儿，他叔父叔母——！
嘶。叔父叔母是不去想的好。
希德重申请他去见马斯特曼时，基普斯——这会儿啤酒和爱尔兰炖肉正让他红光满面——说去就去；希德先朝楼上喊了一嗓子，上头应了一声加一阵咳嗽，两人就上了楼。
「马斯特曼是个宝，」希德侧过胳膊、压低嗓门说。「你该听听他在会场上讲。……要是他状态好的话。」
他敲了敲门，走进一间又大又乱的大屋子。
「这就是基普斯，」他说。「你知道的。我同你说过的那个。一年一千二百镑的。」
马斯特曼坐在那儿啃一支空烟斗，紧挨着炉火，仿佛火正旺着、正是隆冬似的。基普斯先把他端详了一阵，过后才顾上看屋里那些邋遢家具：半掩在一扇漫不经心的屏风后头、显然本该全挡住的小床；炉边围栏旁的痰盂；五斗柜上的一顿剩饭；还有散乱的书籍报纸。马斯特曼的脸告诉他，这是个四十开外的人，额角和眼窝四周有古怪的凹陷。他的眼睛极亮；颧骨上有一块红斑；短短的红鼻子底下，铁黑的髭须用剪子修成了上唇一排刷子。他的牙齿是发黑的断垣残壁。上衣领子翻起来，裹着一条针织的白色围颈，袖口上找不出袖头。他没有起身迎接基普斯，只伸出一只细腕子的手，另一只手朝一把卧室扶手椅一指。
「见到你很高兴。」他说。「坐下，别拘束。抽烟吗？」
基普斯说要抽，掏出他那包烟。他刚要取一支，随即想到礼貌，先把烟包递给马斯特曼和希德。马斯特曼接烟之前，先装作奇怪地发现烟斗已经灭了。插进一段划火柴的小戏。希德把屏风一角推开，在那张就此坦率亮相的床上坐下，带着一种安安静静的满意，准备旁观马斯特曼怎样对付基普斯。
「一年一千二百镑，是个什么滋味？」马斯特曼问，用一种古怪的姿势把香烟举到鼻尖上。
「邪门，」基普斯想了想，吐露道。「觉得邪了门了。」

===Original===

“I never felt it,” said Masterman.
“It takes a bit of getting into,” said Kipps. “I can tell you that.”
Masterman smoked and regarded Kipps with curious eyes.
“I expect it does,” he said presently.
“And has it made you perfectly happy?” he asked, abruptly.
“I couldn’t ’ardly say *that*,” said Kipps.
Masterman smiled. “No,” he said. “Has it made you much happier?”
“It did at first.”
“Yes. But you got used to it. How long, for example, did the real delirious excitement last?”
“Oo, *that*! Perhaps a week,” said Kipps.
Masterman nodded his head. “That’s what discourages *me* from amassing wealth,” he said to Sid. “You adjust yourself. It doesn’t last. I’ve always had an inkling of that, and it’s interesting to get it confirmed. I shall go on sponging for a bit longer on *you*, I think.”
“You don’t,” said Sid. “No fear.”
“Twenty-four thousand pounds,” said Masterman, and blew a cloud of smoke. “Lord! Doesn’t it worry you?”
“It is a bit worrying at times.﻿ ﻿… Things ’appen.”
“Going to marry?”
“Yes.”
“H’m. Lady, I guess, of a superior social position?”
“Rather,” said Kipps. “Cousin to the Earl of Beauprés.”
Masterman readjusted his long body with an air of having accumulated all the facts he needed. He snuggled his shoulder-blades down into the chair and raised his angular knees. “I doubt,” he said, flicking cigarette ash into the atmosphere, “if any great gain or loss of money does﻿—as things are at present﻿—make more than the slightest difference in one’s happiness. It ought to﻿—if money was what it ought to be, the token for given service; one ought to get an increase in power and happiness for every pound one got. But the plain fact is the times are out of joint, and money﻿—money, like everything else, is a deception and a disappointment.”
He turned his face to Kipps and enforced his next words with the index finger of his lean, lank hand. “If I thought otherwise,” he said, “I should exert *myself* to get some. But, if one sees things clearly, one is so discouraged. So confoundedly discouraged.﻿ ﻿… When you first got your money, you thought that it meant you might buy just anything you fancied?”
“I was a bit that way,” said Kipps.
“And you found that you couldn’t.

===Chinese===

「我就没尝过。」马斯特曼说。
「那得适应一阵子。」基普斯说。「这我可以告诉你。」
马斯特曼抽着烟，用好奇的目光打量基普斯。
「我想也是。」他过了一会儿说。
「它叫你完全幸福了吗？」他突然问。
「那可*不敢*说。」基普斯说。
马斯特曼笑了。「不，」他说。「叫你幸福多了吗？」
「起头是的。」
「是啊。可后来就惯了。比方说，那种真正的发狂的兴奋，持续了多久？」
「哦，*那个*！兴许一个礼拜吧。」基普斯说。
马斯特曼点点头。「这就是*我*不想发财的原因，」他对希德说。「你会自我调适。它长久不了。这一点我早有预感，得到证实很有意思。我看我还要再靠*你*揩一阵子油。」
「你哪里是。」希德说。「哪里的话。」
「两万四千镑，」马斯特曼说，吹出一口烟。「老天！它不叫你烦吗？」
「有时候是有点烦。……事儿多。」
「要结婚了吗？」
「要了。」
「嗯。我猜，是位太太，社会地位高些的？」
「可不，」基普斯说。「博普雷伯爵的表亲。」
马斯特曼把他那长长的身子重新安顿了一下，一副所需事实已经采齐的神气。他把肩胛往椅背里塞了塞，抬起棱角分明的膝盖。「我怀疑，」他说，一面把烟灰弹进空气里，「眼下这个世道，钱上头的大进大出，对一个人的幸福顶多产生一丝一毫的差别。本该有的——假如钱是它本该是的东西，即付出服务的凭证；那么每得一镑，就该多得一分权力和幸福。可明摆着的事实是，时代脱了榫，钱——钱同别的一切一样，是一场骗局和一场失望。」
他把脸转向基普斯，用那只瘦长手的食指加强后面的话。「我若不这样想，」他说，「我就会*费力气*去弄一点。可是，一个人把事情看清楚了，就灰心。灰心得要命。……你刚得着钱那会儿，以为它意味着看上什么就能买什么？」
「我是有点那个意思。」基普斯说。
「可你发现买不成。

===Original===

You found that for all sorts of things it was a question of where to buy and how to buy, and what you didn’t know how to buy with your money, straight away this world planted something else upon you﻿—”
“I got rather done over a banjo first day,” said Kipps. “Leastways, my Uncle says.”
“Exactly,” said Masterman.
Sid began to speak from the bed. “That’s all very well, Masterman,” he said, “but, after all, money is Power, you know. You can do all sorts of things﻿—”
“I’m talking of happiness,” said Masterman. “You can do all sorts of things with a loaded gun in the Hammersmith Broadway, but nothing﻿—practically﻿—that will make you or anyone else very happy. Nothing. Power’s a different matter altogether. As for happiness, you want a world in order before money or property, or any of those things that have any real value, and this world, I tell you, is hopelessly out of joint. Man is a social animal with a mind nowadays that goes around the globe, and a community cannot be happy in one part and unhappy in another. It’s all or nothing, no patching any more forever. It is the standing mistake of the world not to understand that. Consequently people think there is a class or order somewhere, just above them or just below them, or a country or place somewhere, that is really safe and happy. The fact is, Society is one body, and it is either well or ill. That’s the law. This society we live in is ill. It’s a fractious, feverish invalid, gouty, greedy and ill-nourished. You can’t have a happy left leg with neuralgia, or a happy throat with a broken leg. That’s my position, and that’s the knowledge you’ll come to. I’m so satisfied of it that I sit here and wait for my end quite calmly, sure that I can’t better things by bothering﻿—in my time, and so far as I am concerned, that is. I’m not even greedy any more﻿—my egotism’s at the bottom of a pond, with a philosophical brick around its neck. The world is ill, my time is short and my strength is small. I’m as happy here as anywhere.”
He coughed and was silent for a moment, then brought the index finger around to Kipps again. “You’ve had the opportunity of sampling two grades of society, and you don’t find the new people you’re among much better or any happier than the old?”
“No,” said Kipps, reflectively. “No. I ’aven’t seen it quite like that before, but﻿—. No. They’re not.”

===Chinese===

你发现，对种种东西来说，问题在于上哪儿买、怎么买；凡是你不懂得怎么买的东西，这个世界立刻就给你另栽一样——」
「我头一天就叫人在一把班卓琴上宰了一刀。」基普斯说。「反正我叔父这么说。」
「正是。」马斯特曼说。
希德在床上开了腔。「话是不错，马斯特曼，」他说，「可钱到底就是权力，你知道。什么事都能办——」
「我说的是幸福。」马斯特曼说。「在哈默史密斯广场拿一支上膛的枪，什么事也办得成，可办不成——实际上——任何叫你或者叫任何人真正幸福的事。一件也办不成。权力是另一码事。至于幸福，你得先有一个上轨道的世界，然后才谈得上钱、财产，以及一切真正有价值的东西；而我告诉你，这个世界是无可救药地脱了榫。人是一种社会动物，如今长了一颗环球的头脑；一个共同体不能这一半幸福、那一半不幸。要么全有，要么全无，永远不能再打补丁了。世人的老错误就在不懂这一点。于是人们总以为，在什么地方有个阶级或阶层，就在他们头顶上一点或脚底下一点，或者有个国家、有个地方，真正又安稳又幸福。事实是，社会是一个身体，它要么健康，要么生病。这就是法则。我们生活在其中的这个社会是病着的。它是个躁急发热的病人，痛风，贪馋，营养不良。神经痛的左腿不可能独个儿幸福，断了腿的嗓子也不可能独个儿幸福。这就是我的立场，也是你终将获得的认识。我对这一点如此确信，以致我坐在这儿，安安静静等我的结局，确信在我这个时代、就我本人而论，操心也改良不了什么。我连贪心都没有了——我的利己主义脖子上拴着一块哲学的砖，沉在塘底了。世界病着，我的时日无多，力气也小。我在这儿同在哪儿一样幸福。」
他咳了一阵，静了片刻，又把食指转回基普斯这边。「你有机会把两个等级的社会各尝了一口，你没发现你如今混迹的新人比旧人好多少、幸福多少？」
「没有，」基普斯若有所思地说。「没。这我从前还没这么想过，可是——。没有。他们不。」

===Original===

“And you might go all up the scale and down the scale and find the same thing. Man’s a gregarious beast, a gregarious beast, and no money will buy you out of your own time﻿—any more than out of your own skill. All the way up and all the way down the scale there’s the same discontent. No one is quite sure where they stand, and everyone’s fretting. The herd’s uneasy and feverish. All the old tradition goes or has gone, and there’s no one to make a new tradition. Where are your nobles now? Where are your gentlemen? They vanished directly the peasant found out he wasn’t happy and ceased to be a peasant. There’s big men and little men mixed up together, that’s all. None of us know where we are. Your cads in a bank holiday train and your cads on a two thousand pound motor; except for a difference in scale, there’s not a pin to choose between them. Your smart society is as low and vulgar and uncomfortable for a balanced soul as a gin palace, no more and no less; there’s no place or level of honour or fine living left in the world; so what’s the good of climbing?”
“ ’Ear, ’ear,” said Sid.
“It’s true,” said Kipps.
“*I* don’t climb,” said Masterman, and accepted Kipps’ silent offer of another cigarette.
“No,” he said. “This world is out of joint. It’s broken up, and I doubt if it will heal. I doubt very much if it’ll heal. We’re in the beginning of the Sickness of the World.”
He rolled his cigarette in his lean fingers and repeated with satisfaction: “The Sickness of the World.”
“It’s we’ve got to make it better,” said Sid, and looked at Kipps.
“Ah, Sid’s an optimist,” said Masterman.
“So are you, most times,” said Sid.
Kipps lit another cigarette with an air of intelligent participation.
“Frankly,” said Masterman, recrossing his legs and expelling a jet of smoke luxuriously, “frankly, I think this civilisation of ours is on the topple.”
“There’s Socialism,” said Sid.
“There’s no imagination to make use of it.”
“We’ve got to *make* one,” said Sid.
“In a couple of centuries perhaps,” said Masterman. “But meanwhile we’re going to have a pretty acute attack of confusion. Universal confusion. Like one of those crushes when men are killed and maimed for no reason at all, going into a meeting or crowding for a train. Commercial and Industrial Stresses. Political Exploitation. Tariff Wars. Revolutions.

===Chinese===

「你顺着阶梯一路往上、一路往下，都会发现同样的事。人是一种群居的兽，一种群居的兽，什么钱也买不出你自己的时代——正如买不出你自己的手艺。阶梯上上下下，处处是同样的不满。谁也拿不准自己站在哪儿，人人都在心焦。兽群不安，躁动发热。旧的传统正在消亡或者已经死了，又没有人来立一个新的。你们的贵族如今何在？你们的绅士如今何在？农民一发现自己不幸福、一不再是农民，他们立刻就散了。剩下的不过是大人物和小人物搅在一处。我们谁也不知道自己身在何地。银行休假日火车上的粗坯，和两千镑一辆汽车的粗坯，除了规模不同，一根针的差别也挑不出来。你们的时髦社交界，对一个健全的灵魂来说，同杜松子酒馆一样下流、粗俗、难受，不多也不少；世上再没有一块荣誉之地、一层精美生活留下了；那还爬个什么劲？」
「有——理。」希德说。
「是真的。」基普斯说。
「*我*就不爬。」马斯特曼说，接受了基普斯默默递来的又一支烟。
「不，」他说。「这个世界脱了榫。它碎掉了，我怀疑它长不拢了。我很怀疑它长不拢。我们正处在『世界大病』的开头。」
他用瘦手指卷着烟，满意地重复了一遍：「世界大病。」

基普斯又点上一支烟，一脸聪明的参与。

「还有社会主义呢。」希德说。
「没有利用它的想象力。」

===Original===

All the bloodshed that will come of some fools calling half the white world yellow. These things alter the attitude of everybody to everybody. Everybody’s going to feel ’em. Every fool in the world panting and shoving. *We’re* all going to be as happy and comfortable as a household during a removal. What else can we expect?”
Kipps was moved to speak, but not in answer to Masterman’s enquiry. “I’ve never rightly got the ’eng of this Socialism,” he said. “What’s it going to do, like?”
They had been imagining that he had some elementary idea in the matter, but as soon as he had made it clear that he hadn’t, Sid plunged at exposition, and in a little while Masterman, abandoning his pose of the detached man ready to die, joined in. At first he joined in only to correct Sid’s version, but afterwards he took control. His manner changed. He sat up and rested his elbow on his knees, and his cheek flushed a little. He expanded his case against Property and the property class with such vigour that Kipps was completely carried away, and never thought of asking for a clear vision of the thing that would fill the void this abolition might create. For a time he quite forgot his own private opulence. And it was as if something had been lit in Masterman. His languor passed. He enforced his words by gestures of his long, thin hands. And as he passed swiftly from point to point of his argument it was evident he grew angry.
“Today,” he said, “the world is ruled by rich men; they may do almost anything they like with the world. And what are they doing? Laying it waste!”
“Hear, hear!” said Sid, very sternly.
Masterman stood up, gaunt and long, thrust his hands in his pockets and turned his back to the fireplace.
“Collectively, the rich today have neither heart nor imagination. No! They own machinery, they have knowledge and instruments and powers beyond all previous dreaming, and what are they doing with them? Think what they are doing with them, Kipps, and think what they might do. God gives them a power like the motor car, and all they can do with it is to go careering about the roads in goggled masks killing children and making machinery hateful to the soul of man!” (“True,” said Sid, “true.”) “God gives them means of communication, power unparalleled of every sort, time and absolute liberty! They waste it all in folly!

===Chinese===

因几个蠢货把白种世界的一半唤作黄种人而流的血。这些事情改变每一个人对每一个人的态度。人人都将尝到滋味。世上的每一个蠢货喘着、挤着。*咱们*全都要像搬家日子里的一户人家那样幸福、那样安逸。还能指望别的什么？」
基普斯被激起说话，却不是回答马斯特曼的问话。「这个社会主义，我一直没真摸着门道。」他说。「它到底要干什么，呃？」
他们原以为他多少懂点皮毛，可他一摆明自己毫无概念，希德立刻扑上去讲解，不多时，连摆好了超然等死架势的马斯特曼也加入了。起初他加入只为纠正希德的讲法，随后他就接管了。他的神气变了。他坐直起来，胳膊肘拄着膝盖，脸颊微微发红。他朝财产和有产阶级发起的控诉如此雄辩，基普斯被整个卷了走，压根儿没想起要求看清：这废除之后腾出的空当，拿什么去填。有一阵他把自己的万贯家私全忘了。仿佛马斯特曼体内有什么被点着了。他的倦怠散了。他用那双瘦长的手比划着加强语气。论点一个接一个飞掠而过，他分明越来越怒。
「今天，」他说，「世界由富人统治着；他们几乎可以为所欲为。而他们在干什么？糟蹋它！」
「有理，有理！」希德说，说得极庄重。
马斯特曼站起来，瘦骨嶙峋，长长的，两手插兜，背对壁炉。
「作为整体，今天的富人既没有心肠，也没有想象力。没有！他们拥有机器，拥有知识和工具，拥有超出以往一切梦想的威力，而他们拿这些在干什么？想想他们拿这些在干什么，基普斯，再想想他们本可以干什么。上帝给了他们汽车这样一样力量，他们能做的，只是戴上防风镜满马路横冲直撞，轧死孩子，把机器弄得叫人类的灵魂憎恶！」（「对，」希德说，「对。」）「上帝给了他们交通的手段，给了他们种种空前的力量、时间和绝对的自由！他们把它统统挥霍在蠢事上！

===Original===

Here under their feet (and Kipps’ eyes followed the direction of a lean index finger to the hearthrug) under their accursed wheels, the great mass of men festers and breeds in darkness, darkness those others make by standing in the light. The darkness breeds and breeds. It knows no better.﻿ ﻿… Unless you can crawl or pander or rob you must stay in the stew you are born in. And those rich beasts above claw and clutch as though they had nothing! They grudge us our schools, they grudge us a gleam of light and air, they cheat us and then seek to forget us.﻿ ﻿… There is no rule, no guidance, only accidents and happy flukes.﻿ ﻿… Our multitudes of poverty increase, and this crew of rulers makes no provision, foresees nothing, anticipates nothing.﻿ ﻿…”
He paused and made a step, and stood over Kipps in a white heat of anger. Kipps nodded in a non-commital manner and looked hard and rather gloomily at his host’s slipper as he talked.
“It isn’t as though they had something to show for the waste they make of us, Kipps. They haven’t. They are ugly and cowardly and mean. Look at their women! Painted, dyed and drugged, hiding their ugly shapes under a load of dress! There isn’t a woman in the swim of society at the present time, wouldn’t sell herself, body and soul, who wouldn’t lick the boots of a Jew or marry a nigger, rather than live decently on a hundred a year! On what would be wealth for you and me! They know it. They know we know it.﻿ ﻿… No one believes in them. No one believes in nobility any more. Nobody believes in kingship any more. Nobody believes there is justice in the law.﻿ ﻿… But people have habits, people go on in the old grooves, as long as there’s work, as long as there’s weekly money.﻿ ﻿… It won’t last, Kipps.”
He coughed and paused. “Wait for the lean years,” he cried. “Wait for the lean years.” And suddenly he fell into a struggle with his cough and spat a gout of blood. “It’s nothing,” he said to Kipps’ note of startled horror.
He went on talking, and the protests of his cough interlaced with his words, and Sid beamed in an ecstasy of painful admiration.
“Look at the fraud they have let life become, the miserable mockery of the hope of one’s youth. What have *I* had? I found myself at thirteen being forced into a factory like a rabbit into a chloroformed box. Thirteen!﻿—when *their* children are babies.

===Chinese===

就在他们脚底下（基普斯的目光顺着那根瘦食指指向炉前地毯），在他们那些可诅咒的车轮底下，人的大众在黑暗里溃烂、滋生——那黑暗，是另外那些人站在光里造出来的。黑暗滋生啊滋生。它不知别的。……你不会爬、不会捧、不会抢，就只好烂在你出生的那锅汤里。而上头那些富畜生还在抓、还在捞，仿佛一无所有！他们舍不得给我们学校，舍不得给我们一线光、一口气，他们欺骗我们，又想忘掉我们。……没有规则，没有引导，只有偶然和侥幸。……我们贫穷的大军在膨胀，而这帮统治者不作任何安排，不预见任何事，不预备任何事。……」
他顿了顿，跨前一步，怒到白热地立在基普斯面前。基普斯不置可否地点着头，说话时死死地、相当阴沉地盯着主人的拖鞋。
「他们糟蹋了我们，倒好像总该有点什么可看，基普斯。他们没有。他们丑陋、怯懦、刻薄。瞧瞧他们的女人！涂的，染的，用药撑着的，一身累赘的衣裳底下藏着丑陋的身段！当今社交场上扎猛子的女人，没有一个不肯出卖自己的——灵魂带肉体一起，没有一个不肯去舔犹太人的靴子、嫁个黑人，也不肯靠一年一百镑体体面面过日子！而那对咱们就算财富了！她们自己知道。她们知道咱们知道。……没有人再信她们。没有人再信高贵。没有人再信王权。没有人再信法律里有公道。……可是人有惯性，人沿着旧辙走下去，只要有工做，只要有礼拜工资。……这长不了，基普斯。」
他咳了一声，停住。「等着荒年吧，」他喊。「等着荒年吧。」突然他同咳嗽扭打起来，咳出一口血。「没事。」他对基普斯那声惊骇的抽气说。
他接着讲，咳嗽的抗议同话语交织，希德在一种痛楚的倾慕里红光满面。
「瞧瞧他们把人生弄成的这场骗局，瞧瞧对青年人希望的可怜嘲弄。*我*得到过什么？十三岁上，我发现自己像一只兔子被塞进氯仿箱似的被塞进了工厂。十三岁！——而*他们*的孩子还是婴孩。

===Original===

But even a child of that age could see what it meant, that Hell of a factory! Monotony and toil and contempt and dishonour! And then death. So I fought﻿—at thirteen!”
Minton’s “crawling up a drain pipe until you die” echoed in Kipps’ mind, but Masterman, instead of Minton’s growl, spoke in a high, indignant tenor.
“I got out at last﻿—somehow,” he said, quietly, suddenly plumping back in his chair. He went on after a pause. “For a bit. Some of us get out by luck, some by cunning, and crawl on to the grass, exhausted and crippled to die. That’s a poor man’s success, Kipps. Most of us don’t get out at all. I worked all day and studied half the night, and here I am with the common consequences. Beaten! And never once have I had a fair chance, never once!” His lean, clenched fist flew out in a gust of tremulous anger. “These Skunks shut up all the university scholarships at nineteen for fear of men like me. And then﻿—do *nothin’*.﻿ ﻿… We’re wasted for nothing. By the time I’d learnt something the doors were locked. I thought knowledge would do it﻿—I did think that! I’ve fought for knowledge as other men fight for bread. I’ve starved for knowledge. I’ve turned my back on women; I’ve done even that. I’ve burst my accursed lung.﻿ ﻿…” His voice rose with impotent anger. “I’m a better man than any ten princes alive! And I’m beaten and wasted. I’ve been crushed, trampled and defiled by a drove of hogs. I’m no use to myself or the world. I’ve thrown my life away to make myself too good for use in this huckster’s scramble. If I had gone in for business, if I had gone in for plotting to cheat my fellow men﻿—ah, well! It’s too late. It’s too late for that, anyhow. It’s too late for anything now! And I couldn’t have done it.﻿ ﻿… And over in New York now there’s a pet of society making a corner in wheat!
“By God!” he cried hoarsely, with a clutch of the lean hand. “By God! If I had his throat! Even now I might do something for the world.”
He glared at Kipps, his face flushed deep, his sunken eyes glowing with passion, and then suddenly he changed altogether.
There was a sound of tea things rattling upon a tray outside the door, and Sid rose to open it.
“All of which amounts to this,” said Masterman, suddenly quiet and again talking against time. “The world is out of joint, and there isn’t a soul alive who isn’t half waste or more.

===Chinese===

可就连那么大的孩子也看得出那是什么意思，那座工厂地狱！单调，苦役，轻蔑，耻辱！然后是死。所以我反抗了——十三岁上！」
明顿那句「顺着排水管往上爬，一直爬到死」在基普斯心里回响；可马斯特曼用的不是明顿那种低吼，而是高亢激愤的男高音。
「我到底出来了——不知怎么出来的。」他说，声音忽然低下来，一屁股坐回椅子里。顿了一顿他又讲。「出来了一阵。我们有的靠运气，有的靠心计，爬到草地上，累垮了，残了，等死。这就是穷人的成功，基普斯。我们大多数人根本出不来。我白天做工，半夜读书，落到如今这一身平常的下场。打败了！而我从来没有得过一次公道的机会，一次也没有！」他瘦削的拳头颤巍巍地挥出去，一阵怒。「这帮臭鼬把十九岁以上的大学奖学金全关了，就防着我这种人。然后呢——*无所作为*。……我们白白地被糟蹋。等我学出点东西，门全锁上了。我原以为知识办得到——我真那样以为！我为知识拼过命，像别人为面包拼命一样。我为知识挨过饿。我把女人背过身去；连这个我都做了。我把这该死的肺拼破了。……”他的嗓音带着无能为力的怒气往上冒。「我比随便哪十个活着的王子加起来都强！可我被打败了，被糟蹋了。我被一群猪撞倒、践踏、糟蹋。我于自己、于世界都没用了。我把自己的一生扔了，把自己修炼得在这场奸商的抢夺里好得没了用处。我要是早进了生意场，早学了算计坑害同胞——啊，唉！太迟了。反正那也迟了。如今什么都迟了！再说我也干不来。……这会儿，纽约那边，社交场的一只宠儿正在囤积小麦！
「天哪！」他哑着嗓子喊，瘦手一攥。「天哪！我要是有他的喉咙！哪怕现在，我兴许还能给世界做点什么。」
他瞪着基普斯，脸涨得通红，深陷的眼睛烧着火，随后忽然整个人变了。
门外响起茶具在托盘上叮当的声音，希德起身去开门。
「这一切归结起来就是，」马斯特曼说，忽然安静下来，又开始抢着时间说话，「世界脱了榫，活人没有一个不是一半以上被浪费。

===Original===

You’ll find it the same with you in the end, wherever your luck may take you.﻿ ﻿… I suppose you won’t mind my having another cigarette?”
He took Kipps’ cigarette with a hand that trembled so violently it almost missed its object, and stood up, with something of guilt in his manner as Mrs. Sid came into the room.
Her eye met his and marked the flush upon his face.
“Been talking Socialism?” said Mrs. Sid, a little severely.
Six o’clock that day found Kipps drifting eastward along the southward margin of Rotten Row. You figure him a small, respectably attired figure going slowly through a sometimes immensely difficult and always immense world. At times he becomes pensive and whistles softly. At times he looks about him. There are a few riders in the Row, a carriage flashes by every now and then along the roadway, and among the great rhododendrons and laurels and upon the greensward there are a few groups and isolated people dressed in the style Kipps adopted to call upon the Walshinghams when first he was engaged. Amid the complicated confusion of Kipps’ mind was a regret that he had not worn his other things.﻿ ﻿…
Presently he perceived that he would like to sit down; a green chair tempted him. He hesitated at it, took possession of it, and leant back and crossed one leg over the other.
He rubbed his under lip with his umbrella handle and reflected upon Masterman and his denunciation of the world.
“Bit orf ’is ’ead, poor chap,” said Kipps, and added: “I wonder.”
He thought intently for a space.
“I wonder what he meant by the lean years?”
The world seemed a very solid and prosperous concern just here, and well out of reach of Masterman’s dying clutch. And yet﻿—
It was curious he should have been reminded of Minton.
His mind turned to a far more important matter. Just at the end Sid had said to him, “Seen Ann?” and as he was about to answer, “You’ll see a bit more of her now. She’s got a place in Folkestone.”
It had brought him back from any concern about the world being out of joint or anything of that sort.
Ann!
One might run against her any day.
He tugged at his little moustache.
He would like to run against Ann very much.﻿ ﻿…
“And it would be juiced awkward if I did!”
In Folkestone! It was a jolly sight too close.﻿ ﻿…

不管你的运气把你带向哪儿，你到头来也会发现一样。……再抽你一支烟，不介意吧？」
他拿基普斯烟的手抖得厉害，差点拿空；他站起身，希德太太进来时，他神色里带着点罪过。
她的眼睛同他一对，看见了他脸上的红。
「又谈社会主义了？」希德太太说，口气有点严厉。
那天下午六点钟，人们发现基普斯顺着罗滕罗骑道的南侧栏杆往东漂。你不妨想象他：一个小小的、衣着体面的身影，缓缓走过一个时而难得出奇、而始终浩大无边的世界。他时时出神，轻轻吹着口哨。他时时四下张望。骑行道上人不多，车道上时时有一辆马车闪过；高大的杜鹃花和月桂树间、草地上，三三两两坐着或立着一些人，衣着的做派，正是基普斯当初订婚头一回拜望沃尔辛厄姆家时采用的那一路。基普斯纷乱的心绪里，夹着一层懊悔：没穿另外那身衣服。……
不多时他觉出想坐下了；一把绿椅子诱惑着他。他对它迟疑了一下，占有了它，向后一靠，跷起了二郎腿。
他拿伞柄蹭着下嘴唇，回味马斯特曼和他对世界的痛骂。
「脑子有点——不正常，可怜的家伙。」基普斯说，又补一句：「也难说。」
他出了好一阵神。
「不知道他说的荒年是什么意思？」
世界在这一带显得十分殷实兴旺，远在马斯特曼那只垂死的手够不着的地方。然而——
怪的是，偏偏叫他想起明顿。
他的心思转到了一桩要紧得多的事上。临分手时希德对他说：「见着安没有？」他刚要答，希德又说：「往后你会常见她的。她在福克斯通有了个位子。」
这一句把他对世界脱不脱榫一类的挂虑全打回去了。
安！
说不定哪天就撞上。
他捻着自己的小胡子。
他倒是极想撞上安。……
「可真撞上了就他妈的尴尬了！」
在福克斯通！这可太近了点。……

===Original===

Then, at the thought that he might run against Ann in his beautiful evening dress on the way to the band, he fluttered into a momentary dream, that jumped abruptly into a nightmare.
Suppose he met her when he was out with Helen! “Oh, Lor’!” said Kipps. Life had developed a new complication that would go on and go on. For some time he wished with the utmost fervour that he had not kissed Ann, that he had not gone to New Romney the second time. He marvelled at his amazing forgetfulness of Helen on that occasion. Helen took possession of his mind. He would have to write to Helen, an easy, offhand letter, to say that he had come to London for a day or so. He tried to imagine her reading it. He would write just such another letter to the old people, and say he had had to come up on business. That might do for *them* all right, but Helen was different. She would insist on explanations.
He wished he could never go back to Folkestone again. That would settle the whole affair.
A passing group attracted his attention, two faultlessly dressed gentlemen and a radiantly expensive lady. They were talking, no doubt, very brilliantly. His eyes followed them. The lady tapped the arm of the left hand gentleman with a daintily tinted glove. Swells! No end.﻿ ﻿…
His soul looked out upon life in general as a very small nestling might peep out of its nest. What an extraordinary thing life was, to be sure, and what a remarkable variety of people there were in it!
He lit a cigarette and speculated upon that receding group of three, and blew smoke and watched them. They seemed to do it all right. Probably they all had incomes of very much over twelve hundred a year. Perhaps not. Probably none of them suspected, as they went past, that he, too, was a gentleman of independent means, dressed, as he was, without distinction. Of course things were easier for them. They were brought up always to dress well and do the right thing from their very earliest years; they started clear of all his perplexities; they had never got mixed up with all sorts of different people who didn’t go together. If, for example, that lady there got engaged to that gentleman, she would be quite safe from any encounter from a corpulent, osculatory Uncle, or Chitterlow, or the dangerously insignificant eye of Pierce.
His thoughts came round to Helen.

===Chinese===

随后，想到自己说不定穿着一身漂亮的晚礼服、在去听音乐会的路上撞见安，他飘进一场短暂的绮梦，又猛地跌进噩梦。
要是他同海伦一起出去时撞见她呢！「哦，老天！」基普斯说。生活生出了一个新的、要没完没了纠缠下去的疙瘯。有好一阵，他极其痛切地希望自己没吻过安，没去成第二趟新罗姆尼。他惊异自己那天竟把海伦忘得那么干净。海伦占据了他的脑子。他得给海伦写信，一封轻松随便的信，说自己来伦敦待一两天。他试着想象她读信的样子。他还得给两位老人写一封一样的，说有事上伦敦来了。给他们那么写也许过得去，海伦可不一样。她会逼着要解释。
他巴不得从此不回福克斯通。那倒干净了。
一群走过的人吸引了他：两位穿得毫无瑕疵的绅士，一位华贵得晃眼的太太。他们谈着话，无疑，谈得极精彩。他的目光跟着他们。那位太太用一只染色雅致的手套，点点左手那位绅士的胳膊。真阔！阔透了。……
他的灵魂朝整个人生探出头去，像一只小小雏鸟从窝里往外张望。人生真是桩奇事，可不是，人生里的人真是五花八门！
他点上一支烟，揣摩那渐渐远去的三个人，喷着烟，望着他们。他们瞧着都应付裕如。多半他们的进项都远远不止一年一千二百镑。也许不是。他们走过去时，多半谁也没疑心，他也是一位经济独立的绅士，只是穿得不起眼。当然，人家事事都容易些。人家从最小就穿得好、做得对，起跑就清清爽爽，没有他这堆困惑；人家从来没同各式各样凑不到一块儿的人搅和到一起过。比方说，那边那位太太若同那位绅士订婚，她保险不会撞上一个肥胖的、见人就亲的叔父，撞不上奇特洛，也挨不上皮尔斯那双危险的不起眼的小眼睛。
他的念头又转回海伦。
