「哦，该死！谁在乎？」基普斯说，用气得发抖的手指抓起一块大得反常的糖添上，把杯子往凹室柜上重重一放，重得过了分。谁在乎？
「就是二十镑，」他同既成事实稳步较着劲，「这事我也不愿意它出。」
他阴沉着，足足沉默了一分来钟。随后安说了那句引爆他的致命的话。「阿蒂！」她说。
「什么？」
「那边有黄油——茶饼！在你脚边！」一停，夫妻对望。
「黄油茶饼！」他说。「你把拜客的事搅得一团糟，回头又拿黄油茶饼来堵我的嘴！还黄油茶饼！咱们头一回有机会认识像样点的、配来往的人——。这么着，安！告诉你——你非去回这个拜不可。」
「回这个拜！」
「对，你非回不可。你就得这么办！我知道——」他朝凹室里那堆杂七杂八的书虚虚一挥。「《礼俗与上流社会规范》里有。你得弄清该留几张名片，然后去留下。明白？」
安的脸上现出恐惧。「可是，阿蒂，我*怎么*能？」
「你怎么能？你怎么能？反正你得去。她们认不出你——戴上你邦德街那顶帽子就认不出！真认出来，她们也不会说什么。」
他的嗓音带上了恳求。「你必须，安。」
「我不行。」
「你必须。」
「我不行，也不干。合情合理的事我都依你，可再叫我见那家人的面，我不行——出了这样的事以后。」
「你不干？」
「不！」……
「那就吹了——吹了！咱们再也见不着他们！就这么下去！就这么下去！咱们谁也不认识，往后*也*不会认识谁！你连一点小委屈也不肯受，也不肯费心学学事情该怎么办。」
可怕的停顿。
「我真不该嫁给你，阿蒂，这是实话。」
「哦！*别*翻那个。」

===Original===

“I never ought to ’ave merried you, Artie. I’m not equal to the position. If you ’adn’t said you’d drown yourself﻿—” She choked.
“I don’ see why you shouldn’t *try*, Ann. *I’ve* improved. Why don’t you? ’Stead of which you go sending out the servant and ’namelling floors, and then when visitors come﻿—”
“ ’Ow was *I* to know about y’r old visitors?” cried Ann in a wail, and suddenly got up and fled from amidst their ruined tea, the tea of which “toce, all buttery,” was to be the crown and glory.
Kipps watched her with a momentary consternation. Then he hardened his heart. “Ought to ’ave known better,” he said, “goin’ on like that!” He remained for a space rubbing his knees and muttering. He emitted scornfully: “I carn’t an’ I won’t.” He saw her as the source of all his shames.
Presently, quite mechanically, he stooped down and lifted the flowery china cover. “Ter dash ’er Buttud Toce!” he shouted at the sight of it, and clapped the cover down again hard.﻿ ﻿…
When Gwendolen came back she perceived things were in a slightly unusual poise. Kipps sat by the fire in a rigid attitude reading a casually selected volume of the *Encyclopaedia Britannica*, and Ann was upstairs and inaccessible﻿—to reappear at a later stage with reddened eyes. Before the fire and still in a perfectly assimilable condition was what was evidently an untouched supply of richly buttered toast under a cracked cover.
“They’ve ’ad a bit of a tiff,” said Gwendolen, attending to her duties in the kitchen, with her outdoor hat still on and her mouth full. “They’re rummuns﻿—if ever! My eye!”
And she took another piece of Ann’s generously buttered toast.
The Kippses spoke no more that day to one another.
The squabble about cards and buttered toast was as serious to them as the most rational of differences. It was all rational to them. Their sense of wrong burnt within them; their sense of what was owing to themselves, the duty of implacability, the obstinacy of pride. In the small hours Kipps lay awake at the nadir of unhappiness and came near groaning. He saw life as an extraordinarily desolating muddle; his futile house, his social discredit, his bad behaviour to Helen, his low marriage to Ann.﻿ ﻿…
He became aware of something irregular in Ann’s breathing.﻿ ﻿…
He listened. She was awake and quietly and privately sobbing!
He hardened his heart; resolutely he hardened his heart.

===Chinese===

「我真不该嫁给你，阿蒂。我配不上这个身份。要不是你说你要投河——」她哽住了。
「我不明白你为什么就不能*试试*，安。*我*就长了进。你怎么不？你倒好，打发女仆出门，自个儿搪地板，客人一来——」
「我哪知道你那套宝贝客人！」安哭喊出声，忽然从他们那顿毁了茶的茶桌边站起来，跑了——那顿茶，「抹黄油，多多的黄油」，本该是顶点的荣光。
基普斯看她跑走，一时惊慌。随后他把心一横。「真该懂点事，」他说，「闹成这样！」他搓着膝盖坐了一阵，喃喃着。他轻蔑地迸出一句：「我不行，我不干。」他把她看作他一切羞辱的源头。
过了一会儿，他全然机械地弯下腰，掀开花瓷罩。「去她的黄油茶饼！」他一见那东西就喊，又狠狠把罩子扣上。……
格温多林回来时，察觉家里气氛略有异样。基普斯直挺挺坐在炉边，读着一册随手抽出的《不列颠百科全书》；安在楼上，够不着——晚些时候才红着眼睛再露面。炉子前，罩在一只有裂纹的罩子底下的，是一份显然原封未动的、黄油抹得厚厚的吐司。
「他们俩拌了两句嘴。」格温多林在厨房里一边干活一边说，头上还戴着出门的帽子，嘴里塞得满满的。「真是怪人——少见！我的天！」
她又拿了一块安那抹得慷慨的黄油吐司。
基普斯夫妇那天再没说话。
这场关于名片和黄油吐司的口角，在他们看，同最理性不过的分歧一样严重。在他们看，全都理性。委屈感在他们心里烧；自尊被亏欠之感、绝不屈服的义务、骄傲的固执。后半夜，基普斯躺在不幸的谷底，醒着，几乎要呻吟出声。他看人生是一团异常荒凉的乱麻：他那座没用的宅子，他的社交失势，他对海伦的恶劣行径，他同安的低就婚姻。……
他察觉安的呼吸有点不对劲。……
他侧耳听。她醒着，在悄悄地、独自抽泣！
他把心一横；坚决地把心一横。

===Original===

The stupid little tragedies of these clipped and limited lives!
What is the good of keeping up the idyllic sham and pretending that ill-educated, misdirected people “get along very well,” and that all this is harmlessly funny and nothing more? You think I’m going to write fat, silly, grinning novels about half-educated, under-trained people and keep it up all the time, that the whole thing’s nothing but funny!
As I think of them lying unhappily there in the darkness, my vision pierces the night. See what I can see! Above them, brooding over them, I tell you there is a monster, a lumpish monster, like some great, clumsy griffin thing, like the Crystal Palace labyrinthodon, like Coote, like the leaden goddess Dullness Pope Abhorred, like some fat, proud flunkey, like pride, like indolence, like all that is darkening and heavy and obstructive in life. It is matter and darkness, it is the anti-soul, Stupidity. My Kippses live in its shadow. Shalford and his apprenticeship system, the Hastings Academy, the ideas of Coote, the ideas of the old Kippses, all the ideas that have made Kipps what he is, all these are its shadow. But for that monster they might not be groping among false ideas and hurt one another so sorely and so stupidly; but for that, the glowing promise of childhood and youth might have had a happier fruition, thought might have awakened in them to meet *the* thought of the world, the quickening sunshine of literature pierced to the substance of their souls, their lives might not have been divorced, as now they are divorced forever, from the apprehension of beauty that we favoured ones are given﻿—the vision of the Grail that makes life fine forever. I have laughed, and I laugh at these two people; I have sought to make you laugh.﻿ ﻿…
But I see through the darkness the souls of my Kippses, as they are, as little pink strips of living stuff, like the bodies of little, ill-nourished, ailing, ignorant children, children who feel pain, who are naughty and muddled and suffer and do not understand why. And the claw of this Beast rests upon them!

===Chinese===

这些被修剪、被局限的小小人生里，那些愚蠢的小悲剧！
把田园诗的假象撑下去，假装没受过教育、被引错路的人们「过得很好」，假装这一切无非无害的笑料，有什么好处？你以为我要写那种肥头大耳、咧嘴傻笑的小说，专写半吊子教育、欠训练的人们，从头笑到尾，把这一切写成纯乎其纯的笑话！
我一想到他们两个不幸福地躺在黑暗里，我的目光就穿透了黑夜。瞧我能看见什么！在他们上方，笼罩着他们，我告诉你，有一个怪物，一团笨重的怪物，像某种硕大而笨拙的狮鹫，像水晶宫里的大蝾螈，像库特，像教皇所痛斥的铅灰女神「愚钝」，像某个肥头大耳、趾高气扬的仆役，像骄傲，像惰怠，像人生里一切遮天蔽日、沉重滞塞的东西。它是物质与黑暗，它是反灵魂：愚蠢。我的基普斯夫妇就活在它的影子里。沙尔福德和他的学徒制，黑斯廷斯学堂，库特的一套观念，老基普斯夫妇的一套观念，一切把基普斯造成今日之基普斯的观念，全是它的影子。若不是那怪物，他们本不至于在谬误里摸索、彼此伤得那么重、那么蠢；若不是它，童年少年那份发亮的允诺本可以结更甜的果，思想本可以在他们身上醒来，去会一会*世界*的思想，文学的催芽的阳光本可以照进他们灵魂的实质，他们的生命本不至于同美的领悟割裂——如今是永远割裂了——而我们这些受优待的人是被给了那份领悟的：那份使人生永远高贵的圣杯之视。我笑过，也还在笑这两个人；我竭力叫你们笑。……
可我透过黑暗看见了我的基普斯夫妇的灵魂，看见它们本来的样子：两小条粉红的、活着的肉，像一个个小小的、营养不良的、多病的、无知的孩子，会疼，会胡闹，会糊涂，会受苦，不懂为什么。那头巨兽的爪子，正搭在他们身上！

## II The Emporium / 二 商场
===Original===

When Kipps left New Romney, with a small yellow tin box, a still smaller portmanteau, a new umbrella, and a keepsake half-sixpence, to become a draper, he was a youngster of fourteen, thin, with whimsical drakes’ tails at the poll of his head, smallish features, and eyes that were sometimes very light and sometimes very dark, gifts those of his birth; and by the nature of his training he was indistinct in his speech, confused in his mind, and retreating in his manners. Inexorable fate had appointed him to serve his country in commerce, and the same national bias towards private enterprise and leaving bad alone, which entrusted his general education to Mr. Woodrow, now indentured him firmly into the hands of Mr. Shalford, of the Folkestone Drapery Bazaar. Apprenticeship is still the recognised English way to the distributing branch of the social service. If Mr. Kipps had been so unfortunate as to have been born a German he might have been educated in an elaborate and costly special school (“over-educated﻿—crammed up”﻿—Old Kipps) to fit him for his end﻿—such being their pedagogic way. He might.﻿ ﻿… But why make unpatriotic reflections in a novel? There was nothing pedagogic about Mr. Shalford.
He was an irascible, energetic little man, with hairy hands, for the most part under his coat tails, a long, shiny, bald head, a pointed, aquiline nose a little askew, and a neatly trimmed beard. He walked lightly and with a confident jerk, and he was given to humming. He had added to exceptional business “push,” bankruptcy under the old dispensation, and judicious matrimony. His establishment was now one of the most considerable in Folkestone, and he insisted on every inch of frontage by alternate stripes of green and yellow down the houses over the shops. His shops were numbered 3, 5 and 7 on the street, and on his billheads 3 to 7. He encountered the abashed and awestricken Kipps with the praises of his system and himself. He spread himself out behind his desk with a grip on the lapel of his coat and made Kipps a sort of speech. “We expect y’r to work, y’r know, and we expect y’r to study our interests,” explained Mr. Shalford in the regal and commercial plural. “Our system here is the best system y’r could have. I made it, and I ought to know. I began at the very bottom of the ladder when I was fourteen, and there isn’t a step in it I don’t know. Not a step. Mr.

===Chinese===

基普斯离开新罗姆尼去当绸布伙计的时候，带着一只黄色小铁箱、一只更小的旅行包、一把新伞和半块六便士的纪念物，年方十四，瘦瘦的，头顶翘着几绺淘气的「鸭尾巴」，五官小小的，一双眼睛有时极浅、有时极深——那是娘胎里带来的禀赋；而由于他所受训练的天性，他口齿含混，头脑糊涂，举止退缩。铁面无私的命运指派他在商业里为国效力；也正是这股偏向私人企业、放任弊病不管的国民风气——先前把他的一般教育托付给了伍德罗先生——如今又一纸契约把他牢牢交到福克斯通绸布市场沙尔福德（Shalford）先生手里。学徒制至今仍是英国通往社会服务业销售分支的公认途径。基普斯先生若不幸生为德国人，多半会被送进一所精心而昂贵的专门学校（「念过头了——填鸭」——老基普斯语），把他造就得称职——那是他们教育学的一路。兴许吧。……可干吗在小说里发不爱国的牢骚？沙尔福德先生身上没有一丝教育学。
他是位火性子、精力旺盛的小个子男人，两手多毛，多半收在燕尾底下，一颗油光光的长秃头，一只稍稍歪斜的鹰钩尖鼻，胡子修得整齐。他走路轻快，带着自信的一颠一颠，爱哼小曲。他在出众的生意「闯劲」之外，还添了旧规矩下的破产史，和一门精明的亲事。他的铺面如今是福克斯通数一数二的，他还用绿黄相间的条纹顺着店面上方的墙面一直刷下去，把每一寸门面都占满。他的铺子在街上是三号、五号、七号，在信笺头上是「三至七号」。他以他的制度和他自己的颂词，迎接了那又窘又怯的基普斯。他在写字台后舒展开来，一手抓住自己的衣领，对基普斯发表了一篇小小的演说。「咱们指望你干活，你懂吧，也指望你替咱们的利益着想，」沙尔福德先生用帝王的兼商业的复数解释道。「咱们这儿的制度是顶好的制度，你能摊上的最好的。我立的它，我说了算。我十四岁从梯子最底下起家，梯子上没有一级我不知道的。一级也没有。写字台里的布奇先生会给你规矩和罚款的单子。稍等片刻。」

===Original===

Booch in the desk will give y’r the card of rules and fines. Jest wait a minute.” He pretended to be busy with some dusty memoranda under a paperweight, while Kipps stood in a sort of paralysis of awe regarding his new master’s oval baldness. “Two thous’n three forty-seven pounds,” whispered Mr. Shalford audibly, feigning forgetfulness of Kipps. Clearly a place of great transactions!
Mr. Shalford rose, and handing Kipps a blotting-pad and an inkpot to carry﻿—mere symbols of servitude, for he made no use of them﻿—emerged into a countinghouse where three clerks had been feverishly busy ever since his door handle had turned. “Booch,” said Mr. Shalford, “ ’ave y’r copy of the rules?” and a downtrodden, shabby little old man with a ruler in one hand and a quill pen in his mouth, silently held out a small book with green and yellow covers, mainly devoted, as Kipps presently discovered, to a voracious system of fines. He became acutely aware that his hands were full, and that everybody was staring at him. He hesitated a moment before putting the inkpot down to free a hand.
“Mustn’t fumble like *that*,” said Mr. Shalford as Kipps pocketed the rules. “Won’t do here. Come along, come along,” and he cocked his coat tails high, as a lady might hold up her dress, and led the way into the shop.
A vast interminable place it seemed to Kipps, with unending shining counters and innumerable faultlessly dressed young men and presently Houri-like young women staring at him. Here there was a long vista of gloves dangling from overhead rods, there ribbons and baby-linen. A short young lady in black mittens was making out the account of a customer, and was clearly confused in her addition by Shalford’s eagle eye.
A thickset young man with a bald head and a round, very wise face, who was profoundly absorbed in adjusting all the empty chairs down the counter to absolutely equal distances, awoke out of his preoccupation and answered respectfully to a few Napoleonic and quite unnecessary remarks from his employer. Kipps was told that this young man’s name was Mr. Buggins, and that he was to do whatever Mr. Buggins told him to do.
They came round a corner into a new smell, which was destined to be the smell of Kipps’ life for many years, the vague, distinctive smell of Manchester goods.

===Chinese===

他装模作样地翻一份镇纸底下落灰的备忘录，基普斯僵在敬畏里，端详着新东家那颗椭圆形的秃头。「两千三百四十七镑，」沙尔福德先生大声耳语，装作把基普斯忘了。好一个做大买卖的地方，不言自明！
沙尔福德先生站起身，把吸墨纸架和墨水瓶递给基普斯端着——纯粹是奴役的象征，因为他根本用不着——走进一间账房，三个打他拧门把手起就在发狂忙碌的文书。「布奇，」沙尔福德先生说，「规矩的抄本你拿着没？」一个受尽欺压、寒酸的小老头，一手拿尺，嘴里叿一支鹅毛笔，无声地递出一本绿黄封皮的小书——基普斯随后发现，那书主要是一部贪得无厌的罚款制度。他猛然觉察自己两手都占着，人人都在盯着他。他把墨水瓶放下来腾出一只手之前，迟疑了一下。
「不许这样*抓瞎*，」基普斯把规矩揣进口袋时沙尔福德先生说。「在这儿行不通。来吧，来吧，」他把燕尾高高掠起，像太太提起裙摆，领头进了店堂。
在基普斯眼里，那是一个没边没沿的大地方：亮闪闪的柜台望不到头，数不清的、穿戴无懈可击的年轻男人，还有那不久便现身的、天仙似的年轻女人，都在看他。这边是一长溜从头顶横杆上垂下来的手套，那边是缎带和婴儿衣。一位戴黑露指手套的矮个子年轻姑娘正给一位顾客开账单，被沙尔福德那只鹰眼盯得加法连连出错。
一个矮胖结实、秃头、圆脸、一脸精明的年轻人，正专心致志把柜台边一排空椅子调成绝对相等的间距，闻声从专心里醒过来，对东家几句拿破仑式的、全无必要的指示恭敬应答。基普斯得知这位年轻人叫巴金斯（Buggins）先生，巴金斯先生叫他做什么他就得做什么。
他们拐过一个街角，撞进一种新的气味——日后多年要做基普斯生命之气味的、曼彻斯特货那种说不清的独特气味。

===Original===

A fat man with a large nose jumped﻿—actually jumped﻿—at their appearance, and began to fold a pattern of damask in front of him exactly like an automaton that is suddenly set going.
“Carshot, see to this boy tomorrow,” said the master. “See he don’t fumble. Smart’n ’im up.”
“Yussir,” said Carshot fatly, glanced at Kipps, and resumed his pattern-folding with extreme zeal.
“Whatever Mr. Carshot says y’r to do, ye *do*,” said Mr. Shalford, trotting onward; and Carshot blew out his face with an appearance of relief.
They crossed a large room full of the strangest things Kipps had ever seen. Ladylike figures, surmounted by black wooden knobs in the place of the refined heads one might have reasonably expected, stood about with a lifelike air of conscious fashion.
“Costume room,” said Shalford.
Two voices engaged in some sort of argument﻿—“I can assure you, Miss Mergle, you are entirely mistaken﻿—entirely, in supposing I should do anything so unwomanly,”﻿—sank abruptly, and they discovered two young ladies, taller and fairer than any of the other young ladies, and with black trains to their dresses, who were engaged in writing at a little table. Whatever they told him to do, Kipps gathered he was to do. He was also, he understood, to do whatever Carshot and Booch told him to do. And there were also Buggins and Mr. Shalford. And not to forget or fumble!
They descended into a cellar called “The Warehouse,” and Kipps had an optical illusion of errand boys fighting. Some aerial voice said, “Teddy!” and the illusion passed. He looked again, and saw quite clearly that they were packing parcels and always would be, and that the last thing in the world that they would or could possibly do was to fight. Yet he gathered from the remarks Mr. Shalford addressed to their busy backs that they had been fighting﻿—no doubt at some past period of their lives.
Emerging in the shop again among a litter of toys and what are called “fancy articles,” Shalford withdrew a hand from beneath his coat tails to indicate an overhead change-carrier. He entered into elaborate calculations to show how many minutes in one year were saved thereby, and lost himself among the figures. “Seven tums eight seven nine﻿—was it? Or seven eight nine? Now, *now*! Why, when I was a boy your age I c’d do a sum like that as soon as hear it. We’ll soon get y’r into better shape than that. Make you Fishent.

一个鼻子肥大的胖子见他们来了，一跳——真的一跳——起来，像忽然上足了发条的自动人偶，当着他们的面折叠一方锦缎样品。
「卡肖特（Carshot），明天照应这孩子，」东家说。「看着他别抓瞎。把他拾掇利索。」
「是的，先生。」卡肖特油腔油调地说，瞟了基普斯一眼，以极度热情恢复了折样。
「卡肖特先生叫你干什么，你*就*干什么。」沙尔福德先生小跑着往前说；卡肖特松了口气，把脸吹得鼓起来。
他们穿过一间堆满基普斯生平仅见的奇物的屋子。淑女身形，头顶该长那颗文雅脑袋的地方顶着一颗黑木疙瘯，姿态入时、栩栩然地各处站着。
「服装间。」沙尔福德说。
两个正吵着什么的声音——「我向您保证，默格尔小姐，您全弄错了——全弄错了，居然以为我会做那种不像女人的事」——骤然低下去，他们发现两位年轻姑娘，比别的姑娘都更高更白，裙裾曳地，正伏在一张小桌上写字。她们叫他做什么，基普斯领会，他就得做什么。他还领会，卡肖特和布奇叫他做什么他也得做。此外还有巴金斯，还有沙尔福德先生。还有：不许忘事，不许抓瞎！
他们下到一个叫「货栈」的地窖，基普斯眼前出了一场送货车夫们打架的幻象。半空里一个声音喊：「泰迪！」幻象过去了。他再看，看得很清楚：他们在打包，而且永远只会打包，世上他们最不可能、最没本事做的事就是打架。可他从沙尔福德先生对着他们忙碌的脊背发表的评论里听出来，他们打过架——多半在他们一生的某个往昔。
重新上到店堂，置身于玩具和所谓「花哨零碎」的一堆里，沙尔福德从燕尾底下抽出一只手，指了指头顶的找零传送器。他进行了一番精心的计算，说明它一年省下多少分钟，算着算着把自己算丢了。「七乘八七九——是吗？还是七八九？瞧，瞧！我像你这么大的时候，这种题耳朵一听手就下来了。咱们很快就能把你练出个样子。把你练得高—效。」

===Original===

Well, y’r must take my word, it comes to pounds and pounds saved in the year﻿—pounds and pounds. System! System everywhere. Fishency.” He went on murmuring “Fishency” and “System” at intervals for some time.
They passed into a yard, and Mr. Shalford waved his hand to his three delivery vans all striped green and yellow﻿—“uniform﻿—green, yell’r﻿—System.” All over the premises were pinned absurd little cards. “This door locked after 7:30.﻿—By order, Edwin Shalford,” and the like.
Mr. Shalford always wrote “By order,” though it conveyed no earthly meaning to him. He was one of those people who collect technicalities upon them as the Reduvius bug collects dirt. He was the sort of man who is not only ignorant, but absolutely incapable of English. When he wanted to say he had a sixpenny-ha’penny longcloth to sell, he put it thus to startled customers: “Can D.O. you one, six half if y’ like.” He always omitted pronouns and articles and so forth; it seemed to him the very essence of the efficiently businesslike. His only preposition was “as” or the compound “as per.” He abbreviated every word he could; he would have considered himself the laughingstock of Wood Street if he had chanced to spell *socks* in any way but “sox.” But, on the other hand, if he saved words here, he wasted them there: he never acknowledged an order that was not an esteemed favour, nor sent a pattern without begging to submit it. He never stipulated for so many months’ credit, but bought in November “as Jan.” It was not only words he abbreviated in his London communications. In paying his wholesalers his “System” admitted of a constant error in the discount of a penny or twopence, and it “facilitated business,” he alleged, to ignore odd pence in the cheques he wrote. His ledger clerk was so struck with the beauty of this part of the System, that he started a private one on his own account with the stamp box, that never came to Shalford’s knowledge.
This admirable British merchant would glow with a particular pride of intellect when writing his London orders.
“Ah! do y’r think *you*’ll ever be able to write London orders?” he would say with honest pride to Kipps, waiting impatiently long after closing time to take these triumphs of commercial efficiency to post, and so end the interminable day.
Kipps shook his head, anxious for Mr. Shalford to get on.
“Now, here, f’ example, I’ve written﻿—see?

「总之你信我的，一年省下的就是几镑又几镑——几镑又几镑。制度！处处是制度。高效。」他往前走，隔一阵咕哝一句「高效」和「制度」，咕哝了好一阵。
他们走进一个院子，沙尔福德先生朝他三辆绿黄条纹的送货篷车挥挥手——「划一——绿，黄——制度。」整个铺子到处别着可笑的小卡片。「此门七点半后上锁。——埃德温·沙尔福德谨谕」云云。
沙尔福德先生总写「谨谕」，虽然这话对他自己毫无意义。他是那种把行话往身上攒的人，像臭虫往身上攒灰。他是那种不但无知、而且全然不会英语的人。他想说他有一匹六便士半的细布要卖，就对受惊的顾客这样讲：「能 D.O. 你一匹，六便士半，随你。」他永远省掉代词、冠词等等；在他看来，那正是生意效率的精髓。他唯一用的介词是 as，或复合的 as per。能缩写的词一概缩写；倘若他一时手滑把 socks（短袜）拼成了别的拼法，他会觉得自己是伍德街的头号笑柄。可话又说回来，这边省了字，那边他又糟蹋：回函必称「尊惠顾为荣」，寄样必曰「谨呈请鉴」。赊账从不写明几个月，而是十一月进货「按一月」。在伦敦往来信函里缩写的不光是字。付批发商的钱，他的「制度」容许折扣里常驻一两便士的差数；他还说，他开的支票抹去零头便士，「方便生意」。他的账房文书被制度这一部分的妙处迷住了，自己也拿邮票盒私开了一个小制度，从未叫沙尔福德发觉。
这位可敬的英国商人写伦敦订货单的时候，会因智力而泛出一种特别的光。
「哈！*你*这辈子写得来伦敦订货单吗？」他会带着老实的得意对基普斯说——那孩子打了烊还得苦等半天，把这些商业效率的杰作送去邮局，好叫这没完没了的一天到头。
基普斯摇头，巴不得沙尔福德先生赶紧的。
「喏，这儿，比方说，我写了——瞧？

“And then, ‘2 ea. silk net as per patts herewith’: *ea.*, eh?”
“Dunno, sir.”
It was not Mr. Shalford’s way to explain things. “Dear, dear! Pity you couldn’t get some c’mercial education at your school. ’Stid of all this lit’ry stuff. Well, my boy, if y’ don’t ’ussel a bit y’ll never write London orders, *that’s* pretty plain. Jest stick stamps on all those letters, and mind y’r stick ’em right way up, and try and profit a little more by the opportunities your aunt and uncle have provided ye. Can’t say *what*’ll happen t’ye if ye don’t.”
And Kipps, tired, hungry, and belated, set about stamping with vigour and despatch.
“Lick the *envelope*,” said Mr. Shalford, “lick the *envelope*,” as though he grudged the youngster the postage-stamp gum. “It’s the little things mount up,” he would say; and, indeed, that was his philosophy of life﻿—to bustle and save, always to bustle and save. His political creed linked Reform, which meant nothing, with Efficiency which meant a sweated service, and Economy which meant a sweated expenditure, and his conception of a satisfactory municipal life was to “keep down the rates.” Even his religion was to save his soul, and to preach a similar cheeseparing to the world.
The indentures that bound Kipps to Mr. Shalford were antique and complex: they insisted on the latter gentleman’s parental privileges; they forbade Kipps to dice and game; they made him over body and soul to Mr. Shalford for seven long years, the crucial years of his life. In return there were vague stipulations about teaching the whole art and mystery of the trade to him; but as there was no penalty attached to negligence, Mr. Shalford, being a sound, practical business man, considered this a mere rhetorical flourish, and set himself assiduously to get as much out of Kipps and to put as little into him as he could in the seven years of their intercourse.
What he put into Kipps was chiefly bread and margarine, infusions of chicory and tea-dust, colonial meat by contract at threepence a pound, potatoes by the sack, and watered beer. If, however, Kipps chose to buy any supplementary material for growth, Mr. Shalford had the generosity to place his kitchen resources at his disposal free﻿—if the fire chanced to be going.

===Chinese===

——『1 匹 1 寸黑棉松紧带 1/ 奥尔』。那个「奥尔」，我是啥意思，呃？——你知道不？」
基普斯自然是连一星半点概念也没有。
「再有：『2 伊 silk net as per patts herewith（样附）』：「伊」，呃？」
「不知道，先生。」
沙尔福德先生的作风是不解释的。「哎呀呀！可惜你那学堂没给你一点商业教育。光念那些文诌诌的东西。这样，我的孩子，你要不加点劲，一辈子写不来伦敦订货单，这可是明摆的。去给那些信统统贴上邮票，当心贴正了，再想想你姑妈叔叔给你挣下的机会，多沾点光。不然的话，说不上你会落个什么下场。」
于是基普斯——又累又饿又误了时辰——起劲而麻利地贴起邮票来。
「舐*信封*，」沙尔福德先生说，「舐*信封*，」仿佛心疼那孩子蹭了邮票上的胶。「积少成多嘛，」他会说；这也确实是他的人生哲学——奔忙加节省，永远奔忙加节省。他的政治信条把改革（毫无所指）、效率（即压榨职工的待遇）和节约（即克扣的开销）联成一体；他对满意的城市生活的概念，是「压低捐税」。连他的宗教也是拯救自己的灵魂，并向世界布讲同样的抠门。
把基普斯绑给沙尔福德先生的那张师徒契，古旧而繁复：它坚持了后者做家长的种种特权；它禁止基普斯掷骰聚赌；它把他连身带心交割给沙尔福德先生整整七年——他一生最要紧的七年。作为回报，契约含糊地许诺把本行的全部技艺与奥妙教给他；但因疏忽并无罚则，沙尔福德先生既是一位健全务实的生意人，便把这一条看作纯粹的官样点缀，在那七年交往里一门心思地安排：从基普斯身上尽量榨取，往他身上尽量少放。
他放进基普斯体内的，主要是面包和人造黄油，菊苣和茶末的冲剂，合同价三便士一磅的殖民地肉，麻袋装的土豆，和兑了水的啤酒。不过，基普斯若愿意自己掏钱添置任何补助性的、长身体的材料，沙尔福德先生倒也大方：他家厨房的设备可以免费供他使用——只要炉子恰好烧着。

===Original===

He was also allowed to share a bedroom with eight other young Englishmen, and to sleep in a bed which, except in very severe weather, could be made with the help of his overcoat and private underlinen, not to mention newspapers, quite sufficiently warm for any reasonable soul. In addition Kipps was taught the list of fines; and how to tie up parcels; to know where goods were kept in Mr. Shalford’s systematised shop; to hold his hands extended upon the counter and to repeat such phrases as “What can I have the pleasure﻿ ﻿… ?” “No trouble, I ’ssure you,” and the like; to block, fold, and measure materials of all sorts; to lift his hat from his head when he passed Mr. Shalford abroad, and to practise a servile obedience to a large number of people. But he was not, of course, taught the “cost” mark of the goods he sold, nor anything of the method of buying such goods. Nor was his attention directed to the unfamiliar social habits and fashions to which his trade ministered. The use of half the goods he saw sold and was presently to assist in selling he did not understand; materials for hangings, cretonnes, chintzes, and the like, serviettes and all the bright, hard white wear of a well-ordered house, pleasant dress materials, linings, stiffenings﻿—they were to him from first to last no more than things heavy and difficult to handle in bulk, that one folded up, unfolded, cut in lengths, and saw dwindle and pass away out into that mysterious happy world in which the customer dwells. Kipps hurried from piling linen tablecloths, that were collectively as heavy as lead, to eat off oilcloth in a gas-lit dining-room underground; and he dreamt of combing endless blankets beneath his overcoat, spare undershirt, and three newspapers. So he had at least the chance of learning the beginnings of philosophy.
In return for these benefits he worked so that he commonly went to bed exhausted and footsore. His round began at half-past six in the morning, when he would descend unwashed and shirtless, in old clothes and a scarf, and dust boxes and yawn, and take down wrappers and clean the windows until eight. Then in half an hour he would complete his toilet and take an austere breakfast of bread and margarine and what only an Imperial Englishman would admit to be coffee, after which refreshment he ascended to the shop for the labours of the day.

===Chinese===

他还获准同另外八个英国青年合住一间卧室，睡一张床。那张床，除了极冷的天气，靠了他的大衣和私人内衬——还有报纸——总能弄得足够暖和，对任何讲理的灵魂都够了。此外，基普斯学会了罚款单；学会了打包裹；学会了在沙尔福德先生系统化的铺子里认货放在哪里；学会了把两手平伸在柜台上，重复「今天想看点什么……？」「不麻烦，我向您保证」一类的套话；学会了把各样料子打卷、折叠、量码；学会了在外头路过沙尔福德先生时举帽，学会了对一大批人实践奴性的服从。可他自然没人教他所卖货物的「成本」标记，也没人教进货的门道。他的注意力也没人引向他这门行业所伺候的、他不熟悉的那些社交习惯与时尚。他看着卖出、不久又帮着卖的货，一半的用途他不明白：挂帘料、印花装饰布、条格细布之类，餐巾，一座井井有条的宅子里一切发亮的、硬挺的白货，好看的衣料、里子、浆衬——它们从头到尾对他不过是些论堆又沉又难拿的东西，叠上，抖开，裁码，眼看着减少、流走，流进顾客居住的那个神秘的快活世界。基普斯从摞在一起沉得像铅的亚麻桌布边跑开，钻进煤气灯照明的地下饭厅，就着油布吃饭；夜里做梦都在大衣、备用内衣和三份报纸底下梳理数不清的毯子。这样，他至少得到了学哲学开头的机缘。
为回报这些恩惠，他干得常常是拖着筋疲力尽的身子、踩着肿痛的脚上床。他的一天从早晨六点半开始：不洗脸、不穿衬衫，一身旧衣裹条围巾下楼，掸箱子，打呵欠，取下窗罩、擦玻璃，直干到八点。随后用半个钟头完成盥洗，吃一顿清苦的早饭——面包、人造黄油，和只有帝国英国人才肯认作咖啡的东西——茶点已毕，上店堂开始当天的劳作。

===Original===

Commonly these began with a mighty running to and fro with planks and boxes and goods for Carshot, the window-dresser, who, whether he worked well or ill, nagged persistently by reason of a chronic indigestion, until the window was done. Sometimes the costume window had to be dressed, and then Kipps staggered down the whole length of the shop from the costume room with one after another of those ladylike shapes grasped firmly, but shamefully, each about her single ankle of wood. Such days as there was no window-dressing, there was a mighty carrying and lifting of blocks and bales of goods into piles and stacks. After this there were terrible exercises, at first almost despairfully difficult: certain sorts of goods that came in folded had to be rolled upon rollers, and for the most part refused absolutely to be rolled, at any rate by Kipps; and certain other sorts of goods that came from the wholesalers rolled had to be measured and folded, which folding makes young apprentices wish they were dead. All of it, too, quite avoidable trouble, you know, that is not avoided because of the cheapness of the genteeler sorts of labour and the dearness of forethought in the world. And then consignments of new goods had to be marked off and packed into proper parcels; and Carshot packed like conjuring tricks, and Kipps packed like a boy with tastes in some other direction﻿—not ascertained. And always Carshot nagged.
He had a curious formula of appeal to his visceral economy, had Carshot, that the refinement of the times and the earnest entreaties of my friends induce me to render by an anaemic paraphrase.
“My heart and lungs! I never see such a boy,” so I present Carshot’s refrain; and even when he was within a foot or so of the customer’s face the disciplined ear of Kipps would still at times develop a featureless, intercalary murmur into﻿—well, “my heart and lungs!”
There came a blessed interval when Kipps was sent abroad “matching.” This consisted chiefly in supplying unexpected defects in buttons, ribbon, lining, and so forth in the dressmaking department. He was given a written paper of orders with patterns pinned thereto, and discharged into the sunshine and interest of the street. Then, until he thought it wise to return and stand the racket of his delay, he was a free man, clear of all reproach.

===Chinese===

通常，这些劳作以替橱窗师傅卡肖特搬木板、箱子和货的大往返开场。卡肖特无论干得好坏，都因慢性消化不良而唠叨个没完，直到橱窗布好。有时要布服装橱窗，基普斯就得从服装间把那些淑女身形一个又一个抱来，穿过整个店堂——一手牢牢地、羞耻地攥着她那根独一的木头脚踝，一路打晃。没有橱窗活的日子，就有把货包货件搬上垛、码成堆的大搬运。此后是可怕的操练，起初难得叫人绝望：某些折叠来的货得上卷轴打卷，而它们多半死活不肯上卷，至少在基普斯手里；另一些从批发商手里打卷来的货得量码、折叠——这折叠叫小学徒恨不得死了拉倒。而这一切，你们知道，全是可以避免的麻烦，只因世上文雅些的劳力便宜、先见昂贵，便避免不得。再往后，新到的货要核标记、打成像样的包裹；卡肖特打包像变戏法，基普斯打包像一个心思在别处的孩子——在哪儿，无从查考。而卡肖特永远在唠叨。
卡肖特有一套对他五脏庙的古怪祷文，因时代的斯文和朋友们的恳求，我且以贫血的转述呈上。
「我的心和肺！我从没见过这样的孩子，」这就是卡肖特的口头禅；哪怕离顾客的脸只有一尺，基普斯那训练有素的耳朵也照样能从一片含混的、插入性的低语里分辨出——不错，「我的心和肺！」
后来有一段蒙福的日子，基普斯被打发到外面「配色」去了。这活儿主要是给裁缝间补足纽扣、缎带、衬里等等意外的短缺。人家发他一张写着订单、别着料样的纸，把他发射到街上的阳光和热闹里。此后，直到他自己觉得该回去领受耽搁的那顿数落，他就是个自由人，无可指摘。

===Original===

He made remarkable discoveries in topography, as for example that the most convenient way from the establishment of Mr. Adolphus Davis to the establishment of Messrs. Plummer, Roddis & Tyrrel, two of his principal places of call, is not as is generally supposed down the Sandgate Road, but up the Sandgate Road, round by West Terrace, and along the Leas to the lift, watch the lift up and down *twice*, but not longer, because that wouldn’t do, back along the Leas, watch the Harbour for a short time, and then round by the churchyard, and so (hurrying) into Church Street and Rendezvous Street. But on some exceptionally fine days the route lay through Radnor Park to the pond where the little boys sail ships and there are interesting swans.
He would return to find the shop settling down to the business of serving customers. And now he had to stand by to furnish any help that was necessary to the seniors who served, to carry parcels and bills about the shop, to clear away “stuff” after each engagement, to hold up curtains until his arms ached, and what was more difficult than all, to do nothing, and not stare disconcertingly at customers when there was nothing for him to do. He plumbed an abyss of boredom, or stood a mere carcass, with his mind far away, fighting the enemies of the Empire, or steering a dream ship perilously into unknown seas. To be recalled sharply to our higher civilisation by some bustling senior’s “Nar then, Kipps. *Look* alive! Ketch ’old. (My heart and lungs!)”
At half-past seven o’clock﻿—except on late nights﻿—a feverish activity of “straightening up” began, and when the last shutter was up outside, Kipps with the speed of an arrow leaving a bow would start hanging wrappers over the fixtures and over the piles of wares upon the counters, preparatory to a vigorous scattering of wet sawdust and the sweeping out of the shop.
Sometimes people would stay long after the shop was closed﻿—“They don’t mind a bit at Shalford’s,” these ladies used to say﻿—it is always ladies do this sort of thing﻿—and while they loitered it was forbidden to touch a wrapper, or take any measures to conclude the day until the doors closed behind them.
Mr. Kipps would watch these later customers from the shadow of a stack of goods, and death and disfigurement was the least he wished for them.

===Chinese===

他在地理上做出了可观的发现。比如，从他常去的主要站点之一阿道弗斯·戴维斯铺子到普卢默、罗迪斯与蒂勒尔商号，最方便的走法并非众人以为的沿桑德盖特路往下，而是沿桑德盖特路上行，绕西台地，顺利斯草坪走到升降机，看升降机升升降降*两回*，不许更多，多了就不成体统——再沿利斯草坪回来，朝海港张望一小会儿，然后绕教堂墓地，（加快脚步）进教堂街和龙德武街。可赶上特别晴的日子，路线改经拉德纳公园，到那片小男孩放帆船、有好看天鹅的池塘。
他回来时，店堂正安定下来做接待顾客的生意。这时他就得站在一旁，随时给管接待的资深伙计打下手：在店里跑腿送包裹送账单，每一单过后收拾「家伙」，举帘子举到胳膊酸痛，还有比一切都难的：没活的时候待着不动，还不许看顾客看得叫人家发窘。他探测过无聊的深渊，也当过一具行尸，心思远在千里之外，同帝国的敌人厮杀，或者驾一条梦中之船出生入死驶进不知名的海域——却被哪位忙人前辈的一句「我说基普斯。*放*机灵点！接住。（我的心和肺！）」猛地唤回咱们这个高级文明。
七点半钟——不是夜市的日子——「归置」的热火朝天开始了；外面最后一块排板一上，基普斯便离弦之箭一般，往货架上、柜台的货堆上罩罩布，预备大撒湿锯末、把店堂扫出去。
有时人们打了烊还赖着不走——「在沙尔福德家没关系的」，这类太太们常说——干这种事的永远是太太们——她们赖着的时候，不许碰一块罩布，不许采取任何结束这一天的措施，直到门在她们身后关上。
基普斯会从一堆货的影子里瞪着这些晚来的顾客，心里给他们许的愿，最轻也是死和破相。

===Original===

Rarely much later than nine, a supper of bread and cheese and watered beer awaited him upstairs, and, that consumed, the rest of the day was entirely at his disposal for reading, recreation, and the improvement of his mind.﻿ ﻿…
The front door was locked at half-past ten, and the gas in the dormitory extinguished at eleven.
On Sundays he was obliged to go to church once, and commonly he went twice, for there was nothing else to do. He sat in the free seats at the back; he was too shy to sing, and not always clever enough to keep his place in the prayerbook, and he rarely listened to the sermon. But he had developed a sort of idea that going to church had a tendency to alleviate life. His aunt wanted to have him confirmed, but he evaded this ceremony for some years.
In the intervals between services he walked about Folkestone with an air of looking for something. Folkestone was not so interesting on Sundays as on weekdays, because the shops were shut; but on the other hand there was a sort of confusing brilliance along the front of the Leas in the afternoon. Sometimes the apprentice next above him would condescend to go with him; but when the apprentice next but one above him condescended to go with the apprentice next above him, then Kipps, being habited as yet in ready-made clothes without tails, and unsuitable therefore to appear in such company, went alone.
Sometimes he would strike out into the country﻿—still as if looking for something he missed﻿—but the rope of mealtimes haled him home again; and sometimes he would invest the major portion of the weekly allowance of a shilling that old Booch handed out to him, in a sacred concert on the pier. He would sometimes walk up and down the Leas between twenty and thirty times after supper, desiring much the courage to speak to some other person in the multitude similarly employed. Almost invariably he ended his Sunday footsore.
He never read a book; there were none for him to read, and besides, in spite of Mr. Woodrow’s guidance through a cheap and cheaply annotated edition of *The Tempest* (English Literature) he had no taste that way; he never read any newspapers, except occasionally *Tit-Bits* or a ha’penny “comic.” His chief intellectual stimulus was an occasional argey-bargey that sprang up between Carshot and Buggins at dinner.

===Chinese===

很少晚过九点，楼上有面包、干酪和兑水啤酒等着他当晚饭；吃完，这一天的剩余时间便全归他支配，随他读书、消遣、长见识。……
前门十点半上锁，宿舍的煤气灯十一点熄。
礼拜天他必须上一次教堂，通常上两次，因为再没别的可干。他坐在后排的免费座位上；他害羞，不敢唱诗，祷文又常常跟不上趵，布道他难得听。可他形成了一种观念：上教堂有减轻人生的倾向。他姑妈想给他行坚振礼，这仪式让他推了好几年。
礼拜之间，他在福克斯通到处走，一副找什么东西的神气。礼拜天的福克斯通不如平日有意思，因为铺子都关了；可另一方面，下午利斯草坪一带有一种叫人发懵的辉煌。有时，比他高一级的学徒肯屈尊陪他；可比他高两级的高一级学徒屈尊陪高一级的时候，基普斯——身上还是没下摆的现成衣服，不配上那种场面——就独自去。
有时他一头扎进乡间——还是那副丢了什么在找的神气——可饭点的绳索把他拽回家；有时他把老布奇每周发给的一先令零用，大头花在防波堤上的一场圣乐音乐会。晚饭后他常在利斯草坪上来回走上二三十趵，巴不得有胆量同那些同样在散步的人群里随便哪一个搭话。礼拜天他几乎回回以脚痛收场。
他从不读书；也没有书可读；再说，尽管伍德罗先生领他读过廉价加注版的《暴风雨》（英国文学），他也没那份口味；他从不读报，偶尔一份《点滴》或半便士的「漫画」不算。他主要的智力刺激，是吃饭时卡肖特和巴金斯之间偶尔爆发的拌嘴。

===Original===

Kipps listened as if to unparalleled wisdom and wit, and treasured all the gems of repartee in his heart against the time when he, too, should be a Buggins and have the chance and courage for speech.
At times there came breaks in this routine﻿—sale times, darkened by extra toil and work past midnight, but brightened by a sprat supper and some shillings in the way of “premiums.” And every year﻿—not now and then, but every year﻿—Mr. Shalford, with parenthetic admiration of his own generosity and glancing comparisons with the austerer days when *he* was apprenticed, conceded Kipps no less than ten days’ holiday﻿—ten whole days every year! Many a poor soul at Portland might well envy the fortunate Kipps. Insatiable heart of man! but how those days were grudged and counted as they snatched themselves away from him one after another!
Once a year came stocktaking, and at intervals gusts of “marking off” goods newly arrived. Then the splendours of Mr. Shalford’s being shone with oppressive brilliancy. “System!” he would say, “system. Come! ’ussel!” and issue sharp, confusing, contradictory orders very quickly. Carshot trotted about, confused, perspiring, his big nose up in the air, his little eye on Mr. Shalford, his forehead crinkled, his lips always going to the formula “Oh, my heart and lungs!” The smart junior and the second apprentice vied with one another in obsequious alacrity. The smart junior aspired to Carshot’s position, and that made him almost violently subservient to Shalford. They all snapped at Kipps. Kipps held the blotting-pad and the safety inkpot and a box of tickets, and ran and fetched things. If he put the ink down before he went to fetch things Mr. Shalford usually knocked it over, and if he took it away Mr. Shalford wanted it before he returned. “You make my tooth ache, Kipps,” Mr. Shalford would say. “You gimme n’ralgia. You got no more System in you than a bad potato.” And at the times when Kipps carried off the inkpot Mr. Shalford would become purple in the face and jab round with his dry pen at imaginary inkpots and swear, and Carshot would stand and vociferate, and the smart junior would run to the corner of the department and vociferate, and the second apprentice would pursue Kipps, vociferating, “Look Alive, Kipps! Look Alive! Ink, Man! Ink!”

===Chinese===

基普斯听得如同聆听旷世的智慧和妙语，把警句的珠子一颗颗收进心里，留待他日后也成了巴金斯、也有了说话的机会和胆量的时候用。
这常规里也有间歇——大减价的日子，被额外的辛劳和过半夜的加班弄得天昏地暗，又被一顿西鯖晚饭和几个先令的「赏钱」照得亮堂。而且每年——不是隔三差五，是每年——沙尔福德先生都要带着对自己慷慨的括号式赞叹，夹几句「想当年*我*学徒时」的严峻对照，批给基普斯整整十天的假——每年整整十天！波特兰的多少可怜人真该眼红幸运的基普斯。人心不足啊！可那些日子怎么被人一天一天从他手里抢走，他就怎么在心里记账、痛惜！
一年一回盘货，间或一阵子给新到的货「打标记」。这时候，沙尔福德先生的堂皇以压人的亮度放光。「制度！」他会说，「制度。来！加点劲！」随即飞快下达又尖利、又混乱、又自相矛盾的号令。卡肖特小跑着，晕头转向，汗流浃背，大鼻子朝天生着，小眼睛盯着沙尔福德先生，额头上全是褶子，嘴唇永远走向那句「哦，我的心和肺！」那位精明的初级店员和二学徒争着比谁更殷勤麻利。精明的初级店员惦记着卡肖特的位子，因此对沙尔福德几乎暴力地恭顺。人人都冲基普斯喝斥。基普斯捧着吸墨纸架、保险墨水瓶和一盒标签，跑着拿东西。他去拿东西之前若把墨水瓶放下，沙尔福德先生多半把它碰翻；他若带走，沙尔福德先生又要在他还没回来时就要。「你害我牙疼，基普斯，」沙尔福德先生会说。「你叫我神经痛。你肚里的制度还不顶一个坏土豆。」轮到基普斯端走墨水瓶的那些时刻，沙尔福德先生会涨紫了脸，用干笔杆朝想象中的墨水瓶乱戳，骂骂咧咧；卡肖特站着吼，精明的初级店员跑到部门角落里吼，二学徒追着基普斯吼：「放机灵点，基普斯！放机灵点！墨水，伙计！墨水！」

===Original===

A vague self-disgust, that shaped itself as an intense hate of Shalford and all his fellow-creatures, filled the soul of Kipps during these periods of storm and stress. He felt that the whole business was unjust and idiotic, but the why and the wherefore was too much for his unfortunate brain. His mind was a welter. One desire, the desire to dodge some at least of a pelting storm of disagreeable comment, guided him through a fumbling performance of his duties. His disgust was infinite! It was not decreased by the inflamed ankles and sore feet that form a normal incident in the business of making an English draper; and the senior apprentice, Minton, a gaunt, sullen-faced youngster with close-cropped, wiry, black hair, a loose, ugly mouth, and a moustache like a smudge of ink, directed his attention to deeper aspects of the question and sealed his misery.
“When you get too old to work they chuck you away,” said Minton. “Lor! you find old drapers everywhere﻿—tramps, beggars, dock labourers, bus conductors﻿—Quod. Anywhere but in a crib.”
“Don’t they get shops of their own?”
“Lord! ’*Ow* are they to get shops of their own? They ’aven’t any capital! How’s a draper’s shopman to save up five hundred pounds even? I tell you it can’t be done. You got to stick to cribs until it’s over. I tell you we’re in a blessed drainpipe, and we’ve got to crawl along it till we die.”
The idea that fermented perpetually in the mind of Minton was to “hit the little beggar slap in the eye”﻿—the little beggar being Mr. Shalford﻿—“and see how his blessed System met that.”
The threat filled Kipps with splendid anticipations whenever Shalford went marking off in Minton’s department. He would look at Minton and look at Shalford, and decide where he would best like Shalford hit.﻿ ﻿… But for reasons known to himself Shalford never pished and tushed with Minton, as he did at the harmless Carshot, and this interesting experiment upon the System was never attempted.
There were times when Kipps would lie awake, all others in the dormitory asleep and snoring, and think dismally of the outlook Minton pictured. Dimly he perceived the thing that had happened to him﻿—how the great, stupid machine of retail trade had caught his life into its wheels, a vast, irresistible force which he had neither strength of will nor knowledge to escape. This was to be his life until his days should end.

===Chinese===

在这样的风暴与压力期，一种说不清的自我厌憎——它给自己塑形为对沙尔福德及其全体同类的切齿仇恨——灌满了基普斯的灵魂。他觉得这整个营生不公、愚蠢，可个中的所以然，又非他那可怜的脑子所能及。他的头脑是一锅粥。只有一个愿望——躲开劈头盖脸的恶评的至少几发——引着他磕磕绊绊地履行职守。他的厌憎无边无际！它也不曾因脚踝肿痛、脚底酸疼而稍减——那是制造一个英国绸布伙计的正常插曲；而资深学徒明顿（Minton）——一个瘦长、阴沉脸的年轻人，乌黑的硬发剪得极短，一张松垮的丑嘴，一撇像墨渍的髭——把他的注意力引向问题的更深处，给他的痛苦加了封。
「等你老得干不动了，人家就把你扔了，」明顿说。「嚯！老绸布伙计哪儿碰不见——流浪的，要饭的，码头上扛包的，公共汽车上卖票的——蹲班房。哪儿都去得，就是进不了铺子。」
「他们自己不开铺子？」
「老天！他们*怎么*开自己的铺子？本钱呢？一个绸布伙计哪攒得出五百镑？我告诉你，办不到。你就得吊在窝里吊到底。我告诉你，咱们在一条天赐的排水管里，得顺着它爬，爬到死。」
明顿脑子里永远发酵着的那个念头，是「朝那小畜生的眼睛上来一记狠的」——小畜生者，沙尔福德先生也——「再瞧瞧他那宝贝制度怎么接。」
每当沙尔福德到明顿的部门打标记，这个威胁就给基普斯灌满壮丽的期待。他看看明顿，看看沙尔福德，盘算沙尔福德挨在哪里最好。……可沙尔福德自有分寸，从不像对无害的卡肖特那样对明顿喳喳皱眉，于是这场关于制度的有意思的实验始终没有做。
有些夜晚，别人都睡着打着鼾，基普斯醒着，凄凄地想明顿描画的前景。他朦朦胧胧看出了落到自己头上的事：零售业那台巨大而愚蠢的机器，把他的生活咬进了轮齿——一股浩大的、不容抗拒的力量，他没有意志的力气，也没有知识，逃不出去。这将是他的生活，直到他的日子到头。

===Original===

No adventures, no glory, no change, no freedom. Neither﻿—though the force of that came home to him later﻿—might he dream of effectual love and marriage. And there was a terrible something called the “swap,” or “the key of the street,” and “crib hunting,” of which the talk was scanty but sufficient. Night after night he would resolve to enlist, to run away to sea, to set fire to the warehouse, or drown himself; and morning after morning he rose up and hurried downstairs in fear of a sixpenny fine. He would compare his dismal round of servile drudgery with those windy, sunlit days at Littlestone, those windows of happiness shining ever brighter as they receded. The little figure of Ann seemed in all these windows now.
She, too, had happened on evil things. When Kipps went home for the first Christmas after he was bound, that great suspended resolve of his to kiss her flared up to hot determination, and he hurried out and whistled in the yard. There was a still silence, and then old Kipps appeared behind him.
“It’s no good your whistling there, my boy,” said Old Kipps in a loud, clear tone, designed to be audible over the wall. “They’ve cleared out all you ’ad any truck with. *She’s* gone as help to Ashford, my boy. *Help!* Slavey is what we used to call ’em, but times are changed. Wonder they didn’t say lady-’elp while they was about it. It ’ud be like ’em.”
And Sid? Sid had gone, too. “Arrand boy or somethink,” said Old Kipps. “To one of these here brasted cicycle shops.”
“*Has* ’e!” said Kipps, with a feeling that he had been gripped about the chest, and he turned quickly and went indoors.
Old Kipps, still supposing him present, went on to further observations of an anti-Pornick hue.﻿ ﻿…
When Kipps got upstairs safe in his own bedroom, he sat down on the bed and stared at nothing. They were caught﻿—they were all caught. All life took on the hue of one perpetual, dismal Monday morning. The Hurons were scattered, the wrecks and the beach had passed away from him, the sun of those warm evenings at Littlestone had set forevermore.﻿ ﻿…
The only pleasure left for the brief remainder of his holiday after that was to think he was not in the shop. Even that was transient. Two more days﻿—one more day﻿—half a day. When he went back there were one or two very dismal nights indeed.

===Chinese===

没有奇遇，没有光荣，没有变化，没有自由。也——这一层是后来才痛切体会到的——不许他梦想有效的爱情与婚姻。还有一件可怕的东西，叫「炒」或「街头之钥」，还有「找窝」，谈起它们的言谈不多，但足够。一夜又一夜，他下决心去投军、去跑海、去放火烧货栈、去投河；一个早晨又一个早晨，他爬起来，怕着一笔六便士的罚款，慌慌张张下楼。他把他这一圈阴惨的奴工，同利特尔斯通那些刮风的、太阳的日子相比，同那些越退越远、越退越亮的幸福之窗相比。如今这一切窗子里，都是安的小小身影。
她也撞上了恶运。基普斯学徒后头一个圣诞回家，那个悬了几年的吻她的决心轰然烧成白热，他跑到院子里打呼哨。院里一片死寂，随后老基普斯出现在他背后。
「你在这儿打呼哨没用，孩子，」老基普斯大声清晰地——故意叫墙那边听得见——说。「凡是跟你有一手的都搬走了。*她*上阿什福德帮工去了，孩子。帮工！咱们从前管那种人叫小丫头片子，如今时兴叫法不同了。亏得他们没顺嘴叫成『女帮办』呢。他们就干得出来。」
希德呢？希德也走了。「送信的小伙计什么的，」老基普斯说。「进了一家骑自行车的破铺子。」
「是么！」基普斯说，胸口像叫人攞了一把，赶紧转身进屋去了。
老基普斯只当他还在，继续发表了一番反波尼克的高论。……
基普斯上了楼，安全进了自己的卧室，坐到床上，瞪着虚空。他们被逮住了——全都被逮住了。整个生活换上了那永远的、阴惨的星期一早晨的颜色。易洛魁人散了，沉船和海滩从他手里过去了，利特尔斯通那些暖和傍晚的太阳永远落了。……
此后他那假期短短的残余里，仅剩的乐趣就是想「我不在铺子里」这件事。连这个也是过眼云烟。还有两天——还有一天——还有半天。回去之后，有好几夜当真是凄凄惨惨。

===Original===

He went so far as to write home some vague intimation of his feelings about business and his prospects, quoting Minton. But Mrs. Kipps answered him, “Did he want the Pornicks to say he wasn’t good enough to be a draper?” This dreadful possibility was of course conclusive in the matter. “No,” he resolved they should not say he failed at that.
He derived much help from a “manly” sermon delivered in an enormous voice by a large, fat, sun-red clergyman, just home from a colonial bishopric he had resigned on the plea of ill-health, exhorting him that whatever his hand found to do, he was to do with all his might; and the revision of his Catechism preparatory to his confirmation reminded him that it behooved him “to do his duty in that state of life unto which it shall please God to call him.﻿ ﻿…”
After a time the sorrows of Kipps grew less acute, and save for a miracle the brief tragedy of his life was over. He subdued himself to his position even as his Church required of him, seeing moreover no way out of it.
The earliest mitigation of his lot was that his soles and ankles became indurated to the perpetual standing. The next was an unexpected weekly whiff of freedom that came every Thursday. Mr. Shalford, after a brave stand for what he called “Innyvishal lib’ty” and the “Idea of my System,” a stand which he explained he made chiefly on patriotic grounds, was at last, under pressure of certain of his customers, compelled to fall in line with the rest of the local Early Closing Association, and Mr. Kipps could emerge in daylight and go where he listed for long, long hours. Moreover Minton, the pessimist, reached the end of his appointed time and left﻿—to enlist in a cavalry regiment and go about this planet leading an insubordinate but interesting life, that ended at last in an intimate, vivid and really you know by no means painful or tragic night grapple in the Terah Valley. In a little while Kipps cleaned windows no longer; he was serving customers (of the less important sort) and taking goods out on approval; and presently he was third apprentice, and his moustache was visible, and there were three apprentices whom he might legally snub and cuff. But one was (most dishonestly) too big to cuff in spite of his greener years.
There came still other distractions, the natural distractions of adolescence, to take his mind off the inevitable.

===Chinese===

他甚至写信回家，含糊其辞地透了一点他对生意和前途的心情，还引了明顿的话。可基普斯太太回信问：他是想让波尼克家说他连个绸布伙计都当不好吗？这个可怕的可能是当然一锤定音。「不，」他打定主意，不能叫他们说他这个也干砸了。
他还从一篇「大丈夫气」的布道里得了不少帮助——布道的教区牧师胖大、晒得通红，刚从殖民地主教任上以健康为由辞了职回来，嗓门震天，劝勉他凡他手所当做的事，都要尽力去做；行坚振礼前的《教理问答》温习又提醒他：他当在本分上尽「上帝乐于召他身处的那一等人」的本分。……
过了一阵，基普斯的悲痛不那么尖锐了，若非有奇迹，他一生那出短短的悲剧就算演完了。他把自己驯服于他的地位，正如他的教会所要求的，何况他也看不出别的路。
他这份命运最早的缓解，是脚掌和脚踝在长年的站立中磨出了茧。其次是一礼拜一回、逢星期四降临的一阵意外的自由。沙尔福德先生为着他所谓的「个人自由」和「我制度的理念」勇敢抵抗了一阵——他解释说，这抵抗主要是出于爱国——末了迫于一部分顾客的压力，不得不随本地提早打烊协会的大流，基普斯先生这才得以在白昼出门，爱去哪儿去哪儿，一去老半天。再者，那位悲观主义者明顿期满了，走了——投了骑兵，在这颗行星上过起一种不驯服而有趣的生活，最后在特拉山谷一场肉搏夜里了结——那一场贴身的、有声有色的、老实说也绝不怎么痛苦或悲惨的夜战。不多时，基普斯不再擦窗户了；他开始接待顾客（次要的那一等），送试穿试用的货上门；不久他升到三学徒，小胡子露了头，底下有了三个名分上归他喝斥、捫打的学徒。只是其中一个（顶不讲理地）块头太大，虽然年资浅，打不得。
还有别的消遣也来了——青春期的自然消遣——替他岔开那躲不掉的。

===Original===

His costume, for example, began to interest him more; he began to realise himself as a visible object, to find an interest in the costume-room mirrors and the eyes of the girl apprentices.
In this he was helped by counsel and example. Pierce, his immediate senior, was by way of being what was called a Masher, and preached his cult. During slack times grave discussions about collars, ties, the cut of trouser legs, and the proper shape of a boot-toe, were held in the Manchester department. In due course Kipps went to a tailor, and his short jacket was replaced by a morning coat with tails. Stirred by this, he purchased at his own expense three stand-up collars to replace his former turndown ones. They were nearly three inches high, higher than those Pierce wore, and they made his neck quite sore and left a red mark under his ears.﻿ ﻿… So equipped, he found himself fit company even for this fashionable apprentice, who had now succeeded Minton in his seniority.
Most potent help of all in the business of forgetting his cosmic disaster was this, that so soon as he was in tail coats the young ladies of the establishment began to discover that he was no longer a “horrid little boy.” Hitherto they had tossed heads at him and kept him in his place. Now they discovered that he was a “nice boy,” which is next door at least to being a “feller,” and in some ways even preferable. It is painful to record that his fidelity to Ann failed at their first onset. I am fully sensible how entirely better this story would be from a sentimental point of view if he had remained true to that early love. Only then it would have been a different story altogether. And at least Kipps was thus far true, that with none of these later loves was there any of that particular quality that linked Ann’s flushed face and warmth and the inner things of life so inseparably together. Though they were not without emotions of various sorts.
It was one of the young ladies in the costume-room who first showed by her manner that he was a visible object and capable of exciting interest. She talked to him, she encouraged him to talk to her, she lent him a book she possessed, and darned a sock for him, and said she would be his elder sister. She allowed him to escort her to church with a great air of having induced him to go.

===Chinese===

比方说，他的穿戴开始叫他上心了；他开始把自己当作一件可见之物来意识，对服装间的镜子和女学徒们的眼睛发生了兴趣。
在这一层上，他得了参谋和榜样。他的顶头上司皮尔斯（Pierce），算得上当时所谓的「时髦尖子」，并且传布他的宗。清淡时节，曼彻斯特部里举行关于领子、领带、裤腿裁法、鞋头正型的庄严讨论。届期基普斯上了裁缝铺，短上衣换成了一件带下摆的晨礼服。受了鼓舞，他又自费购置了三副立式领，替换原先的翻式。领子将近三寸高，比皮尔斯的还高，把脖子磨得生疼，耳朵底下勒出红印。……如此装备起来，他自觉同这位时新学徒也配得上一桌了——此人如今已接了明顿的班，资深一级。
在遗忘他这场宇宙灾难的事业里，最灵的一剂是：他一穿上燕尾服，铺子里的年轻姑娘们便发现他不再是「讨厌的小男孩」了。此前她们对他甩头，叫他安分。如今她们发现他是个「可人的男孩」——离「小伙子」至少只隔一层，某些方面还更可取。说来痛心，头一阵攻势，他对安的忠贞就失守了。我完全明白，从感伤的角度看，他若守住那份初恋，这个故事会好上多少。只是那样一来，它就成了另一个故事了。而且至少基普斯守住了这一条：这些后来的情爱里，没有一桩有过那种把安绯红的脸、她的暖、和生活的里子不可分解地连在一起的特定质地。虽说各种名目的情绪也不是没有。
头一个用态度表明他是可见之物、能激起兴趣的，是服装间的一位年轻姑娘。她同他说话，鼓励他同她说话，借书给他，替他补袜子，说愿意做他的大姐。她还允许他护送她上教堂，一副硬拉他去的大功告成的神气。

===Original===

Then she investigated his eternal welfare, overcame a certain affectation of virile indifference to religion, and extorted a promise that he would undergo “confirmation.” This excited the other young lady in the costumes, her natural rival, and she set herself with great charm and subtlety to the capture of the ripening heart of Kipps. She took a more worldly line. She went for a walk with him to the pier on Sunday afternoon, and explained to him how a gentleman must always walk “outside” a lady on a pavement, and how all gentlemen wore, or at least carried gloves, and generally the broad beginnings of the British social ideal. Afterwards the ladies exchanged “words,” upon Sabbatical grounds. In this way was the toga virilis bestowed on Kipps, and he became recognised as a suitable object for that Platonic Eros whose blunted darts devastate even the very highest-class establishments. In this way, too, did that pervading ambition of the British young man to be, if not a “gentleman,” at least mistakably like one, take root in his heart.
He took to these new interests with quite natural and personal zest. He became initiated into the mysteries of “flirting,” and﻿—at a slightly later stage, and with some leading hints from Pierce, who was of a communicative disposition in these matters﻿—of the milder forms of “spooning.” Very soon he was engaged. Before two years were out he had been engaged six times, and was beginning to be rather a desperate fellow, so far as he could make out. Desperate, but quite gentlemanly, be it understood, and without let or hindrance to the fact that he was, in four brief lessons, “prepared” by a distant-mannered and gloomy young curate, and “confirmed” a member of the Established Church.
The engagements in drapery establishments do not necessarily involve a subsequent marriage. They are essentially more refined, less coarsely practical, and altogether less binding than the engagements of the vulgar rich. These young ladies do not like not to be engaged﻿—it is so unnatural; and Mr. Kipps was as easy to get engaged to as one could wish. There are, from the young lady’s point of view, many conveniences in being engaged. You get an escort for church and walks and so forth.

===Chinese===

随后她过问了他灵魂的永恒福祉，克服了他那点男子汉对宗教的假意冷淡，勒取了他去受「坚振礼」的承诺。这可刺激了服装间另一位姑娘——她的天敌——于是她也拿出十足的魅力和手腕，来攻取基普斯这颗熟透中的心。她走的是更世俗的一路。一个礼拜天下午，她同他去防波堤散步，讲解绅士在人行道上必须永远走在女士的「外侧」，讲解绅士们人人戴手套、至少人人带手套，以及英国社交理想的一应宽泛的基础。事后两位姑娘在「安息日」的题目上交换了「意见」。成人袍就是这样披上了基普斯的身，他被公认为一个合适的对象，配得上那种柏拉图式的爱神——那支钝了的箭，连最高级的铺子也要杀伤。也是这样，英国青年那种弥漫的雄心——做不成「绅士」，至少也要像到认不出——在他心里扎了根。
他怀着自然而个人的兴头投入了这些新趣味。他受了「调情」的秘传；又——略晚一步，并得力于皮尔斯几番提点，此君在这些事上素有传道之热忱——受了「缠绵」诸温和法门的秘传。不多时他订了婚。不出两年，他订过六回婚，并且——就他自己分辨得出的而言——开始算得上一个亡命的情郎了。亡命，但十足绅士风度，务必明白；而且毫不妨碍他由一位神情疏远、面色阴郁的年轻副牧师「预备」了短短四课，「坚振」为国教的正式会友。
绸布铺里的婚约并不必然牵动日后的结婚。它们本质上比暴发户们的婚约更文雅，更不粗陋切实，也更少约束。这些年轻姑娘不喜欢没有婚约——那太不自然了；而基普斯先生又好订之极，随叫随订。从姑娘的角度看，订婚有种种便利。上教堂、散步等等，有人护送。

===Original===

It is not quite the thing to walk abroad with a “feller,” much more to “spoon” with him, when he is neither one’s fiancé nor an adopted brother; it is considered either a little *fast*, or else as savouring of the “walking-out” habits of the servant girls. Now, such is the sweetness of human charity, that the shop young lady in England has just the same horror of doing anything that savours of the servant girl as the lady journalist, let us say, has of anything savouring of the shop girl, or the really quite nice young lady has of anything savouring of any sort of girl who has gone down into the economic battlefield to earn herself a living.﻿ ﻿… But the very deepest of these affairs was still among the shallow places of love; at best it was paddling where it is decreed that men must sink or swim. Of the deep and dangerous places, and of the huge buoyant lift of its waves, he tasted nothing. Affairs of clothes and vanities they were, jealousies about a thing said, flatteries and mutual boastings, climaxes in the answering grasp of hands, the temerarious use of Christian names, culminations in a walk, or a near confidence, or a little pressure more or less. Close-sitting on a seat after twilight, with some little fondling, was indeed the boldest of a lover’s adventures, the utmost limit of his enterprises in the service of that stark Great Lady, who is daughter of Uranus and the sea. The “young ladies” who reigned in his heart came and went like people in an omnibus: there was the vehicle, so to speak, upon the road, and they entered and left it without any cataclysm of emotion. For all that, this development of the sex interest was continuously very interesting to Kipps, and kept him going as much as anything through all these servile years.
For a tailpiece to this chapter one may vignette one of those little affairs.
It is a bright Sunday afternoon; the scene is a secluded little seat halfway down the front of the Leas, and Kipps is four years older than when he parted from Ann. There is a quite perceptible down upon his upper lip, and his costume is just as tremendous a “mash” as lies within his means. His collar is so high that it scars his inaggressive jawbone, and his hat has a curly brim, his tie shows taste, his trousers are modestly brilliant, and his boots have light cloth uppers and button at the side.

===Chinese===

同「小伙子」上街——尤其同他「缠绵」——倘若他既不是未婚夫又不是干哥哥，那就有点不像话；不是有点*轻浮*，就是沾了女仆们「处对象」的习气。须知人情慈善之甘美如此：英国的铺子姑娘对一切沾女仆气的事物的恐惧，恰如——打个比方——女记者对一切沾铺子姑娘气的事物、真正体面的小姐对一切沾「下海谋生的任何一种姑娘」气的事物的恐惧。……可这些情事里顶深的一场，也还留在爱情的浅水区；至多是齐膝的水，而天意却命人在这里沉浮。深处的危险，巨浪的浮托之力，他一概没有尝到。那都是些衣裳和虚荣的事件，为一句话吃醋，互相吹捧，高潮是握手时的一紧，是大胆互称教名，是散一次步、交一次心、或多或少的轻轻一压。暮色后在长椅上挨坐，带一点爱抚，已是情郎冒险里最大胆的一档，是他在那位赤裸的伟大女性——乌拉诺斯与大海之女——麾下功业的极限。在他心里称孤道寡的「年轻姑娘」们来了又去，像公共汽车里的人：可以说有那么一辆车在路上跑，她们上车、下车，没有一点情绪的地震。尽管如此，这份性兴趣的发展对基普斯始终趣味盎然，同别的什么一起，撑着他熬过了这些奴性的年头。
本章的尾花，可以拿这样一件小事做个小插图。
一个晴朗的礼拜天下午；场景是利斯草坪正面半坡上一张僻静的小长椅；基普斯同比安分手时大四岁。他上唇已经有了一层看得出的小胡子，一身行头是他财力所及的范围内顶「帅」的一套。领子高得磨着他那不好斗的颢骨，帽子卷边，领带见品味，裤子端着亮，靴子是浅布帮、旁系扣。

===Original===

He jabs at the gravel before him with a cheap cane, and glances sideways at Flo Bates, the young lady from the cash desk. She is wearing a brilliant blouse and a gaily trimmed hat. There is an air of fashion about her that might disappear under the analysis of a woman of the world, but which is quite sufficient to make Kipps very proud to be distinguished as her particular “feller,” and to be allowed at temperate intervals to use her Christian name.
The conversation is light and gay in the modern style, and Flo keeps on smiling, good temper being her special charm.
“Ye see, you don’ mean what *I* mean,” he is saying.
“Well, what do *you* mean?”
“Not what you mean!”
“Well, tell me.”
“*Ah!* That’s another story.”
Pause. They look meaningly at one another.
“You *are* a one for being roundabout,” says the lady.
“Well, you’re not so plain, you know.”
“Not plain?”
“No.”
“You don’t mean to say I’m roundabout?”
“No. I mean to say﻿ ﻿… though﻿—”
Pause.
“Well?”
“You’re not a bit plain﻿—you’re” (his voice jumps up to a squeak) “pretty. See?”
“Oh, get *out*!” her voice lifts also﻿—with pleasure.
She strikes at him with her glove, then glances suddenly at a ring upon her finger. Her smile disappears momentarily. Another pause. Eyes meet and the smile returns.
“I wish I knew﻿—” says Kipps.
“Knew﻿—?”
“Where you got that ring.”
She lifts the hand with the ring until her eyes just show (very prettily) over it. “You’d just *like* to know,” she says slowly, and smiles still more brightly with the sense of successful effect.
“I dessay I could guess.”
“I dessay you couldn’t.”
“Couldn’t I?”
“No!”
“Guess it in three.”
“Not the name.”
“Ah!”
“*Ah!*”
“Well, anyhow lemme look at it.”
He looks at it. Pause. Giggles, slight struggle, and a slap on Kipps’ coatsleeve. A passerby appears down the path, and she hastily withdraws her hand.
She glances at the face of the approaching man. They maintain a bashful silence until he has passed.

===Chinese===

他拿一根便宜手杖戳着面前的砂砾，斜眼瞟着弗洛·贝茨，出纳台的那位年轻姑娘。她穿一件亮丽的罩衫，戴一顶花哨的帽子。她身上那股时兴气，经世故女人一分析也许就散了，可在基普斯看来尽够了——够他非常骄傲地被指认为她专有的「那一位」，够他在有节制的间隙里称呼她的教名。
谈话是摩登一路的轻快，弗洛一直微笑着，好脾气是她的独门魅力。
「你瞧，你说的*不是*我的意思，」他正说着。
「那你的*什么*意思？」
「不是你那个意思！」
「那说说呀。」
「*啊！*那就是另一回事了。」
停顿。两人意味深长地对看。
「你可真能绕，」姑娘说。
「你也不直率，你知道。」
「不直率？」
「不。」
「你是说我绕？」
「不。我是说……不过——」
停顿。
「嗯？」
「你不是不直率——你是」（他的嗓门跳上一个尖）「俊。明白？」
「哦，去你的！」她的嗓门也扬起来——扬的是欢喜。
她拿手套打了他一下，随即忽然看了一眼手上的一枚戒指。笑容一时隐去。又停顿。目光相遇，笑容回来。
「我恨不得知道——」基普斯说。
「知道——？」
「你那戒指是哪儿来的。」
她把戴戒指的手抬到眼睛刚从上面露出（很俏皮）的高度。「你就是*想*知道，」她慢慢地说，为成功的效果笑得更亮了。
「我猜我猜得着。」
「我猜你猜不着。」
「猜不着？」
「猜不着！」
「三回里猜着它。」
「名字可猜不着。」
「啊！」
「*啊！*」
「好吧，反正让我看看。」
他看了看。停顿。咯咯笑，小小的扭打，基普斯的袖子上挨了一巴掌。小径那头来了个过路人，她慌忙把手抽回去。
她瞟了眼来人的脸。两人羞怯地沉默着，等他走过去。

## I The Housing Problem / 一 住房问题
===Original===

Honeymoons and all things come to an end, and you see at last Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Kipps descending upon the Hythe platform﻿—coming to Hythe to find that nice *little* house﻿—to realise that bright dream of a home they had first talked about in the grounds of the Crystal Palace. They are a valiant couple, you perceive, but small, and the world is a large incongruous system of complex and difficult things. Kipps wears a grey suit, with a wing-poke collar and a neat, smart tie. Mrs. Kipps is the same bright and healthy little girl woman you saw in the marsh; not an inch has been added to her stature in all my voluminous narrative. Only now she wears a hat.
It is a hat very unlike the hats she used to wear on her Sundays out, a flourishing hat with feathers and buckle and bows and things. The price of that hat would take many people’s breath away﻿—it cost two guineas! Kipps chose it. Kipps paid for it. They left the shop with flushed cheeks and smarting eyes, glad to be out of range of the condescending saleswoman.
“Artie,” said Ann, “you didn’t ought to ’ave﻿—”
That was all. And you know, the hat didn’t suit Ann a bit. Her clothes did not suit her at all. The simple, cheap, clean brightness of her former style had given place not only to this hat, but to several other things in the same key. And out from among these things looked her pretty face, the face of a wise little child﻿—an artless wonder struggling through a preposterous dignity.
They had bought that hat one day when they had gone to see the shops in Bond street. Kipps had looked at the passersby and it had suddenly occurred to him that Ann was dowdy. He had noted the hat of a very proud-looking lady passing in an electric brougham and had resolved to get Ann the nearest thing to that.
The railway porters perceived some subtle incongruity in Ann, the knot of cabmen in the station doorway, the two golfers and the lady with daughters, who had also got out of the train. And Kipps, a little pale, blowing a little, not in complete possession of himself, knew that they noticed her and him. And Ann﻿—. It is hard to say just what Ann observed of these things.
“ ’Ere!” said Kipps to a cabman, and regretted too late a vanished *H*.
“I got a trunk up there,” he said to a ticket inspector, “marked A. K.”
“Ask a porter,” said the inspector, turning his back.
“Demn!” said Kipps, not altogether inaudibly.

===Chinese===

蜜月和一切都有到头的时候，末了你看见亚瑟·基普斯夫妇俩降落在了海斯月台上——到海斯来找那座漂亮的*小*房子——来兑现那个明亮的家的梦，他们最初在水晶宫园子里谈起过的那个。他们是勇敢的一对，你看得出，只是都小，而世界是一套庞大、错杂、难缠的事物体系。基普斯穿一身灰西装，翼尖立领，一条利落的时髦领带。基普斯太太还是你在沼泽地见过的那个明亮健康的小姑娘似的妇人；在我这洋洋大观的长篇里，她一寸也没长高。只是如今她戴了一顶帽子。
那顶帽子同她从前礼拜天出门戴的帽子全然不同，一顶花枝招展的帽子，羽毛、带扣、蝴蝶结，样样俱全。那顶帽子的价钱会叫许多人倒抽冷气——两几尼！基普斯挑的。基普斯付的钱。他们出了铺子，两颊发烧，眼睛刺痛，庆幸总算走出了那位居高临下的女售货员的射程。
「阿蒂，」安说，「你实在不该——」
就这一句。而且你知道，那帽子安戴着一点也不配。她的衣着全都不配。她先前那种朴素、便宜、干净、鲜亮的路数，不但让位给了这顶帽子，还让位给了同一调门的另外几样东西。从这些东西中间探出来的，是她那张好看的脸，一张聪明的小孩子的脸——一分天真的惊异，挣扎着穿过一身荒唐的体面。
那顶帽子是有一天他们逛邦德街的铺子时买的。基普斯看看来往的行人，忽然想到安穿得寒酸。他留意到一位神气极傲的太太坐电动四轮马车经过时的帽子，打定主意给安买一顶最接近那个的。
铁路搬运夫们觉察出安身上某种说不出的不称；车站门口那一簇马车夫、两个打高尔夫的和一位带着几个女儿的太太——也是这趤车下来的——也觉察出来了。而基普斯，有点白，微微喘着，不是完全做得了自己的主，知道他们在注意她和他。至于安——安对这些觉察了什么，很难说清。
「喂！」基普斯对一位马车夫说，随即为那个失踪的 H 懊悔已晚。
「我有只箱子在上头，」他对一个查票员说，「标着 A. K.。」
「问搬运夫。」查票员说着，转过背去。
「该死！」基普斯说，声音并不全然听不见。

===Original===

It is all very well to sit in the sunshine and talk of the house you will have, and another altogether to achieve it. We English﻿—all the world indeed today﻿—live in a strange atmosphere of neglected great issues, of insistent, triumphant petty things, we are given up to the fine littlenesses of intercourse; table manners and small correctitudes are the substance of our lives. You do not escape these things for long even by so catastrophic a proceeding as flying to London with a young lady of no wealth and inferior social position. The mists of noble emotion swirl and pass and there you are divorced from all your deities and grazing in the meadows under the Argus eyes of the social system, the innumerable mean judgments you feel raining upon you, upon your clothes and bearing, upon your pretensions and movements.
Our world today is a meanly conceived one﻿—it is only an added meanness to conceal that fact. For one consequence, it has very few nice little houses, such things do not come for the asking, they are not to be bought with money during ignoble times. Its houses are built on the ground of monstrously rich, shabbily extortionate landowners, by poor, parsimonious, greedy people in a mood of elbowing competition. What can you expect from such ridiculous conditions? To go househunting is to spy out the nakedness of this pretentious world, to see what our civilization amounts to when you take away curtains and flounces and carpets and all the fluster and distraction of people and fittings. It is to see mean plans meanly executed for mean ends, the conventions torn aside, the secrets stripped, the substance underlying all such Chester Cootery, soiled and worn and left.
So you see our poor, dear Kippses going to and fro, in Hythe, in Sandgate, in Ashford and Canterbury and Deal and Dover﻿—at last even in Folkestone, with “orders to view,” pink and green and white and yellow orders to view, and labelled keys in Kipps’ hand and frowns and perplexity upon their faces.﻿ ﻿… They did not clearly know what they wanted, but whatever it was they saw, they knew they did not want that. Always they found a confusing multitude of houses they could not take, and none they could. Their dreams began to turn mainly on empty, abandoned-looking rooms, with unfaded patches of paper to mark the place of vanished pictures and doors that had lost their keys.

===Chinese===

坐在太阳底下谈谈你将来要有的房子是一回事，把它办成是另一回事。我们英国人——今天全世界其实都一样——活在一种古怪的氛围里：大事被擱置，小事得胜又咄咄逼人；我们沉溺于交往的种种精致的小零碎；餐桌规矩和细枝末节的得体，就是我们生活的实质。哪怕采取飞到伦敦、同一个没有财产、社会地位低微的年轻姑娘结婚这样灾难性的步骤，也逃不开这些东西多久。高贵情感的迷雾打着旋过去了，你却发现自己众神尽失，在社交体系那阿尔戈斯般的百眼底下吃草——无数细小的评判，你感到它们像雨一样落在你身上，落在你的衣着和仪态上，落在你的自命和举动上。
我们今天这个世界构想得小气——再小气一点，只为遮掩这一事实。后果之一，便是极少有漂亮的小房子；这样的东西不是开口就有的，在不高贵的时代，有钱也买不到。它的房子盖在富得吓人、盘剥得寒酸的大地主的地皮上，由一群穷苦、吝啬、贪心的人在你推我搡的竞争情绪里盖起来。这样荒唐的条件下能指望什么？去找房子，就是去窥探这个自负世界的赤裸，就是看看撤掉窗帘、荷叶边、地毯和一切人声与陈设的忙乱纷扰之后，我们的文明还剩下什么。就是看见为小气的目的马虎执行的小气图纸，看见俗套被扯到一边，秘密被剥光，一切「切斯特·库特作风」底下那点实质——又脏，又旧，擞在那儿。
于是你看见我们可怜的、亲爱的基普斯夫妇跑来跑去，在海斯，在桑德盖特，在阿什福德、坎特伯雷、迪尔和多佛——最后连福克斯通也跑了——手里捏着「看屋单」，粉的绿的白的黄的看屋单，一串标着号的钥匙攥在基普斯手里，脸上挂着皱眉和困惑。……他们说不清自己要什么；可无论看见什么，他们都知道：他们要的不是这个。他们回回发现一大堆没法要的房子，却一所也没有能要的。他们的梦开始主要围着空荡荡、像被遗弃的房间打转：壁纸上留着没褪色的方块，标记着消失的图画的旧址；门上丢了钥匙。

===Original===

They saw rooms floored with boards that yawned apart and were splintered, skirtings eloquent of the industrious mouse, kitchens with a dead black-beetle in the empty cupboard, and a hideous variety of coal holes and dark cupboards under the stairs. They stuck their little heads through roof trapdoors and gazed at disorganised ball taps, at the bleak filthiness of unstoppered roofs. There were occasions when it seemed to them that they must be the victims of an elaborate conspiracy of house agents, so bleak and cheerless is a secondhand empty house in comparison with the humblest of inhabited dwellings.
Commonly the houses were too big. They had huge windows that demanded vast curtains in mitigation, countless bedrooms, acreage of stone steps to be cleaned, kitchens that made Ann protest. She had come so far towards a proper conception of Kipps’ social position as to admit the prospect of one servant﻿—“but lor’!” she would say, “you’d want a manservant in this ’ouse.” When the houses were not too big, then they were almost invariably the product of speculative building, of that multitudinous hasty building for the extravagant multitude of new births that was the essential disaster of the nineteenth century. The new houses Ann refused as damp, and even the youngest of these that had been in use showed remarkable signs of a sickly constitution, the plaster flaked away, the floors gaped, the paper mouldered and peeled, the doors dropped, the bricks scaled and the railings rusted, Nature in the form of spiders, earwigs, cockroaches, mice, rats, fungi and remarkable smells, was already fighting her way back.﻿ ﻿…
And the plan was invariably inconvenient, invariably. All the houses they saw had a common quality for which she could find no word, but for which the proper word is incivility. “They build these ’ouses,” she said, “as though girls wasn’t ’uman beings.” Sid’s social democracy had got into her blood perhaps, and anyhow they went about discovering the most remarkable inconsiderateness in the contemporary house. “There’s kitching stairs to go up, Artie!” Ann would say. “Some poor girl’s got to go up and down, up and down, and be tired out, jest because they haven’t the sense to leave enough space to give their steps a proper rise﻿—and no water upstairs anywhere﻿—every drop got to be carried! It’s ’ouses like this wear girls out.

===Chinese===

他们看过地板豁着缝、起着毛刺的房间，踢脚线述说着勤劳的老鼠；厨房空碗柜里一只死蟑螂；还有各色其极的煤窖和楼梯底下黑黢黢的储物间。他们把小脑袋从屋顶天窗探进去，凝望乱成一团的浮球阀，凝望没有塞严的屋顶那光秃秃的肮脏。有几次他们几乎认定自己是房屋中介们一场精心密谋的受害者——同最简陋的住家相比，一所二手的空房子就是那么荒凉、那么无生气。
通常房子都太大。大窗户要求大片窗帘来缓解；卧房数不清；一英亩的石阶等着擦；厨房大得叫安抗议。她对基普斯的社会地位的正确概念已经进步到容许一个仆人的前景——「可是老天！」她会说，「这房子里你得雇个男仆。」房子不大的，又几乎一概是投机建筑的产物——那场为暴涨的生育大军而进行的大批草率建筑，正是十九世纪的根本灾难。新房子安嫌潮；就连启用没几年的，也显出病体的种种迹象：灰泥脱皮，地板张口，墙纸霉烂剥落，门下坠，砖酥落，栏杆生锈；大自然——以蜘蛛、蠼螋、蟑螂、老鼠、真菌和种种怪味的形态——已经杀回来了。……
而且格局永远不方便，永远。他们看的所有房子有一种共同的品性，她找不出词来，可正确的词是：不体贴。「他们盖这些房子，」她说，「好像姑娘们就不是人似的。」希德的社会民主主义也许渗进了她的血；反正他们四处发现当代房子里最惊人的漠然。「这儿有厨房楼梯要爬，阿蒂！」安会说。「哪个可怜的姑娘就得这么上上下下、上上下下，累个半死，就因为他们不肯多留点地方、把台阶修出个正当的坡度——楼上哪儿也没有水——一滴都得提！就是这样的房子把姑娘们熬干的。

===Original===

“It’s ’aving ’ouses built by men, I believe, makes all the work and trouble,” said Ann.﻿ ﻿…
The Kippses, you see, thought they were looking for a reasonably simple little contemporary house, but indeed they were looking either for dreamland or 1975 AD or thereabouts, and it hadn’t come.
But it was a foolish thing of Kipps to begin building a house.
He did that out of an extraordinary animosity for house agents he had conceived.
Everybody hates house agents just as everybody loves sailors. It is no doubt a very wicked and unjust hatred, but the business of a novelist is not ethical principle but facts. Everybody hates house agents because they have everybody at a disadvantage. All other callings have a certain amount of give and take; the house agent simply takes. All other callings want you; your solicitor is afraid you may change him, your doctor cannot go too far, your novelist﻿—if only you knew it﻿—is mutely abject towards your unspoken wishes﻿—and as for your tradespeople, milkmen will fight outside your front door for you, and greengrocers call in tears if you discard them suddenly; but who ever heard of a house agent struggling to serve anyone? You want to get a house; you go to him, you dishevelled and angry from travel, anxious, enquiring; he calm, clean, inactive, reticent, quietly doing nothing. You beg him to reduce rents, whitewash ceilings, produce other houses, combine the summer house of No. 6 with the conservatory of No. 4﻿—much he cares! You want to dispose of a house; then he is just the same, serene, indifferent﻿—on one occasion I remember he was picking his teeth all the time he answered me. Competition is a mockery among house agents, they are all alike, you cannot wound them by going to the opposite office, you cannot dismiss them, you can at most dismiss yourself. They are invulnerably placed behind mahogany and brass, too far usually even for a sudden swift lunge with an umbrella, and to throw away the keys they lend you instead of returning them is larceny and punishable as such.
It was a house agent in Dover who finally decided Kipps to build. Kipps, with a certain faltering in his voice, had delivered his ultimatum, no basement, not more than eight rooms, hot and cold water upstairs, coal cellar in the house but with intervening doors to keep dust from the scullery and so forth. He stood blowing.

===Chinese===

「依我看，就是房子都归男人盖，才弄出这许多活计和麻烦。」安说。……
你瞧，基普斯夫妇自以为是在找一所合理的、简单的当代小房子；实际上他们找的要么是梦境，要么是公元1975年前后——而它还没有来。
可基普斯动手盖房子，是件蠢事。
他那么干，是出自他对房屋中介生出的一股异乎寻常的敌意。
人人恨房屋中介，正如人人爱水手。这无疑是一种邪恶而不公的恨；可小说家管的是事实，不是伦理原则。人人恨房屋中介，因为中介捏着每个人的短处。别的行当多少有来有往；房屋中介只往里拿。别的行当都需要你：你的律师怕你换人，你的医生不敢过分，你的小说家——只要你知道——对你的未出口的愿望默默卑躬；至于你的商家，送奶的会在你家门口为你打架，菜贩子被你突然抛弃会哭着上门；可谁听说过房屋中介抢着伺候谁？你要找房；你去找他，你风尘仆仆、满腔怒火，又急又问；他呢，安静、干净、无所事事、寡言，静静的不干事。你求他减租，刷白天花板，换几所房子，把六号的凉亭同四号的暖房并一并——他理你吗！你要出手房子；他照样，安详，漠然——我记得有一回，他一边答我的话一边剔了一路的牙。房屋中介之间竞争是句空话，他们全是一路货，你走到对面那家也伤不着他们，你炒不掉他们，至多炒掉你自己。他们刀枪不入地坐在红木和铜栏杆后面，通常隔着一段连伞都突刺不及的距离；他们借你的钥匙你要是扔了不还，那是盗窃，照盗窃办。
最终叫基普斯下决心盖房的，是多佛的一个中介。基普斯声音里带着点哆嗦，交出了他的最后通牒：不要地下室；至多八个房间；楼上冷热水；煤窖在宅内，但隔门防灰进洗涤间；等等。他站着直喘。

===Original===

“You’ll have to build a house,” said the house agent, sighing wearily, “if you want all that.” It was rather for the sake of effective answer than with any intention at the time that Kipps mumbled, “That’s about what I shall do﻿—this goes on.”
Whereupon the house agent smiled. He smiled!
When Kipps came to turn the thing over in his mind he was surprised to find quite a considerable intention had germinated and was growing up in him. After all, lots of people *have* built houses. How could there be so many if they hadn’t? Suppose he “reely” did! Then he would go to the house agent and say, “ ’Ere, while you been getting me a sootable ’ouse, blowed if I ’aven’t built one!” Go round to all of them; all the house agents in Folkestone, in Dover, Ashford, Canterbury, Margate, Ramsgate, saying that! Perhaps then they might be sorry. It was in the small hours that he awoke to a realisation that he had made up his mind in the matter.
“Ann,” he said, “Ann,” and also used the sharp of his elbow.
Ann was at last awakened to the pitch of an indistinct enquiry what was the matter.
“I’m going to build a house, Ann.”
“Eh?” said Ann, suddenly, as if awake.
“Build a house.”
Ann said something incoherent about he’d better wait until the morning before he did anything of the sort, and immediately with a fine trustfulness went fast asleep again.
But Kipps lay awake for a long while building his house, and in the morning at breakfast he made his meaning clear. He had smarted under the indignities of house agents long enough, and this seemed to promise revenge﻿—a fine revenge. “And, you know, we might reely make rather a nice little ’ouse out of it﻿—like we want.”
So resolved, it became possible for them to take a house for a year, with a basement, no service lift, blackleading to do everywhere, no water upstairs, no bathroom, vast sash windows to be cleaned from the sill, stone steps with a twist and open to the rain into the coal cellar, insufficient cupboards, unpaved path to the dustbin, no fireplace to the servant’s bedroom, no end of splintery wood to scrub﻿—in fact, a very typical English middle-class house.

===Chinese===

「那您就得自己盖房了，」房屋中介疲惫地叹道，「想要这些的话。」基普斯咕哝了一句「那我就这么办——再拖下去」，多半是为了答得响亮，当时并没什么打算。
谁知那中介笑了。他居然笑了！
等基普斯回头细想这事，他吃惊地发现，一个相当可观的心愿已经在他心里发芽抽条。毕竟，盖过房子的*人多*得很。不然哪来这么多房子？没准儿他「真的」就盖呢！那他就去找那中介说：「喏，你替我找合适房子找了这么久，瞧，我自己盖出一所来了！」挨家挨户地去；福克斯通、多佛、阿什福德、坎特伯雷、马盖特、拉姆斯盖特的所有中介，挨家说去！那时候他们也许就悔了。后半夜他才恍然：这事他已经拿定主意了。
「安，」他说，「安，」又用了胳膊肘的尖。
安总算醒到含含糊糊问一声怎么了的程度。
「我要盖房子了，安。」
「嗯？」安说，猛地像醒透了。
「盖房子。」
安语无伦次地说了句这类事最好等天亮再办，随即怀着美好的信任又沉沉睡去。
可基普斯醒着躺了好久，盖他的房子；早晨吃早饭时把意思挑明了。房屋中介的气他受够了，这一手像是报仇——一场漂亮的报仇。「再说，你晓得，咱们兴许真能盖出一所怪不错的小房子——照咱们想要的。」
这么一定下来，他们就能够先租一年房子了：有地下室，没有仆梯，到处要打黑色炉铅，楼上没水，没有浴室，大得要命的上下推拉窗得站在窗台里外擦，石阶一拐弯、露天通进煤窖，柜子不够，去垃圾箱的路没铺，女仆卧房没有壁炉，擦不完的毛刺木头——事实上，一所非常典型的英国中产房子。

===Original===

And having added to this house some furniture, and a languid young person with unauthentic golden hair named Gwendolen, who was engaged to a sergeant-major and had formerly been in an hotel, having “moved in” and spent some sleepless nights varied by nocturnal explorations in search of burglars, because of the strangeness of being in a house for which they were personally responsible, Kipps settled down for a time and turned himself with considerable resolution to the project of building a home.
At first Kipps had gathered advice, finding an initial difficulty in how to begin. He went into a builder’s shop at Seabrook one day, and told the lady in charge that he wanted a house built; he was breathless but quite determined, and he was prepared to give his order there and then, but she temporised with him and said her husband was out, and he left without giving his name. Also he went and talked to a man in a cart who was pointed out to him by a workman as the builder of a new house near Saltwood, but he found him first sceptical and then overpoweringly sarcastic. “I suppose you build a ’ouse every ’oliday,” he said, and turned from Kipps with every symptom of contempt.
Afterwards Carshot told alarming stories about builders, and shook Kipps’ expressed resolution a good deal, and then Pierce raised the question whether one ought to go in the first instance to a builder at all and not rather to an architect. Pierce knew a man at Ashford whose brother was an architect, and as it is always better in these matters to get someone you know, the Kippses decided, before Pierce had gone, and Carshot’s warning had resumed their sway, to apply to him. They did so﻿—rather dubiously.
The architect who was brother of Pierce’s friend appeared as a small, alert individual with a black bag and a cylindrical silk hat, and he sat at the dining-room table, with his hat and his bag exactly equidistant right and left of him, and maintained a demeanour of impressive woodenness, while Kipps on the hearthrug, with a quaking sense of gigantic enterprise, vacillated answers to his enquiries. Ann held a watching brief for herself, in a position she had chosen as suitable to the occasion beside the corner of the carved oak sideboard. They felt, in a sense, at bay.
The architect began by asking for the site, and seemed a little discomposed to discover this had still to be found.

===Chinese===

再往这房子里添些家具，添一个名叫格温多林（Gwendolen）的、头发金得不真、人有气无力的年轻姑娘——她同一位军士长订了婚，从前在旅馆做过——「搬」了进去，度过几个不眠之夜，间或夜探窃贼（因为这房子头一回归他们个人负责，处处透着生疏）；这样安顿之后，基普斯定下神来，以相当大的决心投入了建房大业。
起头基普斯四处收罗主意，头一个难题是怎么开头。有一天他进了西布鲁克一家营造铺子，对管柜台的太太说他要盖一所房子；他喘着，可是决意已定，准备当场下单；太太同他磨，说她丈夫出去了，他没留姓名就走了。他还去找过一个坐在马车上的男人——是一个工人指给他看的，说是索尔特伍德新房子的营造商——可他发现那人先是怀疑，继而讽刺得叫人招架不住。「我看你每个假日都盖一所房子吧。」他说，带着满身轻蔑把脸从基普斯这边转开去。
随后卡肖特讲了些营造商的骇人故事，把基普斯表过的态摇得七荤八素；皮尔斯又提出一个问题：头一步到底该不该找营造商，还是该找建筑师。皮尔斯认得阿什福德的一个人，那人的哥哥是建筑师；这类事总是找认识的人强，于是基普斯夫妇赶在皮尔斯走了、卡肖特的警告重新掌权之前，决定去求他。他们去了——心里七上八下。
皮尔斯的朋友的那位建筑师来了，一个矮小精干的人物，拎一只黑包，戴一顶圆筒绸帽；他在饭桌旁坐下，帽子和包在他左右两边摆得绝对等距，维持着一种肃穆的木头神气；基普斯站在炉前地毯上，怀着干一桩惊天事业的心惊，对他的提问答得摇摆不定。安为自己守着一份监军的差事，站的位置是她挑的、合乎场合的：雕橡木餐具柜的角边。他们感到，某种意义上，是被逼到了墙角。
建筑师开口就要宅基地，听说地还没找着，似乎有点乱。

===Original===

“I thought of building just anywhere,” said Kipps. “I ’aven’t made up my mind about that yet.” The architect remarked that he would have preferred to see the site in order to know where to put what he called his “ugly side,” but it was quite possible of course to plan a house “in the air,” on the level, “simply with back and front assumed”﻿—if they would like to do that. Kipps flushed slightly, and secretly hoping it would make no great difference in the fees, said a little doubtfully that he thought that would be all right.
The architect then marked off as it were the first section of his subject, with a single dry cough, opened his bag, took out a spring tape measure, some hard biscuits, a metal flask, a new pair of dogskin gloves, a clockwork motorcar partially wrapped in paper, a bunch of violets, a paper of small brass screws, and finally a large, distended notebook; he replaced the other objects carefully, opened his notebook, put a pencil to his lips and said: “And what accommodation will you require?” To which Ann, who had followed his every movement with the closest attention and a deepening dread, replied with the violent suddenness of one who has long lain in wait, “Cubbuds!”
“Anyhow,” she added, catching her husband’s eye.
The architect wrote it down.
“And how many rooms?” he said, coming to secondary matters.
The young people regarded one another. It was dreadfully like giving an order.
“How many bedrooms, for example?” asked the architect.
“One?” suggested Kipps, inclined now to minimise at any cost.
“There’s Gwendolen,” said Ann.
“Visitors perhaps,” said the architect, and temperately, “You never know.”
“Two, p’raps?” said Kipps. “We don’t want no more than a *little* ’ouse, you know.”
“But the merest shooting-box﻿—,” said the architect.
They got to six; he beat them steadily from bedroom to bedroom, the word “nursery” played across their imaginative skies﻿—he mentioned it as the remotest possibility﻿—and then six being reluctantly conceded, Ann came forward to the table, sat down and delivered herself of one of her prepared conditions: “ ’Ot and cold water,” she said, “laid on to each room﻿—any’ow.”
It was an idea long since acquired from Sid.
“Yes,” said Kipps, on the hearthrug, “ ’Ot and cold water laid on to each bedroom﻿—we’ve settled on that.”

===Chinese===

「我想随便在哪儿盖，」基普斯说。「这个我还没拿定主意。」建筑师发表意见说，他本想先看看宅地，好知道把他所谓的「丑的一面」安在哪儿；不过「悬空」规划当然也完全可以，按平地算，「前后就先假定」——要是他们愿意这么办。基普斯微微红了脸，暗暗指望这不会使收费大不相同，有点迟疑地说他觉得那也行。
建筑师用一声干咳给自己的题目划出第一段，打开提包，取出一条卷尺、几块硬饼干、一只金属瓶、一副新的狗皮手套、一辆半裹着纸的发条汽车、一束紫罗兰、一纸包小铜螺丝，末了一只鼓囊囊的大笔记本；他把别的物件仔细放回去，翻开笔记本，铅笔往嘴唇上一搭：「你们需要什么样的房间配置？」安一直以最密切的注意、越积越厚的惊恐追着他的一举一动，此刻以埋伏已久者的一声突发作答：「柜——柜子！」
「反正得有。」她接了一句，接住了丈夫的眼色。
建筑师记下了。
「几间房？」他问到次要问题。
两个年轻人对望。这太像下一张订单了，可怕。
「几间卧房，比方说？」建筑师问。
「一间？」基普斯提议，这会儿是不惜代价往小里缩。
「还有格温多林呢。」安说。
「兴许还有客人，」建筑师温和地说。「这可说不准。」
「两间，兴许？」基普斯说。「我们要的不过是个*小*房子，你明白。」
「那顶多算个猎屋——」建筑师说。
他们被顶到了六间；他一间一间稳稳地攻下来，「育婴室」这个词在他们的想象的天幕上掠过——他把它作为最遥远的可能性提了一句——六间既已勉强承认，安走上前到桌边坐下，端出了她预备好的条件之一：「冷热水，」她说，「通到每个房间——不管怎样。」
这是从希德那儿早就学来的主意。
「对，」基普斯在炉前地毯上说，「冷热水通到每间卧房——这个我们定了。」

===Original===

It was the first intimation to the architect that he had to deal with a couple of exceptional originality, and as he had spent the previous afternoon in finding three large houses in *The Builder*, which he intended to combine into an original and copyright design of his own, he naturally struggled against these novel requirements.
